Who Am I

How a person is assembled — from conception to legacy. And how to use the system without becoming its feed

How to read this book

This book is about what the self is made of — and who else is holding its wheel.

Here the self is not a thing but work. The brain keeps assembling a model of “me” from three layers: the body (signals from inside, chemistry, sleep), ties (parents, family line, the pack, school, country, faith) and the story (who you think you are, what you value, what you live for). The ego is how the self sees itself, defends itself and presents itself. Humans have seven ancient tasks — safety, avoiding infection, belonging, rank in the group, finding a mate, keeping a mate, raising your own — and an eighth, human one: meaning. Each of them can be pulled like a lever.

The book follows age — from conception to old age, in seven-year stages. That is an old frame for reading, not a law of biology: where real transitions fall at other ages, the text says so. At the end come the part “Keeping hold of the wheel” with tools, and two bonus chapters.

Every chapter is built the same way: the key fact → how it works → what this means for the self → myths → who plays on this → what to do about it → questions for yourself. A “When to see a specialist” section appears only where there are concrete warning signs.

As in our earlier books, everything rests on checked research: every source has been verified against scientific databases — it exists and has not been retracted. A number in square brackets is a source: tap it to open its card; the full list is at the end of the book. Where data are scarce, come only from mice, or do not exist at all, the text says so.

Prologue

00. Who Am I: assembled from body, people and stories — and who wants to hold the wheel

There is no place in the brain where the self lives. There is a process: in this book’s working model, the brain assembles a model of “me” every second from body signals, from other people’s expectations and from the story it tells about itself. The assembly never stops, so it can be rebuilt — and so others keep trying to build themselves into it.

How it works

No headquarters — a network

When people think about themselves — rate their own traits, recognize their own face, recall their own past — regions along the midline of the cortex light up on brain scans again and again1; a synthesis of many studies showed this. But the same regions can also be active when people remember, imagine the future or think about others. So this is not a “self module” but a hub that many different tasks pass through.

The default mode network — brain regions that become active when attention turns from the outside world inward — takes part in thinking about yourself, but that is not its only job. Recognizing your body, directing a movement and retrieving your life story from memory are different jobs, not necessarily done by the same systems. That is why a brain scan cannot show where a person “is.”

The popular scheme “the brain predicts, and the self is its model” is a theory, not a discovery. It is convenient as a language, but neurophysiological tests of it are still limited (though growing), and critics say that as a unified theory of everything it has stalled. Distributed doesn’t mean made up: the process is real; only its ultimate explanation is disputed.

The working chain that runs through the whole book: signals from the body and environment → an interpretation of “what is happening to me” → a feeling and possible actions → a check against memory and others’ expectations → action → feedback. This is a composite scheme, not a single tested circuit.

The first layer: the body speaks, the brain interprets

Interoception is sensing and interpreting the body’s internal state: heart, breathing, gut, temperature. It has at least three aspects: how accurately you detect a signal, how confident you are that you detect it, and how well your confidence matches your accuracy. In adults these turned out to be separate things (one study). “I feel my heart strongly” is not the same as “I feel it accurately.”

The best-known test — counting your own heartbeats — mostly measures knowledge and guesses about your own pulse. When people were told to rely on nothing but sensation, their scores dropped by about half2. The link between signal accuracy and how intense emotions feel is weak and shows up only when an emotion is deliberately induced3.

The old idea “the body trembles — the mind calls it fear” has taken a new form: the brain predicts the body’s state in advance and sorts it into concepts supplied by culture (a theory supported by syntheses, but disputed). No clear-cut fingerprints of emotions have been found — not in heart rate or sweating, not in specific brain areas. Facial expressions are partly shared: in videos from 144 countries, 16 expressions appeared in similar situations4, but you cannot reliably read from a face what a person feels. For more on emotions in couples, see Feeling for Two — and More.

Sex differences here are averages, not two types of people. On average, men sense their heartbeat more accurately, but not their stomach. Difficulty describing feelings (alexithymia — trouble recognizing and naming your own emotions) is on average slightly higher in men, but the distributions overlap almost completely5. The difference lies mostly in how people think and talk about feelings, not in whether they feel them. It is a scale, not a yes/no diagnosis. With age, adults’ accuracy for the heart signal declines, partly through body weight (as a mediating factor).

The second layer: ties and motives

A person reaches for several things at once: safety, avoiding infection, belonging, rank in the group, finding a mate, keeping a mate, raising your own — and meaning. In the evolutionary reading, these tasks are not steps but competitors: a signal from the environment puts one of them in charge for a while, attention shifts, behavior changes, and other people’s reactions reshape the priorities for next time.

The evidence doesn’t support Maslow’s pyramid as a rigid ladder, and autonomy is the sense that you act of your own will, not necessarily separateness from family (more in chapter 45, which also covers how these tasks become levers).

The third layer: a story the brain writes after the fact

People know well when they are in pain or like something. They know less well why they made a choice. In “choice blindness” experiments, the option a person chose is secretly swapped — and some people confidently explain what they liked about the thing they didn’t choose. This does not mean all choices are invented. It means a sincere explanation is not a report of the cause.

The famous experiments in which a brain signal predicts a button press before the person “decided” involve narrow, arbitrary movements. There is an alternative explanation for these signals — background noise that builds up to a threshold6. They imply neither fatalism nor an end to responsibility.

Not everyone has an inner voice to the same degree: some people think in words almost all the time, others rarely. A complete absence has not yet been proven, and science gives no percentage of “people without a voice.”

From memories, goals and listeners’ responses, a person composes a story about themselves — narrative identity: the past, reassembled in the service of the future. Researchers describe two patterns: “redemption” (bad led to good) and “contamination” (good spoiled by what came after). The first is more often linked to well-being, but this is a link, not a cure: making someone tell the “happy” version is not a proven way to heal them. A story can be coherent and still wrong, and flexibility is not confusion.

Time: what in you is old and what is new

“All your cells renew every seven years” is false. A marker left in the atmosphere by nuclear testing has made it possible to date the DNA of human cells. No new cortical neurons appear in adulthood7: yours are as old as you are. Lens proteins don’t renew at all. Heart muscle cells renew at about 1% a year at age 25 and less in old age — fewer than half are replaced over a lifetime. Fat cells: about a tenth a year. The body replaces a huge number of cells every day, almost all of them blood cells. Whether new neurons are born in the adult hippocampus (the memory area) is still debated.

Where “seven years” came from: in 2005 a newspaper reported a researcher’s estimate that the average age of an adult’s cells may be 7–10 years8. That is an average age, not a replacement schedule. The number itself is much older: Solon divided life into ten seven-year stages, Hippocrates into seven ages, and the Chinese canon Huangdi Neijing measured a woman’s life in sevens and a man’s in eights.

Biology has no such clock. The onset of puberty in girls moved earlier by about three months per decade between 1977 and 20139. Different brain measures peak at different ages; cortical gray matter volume is largest in childhood and declines in the second decade, while white matter grows quickly until mid-to-late adolescence and adds more slowly after that. “The brain matures by 25” is a convention: there is no single marker of maturity. The book proceeds in seven-year stages as a frame for reading, not as a law.

What this means for the self

Given. The cortical neurons you are reading this sentence with are almost all the same ones you had as a child. Also given: the body, which sends signals with varying accuracy, and the culture, which handed out the words for those signals in advance. You don’t choose these.

Built. Everything that makes the self recognizable: connections between neurons, habits, words for feelings, the story about yourself. Change comes through connections and repeated actions, not through cell replacement — no new self shows up by itself on schedule.

The ego is how the self sees, defends and presents itself. Its working tool is explanation after the fact. The brain fills in plausible causes, and the ego probably prefers the ones that protect its image (there is no direct test): “it’s not laziness, I’m just tired,” “that’s just my character.” Sincerity doesn’t make an explanation true, and the certainty “I know my own motives better than anyone” is a poor guide.

But “other people know the real you” is also false. According to the model that describes this, people themselves see their inner states (anxiety, shame) better, while what is visible and judged by others — generosity, harshness — can be seen better from outside. Self-knowledge grows when the inside and outside views are checked against specific actions.

Freedom is not about finding a “true self” and locking it in forever. Personality traits change when behavior changes: not without limit, but for real (the size and durability of the changes are not fully tested). The lever here is not willpower but how the situation is set up: which signals surround you and which response is tied to them. People with high self-control, judging by observational data (not clean experiments), have fewer conflicts with temptation and more useful habits. And whoever controls your situations and the words for your feelings holds part of the wheel.

Myths

Myth: we use only 10% of our brain. In fact: the whole brain works, damage to almost any area takes something away, and no source for the figure has been found. “We could use our minds better” is a different claim, and no one has measured it.

Myth: people are either “left-brained” logicians or “right-brained” creatives. In fact: in a large sample of children and young adults, asymmetry was found only in particular connections, and there are no “left” or “right” people. Even so, 80% of surveyed pre-service teachers in Russia believe in it10.

Myth: beneath the rational cortex sits a “reptilian brain” that commands the instincts. In fact: the brain didn’t grow in layers like an onion; fast habitual reactions and slow deliberation are the work of networks that interact all the time. “That’s the lizard in me” removes responsibility but doesn’t explain behavior.

Myth: willpower is a tank that empties during the day, and sugar refills it. In fact: a test across 36 labs did not confirm the strong version of “ego depletion”11; fatigue is real, but sugar doesn’t refill willpower (more in chapter 46).

Who plays on this

School. Brain myths persist among teachers too — in Russia, China, Europe. “Right-brained child,” “visual learner,” “use your hidden 90%” turn into advice about who you are and how you should learn. Sign: you’re assigned a type by a single test, and the type explains everything.

The market for “reading” people. Microexpressions, “emotion AI” and behavior detectors are sold as a way to see a feeling on a face. A review by the US Government Accountability Office found no support for “behavioral indicators” in airport security, and a review of the research warns against drawing conclusions from faces; the errors hit minorities and neurodivergent people harder. Sign: a decision about a person is made from their face, not from their actions and context.

Gurus, coaches, groups. They sell “ego death,” a “reboot every seven years,” poses for “confidence hormones,” one “authentic identity” forever — none of this has been tested as a whole package. A group may demand a “redemptive” story of your pain: rewrite harm as a lesson. Signs: a promise to find your real self in one step; your story gets corrected until it fits; failure is declared “ego resistance.” More in chapters 25 and 29; for why the self so readily hands the wheel to horoscopes and fate, see chapter 48.

The meditation industry. Eight-week programs give small to moderate benefit for anxiety, depression and pain. But there are many promises beyond the data, and adverse effects occur even in people with no history of problems. Sign: the practice is sold as “switching off the ego” and a path for everyone, with no caveats.

What to do about it

An “if — then” plan. Pick one achievable wish, imagine what it would give you, name the main obstacle currently in your way and tie it to an action: “If X, then I do Y.” According to a synthesis of studies, this gives small to moderate benefit (with open questions about the completeness of what was published)12. Whether it works shows in how often you follow through in comparable situations, not in your mood. It is no substitute for real solutions if the obstacle is a lack of money, safety or treatment: don’t relabel an external barrier as a personal weakness.

Two words for a feeling. When it hits, briefly name it with at least two words: “irritation and shame.” Naming changes the brain’s response and reduces the body’s fear response later (experiments, for example, with fear of spiders), though the feeling doesn’t necessarily fade right away. Then check whether your behavior changed. Sign it’s working: your notes contain more and more different words for feelings, and your behavior changes.

Talk to yourself by name. Before something hard, ask yourself in the voice of a fair-minded adviser: “What does Sam need to do next?” In seven lab studies this helped people cope with anxiety; how it holds up in everyday life is less well known. Track your anxiety before the task and whether you saw it through.

Check inside against outside. When you explain an action to yourself, ask three questions: what did I actually do? what else could explain it? what specific example is other people’s feedback about me based on? This is not a tested method but a technique consistent with the data on the limits of introspection. Translate the wish to “become someone else” into observable actions and look at repetitions, not at promises to yourself.

Questions for yourself

  • Which explanation of my behavior do I repeat most often — and has it ever been checked against facts?
  • What part of my story about myself did other people once tell me?
  • Where am I counting on “willpower” when I could change the situation?
  • Who benefits from seeing me as a certain type?

When to see a specialist

If you have a history of severe trauma or psychosis, discuss it with a specialist before intensive meditation or body practices. If attention to your heartbeat and body increases fear for your heart or panic, stop: in one trial of body-focused training there was a case of an adverse effect — fear for the heart.

Part I. Before Birth: What You Start With

01. Conception: Is It True That Everyone Starts Out Female

At the moment of conception, only one thing about sex is settled: the set of sex chromosomes, XX or XY. Until week 6 everyone has the same sex organs; after that a chain of switches takes over, and the slogan “everyone starts out female” holds only for its last links. The word “boy” or “girl” comes from outside: it is read off the body long before anyone exists to say “I.” The self is assembled the same way: little is settled at once, switches do the rest, and other people apply the first label.

How it works

The first second: a set, not an outcome

The sperm brings an X or a Y, and from fertilization on, every cell of the embryo is XX or XY. Along with that chromosome, the sperm brings half the genome and the marks on its DNA; the other half of the genome comes from the egg.

Differences begin before any sex gland (gonad) exists. XY embryos grow faster even before a testis appears — in several mammal species and, on limited data, in humans; in mice whose sex chromosomes and gonads were genetically uncoupled, XX and XY animals differ in weight, fat and metabolism whatever gonads they have1, 2. Hormones are not the whole story of sex: some differences are written into the cells themselves.

In every XX cell, one of the two X chromosomes goes silent early: in some cells the first, in others the second. An XX body is a mosaic of two kinds of cells, which is why diseases driven by genes on the X show up differently in female carriers. The silencing is incomplete: at least a fifth of the genes on the “switched-off” X still work, at least partly, and this produces measurable sex differences in gene activity across tissues3.

Six weeks for both options

Sources count weeks differently — from conception or obstetrically (two weeks more) — so each milestone is accurate only to within about two weeks. In weeks 4–5 the gonadal primordium forms — the future testes or ovaries. By weeks 5–6 every embryo has both duct systems: the Wolffian ducts, precursors of the male internal tract, and the Müllerian ducts, precursors of the female one.

The external organs are shared too: both the penis and the clitoris grow from the same genital tubercle (how they are built in adults is covered in Alchemy Without Magic). Until week 6, sex cannot be told from the gonads.

The switch: how a testis is built

The SRY gene on the Y chromosome is not a “masculinity gene” but a trigger. It turns on in the supporting cells of the gonad and pushes a related gene, SOX9, above a threshold; SOX9 turns these cells into Sertoli cells, which build the testis and actively silence ovarian genes.

In humans, both genes turn on around day 40 after conception. Genetics showed that it really is a switch: a fragment of the mouse Sry gene launches male development in XX mice, and a broken SOX9 in humans turns XY development toward the female side. The human timing is known from a few dozen embryonic gonads; the threshold mechanics were worked out mostly in mice4, 5, 6.

By day 53 the testis turns on the genes for two signals:

  • Anti-Müllerian hormone dismantles the Müllerian ducts. The female tract is laid down in everyone, and in an XY embryo it is removed — by week 10 it is gone.
  • Testosterone from the testis (from about week 8, peaking in weeks 11–14) preserves the Wolffian ducts and turns them into the epididymides, vasa deferentia and seminal vesicles. Testosterone is converted into dihydrotestosterone, which from about week 9 shapes the external organs; by week 14 the male urethra is complete.

The male path is not an add-on to the female one but an active reworking of a shared blueprint: the testis is built, the female tract is dismantled, the external organs are shaped by a hormone.

The ovary is a construction project too

The ovary has its own program, and it too suppresses the other one. In rare human families, a broken copy of either of two genes in this program turns XX development male without any SRY, and an extra dose of one of them shifts XY development toward the female side. In mice the ovary has to be maintained for life: switch off one of these genes in an adult female, and ovarian cells remodel into something like testis cells7, 8.

Reviews describe the choice of path as a network of two programs, each suppressing the other — the design of a switch, not a volume knob. The word “default” came from experiments in the 1940s: the female tract formed even when there was no gonad at all. It then stuck to female development as a whole, although the researcher behind those experiments did not read it that broadly.

Where “default” really applies

The ducts and external organs follow the female path if the testis hormones do not act: either they are absent or the cells cannot hear them. With androgens (male hormones such as testosterone), the folds around the future urethra fuse into the urethra of the penis; without them they stay open and become the labia minora. A natural experiment confirms this: in XY people with complete androgen insensitivity, the testes make the hormone, but the receptor — the receiving protein on the cells — does not register it, and the body looks female on the outside. In Denmark, androgen insensitivity (mostly complete) was typically diagnosed at age seven and a half9, 10, 11.

When a link works differently

Differences of sex development — the term doctors have used since 2006 for what used to be called “intersex” — are grouped by the step that went differently:

  • number of chromosomes — one X instead of a pair (Turner syndrome), an extra X alongside a Y (Klinefelter syndrome);
  • gonad assembly — changes in the genes that build it (SRY, SOX9 and others);
  • hormone production — congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH): the adrenal glands make excess androgens, and the external organs of an XX embryo shift toward the male type, while in XY boys with CAH, prenatal androgen levels are mostly normal;
  • hormone action — androgen insensitivity.

What the father brings

A sperm cell takes about 64 days to mature, so the father’s lifestyle in the 2–3 months before conception can show up in the sperm. Sperm precursor cells, meanwhile, keep dividing throughout life, and over the years they accumulate typos — new mutations that neither parent carries.

The genomes of more than fifteen hundred families show that each year of the father’s age adds about one and a half such mutations to the child. Mothers pass them on too, but even in young parents the father contributes several times as many — apparently not only from copying errors but also from DNA damage. Some mutations give precursor cells a growth advantage, and their descendants multiply in an aging testis: this is how rare “paternal” syndromes arise, such as achondroplasia, a disorder of bone growth12, 13, 14, 15.

In humans, the marks on sperm DNA change with age and after weight-loss surgery, and differ with obesity, smoking and cannabis use (small samples). What this means for the child is almost unknown: in humans the links are weak and inconsistent; the mechanism has been proven only in animals. Marks and “family programs” are covered in chapter 04; the biological clocks of both parents, in chapter 31.

Who survives to birth

According to the most complete reconstruction, boys and girls are roughly equal in number at conception; this cannot be counted directly, and the estimate has opponents. After that, losses come in phases: in the first week male embryos die more often, in the next 10–15 weeks female ones, in weeks 28–35 male ones again, and over the whole pregnancy female losses are greater. Slightly more boys are born, but in the second half of pregnancy and after it they are the more vulnerable: in data on almost two million births, boys have more spontaneous preterm births and newborn illnesses, and under natural conditions, more deaths before age five16, 17, 18. Why remains a matter of hypothesis (for example, male fetuses are larger and grow faster).

What this means for the self

All three layers of the self are visible here, and they arrive at different times.

The body in the first second is given. A set of chromosomes, half the genome from each parent, new mutations whose number grows with the father’s age, DNA marks whose weight is not yet known. None of this is chosen or earned. Part of what makes a person different from both parents is, literally, new typos in their DNA.

The body in the first months is built. Gonads, ducts, external organs, the X mosaic (what the hormones of the womb do to the brain is covered in chapter 02). Each switch is a threshold in a network, not a button: SRY only pulls the trigger, and the ovary is maintained by its own program. Mouse experiments add something unexpected: the sex of ovarian tissue, even in an adult mouse, is not a state but ongoing maintenance. This is the model for the whole self in this book: stable does not mean static.

Ties: the label comes from outside. The word “boy” or “girl” is read off the latest and most hormone-dependent layer — the look of the body — and when the look is ambiguous, it is assigned. The layers usually match, so the seam is invisible; where they diverge, it is clear they are different things. A girl with XY chromosomes and a nonfunctional androgen receptor often lives with the label for years before the deeper layer surfaces. Usually no one checks the chromosome set, but everyone knows the label.

The “I” appears later (how is covered in chapter 07), and by then the label has already taken root. The self-image is built not from scratch but around someone else’s word: the ego then defends it as its own, hangs self-esteem on it (“am I man enough?”, “am I woman enough?”) and cuts a mask for others to fit it.

It is easy to hang a hierarchy on the label: “male is primary, female is what’s left” or “female is the original, male is the add-on.” Biology supports neither: both programs are active and suppress each other. There is no “stronger sex” in these data either: each has its own windows of fragility. The sex label is the first lever that others pull later, through the task of belonging (being “one of us”); how “the real man” and “the proper girl” are sold is covered in chapter 20.

Story is freedom. The cells cannot be rebuilt: the chromosome set and the typos remain, and the organs are mostly built before birth. What can be rebuilt is something else — what the label means to you.

With differences of sex development, doctors openly admit that an individual’s gender development — which sex a person will come to feel they are — cannot be predicted with certainty19, 20. Data from a small sample: more than half of XY children with a severe congenital malformation who were assigned female later declared themselves boys. The first label is a decision made by other people before you can be asked; what the self does with it is decided later and in another layer — that is the subject of chapter 19.

Myths

Myth: Everyone starts out female: the female is the passive original form, and the male is added on top. In fact: Everyone starts out XX or XY with a blueprint for both options, and both programs are active: the testis is built on the SRY signal, the ovary by its own genes, which in mice maintain it for life. Only the ducts and external organs become female “by default” — when the testis hormones do not act.

Myth: 1.7% of people are born intersex. In fact: That figure uses the broadest definition — any deviation from the “ideal” male or female body, including Klinefelter and Turner syndromes and late-onset forms of CAH. By the narrow medical definition — atypical genitals, or chromosomes that do not match anatomy — it is about 0.02%, and if chromosomal and hormonal diagnoses are added together, on the order of 0.1–0.2% (a rough count from registries)21, 22. Only a figure stated together with its definition is honest.

Myth: An older father passes on “damaged genes.” In fact: There really are more new mutations, but the absolute risk to the child is small23 (more in chapter 31).

Who plays on this

Family and culture — even before birth. Where sons are preferred, sex is found out in advance and pregnancies with girls are terminated: the skewed sex ratios at birth in China, India, Armenia, Azerbaijan and Georgia are the trace of such abortions; for China alone the estimate is 11.9 million “missing” female births, while Russia shows no skew. In China and India, girls under five also die more often than expected, which points to discrimination18, 26. The sign: noticeably more boys at birth than is natural — here the label decides whether you are born at all.

The headline and self-medication. “Sperm counts have halved” is the conclusion of large pooled analyses spanning almost half a century; studies that used a single method for two decades show a flat trend, and the debate is not settled. Even a real decline from a high average may leave most men able to conceive, but more of them fall below the level where more sperm still means a better chance of conception. What’s dangerous is the reaction: testosterone and anabolic steroids suppress sperm production so strongly that testosterone was tested as a male contraceptive — in more than half of healthy men, sperm disappeared completely, and after the men stopped, it took months to come back27, 28, 29, 30. The sign: someone who wants children is offered testosterone or anabolic steroids (how “male power” is sold is covered in chapter 29).

What to do about it

  • A number only with its definition. When you hear a percentage of “such people,” ask what exactly was counted. It works if you can see how one topic yields figures that differ almost a hundredfold.
  • Relative risk into absolute risk. “One and a half times higher” says nothing without a baseline: convert it to “how many out of a hundred.” It works if, for a headline about parental age, you can say how many cases there were and how many there are now.
  • Preparation — at least 2–3 months. A major medical review calls on both parents to prepare for conception; for sperm, the basic set is a normal weight, no smoking, no heavy drinking and no cannabis. No testosterone or anabolic steroids. The measure is a semen analysis before and after 3 months against the WHO lower limits (a single sample fluctuates), plus time to pregnancy. The limit of knowledge: rapid weight loss or surgery may not restore sperm quality, and no human trial has shown the effect of any of this on the child’s health.

Questions for yourself

  • What words about you were spoken before you had an “I” — and which of them do you still consider your own?
  • In your idea of your own sex, what was settled in the cells, what was built by hormones, and what were you told?
  • What in you defends itself when someone touches your sex label — your body or your self-image?
  • What “fact” about men or women do you repeat without knowing what was counted in it?

When to see a specialist

A newborn with atypical genitals needs a multidisciplinary team of specialists urgently; parents need full information and support groups; early surgery that alters the genitals or removes the germ cells calls for caution. If such a baby loses weight or feeds poorly in the first weeks, it may be the salt-wasting form of CAH (the body loses salt): it is life-threatening, and the risk depends on how quickly the diagnosis is made.

02. Hormones in the womb: how a “male” and a “female” brain get drawn — and why the brain is still a mosaic

From a single brain scan, a computer guesses a person’s sex correctly 93 times out of a hundred. Take head size out of the scan, and accuracy drops to about 70%. Hormones in the womb really do draw differences on the brain, but what they draw are averages across a crowd, not two finished templates. The self starts out with a mix of traits, not a “male” or “female” kit.

How it works

Two actions of one hormone

In 1959, researchers injected pregnant guinea pigs with testosterone, and their daughters grew up with permanently altered mating behavior1. This gave the field its central framework: in an early window a hormone organizes the brain for the long term, while in adulthood it only activates what is already built, and the effect fades once the hormone is gone. It later turned out that the line between the two actions is blurry, and that sex chromosomes act on brain cells directly, without hormones. So differences have three sources: hormones, genes inside brain cells, and experience. They add up, and a single observed difference won’t tell you which one was at work.

When the window is open

A male fetus has higher testosterone than a female fetus from roughly week 8 to week 24 of pregnancy. In amniotic fluid at weeks 11–21, boys’ levels are on average several times higher, and in girls the hormone is often below the detection limit2. Where the signal comes from — how the forming testes start production — is covered in chapter 01.

A second window opens after birth. This is “mini-puberty,” a brief surge of sex hormones in the infant. In boys, testosterone peaks at 1–3 months and subsides by six months; in girls, the ovaries switch on, and this activity lasts up to age two. Why humans have this surge is not fully understood. Evidence that it affects behavior comes from dozens of infants and is contradictory.

Some propose counting adolescent puberty as a third window, but there is little evidence of a long-term effect. What hormones do in adolescence is covered in chapter 15.

A natural experiment

Hormone experiments on human fetuses are off limits, so the key knowledge came from a disease. In congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH), the fetal adrenal glands release excess androgens — “male” hormones — from the very start. In a girl with CAH, the brain receives that excess in the womb.

The finding has held up across many independent samples: these girls play noticeably more “boyishly” than other girls3. Boys with CAH barely differ from other boys: their androgens are high anyway. The effect grows with the severity of the genetic defect, that is, with the dose. Parents of these girls nudged them toward “girl” toys and praised them for it even more than parents of unaffected daughters did — and the daughters still more often chose “boy” toys4.

An important limit: this is a large hormone excess. Within the normal range in healthy fetuses, testosterone shows an inconsistent link to children’s play: some studies find a link (stronger in girls), others don’t. One reason is that the hormone is measured once, in the mother’s blood or the amniotic fluid, not where it acts.

Toys: biology and fast learning

The difference between boys and girls in toy choice is one of the largest in psychology, and the gap widens with age. Male rhesus macaques also reach for wheeled toys more often. That argues for a biological component.

But at 12 months, both boys and girls preferred dolls to cars5. The difference shows up in the second year, and a child who learned the words “boy” and “girl” earlier (on average around 19 months) starts playing “by sex” earlier6. Pink and blue appear as preferences only after age two and are unstable7. The picture: hormones tilt interest, and the child, having learned their category, starts acting it out and amplifies the tilt.

The brain: size, mosaic and classifier

Men’s brains are on average about 11% larger, and this difference is present at birth8. Size alone shifts proportions: the share of white matter, connections within and between hemispheres, the volumes of many regions. Correct for skull size, and differences in individual regions become small, with sex explaining about 1% of the variation. Loud claims about teenagers’ “differently wired” brains run into the same size problem and replicate poorly in other samples.

Two facts are often set against each other. First: looking at regions one by one, almost everyone’s brain is a mosaic — some traits sit closer to the “male” end, some to the “female” end, and brains with all traits at one end are very rare9. Second: add up hundreds of traits, and an algorithm still guesses sex better than chance — up to 93% with size and about 70% without10. There is no contradiction. The groups differ on average and in the sum of features, but overlap heavily, and that does not add up to two brain “types.” Men show, on average, a wider spread on many brain measures.

What shows up in the mind

Across hundreds of pooled results from 106 meta-analyses, the average sex difference in psychology is small: the distributions overlap by about 92%11. Some traits show a large difference: interest in “things versus people” on vocational questionnaires. But these are questionnaire interests, not abilities. So “how do the sexes differ?” can only be answered honestly one trait at a time.

Diagnostic skews are a separate matter. Autism is diagnosed about three times as often in boys, while girls are underdiagnosed. The idea that excess testosterone in the womb is to blame is disputed: one sample found a link; another, and girls with CAH, did not. Depression is more common in girls, but the gap opens around age 12, peaks at 13–15 and narrows in adults — this does not prove an innate “female” brain trait; the causes are mixed.

Orientation: what is laid down before birth

Orientation has no single cause. The best-known fact is the “older brother effect”: in Western samples, each older brother born to the same mother raises a man’s odds of homosexuality by about a third12. Only biological brothers count; step- and adoptive brothers do not, however many years they were raised together13. This points to pregnancy, not upbringing. The hypothesis is a maternal immune response to proteins of a male fetus that strengthens with each son; one study found higher antibody levels in such mothers, but it has not yet been replicated.

In absolute numbers the effect is small: assuming a baseline probability of about 2%, one brother raises it to about 2.6%. In 2023 a large reanalysis called the effect partly a statistical artifact; data from huge Dutch registries say the effect is real — in both men and women. The question is open.

Twin studies put the genetic share at about a third in men and about a fifth in women (wide intervals); the rest is environment, mostly non-shared, which includes conditions in the womb. Shared family environment is close to zero in men and noticeably larger in women14. A genome analysis of nearly half a million people found five DNA regions, and all common variants together do not predict a given person’s orientation15. There is no “gay gene”; one check failed to confirm the famous X-chromosome finding, and a later one found no gene either.

A large androgen excess in the womb also shifts orientation: among women with CAH, bisexuality and homosexuality are more common, and more so the stronger the exposure — but most are still heterosexual. For gender identity — the sense of “I am a man / I am a woman” — hormones likewise tilt rather than decide: among people with XY chromosomes and rare enzyme defects who were raised as girls, between two fifths and two thirds later changed gender, but not all16.

Age and culture: almost all these data come from the West. The older brother effect has also been found in Samoa, where older sisters matter too. There are no verified data on the topic for Russia or China.

What this means for the self

Given: a tilt, not a fate. The hormones of the womb and of mini-puberty are the first layer of the body, the one nobody chooses. It tilts interests, some brain traits and orientation. The tilt is measurable across thousands of people and says almost nothing about one.

Built: a mosaic. Everyone has their own set of traits lying at different ends of the scales. That is why statistically “male” interests and statistically “female” reactions sit comfortably in one person. This is not a glitch or an exception — it is the norm for almost everyone.

Built on top: the category. By age two a child learns which label they carry and starts acting it out — the first brick of self-image. From then on the ego measures itself against the group average: “am I man enough,” “am I woman enough.” But the average line describes a crowd, not you: the distributions overlap by 92% on average, and a mosaic in almost everyone means some of your traits lie at the “other” end. Self-esteem pegged to the average line will keep coming up short.

Defenses. When a fact about yourself doesn’t fit the label, the ego defends the label: it hides the interest, mocks it in others, rewrites it as “just messing around.” This is a defense of the image, not the truth about the body. Biology tilts orientation and identity before birth, and no study has shown that upbringing creates them — so shaming yourself for them means arguing with something that was never chosen.

Where freedom lies. You can’t rebuild the womb-made brain. You can stop reading the average as an order. The question “what is typical of me?” is more honest asked about each trait separately than about “my sex as a whole.” How the label becomes your face in others’ eyes is the subject of chapter 19; how it is sold, of chapter 20.

Myths

Myth: Male and female brains are two different types. In fact: The differences are averages and are mostly explained by brain size; after correction, sex explains about 1% of the variation, and almost every brain is a mosaic8, 9.

Myth: The ratio of index-finger to ring-finger length reveals womb testosterone, character and orientation. In fact: On average men’s ratio is slightly lower, but hormones at birth and in the amniotic fluid are not linked to it, the first headline finding did not replicate, and the link to aggression is near zero. The method is useless for assessing one person.

Myth: Pink is girls’ biological color. In fact: Infants show no color difference; preferences appear after age two and are unstable7.

Myth: Homosexuality comes from upbringing or from one gene. In fact: No study has shown that upbringing creates orientation, and there is no single gene14, 15 (more in chapter 18).

Who plays on this

The market for simple tests. Finger ratio is cheap to measure and widely advertised — as a “window” into hormones, character, athletic or professional potential. The harm: false labels pinned on children and job candidates. The sign: one bodily measurement is promised to tell who you are.

The “different planets” market. A finding about one measured trait gets swapped for the marketable formula “the sexes are different species.” The sign: no specific trait or group is named, but there is a confident “all men” or “all women.”

What to do about it

Translate “men / women” into one trait. When you hear a general claim, ask: which trait, measured in whom, how much do the groups overlap. If there is no answer, it is not knowledge but advertising. It’s working when such phrases no longer sting your self-esteem.

Describe yourself by traits, not by sex. The group average doesn’t tell you what to like or who to become. Test it on yourself: note what you hide as “not right for my sex,” and how your well-being and life change when you stop. That is evidence about you, not a general law.

With children — variety without a diagnosis. Offer different toys and don’t read the choice as a verdict: “cross-sex” play in childhood is statistically linked to later orientation, but it is not a test and not a reason to apply pressure17. The sign it’s working: the child doesn’t ask permission to choose.

Questions for yourself

  • Which of my traits do I consider “wrong” only because it doesn’t match the average for my sex?
  • Whose yardstick of a “real” man or woman do I carry — and who gave it to me?
  • What do I hide so that my image in others’ eyes stays “right”?

When to see a specialist

If a newborn has ambiguous genitalia or suspected CAH, they need care from a team of specialists in a single center: how identity will develop in an individual case cannot be predicted18.

03. Nine Months: What a Baby Hears, Tastes and Learns from Mom — and What Dad Brings

What the brain remembers most reliably before birth is one person’s voice, muffled by tissue and water. By birth, a baby recognizes that voice, responds differently to native and foreign vowels and, judging by small experiments, remembers the flavors of the mother’s food. The womb teaches recognition, not character. Many loud claims of the “pregnancy → whole life” kind shrink to small effects or vanish under strict testing.

How it works

The womb is a low-pass filter

Sound reaches the fetus through the belly, tissue and amniotic fluid. High frequencies fade; low ones pass through and are even amplified. Inside the uterus the mother’s voice is louder than outside, while other voices are quieter: it is the loudest speech in the fetus’s world1.

Hearing switches on in stages. The fetus responds to low tones with movement from 19 weeks, and to high tones, which carry consonants, by 33–35 weeks. So the melody of speech is heard first, words later. Stable sleep and wake states like a newborn’s appear around 38 weeks (none were found before 36), and how the fetus reacts depends on which state it is in.

What the fetus remembers

The mother’s voice is the most reliable fact in this chapter. Newborns change their sucking rhythm to turn on a recording of their mother’s voice rather than another woman’s (a small classic experiment). Full-term fetuses speed up their heart rate in response to the mother’s voice and slow it in response to a stranger’s. Fetuses also respond to the father’s voice after a week of daily reading aloud, but after birth babies still prefer only the mother’s2. The explanation is simple: her voice is louder and always there. The preference at birth is a mix of experience and acoustics.

The melody of language. The melody of the native language can already be heard in a newborn’s cry (more in chapter 10).

A specific sound. A melody played regularly in the last trimester produced a stronger brain response at birth and at four months. The learned response is visible from 34 weeks and lasts at least until 38. This is memory for a particular sound. No study has measured whether music in the womb makes a child smarter.

What this recognition is for is not fully clear. No benefit beyond calming has been proven.

Taste: the mother’s food in the amniotic fluid

Food aromas pass from the mother’s blood into the amniotic fluid and milk; the fetus swallows and smells them. After the mother took garlic capsules, the fluid smelled different. Babies of mothers who ate anise turned toward the smell of anise in their first days of life. Fetuses at 32–36 weeks made a laughter-like face after a carrot capsule and a crying-like face after bitter kale: they tell flavors apart, but this is not yet a preference that lasts for years.

Beyond that, the effect weakens. Babies of mothers who drank carrot juice late in pregnancy grimaced less at carrot cereal but did not eat noticeably more of it3. A review could not link the mother’s diet to the child’s overall eating habits. Repetition after birth works better: after a few days of green beans, infants ate noticeably more of them.

Maternal stress: what reaches the fetus

The proposed chain: stress → cortisol (the stress hormone) → weaker placental protection (a placental enzyme inactivates the mother’s cortisol) → the fetus’s stress system is tuned differently → the child’s behavior. The evidence varies from link to link: a small study found the enzyme weakened with anxiety, and reviews disagree on the effect on the child’s cortisol.

Natural experiments suggest that real hardship matters more than distress. After the Quebec ice storm (a small study), children of mothers with large objective losses had lower intelligence and language scores at age five and a half, while the intensity of distress predicted nothing. After the earthquake in Chile, exposure in early pregnancy did not affect cognition in middle-class children but hit children from poor families hard4.

Overall, depression and anxiety in pregnancy are associated with emotional and behavioral difficulties in children (reviews of dozens of studies). But when genes are taken into account, the link shrinks. In Norway, genes shared by mother and child explained about 40% of such difficulties, and the “through the womb” pathway added nothing beyond the mother’s current mood5. In a study of children born after IVF, a child’s anxiety tracked the mother’s current mood, not the pregnancy. A small window for a prenatal contribution remains: the mother’s depression in pregnancy predicted depression in adolescence, while the same depression in the father did not.

Famine is a separate story: it is undernutrition, not stress. In people exposed in the womb to the Dutch Hunger Winter, the consequences depended on timing: famine early in pregnancy meant heart problems and excess weight; famine late in pregnancy, blood sugar problems. A DNA mark on one gene still differed from their siblings’ six decades later. Some effects of famine were found only in women, and human data on sex differences in stress effects are contradictory.

Substances

Alcohol crosses the placenta. No safe dose has been established. Binge drinking clearly harms a child’s thinking. Light drinking showed no defects in large cohorts — but moderate drinkers are on average wealthier, and that masks harm. In one genetic study, children with variants in alcohol-processing genes had IQs a couple of points lower per variant — but only if the mother drank moderately, not if she drank less6. The risky window is the weeks before the pregnancy is noticed: in two regions of Russia, women cut back on drinking only once they knew.

Smoking reliably harms the body: stillbirth, clefts, lower birth weight. But its link with ADHD nearly disappears in sibling comparisons — a risk that looked doubled becomes indistinguishable from no link at all7. It seems family and genes, not smoke, created this link. Cannabis is linked to preterm birth and low birth weight, but its links with the child’s cognitive development turned out to be the same whether the mother or the father used it — pointing to a shared environment rather than chemistry. Caffeine up to 200 mg a day is, according to medical societies, not a major contributor to miscarriage; observational studies see risk rise with dose (biases are possible), while genetic studies found no effect on miscarriage.

Birth

Labor is a feedback loop: stretching of the uterus triggers oxytocin release in the mother’s brain, oxytocin strengthens contractions, and contractions stretch it further. Oxytocin from an IV drip does not cross the blood-brain barrier (the filter between blood and brain) and does not raise the newborn’s level8. The link between induced labor and autism disappears in sibling comparisons.

Skin-to-skin contact right after birth: more exclusive breastfeeding from discharge to six months, higher blood sugar in the baby (dozens of trials). Starting immediately or after an hour made no difference to feeding at six months. After a C-section, contact raises the breastfeeding rate at discharge, but not later; on the father’s chest it calms the baby. For babies with low birth weight, continuous contact from the first hours cut deaths in the first month by about a quarter9.

A C-section changes the first gut microbes. Asthma and allergies are slightly more common afterward, but C-sections account for only 1–4% of cases, and in sibling comparisons some of the links weaken. Transferring maternal microbes to the baby did not change the microbiome in a pilot trial; outside of research, doctors do not recommend it.

Birth trauma (post-traumatic stress disorder after childbirth) occurs in about 4–5% of mothers and 1% of fathers. It is most strongly linked to a negative birth experience; operative delivery, depression in pregnancy, fear and lack of support also matter. Continuous support during labor helps; a one-time “debriefing” afterward showed almost no benefit.

Feeding

Ordinary observational studies credit breastfeeding with a few IQ points, but mothers with higher intelligence breastfeed more often, and in one sibling analysis the gain drops to a fraction of a point. The strongest trial (Belarus, more than ten thousand pairs) randomized a breastfeeding support program and produced a gain in verbal IQ at six and a half that had nearly faded by sixteen10, 11. It did not reduce overweight. What is confirmed is something else: fewer gut infections and less eczema in the first year. In one study, breastfeeding duration by itself did not predict the mother’s sensitivity or attachment security — intention and sensitivity before birth did.

What dad brings

Half the genome, plus the marks on that DNA; new mutations that come with the father’s age are covered in chapter 01. A sperm cell takes about 64 days to mature, so a man’s lifestyle in the 2–3 months before conception may affect the sperm that fertilizes the egg. Men who smoke, are overweight or use cannabis have different marks in their sperm (small samples). That these marks change the child’s health has been shown only in animals: RNA fragments from the sperm of mice on a high-fat diet, injected into an embryo, caused metabolic disorders. There are no trials in which changing the father’s behavior changed the child’s health.

Fatherhood changes the father’s own body: in Filipino men who became fathers and lived with a partner, morning testosterone fell by a quarter, more so in those who did more childcare (more in chapter 32). Fathers get depression too, more often in the first six months after the birth.

What this means for the self

The body is partly given. Through the placenta comes what the child never agreed to: doses of alcohol, smoke, famine. Strong exposures leave a mark on the body — weight, organs, metabolism. Ordinary ups and downs of mood and daily life, once genes and family are accounted for, leave a small mark that is hard to separate from other influences.

Ties come before the self. The first thing that feels like “mine” is not a person but something familiar: a voice, the melody of a language, a smell. This is not yet attachment (that is built after birth — chapter 06) but familiarity. What it is for, beyond calming, is unknown. The voices of the father, a grandmother, a second language join this circle after birth.

Story is the most dangerous layer in this chapter. Here the ego builds convenient explanations: “Mom was stressed — that’s why I’m anxious,” “I was born by C-section — that’s why my immune system is weak,” “I wasn’t breastfed — I was shortchanged.” Such an explanation protects: the cause lies where you can’t reach it. The data mostly don’t support this: the link with a child’s anxiety is largely explained by shared genes and the mother’s current mood.

Science itself had a similar bias. Out of habit, researchers looked for causes first in the mother and the pregnancy; that habit shaped the questions and then confirmed itself12. In fact, exposures are often social — famine, disaster, poverty — genes are shared, and the father and the environment after birth matter too.

In a parent, the same ego works in reverse: “I ruined my child.” Guilt and shame about how a baby is fed are themselves linked to worse maternal mental health — among both breastfeeding and formula-feeding mothers. Freedom here lies in precision: the womb gave recognition and small shifts in probabilities. There is no verdict in these data.

Myths

Myth: playing Mozart to the belly will make a child smarter. In fact: no one has measured intelligence after music in the womb. The “Mozart effect” itself did not replicate in adults under strict testing and disappeared once arousal and mood were accounted for. The fetus remembers a melody — and that’s all.

Myth: stress in pregnancy programs anxiety for life. In fact: a sizable part of the link is explained by shared genes and the mother’s current mood; follow-ups go up to age 18, and “for life” has not been shown. It seems real hardship does more harm, especially in poverty.

Myth: the first hour of skin-to-skin is the only window; miss it and attachment will suffer. In fact: there are no trials in which missing that hour permanently took anything away (more in chapter 06).

Myth: breastfeeding means intelligence and a slim figure. In fact: the gain in verbal IQ in the trial had nearly disappeared by age 16, and weight was not affected. There is a benefit, but a different one: fewer infections and less eczema in the first year.

Who plays on this

The “development before birth” market. Music and belly speakers are sold with the promise of intelligence no one has measured. On top of that, the uterus amplifies low frequencies, and scientists who measured sound inside it warned that noise could harm the fetus.

Formula marketing. In China, the rate of exclusive breastfeeding to six months fell more than threefold between 1998 and 2013; among the barriers, researchers name formula advertising and care by grandmothers.

“Breast only” campaigns. Rolling out one British hospital program improved breastfeeding only in the first week and may create unrealistic expectations. The risk of depression is highest among those who planned to breastfeed and couldn’t.

The signs of a lever are the same everywhere: a promise of an exceptional result, “the only window,” guilt for deviating from the rule.

What to do about it

  1. Talk and sing — just because. Ordinary speech and singing nearby are enough for the baby to recognize the voice at birth. No belly speakers. Other close people, including the father, get to know the baby after birth: voice, hands, skin-to-skin.
  2. Prepare for conception 2–3 months ahead. A healthy weight; no smoking, heavy drinking or cannabis. That is how long sperm takes to mature; that this improves the child’s health has not been proven. Alcohol is best cut out at the planning stage: the risky weeks come before the pregnancy is noticeable. How to check: a semen analysis before and three months later (a single sample varies a lot).
  3. Birth: support and contact. A continuous trained support person during labor. Right after — the baby on the bare chest of the mother or father, face visible, an adult close by (unsupervised contact is occasionally dangerous). The sign it’s working: a successful first feed.
  4. Check your “before birth” story. If you explain yourself by your mother’s pregnancy, the way you were born or how you were fed, ask what that link rests on. It will often turn out weaker than it seems and be explained by genes and the family after birth.

Questions for yourself

  • Which trait do I explain by what happened before I was born — and what does that explanation take off my shoulders?
  • Whose voice, language and taste feel “my own” simply because they came first?
  • If I’m a parent: where does my guilt rest on data, and where on a rule someone sold me?

When to see a specialist

Depression or anxiety in pregnancy — treat it now: this helps the mother herself, regardless of the debates about the child. A father can become depressed too — the same rule applies. Avoiding everything that reminds you of the birth, including related medical care, is a reason to seek trauma-focused therapy: it helps, while a one-time “debriefing” of the birth showed almost no benefit.

04. Inheritance: genes, epigenetics, family stories — and where “ancestral programs” begin

Across almost 18,000 measured traits, heritability averages about half — and yet no one is “half made of genes.” Three different things come from your ancestors: DNA, traces of what the fetus itself went through, and the stories a family tells and acts out. An “ancestral program” is an attempt to pass off the third as the first: as if a great-grandmother’s fear were written into your cells. This chapter is about what in your inheritance is truly given, what gets built on top, and where knowledge ends.

How it works

What “heritability” actually measures

The twin logic is simple: identical twins share almost all their DNA, fraternal twins about half. If identical twins are more alike, the gap is attributed to genes. A summary of fifty years of such studies puts the average at 49%1. For personality traits it is about 40%: higher in twin studies, almost half that in family and adoption studies (the method noticeably shifts the figure; men and women do not differ significantly)2. The method’s main assumption — that identical and fraternal twins share their environment to the same degree — has been tested: twins who wrongly believed they were identical or fraternal gave the same estimates.

Heritability is a statistic about differences between people in a given country and a given environment. It does not say that half of your character is “from genes,” that half is fixed, or that differences between countries are explained the same way. High heritability and malleability coexist: in Sweden, brothers raised in adoptive families scored on average a few points higher on intelligence tests than their biological brothers who stayed home (men only, intelligence only)3.

For mental health the figures vary: depression about 37%, schizophrenia about 81%. For depression, the contribution of the shared family environment is barely visible in twin data. That doesn’t mean family doesn’t matter: “shared environment” here means only what makes siblings alike, which means each child lives through much of family life in their own way.

Why genes get “louder” with age

The heritability of intelligence rises: 41% at age 9, 55% at 12, 66% at 17 (twin data from four countries). The proposed explanation is not a “genetic switch” flipping on but choice. A child increasingly picks an environment to match their inclinations: books, friends, activities (active gene–environment correlation). They also draw responses to their traits from others: a quiet child gets one kind of treatment, a pushy one another (evocative correlation). On this view, genes act through what a person does and the surroundings they build for themselves.

There is no gene “for” anything

For years researchers hunted for single genes for depression, intelligence, and violence. Almost all of it fell apart. Of novel studies of gene–environment interaction, almost all reported significant results; of replication attempts, about a quarter did4. In a preregistered test on hundreds of thousands of people, not one candidate gene for depression held up.

The most persistent label is the “warrior gene,” a low-activity variant of the MAOA gene. It sits on the X chromosome. In men it slightly strengthens the link between childhood abuse and later antisocial behavior; in women the data are contradictory. Several cohorts confirmed the effect; a large sample of American men did not. It is a weak statistical boost, not a switch. The label itself came from a small experiment in which people punished an offender with hot sauce.

The real genetics of complex traits involves thousands of tiny variants, which are combined into a “polygenic score.” For educational attainment it explains 12–16% of the differences, and a sizable part of that prediction does not run through the child’s own genes. In Iceland, the genes parents did not pass on to a child were still linked to the child’s education — about a third as strongly as the transmitted ones5. This is “genetic nurture”: parents’ genes work through the home they build. Outside Europe such scores predict several times less well — the data are almost all European.

Longevity is a good example of keeping things in proportion. Twin estimates put its heritability at about a quarter; corrected for people’s tendency to choose similar partners, below 10%. In American observational studies, five low-risk habits are linked to 12–14 extra years of life after 50. Genes matter more for whether you reach a hundred: siblings of centenarians reach a hundred several times more often.

Epigenetics: marks on top of the text

Methylation is the attachment of a small chemical group to a letter of DNA. It can keep a gene from being read, but the effect depends on the location in the genome, so “a mark switches a gene off” is a simplification. The marks themselves are largely determined by the DNA sequence: on average the heritability of a mark is about a fifth, and thousands of marks are inherited differently in males and females. So “epigenetics versus genes” is a false dichotomy: according to a critical review, the epigenome is shaped mainly by the DNA itself and how it is read6.

The environment does leave marks — on the person who lived through it. Smokers’ blood shows differences at thousands of DNA sites, and some stay altered in those who quit. In the Gambia, marks depend on whether a person was conceived in the hungry season or the season of plenty. Dutch people conceived during the Hunger Winter of 1944–45 had lower methylation of the IGF2 gene six decades later than their same-sex siblings7. Timing mattered: famine early in pregnancy is linked to heart disease and obesity, famine late in pregnancy to impaired glucose metabolism. Schizophrenia (after famine in the first trimester) and weight gain (after famine early in pregnancy) rose only in women. The Chinese famine replicated the rise in schizophrenia, and the Ukrainian famine of 1932–33 the rise in diabetes; in one analysis, the siege of Leningrad left no metabolic trace.

Marks also shift over a lifetime. “Epigenetic clocks” built from hundreds of such marks predict mortality, but they are noisy: a repeat analysis of the same sample differed by up to 9 years. In 19 experienced meditators, immune-gene activity changed over a single day of practice (a small study) — in the meditators themselves, with no change to their DNA and no transmission to children.

Three paths from ancestors — and a fourth, unproven in humans

The confusion around “ancestral programs” clears up with one distinction.

  1. Genes — passed down, working through the body and the choice of environment.
  2. Direct exposure: the fetus itself went through famine, stress hormones, tobacco. This is its own experience, just an early one.
  3. Social transmission: language, habits, fears, how a family fights and makes up.
  4. Transmission through germ cells: an ancestor’s experience, without genes and without a shared household, is recorded in the egg or sperm and shows up in the grandchildren.

The problem with the fourth is structural. A pregnancy acts on three generations at once: the mother, the fetus, and the fetus’s germ cells. So true hereditary transmission can only be claimed if the trace shows up in the third generation after the pregnant woman who lived through the event8. In human germ cells the marks are almost completely erased before birth, and the erasure happens again after fertilization. In mice, fear of an odor has been shown to pass to grandchildren; in humans, it has not.

The human data are small and confounded. Adult children of men who were starved in the womb were 4.9 kg heavier. In Överkalix, Sweden, abundant food in a paternal grandfather’s childhood is linked to grandchildren’s mortality from diabetes — small groups, many tests, no mechanism. The most cited study is of children of Holocaust survivors: a few dozen people; marks on the FKBP5 gene differed, but in opposite directions rather than being copied; in the descendants, the links ran both through their own childhood and through genes9. The counterweight is a summary of 32 samples: in well-designed nonclinical samples, survivors’ children show no general “secondary traumatization”10. This does not negate the suffering of individual people; it negates the idea that trauma is inevitably passed on.

There is a link between stress in pregnancy and a child’s problems, but a sizable part of it is explained by shared genes and the mother’s current state, and objective hardship (poverty, disaster) matters more than subjective distress. More on this in chapter 03.

Family stories

Children who know more about their family — where their parents met, what their grandparents went through — on average rate themselves higher and feel more in control of their lives. But this is a marker, not a treatment: close families both tell more stories and give more support. That memorizing family history makes a child resilient has not been shown11.

Family plots do repeat, but that is a risk, not a verdict. Parental divorce is linked to a higher risk of divorce in the children; in Swedish registries, adoptees’ divorce patterns resembled those of their biological parents, not their adoptive ones. Childhood abuse is only very weakly linked to relationship satisfaction in adulthood. Part of the “family script” runs through genes, part through how conflict and child-rearing play out in the family, and neither is a program.

Constellations

Family constellations (group members “play” the client’s relatives) are poorly studied: across 12 studies, improved well-being was often reported, but the data are of low quality12. In four studies, 5–8% of participants had mild or moderate side effects. No study has shown that the “representatives” pick up hidden family facts or that the cause lay with the ancestors. A group can make you feel better even when its explanation is wrong.

What this means for the self

Given. DNA, new mutations (about one and a half more for each year of the father’s age, chapter 01), traces of what the fetus went through. This is the body layer; it is neither chosen nor earned. But it is given as a range and a set of inclinations, not as a script of fate.

Built. Genes may speak louder by adulthood because the self chooses an environment to suit itself. If so, how closely you resemble your genotype as an adult is partly the result of your own decisions: the self is not only the carrier of the inheritance but also the one who carries it out. Through the same mechanism parents pass on not only genes but the home those genes built for them.

Told. Family history belongs to the story layer, and it is the most flexible of all. You can know it, retell it, check it, look at it from other angles. It gives you a place in a long chain of people, but it does not oblige you to repeat their plots.

The ego uses inheritance in two opposite ways — and both hand over the wheel. The first: “it’s in my genes.” Genetic explanations noticeably strengthen belief in fixedness and predetermination13. It’s convenient: no responsibility, no shame. The second: “it’s an ancestral program.” The cause is moved into a past that can’t be checked, and so can’t be refuted either. Self-esteem is protected: “it’s not me, it’s the family line.” Both defenses turn a population average into a personal fate, and a link into a cause.

Freedom lies in social transmission and in the choice of environment. What is passed on socially is kept alive by what you repeat today: how you respond to shouting, how you make up, what you avoid. This can be observed and tracked. The genetic influence on behavior also runs through the choice of environment — and the choice of environment is the place where the self keeps hold of the wheel.

Myths

Myth: 50% heritability means half of you comes from genes and upbringing is powerless. In fact: It is a statistic about differences in a population, not a share of one person; alongside high heritability, a noticeable effect of environment has been measured.

Myth: A grandmother’s trauma is written into her grandchildren’s genes. In fact: In humans, transmission of psychological trauma through germ cells has not been shown; the marks in germ cells are erased, and the studies are small and confounded by genes and household.

Myth: Thoughts and lifestyle rewrite your genes, and this is passed on to your children. In fact: Lifestyle changes gene activity and marks — in you. That an adult’s habits change their children via sperm or egg has not been proven in humans; researchers call the belief “I control my genes” genetic denial.

Myth: There is a gene for violence, intelligence, depression. In fact: Almost all “candidate gene” findings failed to replicate; the “warrior gene” is a weak boost in men, not a prediction for an individual.

Who plays on this

Sellers of “ancestral programs.” They diagnose “inherited trauma” and sell its “removal.” Signs: an event from the family’s past is declared fact without documentation; “representatives” supposedly know what is hidden; improvement is passed off as proof of the cause. The size of this market has not been estimated.

“Epigenetic age” tests and the supplements that go with them. Signs: “find out your real age,” “get years younger.” The clocks vary by up to 9 years between repeat measurements. “Rejuvenation” has been shown in pilot studies of dozens of people, often on only one clock out of several. That improving the number improves health has not been shown.

Courts and labels. Evidence about MAOA genotype has been presented in murder trials. The risk for the person carrying the label is being judged by a gene rather than by their actions.

What to do about it

  1. Analyze repeating patterns as behavior, not as ancestry. Write down specific situations: what happened, what you did, what came of it. The sign it’s working: the reactions themselves change, not the theory about your great-grandfather.
  2. Share family stories by choice, and stick to the facts. Verified events, several points of view, the right not to answer and not to know. The measure is not whether you know the dates but whether the conversations bring you closer.
  3. A genetic test or an “epigenetic age” is not treatment. Telling a person their genetic risk does not by itself change behavior. Track what is linked to outcomes: blood pressure, blood sugar, blood lipids, sleep, endurance.

Questions for yourself

  • Which of my traits do I explain by “the family line” or “genes” — and what does that explanation spare me from doing?
  • What from my family do I repeat today with my hands, not my cells?
  • Which family story do I know in only one version?
  • What environment am I choosing for myself — and whose inclinations does it strengthen?

When to see a specialist

If there is ongoing violence or a real threat, or if a practitioner pushes you to “remember” or disclose events, declares unverified things to be fact, or demands that you drop your protections because “the ritual has removed the cause” — put safety and an assessment by a qualified psychotherapist or doctor first, not a deeper dive into your family tree.

Part II. 0–7: Body and Attachment — the Self Is Born

05. The brain builds itself: developmental windows and the myth that “everything is decided by age three”

In the first year of life, brain volume doubles. But cortical gray matter peaks around age six, white matter around twenty-nine, and the front of the brain keeps remodeling until thirty. The brain is not cast in its final form by age three; it builds itself throughout childhood and adolescence, out of what happens to it. Early experience carries a lot of weight, but it does not decide everything.

How it works

First surplus, then selection

Most neurons form before birth. After birth, the cortex first overproduces connections, and then experience decides which ones survive: what gets used is strengthened, what stays silent is removed. Synapse density (contacts between neurons) peaks in the auditory cortex at about three months, in the prefrontal cortex after fifteen months; pruning in the auditory cortex ends by roughly age twelve, while in the prefrontal cortex it continues into mid-adolescence (based on postmortem brain tissue)1. The prefrontal cortex is the front part of the brain that inhibits impulses and holds a plan. Its neurons carry two to three times as many spines — contact points — in childhood than in adulthood; the number starts to fall at puberty, and remodeling continues into the third decade2.

Insulating the wires — from back to front

Myelin is a fatty sheath that speeds a signal along a nerve fiber. It is laid down in sequence: first the cerebellum and deep pathways, by 4–6 months the occipital and parietal lobes (vision, space), by 6–8 months the frontal and temporal lobes3. The long pathways that link brain regions to one another keep maturing even after adolescence4. The systems that take in the world mature first; the ones that connect and control mature last.

The growth numbers

The fastest phase runs from birth to age two: in the first year brain volume grows by 101%, in the second by another 15%5. After that, growth does not stop — it changes shape. According to charts built from a huge sample of brain scans, cortical gray matter peaks around age 6, the deep nuclei around 14, cortical surface area by 11–12, and white matter around 29; sensory regions peak earlier than the rest6. Over the second decade gray matter declines and white matter grows — not wear and tear, but selection and insulation.

“The brain matures at 25” belongs to the same family of slogans. There is no single date of maturity, and the term itself is undefined7.

Windows: each system has its own schedule

A sensitive period is a time when a system is especially receptive to a particular experience and especially vulnerable to its absence. In animals (mostly in the visual cortex), the window opens when inhibitory cells mature and closes when molecular “brakes” lock the wiring in place; sometimes it can be reopened (in animals; for humans, a hypothesis)8. There are many windows, and they are not synchronized.

Vision. In children with congenital cataracts, the period during which a lack of light can still damage visual acuity lasts longer than the period in which acuity normally develops. Some losses surface years later — in abilities that mature late, such as holistic face perception (small clinical groups)9. A counterexample: a girl who was blind until surgery at 12 acquired mid- and high-level vision, although her acuity stayed poor (a single case).

Speech sounds. By six months, infants already hear sounds differently depending on the language around them. Between 6–8 and 10–12 months, American infants get better at telling English /r/ from /l/, while Japanese infants, for whom the distinction is foreign, get worse10. Infants who are not growing up with a tonal language stop distinguishing tones by nine months. But this is tuning, not a verdict: twelve sessions with live Mandarin speakers restored the lost distinction in nine-month-old Americans, while the same material on audio or video did nothing11.

Grammar. Here the window is much wider. Data from hundreds of thousands of English speakers show that the ability to learn a second language’s grammar stays high until about 17 (online sample, volunteers)12.

What real deprivation breaks

Deprivation is a severe lack of care, people and stimulation. The most rigorous experiment is the Bucharest project: children from Romanian orphanages, aged 6 to 31 months, were assigned by lottery either to foster families or to remain in the institution.
