Placement led to higher IQ, better growth and fewer psychiatric disorders, and the effect held up to ages 16–18; it was strongest for IQ and attachment disorders13. The younger the child at placement, the larger the IQ gain; placement before age two made secure attachment more likely14, 15. The structure of the prefrontal cortex changed too. Limitations: the lottery did not assign the age of placement, and the institutions were those of 1990s Romania.

The second study followed children adopted from Romanian institutions into British families. By age four, those adopted before six months had nearly caught up with their peers; those adopted between 6 and 24 months caught up substantially, but not fully16. Into adulthood, those who had spent more than six months in deprivation still showed autism-like traits, indiscriminate friendliness toward strangers and attention problems; the IQ lag disappeared; emotional difficulties emerged after age 15. A fifth had no problems at any stage17. A review of all such studies found that institutional care limited to the first 4–6 months adds no noticeable risk in most domains, and hard cutoffs after that are only weakly supported18.

The picture is a gradient, not a cliff. Earlier is better; later is still better than never.

What builds language: not a signal but a response

Learning happens when three things coincide: the child’s attention, a person who responds to it, and a signal the child can understand. Early-development products provide the signal without a responding person and fail at the second step. Children under six learn noticeably less from a screen than in person (the gap may be overestimated), and the gap shrinks with age19. A video call in which an adult responds to the child works: children aged 2–2.5 learned new verbs through it, but not from an identical recording20.

The famous “30-million-word gap” between poor and well-off families is an extrapolation from one hour of observation per month in a small sample of families. A meta-analysis reconciles the two sides: a gap in speech directed at the child exists and is large, there is none in overall speech around the child, and in any case it is nowhere near 30 million21. In a sample from twelve countries, adult speech nearby predicted the child’s speech, while the mother’s education did not. Among one forager-farmer people, adults rarely address infants — the American way of talking to babies is not universal.

Conversational exchange is more closely tied to language than word count is. The number of conversational “turns” at 18–24 months explained 14–27% of the differences in IQ and vocabulary ten years later — adjusted for family income — while the number of adult words mattered little22. The quality of speech is more strongly linked to a child’s language than its quantity, though quantity matters too. The link runs both ways: a child with a larger vocabulary draws more conversation23. As for cause and effect, no trial has yet separated turns from words.

Sex differences. Girls are on average slightly ahead in gestures, vocabulary and combining words, and the gap grows with age; boys, meanwhile, are not more variable (ten European languages)24. In the twelve-country sample, no sex difference in speech was found. Parents treat sons and daughters almost the same; they talk to daughters slightly more. Some apparent “sex differences” in the growth of brain regions shift once head size is taken into account.

What this means for the self

Given: neurons, the schedule of windows, the order of maturation. This is shared by everyone — a species program, not a personal fate.

Built by the environment before anyone can ask: which connections survived. The sounds of a foreign language you cannot tell apart are the result of tuning done in your first year (reversible in infants). This is not a character trait but a trace of the world the brain landed in. The “ties” layer enters the self first through the body — through the wiring, before the first memory (how the self itself appears is in chapter 07; the first “we” is in chapter 06).

Learning language takes a responding person, not sound. Perhaps this is also where the first experience of the self comes from: my signal changes the world — someone answers it (this is an interpretation, not a measured fact). Where there is no response for years, as in institutions, attention and relationships with people suffer, not just vocabulary.

Remodeled: more than people tend to think. The prefrontal cortex — the organ of inhibition and planning — remodels longest of all, until thirty. The apparatus with which the self governs itself is finished not in daycare but in school, in youth and beyond; later experience sustains and develops the early wiring. The capacity for grammar stays high until seventeen, the lost sound distinctions return in infants through live interaction, and IQ largely recovers even after severe deprivation.

The honest limit: not everything is reversible. After long deprivation, attention and social behavior may need dedicated help for years, and some visual losses surface later. It remains an open scientific question whether windows close entirely or just become more expensive to use.

The ego and the myth of age three. The slogan “it’s all decided by three” serves the ego in two ways. For an adult talking about themselves: “I was broken in childhood, there’s nothing to change” — a defense that lifts responsibility for what is being built now (on why the self loves to hand over the wheel, see chapter 48). For a parent: “the window is closing” turns the child into a project with a deadline, and parental self-esteem into that deadline’s hostage: make it in time and you’re a good parent; miss it and you’re guilty. Both versions rest on the same mistake: mistaking an early growth peak for the end of construction.

Freedom here lies in age — in how late systems mature. The later a system matures, the longer it stays open to experience — and the last to mature is the one that keeps hold of the wheel.

Myths

Myth: by age three the brain’s architecture is complete; the first three years decide everything.
In fact: growth is fastest before three, but cortical gray matter peaks around 6, white matter around 29, and the prefrontal cortex remodels into the third decade2, 6. Early experience matters in specific systems, and each has its own gradual window, with no emergency brake at age three.

Myth: Mozart — played to a baby or to a pregnant belly — makes a child smarter.
In fact: the “Mozart effect” in adults disappears once arousal and mood are matched25, 26, and no one has measured IQ after music in the womb (more in chapter 03).

Myth: a second language has to be heard before age three (or seven), or it is lost.
In fact: tuning to sounds narrows by 10–12 months, but in infants twelve sessions with live people brought it back, and the capacity for grammar holds until about 1711, 12.

Myth: the brain matures at 25.
In fact: different systems have their own peaks, and “brain maturity” has no definition7 (more in chapter 21).

Who plays on this

The early-development market. Baby DVDs, flashcards, “reading from the cradle” kits and Mozart for the crib sell the anxiety of a closing window. In trials, DVDs did not teach infants words, and parents who liked the DVD overestimated their child’s progress27; a seven-month trial of a “baby reading” kit produced no reading skills28. Signs: a promise of intelligence or early reading, “act before…” urgency, a signal without a live person.

Brain trainers. Chess, music and memory training promise to “upgrade the whole brain.” The better designed the study, the smaller the effect, and transfer to distant skills is almost never found29. In rigorously designed studies, music lessons yield almost no IQ gain (one trial found a small one). Sign: a promise of transfer — “play one game and everything improves.”

Early academics as a promise. A state pre-K program in Tennessee, tested by lottery, left children with lower scores and more disciplinary infractions by sixth grade30; a program in Boston led to more college enrollment and fewer disciplinary cases. No direct trial of play versus instruction was found; play has expert consensus on its side, while early formal instruction has no evidence that it helps. Sign of manipulation: “the earlier they’re at a desk, the smarter they’ll be.”

What to do about it

Respond, don’t broadcast. With any child in your life: respond to a sound or word, wait, add one word, ask an open question, talk about what the child is looking at. Sign it’s working: the child starts more turns on their own. Counting words is not the goal.

Dialogic shared reading (ages 1–6). Let the child look at the picture, ask “what’s happening here?”, wait, repeat the answer and extend it by a word or two, follow the child’s interest. A meta-analysis of trials finds this noticeably boosts language, but with infrequent sessions the effect is close to zero31. Check: the number of the child’s utterances per session; vocabulary every 2–3 months. If the child is tired or uninterested, set it aside.

Screens before age two — the budget goes to live people. A video call with someone close counts as live interaction. If there is a screen, watch together and talk about what you see.

Don’t write off later ages. Sleep, conversation and safety matter at any age, not only before three. A child in severe deprivation should be removed from it as early as possible, but even after age two IQ and attachment still grow; attention and social behavior may need dedicated help for years.

Questions for yourself

  • What story about your childhood do you use as an alibi for what you aren’t changing now?
  • What in you do you consider character that may actually be a trace of your early environment — your ear for the sounds of languages, for example?
  • What do you buy — for yourself or a child — out of fear of “not making it in time”?
  • Whose signals do you respond to, and to whom do you only broadcast?

When to see a specialist

If a child spent a long time in an institution or under severe neglect and, after moving into a family, still struggles with attention, shows indiscriminate friendliness toward strangers or has trouble in relationships, targeted help is needed, sometimes for a long time.

06. Attachment: the first “we” comes before the first “I”

An infant can’t calm its own stress — the body of the adult nearby does that for it. A child recognizes itself in a mirror only in the second year of life, but the pair “me and the one who comes” forms earlier. So the first model of “me” is a model of a bond: what happens when I feel bad, and will anyone come. This model is fairly durable, but not permanent.

How it works

Attachment is an expectation, not a personality type

In science, attachment is not love and not a “personality type.” It is an expectation: will a caregiver be there when I’m in trouble? In infants it is observed through behavior during a brief separation; in adults, through an interview or questionnaires about a partner. These are different instruments, and you can’t merge them into one “style for life.”

In adults, questionnaire data point not to four boxes but to two scales: anxiety (fear of being abandoned) and avoidance (discomfort with closeness)1. Each is a continuum, not a label. And the same person scores differently with different people: attachment is specific to each bond.

Another body as a stress regulator

The mechanism, step by step. The child feels bad — the stress axis switches on, and cortisol (the hormone of alarm and mobilization) rises. The adult soothes with voice, touch, food. The presence of a sensitive adult dampens the cortisol response; by 12 months this works as an analogue of the “dampened stress” (“stress-hyporesponsive”) period in rodent pups, in which the mother provides the dampening2. The proposed mediators — oxytocin pathways and prefrontal cortex networks (the front part of the brain that brakes impulses and holds a plan) — are still a hypothesis.

Each successful cycle of “bad → someone came → better” turns into an expectation. Repeated cycles build secure attachment and, later, the ability to calm yourself. Children who got more responsiveness at 2–6 months had lower baseline cortisol and at 18 months were more securely attached; security softened the physiological cost of a shy temperament (one small study). Cortisol is not a simple “bad” signal: in neglected children it can also be below normal.

The buffer changes as the child grows. A parent’s presence fully removed the cortisol response to public speaking in 9–10-year-olds — and did not in 15–16-year-olds3. Pubertal stage explains the weakening better than age does.

Touch

Skin-to-skin is the best-tested part of this topic. In babies weighing 1.0–1.8 kg, continuous contact right after birth (about 17 hours a day) cut mortality over the first 28 days from 15.7% to 12.0% — randomized, in five countries4. For healthy full-term babies, contact right after delivery probably extends exclusive breastfeeding and keeps them warmer. A father or another adult can do it, including after a C-section.

In Saint Petersburg, mother–baby pairs were randomly assigned to skin-to-skin, held in arms, or a nursery cot. With contact, babies’ feet were warmer; a year later, pairs that had 25–120 minutes of contact and early suckling interacted better than pairs routinely separated, while tight swaddling reduced maternal responsiveness. At nine months, infants respond distinctly to “pleasant” stroking at moderate speed.

Sensitivity, not synchrony

Sensitivity means noticing a signal, understanding it and responding. It does not mean matching every state. In recordings of mother–infant pairs at 3, 6 and 9 months, coordinated moments took up only a small share of interaction5; ordinary mismatch is normal, not harmful. The popular rule “70% hits, the rest is repair” was not established in this study.

The link between sensitivity and security has been tested on tens of thousands of children: it is moderate and nearly the same for mothers and fathers (the intervals overlap), so both matter. Sensitivity is one factor, not the only one.

The “still face” experiment shows what is being built in response. An adult plays with an infant, then freezes with a stony face, then becomes responsive again. The child smiles less, looks away, gets upset, and some of this carries over after the “return.” The effect is confirmed in dozens of studies, in boys and girls alike. In Chinese infants aged 3–6 months the reaction is similar, only they start smiling later (small sample).

Crying and sleep

Crying is not a malfunction. The norm in the first 6 weeks is about two hours of crying and fussing a day; by 10–12 weeks, about an hour. More carrying means 43% less crying at 6 weeks (randomized)6. And the idea that “if you go to them right away, they’ll cry less later” did not hold up when retested; in fact, the fastest responders were mothers of avoidant children7.

Night sleep consolidates on its own, and unevenly. The biggest jump is between 1 and 4 months; yet at 12 months one in seven still doesn’t sleep a five- or eight-hour stretch, and several wakings a night at 1–2 years are within normal range.

Behavioral sleep methods (“controlled crying,” gradually shifting bedtime) help most families (the studies are small). They have shown no harm to attachment: in a randomized trial, by age 6 the children showed no difference in emotions, stress, or closeness with parents8. Two years on, mothers had depressive symptoms less often. Limits: before 4–6 months there’s no point, families with abuse were not in the trials, and follow-up ends around age 6. The main argument against is a small study with no control group, where cortisol stayed high after the child stopped crying.

Sharing a bed raises the risk of sudden infant death, especially in the first 12 weeks and when the mother smokes9. In absolute numbers, for breastfed infants under 3 months with nonsmoking parents and no other risks, it’s roughly 0.23 versus 0.08 cases per thousand births. Most deaths are linked to sofas, alcohol, medication and smoking.

Oxytocin: not a love button

Oxytocin is needed in labor and nursing; in parents it’s linked to affectionate play and attunement with the child. But measuring it in blood or saliva is nearly pointless: standard assays also pick up other molecules, and the link to resting brain levels is close to zero. The famous “trust spray” failed to replicate in several attempts, including a preregistered one10. Oxytocin shifts attention toward social signals — differently in different people and situations; in one experiment with men, it even increased defensiveness toward outsiders.

How long it lasts

Attachment stability from infancy to adulthood was tested across 21,000 measurements: a correlation of about 0.3911. The shorter the interval, the higher it is; over spans longer than 15 years there is no significant stability. This doesn’t mean the early years have no effect — it means you can’t predict the adult from the infant.

In an American cohort followed to age 18, adult attachment style tracked the quality of care, social skills and best friendship at school — not temperament and not most candidate genes. Transmission from parent to child is real, but weaker than once thought.

The lower bound is severe deprivation. Among Ukrainian preschoolers in institutions, 24% had a clear attachment pattern; among those raised in families, 97% (small study). In a Romanian experiment, institutionalized children were randomly placed in foster families; placement before age two more often led to secure attachment, but later placement also improved things (on developmental windows and the myth that “everything is decided by three,” see chapter 05).

Culture plays a part too. Co-sleeping with parents ranges from a few percent of families in New Zealand to the majority in Vietnam, and critics argue that the “universal” attachment theory was built around Western norms.

Bonds seem to reshape the adult body too: men in couples have lower testosterone than single men, and fathers lower than childless men (a meta-analysis of dozens of studies) — more on this in chapter 32.

What this means for the self

Given — the need. An infant’s brain doesn’t regulate itself; another body serves as the external brake on stress. The first thing the future self learns is not “who am I” but “what happens to me when I call.” Self-soothing later grows out of this external regulator; it doesn’t appear separately.

Built — the expectation. From many “call → response” cycles, the brain apparently derives a rule: the world responds, comes late, or it’s better not to call. It most likely becomes the backdrop of self-esteem (this hasn’t been measured directly).

The ego presents it as character. The anxious pole is fear of being abandoned and testing the bond; the avoidant pole is discomfort with closeness. The ego easily packages them as traits: “I’m independent,” “I’m just sensitive.” Perhaps the mask for others is an old strategy that once worked for reaching an adult (hypothesis).

Rebuildable — more than it seems. Stability is moderate and disappears over long spans; adult style is linked to friendship and the quality of relationships, not to genes. Each bond has its own attachment. A responsive partner softens avoidant defenses in conflict, and situations that repeatedly disprove the old expectation can shift the working model; lasting “healing” is not proven. Freedom here is not in “healing your childhood” but in noticing the rule and collecting new data.

The label is an ego trap. “I’m avoidant,” “an anxious type” — a convenient story that explains everything and changes nothing. A two-axis scale for each bond is more honest than a life sentence.

Myths

Myth: a parent must be in sync with the child almost all the time; misses do harm. In fact: coordinated moments take up a small share of interaction, and mismatch is normal; what matters is sensitivity, not perfect matching5.

Myth: the first hour after birth is the only window; miss it and the bond is damaged. In fact: skin-to-skin contact helps, but no difference was found between starting immediately and an hour later12; children removed from severe deprivation before 6 months had almost fully caught up by age four.

Myth: oxytocin is the hormone of love and trust, and a spray makes people more trusting. In fact: the famous result failed to replicate in registered tests10 (more in chapter 30).

Myth: attachment style is set in childhood for life. In fact: the link to childhood is moderate and weakens over the years, and in an adult it differs with different people11.

Who plays on this

The “booster” market. Sprays “for attachment” and “for trust” rely on a study that didn’t replicate. Sign: a promise of connection through a molecule.

Label pop psychology. “They ghosted — so they’re avoidant,” “how they act after sex reveals their type.” Neither has been validated as a test. Sign: diagnosing a person from a single act.

Parental guilt. “You missed the first hour,” “you didn’t make it by age three.” Sign: an absolute “forever” where the data say “on average” and “under conditions.” A bare “never” without a safe-bed plan is dangerous too: an exhausted adult falls asleep with the baby on the sofa.

What to do about it

  • Carry and hold. More carrying in the first weeks means less crying; skin-to-skin with any caregiving adult, the baby’s face visible, an awake adult nearby. What to track: hours of contact per day, temperature, feeding, weight gain.
  • Safe sleep by checklist. On the back, on a firm flat surface, in the parents’ room but not in their bed; no smoking, alcohol, sleeping pills, sofa or soft bedding. If falling asleep together is likely, prepare the safest possible bed in advance.
  • A sleep method — not before 6 months. Pick one that suits you (gradually shifting bedtime is gentler than “controlled crying”). Keep a log: bedtime, how long it takes to fall asleep, number of wakings — and your own mood.
  • For your own bonds. Instead of a type — two ratings for each close bond: how much I fear losing it and how much I hide from closeness. Note specific triggers and how they change. Sign of a shift: the old expectation (“no one will come”) fires less often in a bond where you’re responded to again and again.

Questions for yourself

  • What do I do first when I feel bad: call out, check, or hide?
  • In which of my bonds do I feel calm, and in which am I on guard — and what makes the difference?
  • Which of my “that’s just how I am” is really an old strategy for getting through to someone?
  • Who becomes calmer next to me — and how do I know?

When to see a specialist

If a child snores constantly or stops breathing during sleep, see a doctor rather than trying a sleep method.

07. The Birth of the Self: The Mirror, “Mine!”, Shame and First Memories

A one-year-old is already recording events in memory, yet as an adult will recall nothing from that time. The self does not switch on in a single day — not on the day of birth, and not in front of a mirror at eighteen months. It is several different skills, and they come together at different times: sensing that an action is mine, recognizing yourself, saying “mine!”, feeling ashamed, grasping that another person has a mind of their own, telling a story about yourself. The first version of the self is built in dialogue — and it is co-authored.

How it works

Not a switch but a chain

A child acts — and notices what comes of it: a foot kicks the rattle, crying summons an adult. This is how the child learns what they control and what responds from outside. Then come words and categories (“I,” “mine,” “boy,” “girl”). Then come conversations about the past, from which a story about yourself takes shape. Each link is measured by different tests, and none of them measures the whole self.

Emotion regulation follows the same path: first an adult soothes the child from outside (more in chapter 06), then the child learns to do it alone. After the first or second year, differences between children in this skill become fairly stable, and the weaker the skill, the more often fights, outbursts and rule-breaking occur (an association, not a proven cause).

The mirror

The classic experiment: a mark is secretly placed on a child’s face, and the child is brought to a mirror. If the child touches the mark on their own face, they are counted as having recognized themselves. In Western samples this most often happens between eighteen months and two years.

But the test measures more than recognition. The child has to notice the mark, understand the situation and perform exactly the action the researcher counts. A child who doesn’t touch it hasn’t shown that they lack a self. Timing depends on culture: among urban middle-class Greek toddlers, self-recognition came earlier, while among children of Nso farmers in Cameroon, self-control came earlier1. Each pattern matched the parenting style: more face-to-face interaction went with an earlier self-image; more physical closeness, with an earlier ability to hold back. The claim that “all the world’s children recognize themselves at 18–24 months” does not hold up.

The mirror is tied to shame. In children from 9 to 24 months, embarrassment went hand in hand with mirror self-recognition, while ordinary wariness of strangers did not2. To be embarrassed, you need to know you are being looked at — and that it is you being looked at.

“Mine!”

By 17 months most children push, hit and grab. During the preschool years most learn other ways of getting what they want. In a random sample of families, a high-aggression path was predicted by circumstances: the mother’s antisocial behavior in her youth, early motherhood, smoking during pregnancy, low income.

“Mine!” is not necessarily plain selfishness. The word can reflect control (what I can hold on to), rules (what belongs to whom) and language (now it has a name). The boundary of a thing is the first boundary of the self. A small child’s inflated opinion of themselves (“I’m the fastest”) is not necessarily a lie or a disorder either: children’s self-esteem cannot be measured with adult questionnaires.

A tantrum is the same mechanism at its limit. In parents’ reports on children aged eighteen months to five years, a typical outburst lasted half a minute to a minute, and three quarters lasted no more than five minutes3. A tantrum has its own internal shape: anger flares at the start and fades, while crying and comfort-seeking build toward the end. Intense anger decreases with age. Among American preschoolers, 84% had tantrums “sometimes,” and 9% had them daily4.

The ability to wait was overrated too. When the famous “marshmallow test” was rerun (with children of mothers without a college degree), its link to academic achievement at age 15 was half as strong as in the original, and after adjusting for living conditions it shrank by another two thirds5.

Shame, guilt and pride

Shame, guilt and pride are emotions about the self. Wanting or fearing is not enough to feel them: you need a self-image and a standard to hold it against. That is why they are tied to the mirror, but there is no single exact age at which they appear.

Which of them gets encouraged depends on the environment. In Nepal, Tamang parents scold a child for anger and talk with a child who is ashamed; Brahman parents do the opposite. The same emotion counts as misbehavior in one family and as normal in another. A child learns not only to feel but also which feelings are “allowed.”

The difference between shame and guilt has real consequences. Shame judges the whole self (“I’m bad”); guilt judges the act (“that was bad”). In a review of more than a hundred studies, proneness to shame was noticeably more strongly linked to depression than proneness to guilt; but guilt that turns into endless self-flagellation is also clearly linked to it6. This is an association, not a proven cause.

Another mind

In the preschool years (in Western samples), children begin to understand that another person can hold a false belief — for example, will look for a toy where they left it, not where it is now. In studies of Chinese children, the order of steps in understanding other minds turned out to be different. This is not a matter of lagging behind on a single ladder: language, experience and exactly which distinction is being tested all matter. And it is not all of empathy or all of morality — only one link.

Sex — the first category of the self

Children start using the words “boy” and “girl” for themselves and others around 19 months (earlier in girls, on average). The earlier a child masters these words, the more “typical” their play later becomes. From three to five, knowledge of stereotypes, the importance of one’s own sex and the rigidity of rules (“boys don’t do that”) all grow; after five, the rigidity usually eases. From about age four, children choose playmates of their own sex on their own, and the more of this play there is, the stronger the typically “boyish” or “girlish” behavior.

The sex difference in toy choice is huge by psychology’s standards — on average, sex differences in psychological traits are several times smaller. Yet at 12 months both boys and girls preferred dolls to toy cars; the differences came later (a small experiment on the gaze of infants aged 3–8 months found them earlier; no large studies have checked this). Preferences for pink and blue split only by age two to three, and they prove unstable. Biology plays a part too: girls exposed in the womb to excess androgens (male sex hormones) because of a congenital adrenal disorder gravitate to “boys’” toys even when parents encourage “girls’” ones (more on womb hormones in chapter 02)7. No one has an exact share for biology, peers and parents.

Parents treat sons and daughters very similarly on average: according to a review of 126 studies, the difference in control is negligible (they talk a bit more with daughters and encourage “typical” activities). Perception, however, is skewed: mothers of 11-month-olds underestimated their daughters’ crawling and overestimated their sons’, although the children crawled equally well8. The environment works fast: two weeks in which preschool teachers constantly emphasized children’s sex strengthened stereotypes and reduced play with children of the other sex. More on how much parents really decide in chapter 08.

First memories

Infants are not “unable to remember.” In an experiment with awake infants, the hippocampus — the brain region that records episodes — was more active when they saw a new picture, and this predicted whether they would recognize it later; this holds from about one year9. It seems early memories do get recorded, and access to them is lost later.

It is lost during the school years. Children who discussed events with their mothers at age three remembered at least 60% of them at 5–7, but less than 40% at 8–910. Why this happens is debated: in rodents, the rapid birth of new neurons in the hippocampus of the young is linked to rapid forgetting (not tested in humans); there is no single theory. There is evidence that a memory becomes part of a life story when it is told and shared. Early memories lag behind the words that could name them. In a small study, children whose mothers, when the children were 2–4, more often elaborated on stories about the past than repeated questions, at 12–13, remember back to an earlier age.

On average, an adult’s first memory dates to about three years and two months. In the largest survey, almost 40% dated their first memory to age two or earlier; the authors consider the earliest of these fictional, while other scientists dispute this11. Children and teenagers date their first memories earlier than adults do: the boundary of forgetting moves forward over the years. In men and women it is nearly the same on average.

Culture moves it as well. American students’ first memory turned out to be almost half a year earlier than Chinese students’, and their accounts were longer and more about themselves. American mothers of three-year-olds worked out a view of an event together with the child; Chinese mothers asked about facts and stressed rules. Perhaps talking about the past teaches not only how to remember but also who is the main character in the story — “I” or “we.”

What this means for the self

Here all three layers of the self meet and, for the first time, merge into a single figure.

The body supplies the agent. The first self is not a thought but a link between movement and consequence: my movement — and something changed. The feeling “I can make things happen” probably grows from here, and it comes before any language.

Ties supply the mirror. Self-image takes shape from how others look at you and what they say about you. Embarrassment appears together with self-recognition: from this moment the self knows it can be seen — and, probably, the mask begins. The ego is partly born as a response to another’s gaze: show one thing, hide another.

The boundary supplies “mine.” First it is a thing, then the body, then an opinion, later status and being right. An adult who defends their point of view like their own toy seems to be using the same mechanism. Within a few years a child learns to get what they want by means other than a shove — and an adult may never learn.

Shame supplies the first self-esteem. How a family handles shame and guilt probably affects what a mistake turns into: “that was bad, and it can be fixed” or “I’m bad.” The second (shame about the whole self) is noticeably linked to depression; the first leaves room for repair. Self-esteem that rests on shame constantly needs defending — that is the subject of chapter 23.

Sex supplies the first category. A child doesn’t just receive a label — they use it themselves, sort the world and themselves with it, and at three to five guard it most rigidly. It is neither an innate program nor pure suggestion but a mix whose proportions are unknown.

The story is co-authored. A person does not remember their early years; what feels like a memory often rests on retellings and photographs. So the earliest chapter of your biography was written largely by others, and after the fact. Here lies the main freedom of this chapter. The facts of childhood can’t be changed, but the story about them is a reconstruction, not a recording. It can be reread: who is the main character, what in it is “my fault,” and what was simply age, culture and circumstance. Chapter 19 covers how the self is rebuilt in adolescence.

Myths

Myth: all children recognize themselves in the mirror at 18–24 months, and those who don’t are behind. In fact: timing depends on culture, and the test depends on attention and on a willingness to perform the action being scored. A lack of response does not by itself prove either the absence of a self or a developmental disorder.

Myth: “I remember myself at one” — or the reverse: infants remember nothing. In fact: the hippocampus records episodes from about one year, but access to them is mostly lost by 8–99, 10. Memories “from age one or two” are poor evidence of what happened.

Myth: tantrums are manipulation or illness. In fact: most last less than five minutes and subside into crying and comfort-seeking. What should raise concern is not tantrums as such but a particular pattern (see below).

Myth: a boy must psychologically separate from his mother to become a man. In fact: this is a psychoanalytic interpretation, not a tested law of development. Independence is another matter: it can be supported while keeping warmth; there is no need to withdraw warmth or shame “girlish” things for the sake of “masculinity.”

Who plays on this

Sellers of “infant memory recovery.” There is no proven method; the authors of the largest survey consider the earliest memories fictional (other scientists disagree). Red flag: a promise to “remember your first year” and conclusions about yourself drawn from these “memories.”

Gurus of “proper” male or female upbringing. Mandatory separation of a son from his mother, “personality resets” every seven years, the search for a single “true self” — no one has properly tested these packages. Red flag: a universal recipe for all children of one sex, and shame as a method.

Home developmental tests. The mirror and the marshmallow get turned into an exam for your own child. Red flag: concluding “something is wrong with them” from a single task.

What to do about it

Remember together — in detail. With children from eighteen months to four years, talk about things you did together: open questions (“who, what, where, why, how did it feel to you”), building on the child’s answers, adding details and feelings, without repeating the same closed question. In trials, children of such parents told richer stories about the past, and after a year-long program their vocabulary and storytelling skills grew (the samples were Western and small). Sign it’s working: in a five-minute conversation you elaborate more than you re-ask, and the child adds details on their own.

Turn shame into action. For yourself and your child: not “you’re bad” but “here’s what happened, here’s how to fix it.” A concrete, fixable action instead of a judgment of the whole self — and instead of endless self-flagellation. Sign: after a mistake, people discuss the repair, not who is to blame.

Watch your expectations, not the toys. Offer a range of toys and don’t read the choice as a verdict. Check your own words about what a child “can handle” and what they are “supposed to feel”: mothers of 11-month-olds misjudged crawling precisely in line with the child’s sex.

As an adult — reassemble the story of your childhood. Separate what you remember from what you were told: family stories and photographs shape the feeling of “I remember.” Ask: is this story mine, or someone else’s version of me?

Questions for yourself

  • My earliest memory — do I remember it, or was I told about it?
  • When I make a mistake, does the voice inside say “that was bad” or “something is wrong with me”?
  • What do I still defend as “mine!” — and is it worth a shove?
  • Whose gaze do I imagine when I feel ashamed?

When to see a specialist

If a child aged 3–6 has near-daily tantrums that are unpredictable, drag on, or come with destruction or attempts to hurt themselves, that is a reason to take them to a child psychiatrist or psychologist4, 12.

08. Who raises a child: mother, father, grandmothers, the village — and how much parents really decide

In historical societies without contraception or medicine, a mother’s death is linked to higher child mortality in all 28 populations studied, while a father’s death is linked to it in fewer than half of the sound studies1. This is not a ranking of parents but a clue: a child is raised by a network, and each node contributes something of its own. Parents decide less than the myth of all-powerful upbringing promises, and more than genetic fatalism allows. The self is assembled where what the child brings meets how others respond.

How it works

A child is raised by a network, not a job title

Humans are cooperative breeders: other people — grandmothers, older children, neighbors — feed, guard and teach the child and stand in for the main adult. This frame asks the right question: who provides what — food, supervision, protection, knowledge, rest for whoever is most exhausted — and at what age. It does not claim that any relative is helpful, that the mother is replaceable, or that there is one right form of family.

A mother matters most for survival early on, and her effect more often fades after weaning; in five studies the link disappears after age two. These are data on survival in the past, not a quota of hours a mother must spend with her child today.

Grandmothers are the best-known example. The maternal grandmother is linked to better grandchild survival in most studies, the paternal grandmother in about half1. But in three studies the link runs the other way: a grandmother’s presence goes with worse survival. The benefit depends on circumstances and on the child’s age. That grandmothers as a class paid off in evolution does not guarantee that any particular grandmother will (why evolution keeps people around past reproductive age — see chapter 42).

The father: less about survival, not less about everything else

A father’s death being unrelated to child mortality in half the studies does not mean a father makes no difference: education, relationships and mental health are separate outcomes. The link between a father’s absence and children’s problems is partly explained by what comes with it — less money and more instability. The stricter the study design, the smaller the effect, but part of the harm remains2. How much of it comes specifically from money and instability has not been measured.

There is evidence that a mother’s and a father’s sensitivity are similarly linked to the security of a child’s attachment (across different studies; for how attachment works, see chapter 06). Sensitivity is a function, not a sex or a family title. If a dangerous adult lives in the home, being the father does not turn their presence into protection.

Genes: resemblance, yes; a verdict, no

Heritability is the share of differences between people in a given population that statistics attributes to genes. Averaged across traits it is about half; for personality, about 40%; for intelligence, it rises with age — perhaps because children increasingly choose environments that suit their own inclinations. It does not mean that genes made half of you and that this half cannot be changed (more in chapter 04).

Even a huge polygenic index for educational attainment (a combined score across many DNA variants, built on three million people) predicts only a small part of the differences in education, and less than a third of that predictive power is a direct effect of the child’s genes; the rest runs through family and surroundings. It gives no forecast for an individual child.

Environment moves what is “written”

The most direct answer to fatalism comes from Swedish brothers. Researchers studied pairs of full brothers in which one was raised by his birth family and the other was adopted. By ages 18–20, the adopted brothers scored on average 4.4 IQ points higher than those who stayed home; the result replicated in half-brothers5. Each pair shares half their genes but not their environment, and the difference shows. Caveats: men only, IQ only; it says nothing about income or education.

A similar Swedish registry shows the other side: in divorce history, adoptees resemble their biological parents, not their adoptive ones. The picture differs by outcome. There is no single “genes versus upbringing” split for a person — different traits get different answers.

What works in parenting — and how much

A synthesis of more than a thousand studies: warmth, clear behavioral control, autonomy support and “warm strictness” are linked to fewer behavior problems — aggression, rule-breaking6. The links range from very weak to weak. Harshness and psychological control are linked to worse outcomes. Many links run both ways: a difficult child draws more control, and a correlation does not show who influenced whom first.

The key line runs between a rule about behavior and control over the inner world. “No hitting” is a rule. “I’ll love you when you feel and think the right way” is control over who you are. Conditional regard — warmth handed out for success and withdrawn for failure — is linked to self-esteem that depends on performance and to symptoms of depression7. The data are mostly correlational: one look of disappointment on a parent’s face does not break a child.

Physical punishment is the most widely studied practice: one synthesis covers more than 160,000 children. Spanking is linked to a higher average risk of behavior problems and is not linked to better behavior. One synthesis cannot prove causation, but nothing in the data supports “at least it makes them obey.”

Depending on temperament, children differ in how sensitive they are to parenting: some are loosely called “orchids” (highly sensitive), others “dandelions” (resilient). But no test can tell an “orchid” from a “dandelion” and predict what will help whom.

Parental conflict and birth order

Chronic unresolved conflict between parents is linked to worse well-being in children; in divorced families the links are mostly weak, and they run partly through the way conflict corrodes parenting itself. An argument that ends in agreement is not the same as a threatening fight that pulls the child in.

Birth order does not set character. In national samples from the US, Britain and Germany — about twenty thousand people, with comparisons both between families and between siblings within a family — there are no stable effects on extraversion, conscientiousness, agreeableness or emotional stability8. There is a small edge in intelligence and self-rated intelligence for older siblings — which is not evidence of leadership. A newer study of tens to hundreds of thousands of online volunteers found small differences in honesty-humility and agreeableness between only children and children from families of six or more9. These are different questionnaires and different people; by calculation, the groups’ distributions overlap almost completely. “The firstborn is a leader” and “the only child is selfish” are not supported.

Boys and girls: parents are similar, expectations are not

On average, parents raise sons and daughters almost identically: on control and autonomy support the difference is close to zero10. One thing differs, and only in North America: encouragement of “gender-appropriate” play. Mothers talk slightly more with daughters. And expectations part ways with behavior: mothers underestimated their 11-month-old daughters’ crawling, although boys and girls crawled equally well11.

Parenting is not the only source of differences. In girls exposed to excess androgens (male hormones) in the womb, parental encouragement did not override their interest in “boys’” toys; testosterone levels during pregnancy are linked to how girls play (more in chapter 02). By age 4.5, children themselves consistently choose same-sex groups. A child learns the gender label around 19 months; stereotypes grow from ages 3 to 5. Nobody has calculated how much of the difference comes from parenting specifically.

Culture changes meaning but does not cancel harm

The same practice can mean different things in different cultures: duty to parents and concern for what others think are the norm in some places. But in nearly five hundred Chinese American families, a supportive style was linked to better outcomes over eight years than “tiger” parenting (harsh and demanding)12. This is a diaspora, not mainland China, and a group’s norm does not define an individual child’s experience (for how a country enters the self, see chapter 10).

What this means for the self

Here the book’s main proportion comes into view: given, built, and rebuildable.

Given — noticeable, but not for any one person. Statistics attributes roughly 40% of the differences in personality between people to genes. That number describes a population, not a share of you. A child does not arrive as a blank slate: they come with a temperament that itself draws responses from adults. Perhaps part of what is later called “that’s how I was raised” was a response to who the child already was.

Built — together, in a loop. Parenting is not imprinting but a conversation: the child sends a signal, the adult responds, and the response changes the next signal. With age, children increasingly choose their own environment and so amplify their inclinations. That is why the family decides less than parents think, but more than nothing: the Swedish brothers are a direct example.

The ego learns the price of love. What a child carries away is not only the rules but an answer to the question “What am I accepted for?” If warmth is handed out for results, self-esteem attaches to results (as correlations show7). Failure then probably reads as a threat to acceptance, and the ego’s defenses — avoiding risk, embellishing, the mask of the “convenient” one or the “best” one — work to keep that threat away. If the rule concerns the act and love is not up for negotiation, self-esteem apparently has a footing that need not be earned every day. What a child takes from a hard family, and why most cope — see chapter 09.

Labels arrive before the self. “The oldest is the responsible one,” “girls don’t crawl as fast,” “you’re just like your father” — adults’ expectations diverge from real differences (crawling, birth order). A child probably tries these labels on as descriptions of themselves before they can check them (for how the self is born, see chapter 07), and the ego later defends these roles as its own (a hypothesis, not directly tested).

Freedom lies in the story. You cannot rewrite your genes or your childhood. What you can rebuild is something else: the story you tell about how you were raised. Both extremes — “my parents made me this way” and “it’s all genes, nothing to be done” — hand over the wheel. The data are consistent with a third view: you are built from what you brought, how others responded, and what you chose yourself — and that third part keeps working.

Myths

Myth: High heritability means parenting can change nothing.
In fact: Heritability and malleability coexist: adopted brothers scored more IQ points on average than their full brothers who stayed home (men only)5.

Myth: Everything about a person is half genetic, so their problems are half inherited.
In fact: 49% is an average across thousands of traits and populations; for personality the number depends on the method (from 22 to 47%)3, 4. It is not a breakdown of one person.

Myth: Firstborns are leaders and more conscientious; only children are selfish.
In fact: Large national samples found no stable effects on character; the differences found in online samples are small and do not create a type8, 9.

Myth: Parents raise boys and girls differently, and that accounts for most sex differences.
In fact: Parents behave almost identically; what differs is their expectations and how they encourage play. Hormones in the womb and peers also play a part10.

Who plays on this

  • The market for “ancestral programs” and diagnoses for children. It sells certainty where science offers none: “your child is highly sensitive,” “a DNA test will show who they’ll become,” “trauma is passed down in the blood.” The tell is a forecast for a specific child. Neither a polygenic index nor a sensitivity type can give one; the size of this market has not been measured.
  • Parenting-program brands. Proven programs exist (PCIT, Incredible Years, Triple P), but a name does not guarantee quality. An honest offer answers these questions: which version, for what age, compared with what, tested on what outcome.
  • “Cultural strictness” as an excuse. Humiliation and coercion are dressed up as tradition. The tell is a rule that targets not the act but what the child must feel and think.

What to do about it

  1. A rule about the act — without threatening love. Name the specific behavior, make the rule clear, respond predictably and without violence, notice improvement. Track the same specific act, not whether the child is “good.” It’s working if that act becomes less frequent.
  2. Check the network, not the family roster. List who provides food, supervision, protection, knowledge and rest for the main adult. Kinship and titles are not the same as care: what matters is who is actually reliable. The sign: the main adult has someone to hand off the shift to.
  3. Keep the child out of the conflict. Argue until you reach agreement; don’t pull the child into alliances against the other adult. More on fights between partners in Feeling for Two — and More.
  4. Check your expectations about sex and birth order. Notice what you say about the child: “he’s a boy, after all,” “you’re the oldest.” If the description runs ahead of what you’ve observed, it’s an expectation, not a fact.

Questions for yourself

  • What was I accepted for as a child — for who I am, or for results? Where am I still paying that price?
  • Which of my traits do I put down to upbringing and which to genes — and why is it convenient for me to keep that explanation?
  • What role was I given by birth order or sex — and did anyone check it against who I actually am?
  • Who in my childhood actually provided care, and who was only nominally there?

When to see a specialist

If a child’s behavior problems persist despite clear, nonviolent rules, see a specialist in proven parent-training programs; measure the same behavior before and during the work. If threatening fights between adults keep happening at home, address those, not just the child.

09. What a child carries out of the family: the narcissistic, anxious, or absent parent — and why most people cope

A childhood-adversity test separates people who will later get sick from those who won’t only slightly better than a coin toss. Yet across hundreds of thousands of people, the same test reliably shows that a hard childhood raises risks. Both are true at once, and the gap between them holds the main point about the self: the family sets probabilities, not fate. What a child carries out of the home is not the parent’s diagnosis but learned answers to the question “on what terms am I accepted?”

How it works

What actually gets passed on: not a label, but repeated experience

Words like “narcissistic mother,” “anxious father,” “absent parent” describe the adult. The child learns not from a diagnosis but from what happens to them day after day: whether they are shamed, whether the adult intrudes on their feelings, whether they have to soothe the adult, whether they feel warmth when they lose. That is why science looks at behavior — humiliation, threats, intrusion, forcing the child to care for the parent — not at the label. The same acts occur in depressed, borderline, and harshly controlling parents; whether the label “narcissism” sets a parent apart from them has not been tested.

Superiority and warmth are two different transmissions

The strongest direct study of where narcissism in children comes from followed children aged 7–11 and their parents for a year and a half, in four waves1. A parent’s “you’re better than other kids, you deserve more” predicted a rise in narcissistic traits. Parental warmth predicted something else — healthy self-esteem. What predicted narcissism was not a lack of love but an instilled sense of rank. The effects are modest, no one changed parenting experimentally, and these children have no diagnoses.

A plausible explanation (not proven) is learning: the child hears again and again that approval is proof of being exceptional, and starts seeking exactly that. Warmth sends a different message: you are valuable without having to outrun everyone else.

There is another side. In teenagers, a mother’s hostility is linked to a later tendency to exploit others, and parental supervision to a weaker one (one study, Mexican-origin families in the US, ages 12–16). Coldness is not harmless, but it acts on a specific facet, not on “narcissism in general.”

Love on condition

A related and better-measured mechanism is conditional regard: warmth is handed out for success, obedience, or the “right” feelings and withdrawn when they’re missing. It is moderately linked to fragile self-esteem that rests only on wins, and also moderately to low mood2. In an adult it sounds like an inner taskmaster: the pressure no longer comes from outside but from “I must.” The data are mostly correlational (they show a link, not a cause).

The dividing line is not between strictness and softness but between a rule about an action and control over the inner world. “Don’t hit your sister” is a rule. “I’m not talking to you until you stop being angry” is trading love for feelings. Warmth, clear rules, and support for autonomy are weakly linked to fewer behavior problems; harsh and psychological control, to more. Part of the link runs the other way: a difficult child draws more control. On spanking, the data are extensive: more problems and not a single sign of improved behavior (a meta-analysis cannot prove causation).

The child as their parent’s parent

When a child spends years responsible for an adult’s feelings or for running the household, this is called parentification. Based on adults’ recollections, this role is linked to later psychological difficulties, but the link is weak. Helping family is normal; what may cause harm is a burdensome role reversal, where the child carries adult responsibility. Almost all studies rely on recollections, and only six followed people over time.

The anxious parent: a loop, not a sentence

A possible loop (a hypothesis) goes like this: the child meets uncertainty, gets scared, avoids it — and gets instant relief; the anxious adult helps them avoid it again and again. The loop can also spin from the other side: a fearful child draws overprotection. In a review of studies, parenting style explains about 4% of the variation in children’s anxiety3. That does not mean parents contribute only 4%, and it does not mean their behavior is unimportant for treatment. In a Swedish study of the children of twins, in this design the link between anxious parent and anxious teenager was better explained by environment than by genetic transmission — but that does not rule out genes for anxiety, and the design does not exclude the child’s influence on the parent4.

Genes sit at the same table

Parent–child resemblance is not, by itself, evidence of upbringing. A German study of twin families estimated the genetic contribution to narcissistic traits at 39–63%; in its model, parent–child resemblance was explained by genes, and no direct transmission through the environment was found5. This does not prove that upbringing never matters, and it does not automatically carry over to other countries. But “he’s like that because his mother is like that” stops being the obvious explanation.

The absent parent

A father’s departure is linked to worse outcomes for children. When researchers compare siblings, use natural experiments, and account for which families break up, the effects shrink but often don’t disappear — especially for finishing school, emotional adjustment, and mental health in adulthood. There is no single figure for how much of this is explained by money and instability. What matters for children is not only divorce but the quality of the parents’ relationship: hostile, cold, and unresolved conflicts between parents are equally linked to children’s problems, and the link holds over time.

The adversity score: strong for a country, weak for a person

The adverse childhood experiences scale adds up events: abuse, neglect, divorce, addiction in the family. Having four or more such events, compared with none, is linked to higher risks across many outcomes, especially those involving addiction and violence. Emotional abuse and neglect are linked to adult depression and anxiety no less than physical abuse, and by some estimates more (they can’t be ranked: they usually occur together)6.

But for an individual, the forecast is poor: in two large cohorts, one British and one from New Zealand, with health assessed at 18 and 45, the scale predicted the outcome little better than chance7. Another crack is memory. What an adult remembers about childhood and what was recorded at the time agree weakly. Neither is a gold standard of truth: they simply capture different things.

Why most people cope

After a potentially traumatic event, about two-thirds of people follow a stable trajectory with low symptoms8. This is not invulnerability, nor does it mean “nothing happened”: it is what particular scales pick up. For some people, symptoms persist or appear later, especially after prolonged abuse in childhood. Resilience is a common outcome, not a rare gift, and not a reason to brush off someone who is struggling.

What this means for the self

Body — partly given. Temperament, anxiety, a tendency toward self-aggrandizement are partly inherited. This shapes what a child reacts to more readily — but not what comes of it.

Ties — repeated scenes. From them the child probably draws a rule: to belong, you must not cry, be the best, watch the adult’s mood, not get in the way (a hypothesis about the mechanism, not a measured chain). This is the first answer to the ancient task of belonging: it sets the terms of attachment, covered in chapter 06.

Story — what the ego builds on these rules. “You’re loved when you’re the best” can grow into self-esteem that needs a constant supply of wins (the link is correlational). “Watch the adult” can grow into the mask of an accommodating person and a nose for other people’s irritation. “Feelings are unnecessary” can grow into a habit of not noticing your own signals. These are not defects but solutions that once worked in a small world.

There is a second trap here: the story an adult later tells about their childhood. Memory and records diverge; the label “narcissistic mother” can be convenient: it makes chaos understandable and removes the question “what about me?” The opposite extreme — “everyone copes, so I have nothing to complain about” — can be a defense against pain. Both stories simplify.

Freedom — in precision. To decide that a behavior is unacceptable, you don’t need to prove the parent’s diagnosis. To stop trading love for wins, you don’t need to rewrite the past. Narcissistic traits weaken with age on average, though a person’s place among peers stays fairly stable: change is possible, but not in one leap. A useful rule: describe traits in degrees, harm in acts, change over time.

Myths

Myth: Praising a child raises a narcissist. In fact: later narcissistic traits were predicted by instilled superiority over others, and healthy self-esteem by warmth. This gives no grounds for holding back warmth.

Myth: A hard childhood is a sentence: a child of divorce will divorce, and someone who was abused won’t build a partnership. In fact: these are risks, not fate; the link between childhood abuse and partner satisfaction in adult couples is weak. How this plays out in a couple is covered in Feeling for Two — and More.

Myth: Anxiety in children comes from anxious parents, through upbringing. In fact: genes, environment, and the child’s own influence on the parent all play a part; parenting style explains a small share of the variation.

Myth: Cutting off contact with a toxic parent is a proven path to well-being. In fact: there are no such causal data. In the US, most estrangements end over time — about 81% with mothers, about 69% with fathers9 — but this says nothing about which is better: estrangement or return.

Who plays on this

The feed and the self-help industry circulate “narcissistic mother” checklists and ready-made recipes for cutting ties. No test was found of whether such a list distinguishes narcissism from depression, borderline traits, or harsh control, nor any estimate of how much money these labels make. Even a study titled “Parental Narcissism Leads to Anxiety and Depression in Children” rests on a survey of adults and a statistical model, not on a diagnosis of the parents10. Signs that someone is playing on this: a diagnosis of a person no one has examined; one rule for everyone (“only cutting ties”); a promise that the label itself will heal.

The opposite extreme is “it’s all trauma”: the adversity score is not a forecast for a specific person, and most people cope.

What to do about it

  1. Describe the acts, not the parent. What was done, how often. “She humiliated me in front of others” is more precise and more useful than “narcissist.”

  2. If you’re raising a child — warmth without rank, rules without trading love. “You worked hard,” “I like being with you” instead of “you’re the best” (the example phrases are editorial, not separately tested). A rule concerns an action, is clear, and comes with a predictable, nonviolent response. Feelings: acknowledge them, name them, limit the harmful action, help the child find a response; that is how a program for parents of preschoolers aged 4–5 works, tested in a randomized trial (parents assigned by chance, compared with a waitlist): six group sessions plus two booster sessions11. The test: you are tracking one specific behavior, not “whether they’re a good kid.”

  3. Boundaries with an adult parent — as a plan, not a verdict. Which behavior, which boundary, what you do when it’s crossed, what you depend on (money, housing, caring for the parent), when you’ll revisit it. Frequency and channel of contact are options too, not just “yes” or “no.” This is a decision tool, not a proven treatment.

  4. Keep a change log. Behavior, setting, consequence, repair, repeat. If you’re changing yourself: “Did I retaliate? Accept a no? Apologize specifically?” If someone near you is causing harm: freedom, fear, sleep, resources, safety — separately from the other person’s apologies. Nice words shouldn’t replace results.

What actually helps when working on yourself is in chapter 46; how to recognize a narcissist near you in adult life, in chapter 24.

Questions for yourself

  • What was warmth given for in my family — and what am I still trying to earn it for?
  • Which label about my parent spares me the question about myself?
  • Which of the things I learned in childhood still work, and which now only get in the way?
  • Do I make decisions about contact with my family based on what is happening now, or on a story about the past?

When to see a specialist

If violence in the family is happening now — safety first: no therapy replaces it. If a preschooler shows signs of traumatic stress after violence between the parents, there is a proven therapy for the child together with an adult — weekly for about a year12; the child doesn’t need to give a coherent account of what happened, and nothing is suggested to them. If an adult’s symptoms persist and get in the way of living, that is a reason for an assessment, not for “everyone had it.”

10. Country and Faith in the Cradle: Language, Feeling Rules and Culture Before Words

A newborn French baby cries with a rising melody, a German one with a falling melody1. There isn’t a single word yet, but the intonation of the native language is already in the cry. Culture enters the self before there is anyone who could notice it, which is why it feels natural rather than learned. Yet what it installs is not a finished personality but a set of recurring situations, expectations and interpretations — and those can be taken apart piece by piece.

How it works

The first layer: melody before meaning

What reaches the fetus before birth is not speech but its melody and rhythm; after birth the baby can already tell apart the mother’s voice and the vowels of its native language, and the smells of her food become familiar (what the fetus hears and tastes is covered in chapter 03). Familiarity is the first form of “ours”: culture attaches to the ancient task of belonging before there is anyone to do the belonging.

The second layer: feeling rules

A feeling is assembled in several steps: a body state → an interpretation of the situation → a decision whether to show it → other people’s reaction to what was shown. Culture can influence each step separately, which is why it matters to tell four things apart: what a person feels, what they would like to feel, what they consider appropriate to show, and what others will read in them.

Display rules. In a survey across 32 countries, people gave different answers about which feelings it is appropriate to show, and to whom3. These are self-reported rules — not a measurement of faces, and not of the feeling itself.

Ideal feeling. A synthesis of data on tens of thousands of people showed that, on average, people in Hong Kong and Asian Americans want a calmer kind of happiness than European Americans do. This concerns the ideal, not what people actually feel: preferring calm doesn’t mean not experiencing delight.

Suppression. Suppressing the outward expression is not the same as feeling less. In experiments, suppressing expression really does change the expression, while trying to suppress the experience itself achieves almost nothing. Reappraisal is, on average, more strongly linked to mental health than suppression, but the strength of that link depends on culture, age and situation, and an international synthesis does not support the simple rule “the East suppresses, the West expresses.”

Faces. Basic expressions are recognized worldwide above chance, but people recognize their own group’s more accurately. An analysis of millions of videos from dozens of countries found 16 expressions that occur in similar situations4. But among Hadza hunter-gatherers, recognition is weak when no ready-made list of words is offered, and the same facial movements don’t reliably tell you what a person feels. There is partial overlap; there is no “same everywhere.”

Words. A comparison of thousands of languages showed that the boundaries of emotion concepts differ, yet almost everywhere feelings are organized along two axes: pleasant/unpleasant and calm/aroused. No word for it doesn’t mean no feeling.

A child probably receives these rules not through lectures but through adults’ reactions: what they notice, name, allow or shame. Gaze and facial synchrony with the mother can be seen as early as 3–9 months. When adults learn to notice and name the feelings of a 4–5-year-old, set a limit on a harmful action and look for a way out together, the child’s understanding of emotions improves, and so, by parents’ reports, does behavior (one study, against a waitlist control).

The third layer: the self in connection or the self apart

The familiar formula is “in the West people define themselves by traits, in the East by relationships.” As a theory it’s a useful starting point; as a fact it isn’t. A synthesis of dozens of studies found no single ladder of “the West is the most individualist”: some groups differ from the US in the expected direction, some don’t5. A survey across dozens of cultural groups identified not one scale but seven separate dimensions: you can be independent in one and connected in another.

The mechanism is plausible: recurring demands and institutions → expectations from others → attention to particular signals → the appropriate action → reinforcement. One example is the “rice theory”: paddy rice requires more shared labor, and people in China’s rice-growing regions behave, on average, in a more “connected” way on a number of measures. In coffee shops in five cities, customers from rice regions were less likely to move a chair that was in their way6, 7. This is observation, not experiment: the history of the region, the city and how people interpreted the task remain alternative explanations.

Other axes include how tight norms are and how much deviance is tolerated (not a synonym for collectivism), and the history of kinship ties, which one study links to the present-day psychology of countries (observational, with a risk of historical confounding). All of this describes group averages. None of these studies shows a sensitive period in childhood, a “national essence” that differs by sex, or stages of culture tied to age.

Duty in the family

Family relationships both provide resources and impose obligations. The “dual filial piety” model distinguishes two different paths to caring for parents: mutual affection and imposed obligation. This is theory and questionnaire data, not switches in the brain; “filial piety,” “face” and “connections” are better broken down into concrete expectations, reputation and exchange than explained by some mysterious national instinct.

The same act by a parent means different things depending on what it tells the child: investment, a predictable duty, rejection or threat. But having a meaning in the culture doesn’t make coercion harmless. An eight-year study of 444 Chinese American families (observational, not experimental, and not mainland China) found the best outcomes with warm, supportive parents, while children of “tiger” parents had worse grades and relationships, more pressure and more depressive symptoms8. A synthesis of more than a thousand studies links warmth plus clear rules to fewer behavior problems, but the link ranges from very small to small, and “in all cultures” is more than the data can support.

A country’s history: inheritance, not breed

Recurring experience — scarcity, unreliable rules, dependence on those in power — can change expectations about which actions pay off, and family stories pass on interpretations of danger and duty. After German reunification, people from the former East more strongly wanted the state to be responsible for certain things (panel data, not random assignment). A study of dishonesty in an economic task found differences, but that’s too little to conclude “socialism makes people liars.” Across three generations of Crimean Tatar families who survived the 1944 deportation, greater suffering in the family is linked to stronger ethnic identity, but not to religious radicalization.

Policy, too, leaves its mark on generations. Among 421 Beijing residents born in 1975–1983, those born after the one-child policy was introduced were, on average, less trusting in economic games, competed less and were more risk-averse9. This is a quasi-experiment in one city, not a verdict on any individual child. Times change, too: in Chinese books from 1970 to 2008, individualist words became more frequent — and this can coexist with a strong sense of family duty, because different sides of the self change at different speeds.

Two languages, one self

Bilinguals, on average, describe themselves differently in different languages. This is a shift in self-presentation that comes with a change of conversation partner and norms, not two personalities. The native language is processed more “automatically” and hits harder (a review; average data): most respondents rate “I love you” and swearwords as stronger in their first language (self-reports), and decisions made in a foreign language shift, too — in moral dilemmas, people are more willing to agree to cause harm for a greater benefit. The famous “bilingual advantage” in self-control did not hold up: after correcting for publication bias, it vanishes in children and adults alike10, 11.

What this means for the self

Culture enters all three layers here, each by its own route.

The body got the familiar. The melody of the language, the mother’s voice, smells. This is neither a choice nor an achievement but a trace of frequency: what sounded more often is what gets recognized. Its power is short-lived: repeated tastings during weaning override the taste from the womb (chapter 03).

Ties gave the rules. Which feelings to show, to whom, how much; what you owe your parents; how far you may deviate. A child receives them before being able to ask “why,” so the rule is probably stored not as a rule but as “this is how the world is” or “this is how I am.” Some of what passes for “reserved by nature” may turn out to be a learned display rule; an “inexpressive people” may look that way because an outsider’s eye reads faces by its own rules.

The story is built on top. Country, faith, family memory of war or deportation supply ready-made stories: who “we” are, who the enemy is, what to fear, what is worth enduring for. The ego takes them gladly: belonging is a cheap prop for self-esteem (“I come from a people who…”), and a group explanation is a convenient defense (“that’s how we do things,” “that’s the Soviet in me”). The mask worn for others is cut to local display rules — and over time may stop feeling like a mask.

The two errors mirror each other. The first is to treat culture as destiny: “I’m Chinese, so I’m obedient,” “Soviet upbringing, so I’m helpless.” The data show no fixed national personality at all; they show concrete expectations that change with the situation. The second is to count everything as culture, humiliation included: “that’s how we raise children here” doesn’t make pressure harmless.

Where the freedom lies. Culture sets group averages, not a specific person. The self has not one dimension but seven, and on each you can stand at a different point. A display rule can be separated from the feeling: hold back the expression when the task requires it, without denying the experience itself. Two languages are not a split but two registers of one self; it may be easier to talk in your second language about what burns in your first (the words hit less hard). What’s given is the melody and the first rules. What’s built is how you interpret them. The interpretation is what can be rebuilt.

Myths

Myth: music played to a pregnant belly will make the child smarter. In fact: no one has measured children’s intelligence after such music, and the adult “Mozart effect” did not replicate12, 13, 14 (more in chapter 03).

Myth: collectivists have no personal self. In fact: different forms of the self coexist; seven separate dimensions don’t reduce to a simple “autonomy versus relationships” axis.

Myth: Soviet upbringing produced learned helplessness. In fact: studies measure specific things — attitudes toward the role of the state, behavior in a task, ethnic identity — and none finds a general “post-Soviet syndrome.”

Myth: you can read a feeling or a lie from a face. In fact: facial expression is not a reliable guide to feelings; an audit of the US program for spotting “suspicious behavior” at airports found no basis for it15.

Who plays on this

The state. Facial-expression reading and “behavior detectors” are used in security, hiring and courts; the errors hit minorities and neurodivergent people harder. Sign: decisions about a person are based on their facial expression. Also in this category is shaping interpretations: the Chinese government fabricates posts en masse to distract, not to argue16; researchers have also studied the effects of a curriculum reform at China’s elite universities and of an independent TV channel on Russia’s 1999 elections (no overall effect size or ranking of countries can be drawn from this). Sign: a ready-made answer to “who are we” and “who threatens us,” with no room for disagreement.

The market. “Smart music” for the belly rests on a debunked effect; facial “emotion recognition” software rests on the same unreliable facial expressions. Sign: a promise to raise general intelligence or to read feelings from a brief exposure.

Family and community. “That’s how we do things” turns humiliation into the norm. Sign: the rule can’t be discussed, and the price of disagreeing is shame or rejection.

What to do about it

Separate the four things. When it seems that “I’m like this” or “they’re like this,” ask: is it a feeling, an ideal feeling, a display rule or someone else’s reading? It’s working if you can pin down exactly what was learned and what is yours.

Delay instead of denial. Holding back an expression is a normal tool in the moment; fighting the experience itself is almost useless. Instead: describe the event, separate fact from interpretation, find one plausible alternative explanation and choose an action (in experiments, reappraisal has a more noticeable effect).
