Sign: anxiety drops, and the new interpretation fits the facts. Don’t reinterpret coercion as harmless.

With children — notice and name. Acknowledge the feeling, name it gently, set a limit on the harmful action, help find a way to respond — that’s how the program tested with parents of 4–5-year-olds is built (six group sessions and two follow-ups; the short technique on its own was not tested separately). Don’t demand that the child calm down at once; track specific episodes, not whether the child is “good.” Warmth, clear rules and age-appropriate independence — together.

Ask the person, not their nationality. With a partner, colleague or child from another culture, ask how they recognize care, what mood is comfortable for them, in which language they can speak about hard things more precisely. Sign: fewer grievances about “coldness” and “insincerity.”

Questions for yourself

Which display rule for feelings do I take to be my character?

Which story about “us” do I defend as if it were a story about me?

Where has “that’s how we do things” justified something that harmed me?

In which language is it easier for me to tell the truth — and why?

Part III. 7–14: School and the Pack — the Self Among Others

11. The Body Before the Storm: Early Puberty and Why Girls Mature Sooner

Puberty begins not at thirteen but at around six — and not with sexual maturation but with the adrenal glands. Over recent decades the first signs in girls have come earlier, while the ovaries’ hormonal start, judging by Danish data, has barely shifted. The body starts changing before the self can make sense of it, and what that change does depends largely not on hormones but on what a person brings to it.

How it works

Two alarm clocks, not one

Puberty is set off by two hormonal clocks that are partly independent of each other.

The first is adrenarche. From about age 6, the inner layer of the adrenal glands starts releasing DHEA and DHEA sulfate — weak androgens, chemically “male” hormones that everyone has. By age 8, children of both sexes develop pubic and underarm hair, body odor, oily skin and acne. The sex glands are not needed for this. An adrenal start this early is found only in humans and some Old World primates; in chimpanzees the age curve is almost the same as ours1.

The second is gonadarche, sexual maturation proper. The hypothalamus — a region at the base of the brain that controls hormones — begins releasing pulses of the hormone GnRH more frequently. In response, the pituitary releases LH and FSH, and the ovaries or testes start making estradiol or testosterone. The first visible sign is breast budding or testicular enlargement.

Both clocks share the same cutoff: signs before age 8 in girls and before 9 in boys count as early. Early adrenarche is more common in girls, is linked to excess weight and excess insulin, and is usually harmless.

How behavior changes at the first alarm clock is poorly studied. In Australian children aged 8–9, higher androgens in boys went along with more difficulties, including with peers; in girls, DHEA sulfate went along with peer difficulties. This is a single snapshot; which causes which is unknown.

Why girls start sooner, and what has shifted

On average, girls start before boys. Breasts begin to grow around age 10 in girls, and the first period (menarche) comes around 13; in boys the testes enlarge around 11–12.

The shift over time in girls is real. Thirty studies from 1977 to 2013 show that the onset of breast growth has moved about three months earlier per decade, with wide variation across countries2. The most striking example is Copenhagen: over 15 years the average age at the start of breast growth fell by almost a year, from 10.9 to 9.9, while menarche moved only three to four months earlier3.

The catch lies in the mechanism. LH and FSH levels in Copenhagen girls in 2006 did not differ from those in 1991, and estradiol at ages 8–10 was even lower. So part of “early puberty” may be early breasts without early ovarian activation — fat, not a hormonal start (not proven). That is why the authors advise against lowering age cutoffs without good reason. An American expert panel judged the evidence sufficient to conclude that breast growth and menarche have come earlier in girls, but insufficient for boys.

In boys the shift is smaller and less certain: in the same Copenhagen study it was three months, and it disappeared once weight was taken into account.

Geography matters too. In China, breast growth in girls starts at around 8.3 in the south and in cities, and at around 10.3 in villages. After 2020, the number of girls seen in clinics for suspected early puberty rose noticeably, but clinical records cannot distinguish a real increase from parents seeing doctors more often.

What moves the hands

  • Genes — roughly half the variation in age at menarche (an imprecise estimate). Hundreds of DNA regions have been found, but they explain only about a quarter of this heritability.
  • Fat. In girls with obesity, the risk of early puberty is more than twice as high4. In boys the picture is split: overweight means earlier, obesity means later. Cause and effect are hard to untangle: early maturation itself predicts higher adult weight.
  • Environmental chemicals. A review of studies found no consistent link with any class of “hormone-like” substances. In Chapaevsk, boys were followed from ages 8–9 to 17–18: dioxins delayed maturation by almost a year, while another group of pollutants sped it up by eight months5. Chemicals push the clock in different directions, not only “earlier.”
  • Threat in childhood. Violence and danger slightly speed up maturation; poverty and neglect do not. In girls, the strongest link is with an absent father. The effects are small, and genes shared by parents and children have not been ruled out: part of the link may be inherited rather than environmental.

The brain: an earlier peak does not mean “more mature”

Total brain volume peaks at around 10.5 in girls and 14.5 in boys; gray matter peaks a year or two earlier in girls6. White matter (the wiring between regions) keeps growing into early adulthood in males, while in females it grows mostly through mid-adolescence.

These are curves of volume, not of maturity. Beyond overall size (the male brain is about 11% larger from birth), sex explains about 1% of differences in structure, and the distributions overlap heavily. An average chart tells you nothing about the “readiness” of a particular girl or the “immaturity” of a particular boy.

When the body gets ahead

Early maturation is linked to more anxiety, low mood and behavior problems. The effect is small and the same in both sexes: more than a hundred studies found no sign that girls fare worse7. What differs is the form: early-maturing girls more often have both internalizing and externalizing problems, boys mostly externalizing ones — fighting and rule-breaking. Boys with an early growth spurt had roughly twice the odds of a depression diagnosis by age 18, although symptom questionnaires at 14 and 18 showed no link.

There are three explanations, and they do not exclude one another: the biology of hormones; a body out of step with peers; selection — children who were vulnerable beforehand also mature earlier. An old cohort study showed that early menarche did not so much create new problems as amplify the ones girls already had8.

Part of the bill comes due later. Among nearly half a million British adults, early puberty is linked to hypertension, type 2 diabetes and angina, even after adjusting for weight9; genetic methods link it to breast, uterine and prostate cancer. These are population-level links, not a forecast for an individual.

The first cycle

Menarche brings a monthly wave of hormones into life. The key experiment was done in adults. When women with PMS had their ovaries switched off, the symptoms went away; when ordinary doses of estradiol or progesterone were added back, the symptoms returned. In women without PMS, the same hormones did not change mood10. What differed was not hormone levels but the nervous system’s sensitivity to normal fluctuations. Data on adolescents are scarce; recommendations are extrapolated from adults. Menstrual pain is nearly universal: most young women have it, and one in five misses classes because of it (by self-report).

What pubertal hormones do to mood and desire is covered in chapter 15; why sleep shifts later into the night, in chapter 17.

What this means for the self

Given — the schedule. Genes set about half of the timing; the rest is fat, environment and experience of threat. You cannot choose when it begins.

Built — the meaning. The body changes, but “early” exists only in comparison with classmates. A nine-year-old with breasts or pubic hair has not grown older inside: on the maturity curve, the brain need not keep pace with the skin. But others may judge the child by their body and treat them as older. Then a gap opens between how the self feels and how it is seen. How the ego responds to this gap — hiding, racing ahead, drifting toward older kids, freezing — has not been measured; these are possible moves, not conclusions. What these choices weigh within the group is the subject of chapter 13; the mirror of other people’s eyes, of chapter 19.

Rebuilt — the interpretation. The data do not say that an early start breaks you. According to one old study, it tends to amplify what a girl already had. Hence the main move for the self: not “something is wrong with me,” but “my body started earlier than my classmates’, and that is not a measure of me.”

The PMS experiment points to the general principle: the same hormone is a different event in different people. Chemistry is not a command but an input that each nervous system reads in its own way.

Then there is the label “girls mature sooner.” Their bodies, yes — on average. Their minds, not necessarily. This label makes it easy to justify both “she’s already big, she’ll figure it out” and “he’s still little, you can’t expect anything of him.” Both apply an average chart to a living person, and the data do not allow that.

Myths

Myth: raging hormones — that’s where teenage meltdowns come from.
In fact: links between hormones and mood exist, but circumstances shape them; in boys, a link between testosterone and mood could not be confirmed (of 27 studies, only one followed children over time). “Storm and stress” is more common in adolescence, but not universal, and weaker in traditional cultures.

Myth: early puberty is caused by hormones in food or by one specific substance.
In fact: no consistent link has been found with any class of such substances, and dioxins delayed maturation in boys. No dedicated study on food was found.

Myth: girls’ brains are more mature.
In fact: they reach peak volume earlier — that is not the same as functional maturity; beyond size, sex explains about 1% of structure11.

Myth: early puberty damages girls.
In fact: the effect is small and the same in both sexes; an early-maturing boy is just as much a reason to keep watch.

What to do about it

  1. Know the cutoffs. Breasts before 8 or testicular enlargement before 9 is a reason to see a pediatrician. Being “earlier than others” within the normal range is not a diagnosis.
  2. An early start is a signal to keep watch, not a verdict. In an early-maturing child (of any sex), watch three things: mood, social circle, and whether there is bullying. Support programs specifically for early maturers have not been tested, so observation is what remains.
  3. A cycle diary. If mood drops before periods or the pain is agonizing, record symptoms every day across several cycles. This gives a doctor something to work with: it shows whether symptoms are tied to the cycle phase. Treatment follows guidelines and ranges from non-drug measures to medication.
  4. Hormonal pills in adolescence — watch your mood. In a Danish registry, girls aged 15–19 on combined pills had almost twice the risk of starting antidepressants12. This is observational and causation is not proven, but it is a reason to watch for changes in mood.

Questions for yourself

  • When did my body start — earlier, later, or at the same time as my classmates? What did I conclude about myself back then?
  • What defense did I develop in the gap between “how I feel” and “how I’m seen” — and is it still holding?
  • What in me did puberty not create, but only make louder?
  • Whose average chart do I still take as the measure of myself?

When to see a specialist

Signs of maturation before 8 in a girl or before 9 in a boy. Period pain that makes you miss classes, or mood that, according to the diary, crashes every cycle. Noticeable mood changes after starting hormonal contraception.

12. School: Grades, Teachers and the State in the Classroom

School is where the self gets a number from adults who are strangers. The number is given for the work, but a child reads it as an answer to the question “what am I like?” Along the way, school teaches which events matter, who the hero is and whom to trust, then measures how accurately you repeat it back. Neither the grade nor the textbook writes the self directly, but they supply a yardstick the ego measures itself against for years.

How it works

A reward changes the meaning of the task

Any activity can be experienced in two ways: “I want this myself and I’m getting stronger at it” or “I’m being made to, and I’m doing it for the sake of something else.” This distinction underlies self-determination theory — an approach that divides motivation into intrinsic and externally imposed. A reward can switch the first into the second: “I draw because I like it” turns into “I draw for the prize.”

This has been tested in 128 experiments1. An expected tangible reward for something that is already interesting reduces the urge to keep doing it later, once the reward is gone. This holds whether the reward is for taking part, for finishing or for performance. Informative praise — “this part worked, and here’s why” — does the opposite: interest and voluntary engagement go up.

It doesn’t follow that money or praise is poisonous in itself. The effect depends on what it’s given for, what is expected and what gets measured afterward. Willingness to keep doing something after the reward is withdrawn is not the same as academic success while the reward is in place. A grade, a written comment and a gift for an A are different interventions, and they can’t be lumped together.

Teachers can learn to give freedom

Autonomy support is a style in which the teacher acknowledges the student’s point of view, explains why a task matters, offers choice within clear limits and still keeps structure. A review of training programs, mostly randomized trials, found that teachers learned this style and their students benefited2. Freedom here doesn’t mean the absence of rules: clear demands and direct instruction are compatible with it.

The limits of what we know: changing a teacher’s style is not the same as a big rise in grades, and little is known about how long the effect lasts.

Teacher expectations: more often a forecast than a cause

Teacher expectations can fulfill themselves — but usually only weakly3. The famous stories about a teacher’s belief raising IQ don’t hold up. The main reason expectations “predict” later success is simpler: teachers see real differences between children and judge them accurately. An accurate forecast and an influence are different things.

Growth mindset: a small effect from a big idea

The idea that “abilities can be developed” became one of the best-known in school psychology. A pooled analysis of almost 100,000 students found a tiny average effect on achievement, and in the highest-quality studies it is indistinguishable from zero4. A large national trial with ninth graders in the US showed a benefit only in some schools and conditions5. A brief message about growth is an add-on to good teaching, not a substitute for it.

Girls’ and boys’ grades

On average, girls get higher grades from teachers — this shows up across hundreds of samples at every level of school6. The gap is small, more noticeable in language and nearly zero in math. Roughly converted into distribution overlap, about 91% of boys and girls fall in the shared zone (this is a calculated illustration, not a measurement). Sex says almost nothing about an individual student.

The gap says nothing about its cause. A grade combines what the child can do with the demands of a particular school and the judgment of a particular teacher. It is not a measure of intelligence or the result of a standardized test. Lagging grades, disengagement from school, reading, math, discipline and well-being are different outcomes, and a “boy crisis” as a single worldwide problem doesn’t follow from these data.

Single-sex schools have to be checked for selection. In comparisons that account for who gets in (hundreds of thousands of students), the advantage in math is negligible for both girls and boys6. The stricter the comparison, the less remains of the idea that single-sex education by itself delivers much.

Age within the class probably also affects how a child is seen and whether ADHD gets diagnosed. Real difficulties in the youngest child in the class can’t be written off as immaturity.

How the state enters the classroom

The mechanism has four steps. The curriculum chooses which events and institutions are visible. Repetition makes some explanations familiar. The exam rewards you for reproducing them. The student may change what they say out loud — but at every step compliance without conviction is also possible. A correct answer on an exam, an answer on a survey and a personal conviction are three different outcomes.

The curriculum really does change stated views: in China, new textbooks from 2004–2010 increased students’ trust in the government7, and in Russia students’ answers are close to the state line, although sincerity can’t be distinguished from conformity8 (more in chapter 37).

A useful contrast is Rwanda after the genocide. A year of a radio drama about reconciliation, listened to in groups, shifted perceptions of what is “done here,” and some behaviors, more noticeably than personal views9. A story can change norms and shared behavior without converting the person inside. So the question has to be specific: what exactly changed — knowledge, the sense of the norm, identity or actions.

The counterweight has been measured too. Teaching critical thinking — comparing claims, evidence, sources and counterarguments — produces a moderate effect on average, although programs vary a lot10. Neither ideological instruction nor instruction in thinking controls the future views of every student.

The body on a schedule

The school bell is also a lever of the state, and it lands on the body. With puberty, sleep timing shifts later into the evening, and an early bell cuts off the morning (more in chapter 17). US pediatricians advise against starting classes before 8:3011. Pushing the start back by 50–65 minutes gave teenagers about 40 extra minutes of sleep on school nights, as measured by a sensor; part of the gain was taken back from weekend catch-up sleep, which shrank12.

What this means for the self

School works on all three layers at once, and each layer works differently here.

Body — given and subordinated to the schedule. The sleep shift in puberty isn’t chosen. A teenager nodding off in first period often hears “lazy” — a label slapped on a mechanism that other mammals have too.

Ties — a new group and a new judge. The teacher is an adult stranger whose judgment is written into the gradebook. A grade touches two ancient tasks at once: belonging and rank in the group (on the group itself, see chapter 13). That’s why the number easily fuses with self-esteem: “I’m a C student,” “I’m a straight-A student.” The ego may start defending not the skill but the number: choosing tasks with no risk, hiding what it doesn’t understand, playing “the smart one” or “I don’t care” (this is an explanation, not a measured fact). Under this mask is the fear that the grade is who I am.

A reward acts on meaning: when the task becomes a means to get an A, “I want to” turns into “they want me to.” Informative praise speaks about the task, not about the person’s worth, and leaves the self as the author.

Story — written at school by others. The curriculum chooses heroes, enemies and causes. The child absorbs not only facts but also which answers are safe. The data distinguish three levels: what a person says, what they take to be the norm around them and what they believe themselves. The state reaches the first level most reliably, the second sometimes, the third less reliably. Inside the self there can remain a gap between the learned answer and a personal position.

Where freedom lies. A grade measures work within a particular system, not the person. Teacher expectations reflect reality more often than they create it — so you shift them with work, not with “believing in yourself.” Being a girl or a boy tells you almost nothing about your abilities. And a correct answer on an exam doesn’t oblige you to believe it: the skill of comparing sources and asking “how is this known” is the tool you keep when school ends.

Dividing life into seven-year stages is a way of telling a story, not a proven boundary. The data in this chapter come mostly from teenagers, students and adults.

Myths

Myth: praise and rewards always kill curiosity.
In fact: what mainly dampens interest is an expected tangible reward for something you already liked; informative praise raises interest1.

Myth: if a teacher believes in a student, the student’s IQ will rise a lot.
In fact: the effect of expectations is usually small, and the dramatic version doesn’t hold up; expectations more often accurately reflect what the teacher already sees3.

Myth: a growth mindset closes the achievement gap on its own.
In fact: the average effect is tiny, and in the best studies about zero; a benefit appears only under suitable conditions4, 5.

Myth: single-sex schools solve boys’ or girls’ problems.
In fact: in a fair comparison the difference is negligible6.

Who plays on this

The state. The curriculum, the history textbook, patriotic education — a proven channel for changing stated views. Signs: a subject has one correct answer to a contested question, sources aren’t compared, rewards go to reproducing a formula rather than to making an argument. Such work most strongly changes what a person says and sees as the norm; what they actually think, less reliably.

School and family as a system of incentives. Money and prizes for A’s. A sign the reward has started to crowd out the activity: the activity is dropped as soon as the prize disappears, or only tasks sure to earn a high mark get chosen.

Sellers of quick fixes. Short motivational programs, “the right mindset” instead of teaching, brain myths in teaching manuals. Sign: they promise a big rise in achievement from a change in belief or mindset, without new lessons, feedback or support.

What to do about it

  1. Separate the number from the person. A grade combines skill with the school’s demands and the teacher’s judgment; it is not a measure of intelligence. A sign it’s working: after a bad grade, the conversation is about what exactly didn’t work in the assignment, not about whether you’re “smart.”

  2. Praise with information, reward with care. Say what worked and why; offer choice within clear limits and explain why the task matters. Don’t introduce prizes for things that are already enjoyed. A sign it’s working: the person takes up the task without an external reason and chooses harder problems.

  3. Compare sources within the subject. For any school claim, ask two questions: how is this known, and what does the other side say. Measure success by reasoning on a new problem, not by agreement with the teacher or a parent.

  4. Protect the morning. Where there’s a choice — classes starting no earlier than 8:30; at home — light in the morning, dim evenings, the phone out of reach at night (more in chapter 17). A sign it’s working: steady sleep and wake times in a sleep diary.

Questions for yourself

  • Which school grade still lives in me as a description of me?
  • What ended for me once people stopped praising or paying me for it?
  • Which of my views are a learned correct answer, and have they ever been tested?
  • Where do I still choose a task I can’t lose?

When to see a specialist

If learning difficulties are persistent and explained neither by age in the class nor by circumstances, a specialist’s assessment is needed — the difficulties of the youngest child in the class can’t be written off as immaturity. If sleep is persistently disrupted and mood is changing, don’t wait: in teenagers, sleep problems more often precede depression than the other way around.

13. The Pack: Status, Bullying and the Price of Belonging

It takes only a couple of minutes: strangers in a computer game stop tossing you the ball, and your sense of belonging, self-esteem, sense of control and meaning drop noticeably. That is the result across 120 studies, regardless of gender, age or country1. At 7–14, the peer pack moves to the foreground: the self increasingly sees itself in the eyes of equals, and the price of belonging becomes real for the first time.

How it works

Belonging is a basic need

People need frequent warm contact within a stable bond. People form bonds easily and resist breaking them; without bonds, physical and mental health suffer. This is one of the ancient tasks — belonging; its first version is attachment to an adult (chapter 06). In the school years, the peer pack starts working on the same task.

An audience changes the price of a choice

The mechanism looks like this: onlookers make approval visible → expected approval raises the value of an act → the person acts → friends’ reactions encourage or discourage a repeat. When peers are watching, a teenager takes more risks, and their reward system responds more strongly than an adult’s does under the same conditions2 (chapter 16 covers the experiments and why the teenage brain is drawn to risk).

This is not a “switched-off brain” or a lack of self-control: what changes is how reward is weighed, not the ability to think. Resistance to peer pressure, by self-report, rises between ages 10 and 30 and is higher in young women on average. But these are group averages — they say nothing about where any one teenager stands, and there is no single age after which the pack loses its influence.

The same mechanism also carries protection. In a school experiment with randomly selected schools, students who were influential among their peers promoted a “no conflict” norm themselves — within a year, reports of conflict fell by 30%4. The pack transmits the local norm, not a pull toward harm: it spreads whatever the norm happens to be.

Visible and liked are different things

Status in the pack comes in two separate forms. Being visible brings access and influence. Being liked brings cooperation and support. Teens who show off “grown-up” behavior are liked by peers at first, but by 15 that advantage disappears (a long-term observation, not an experiment)5. Pseudo-maturity at 13 is no guarantee of success later.

Who attacks, and how

Aggression can be direct — a blow, a threat, an insult to your face — or indirect: rumors, the silent treatment, “don’t let her play.” On average, boys use direct aggression noticeably more often. For indirect aggression, though, the gender difference is close to zero6. Most important: those who use one kind a lot usually use the other too — the two are tightly linked. The formula “boys fight, girls scheme” greatly exaggerates both the gap between genders and the gap between methods.

Why rejection hurts

The first brain scans showed that being excluded from a game activates areas that also work during physical pain. One possible explanation: because of the long human childhood, the attachment system “borrowed” the pain signal so that separation would feel like harm. After a recent, unwanted breakup, a photo of the ex-partner activates even “sensory” areas — the same ones a burned hand does.

But overlapping areas are not the same experience. When an algorithm was trained on the activity patterns, it could tell physical pain from rejection, and a pattern learned for one did not carry over to the other7. A pooled analysis of the early studies also failed to confirm that rejection activates a “pain matrix.” The experiment in which acetaminophen supposedly dulled hurt feelings rests on small samples and has barely been replicated — not a reason to treat rejection with a pill. The conclusion is modest: exclusion signals that a bond is under threat → anxiety and attention shift → the person interprets what happened → withdraws, appeases or fights back. A brain scan does not predict which response it will be.

Bullying: not a quarrel but unequal power

Bullying is repeated aggression where there is an imbalance of power, not just any conflict. It helps to tell apart the victim at school, the victim online, the perpetrator, and someone who is both at once (a separate group that cannot be lumped in with either victims or perpetrators)8. In a survey of schoolchildren aged 11, 13 and 15 across dozens of countries and regions, roughly one in six reported being bullied online — boys and girls almost equally9. This is neither a global figure nor one for any particular country.

The mark lasts a long time. In a British cohort, children assessed at 7 and 11 were followed to age 50: frequent bullying in childhood predicted more depression, anxiety and suicidal thoughts in midlife10. In an American cohort, being bullied at 9–16 predicted anxiety disorders at 19–268. These are associations over time, not a life sentence: no one was assigned to bullying at random, and irreversibility has not been proven. A plausible path: repeated threat → anticipation and avoidance → dropping out of school and relationships → accumulated difficulties.

The majority, authority and the role

People go along with the majority for three different reasons: it seems right, disagreeing threatens belonging, or authority makes obedience a duty. From the outside the behavior looks the same, but each calls for a different defense. Asch’s and Milgram’s famous experiments and the Stanford Prison Experiment are more complicated than the popular retellings: in the last one, the guards were given direct instructions, so it does not isolate the effect of “just the role”12 (more in chapter 35).

What this means for the self

Body — given. Rejection hits hard and fast in everyone: the effect of a couple of minutes of exclusion is huge in every group1. This is not weakness of character but, apparently, an ancient alarm system. You need not be ashamed of it — and you need not take its signal as the truth about yourself.

Ties — built. At 7–14, a second mirror joins the family: the pack. It does not replace the parents all at once: on some matters the group decides, on others the adults still do, and there is no single age of transition. Peers’ judgments become a second mirror, and the self-image is increasingly assembled from them. Bullying is one such judgment, driven home by force and repetition.

The ego learns to defend itself. This is where the first masks for the pack appear — for example, pseudo-maturity or blending into the majority. Pseudo-maturity wins peers’ liking at first — and deflates by 155.

Story — freedom. What gets rebuilt is what the self does with this experience. Someone else’s judgment is information about the pack, not about you. The link “bullying → problems at 50” is a group average, not the fate of an individual10. “I was told to,” “everyone did it,” “that was the role” are not excuses that science backs: instructions, incentives, control and your own choice can be taken apart12. And the same sensitivity to the pack that pulls you toward risk can carry a protective norm4 — it depends on which pack you join and what it rewards.

Myths

Myth: around friends, a teenager’s brain switches off. In fact: the presence of peers changes how reward is weighed in specific tasks; it does not shut down reason, and the same force can spread peaceful norms2, 4.

Myth: popularity at 13 guarantees success in adult life. In fact: visibility, liking and pseudo-maturity are different things; the early payoff from pseudo-maturity disappears by 155.

Myth: rejection is literally the same pain as a burn. In fact: the areas partly overlap, but the activity patterns differ; rejection deserves to be taken seriously — but not as tissue damage, and not with painkillers7.

Who plays on this

Groups with initiation rites. In an old lab experiment, an embarrassing initiation increased liking for the group. This does not prove that pain reliably breeds loyalty, and an experiment on attitudes cannot justify humiliation and pain. More on high-control groups in chapter 25.

Authorities. One reading of Milgram: obedience rests on submission being framed as a duty to a valuable shared mission (an interpretation still being tested). The defense is the right to ask questions.

What to do about it

Check the ties, not the ranking. The question is not “are they popular” but “are there relationships that are mutual and safe.” A sign it’s working: there is someone to turn to when things are bad.

First, stop the repetition. With bullying, what matters most is ending the repeated threat and checking that the protection actually holds — not having a single talk. For schools, a whole-school program has been tested — work in classrooms, shifting peer norms, a response to every incident; in a Finnish trial with children aged 10–12, it improved 7 of 11 measures over nine months. On average, such programs bring moderate benefit, not eradication. A sign: anonymous bullying surveys and the number of repeat incidents go down (a rise in reports at the start may mean people have started telling more). How school as a system shapes the self is covered in chapter 12.

Change the norm through the people others watch. A peaceful norm spreads better through students whom peers actually listen to, not through adults’ favorites4. A sign: fewer incidents — and loners stop being left out of the game.

Make room for dissent. Against majority pressure, it helps to answer without an audience, to have at least one ally, and to have explicit permission to question authority. A sign: in the group you can say “no” and still belong.

Questions for yourself

  • Whose judgment do I still carry as the truth about myself — and who handed it to me?
  • When someone was bullied in front of me, what decided whether I stayed silent or stepped in?
  • In which of my groups can I not say “no” without losing my place?
  • What do I do to belong — and is it worth it?

When to see a specialist

Threats, beatings, sexual coercion, extortion or talk of suicide call for immediate protection and an urgent assessment by a specialist — not “waiting it out” and not a motivational talk8, 10.

14. Screens, Idols and the Feed: From Cartoons to Your First Borrowed Ideas

Children aged 9–11 who were shown influencer posts featuring unhealthy snacks then ate about a quarter more than after neutral posts1. Knowing that something is an ad and being protected from it are two different things, and it is in that gap, between 7 and 14, that part of the self is assembled: whose tastes, whose words and whose ideas I start to count as my own.

How it works

A screen is not a substance but a replacement for time

A screen does not “inject” anything by itself. It acts through what it displaces: live conversation, play, sleep. Or, conversely, through what it adds: a good program and an adult nearby watching too. That is why “hours in front of a screen” is an incomplete measure. You need to know what is watched, with whom and instead of what.

A large review of studies on children under six shows both sides: watching programs is linked to slightly worse cognitive development, co-viewing to slightly better2. Both links are weak, and both are observational: there is no experiment here where children are randomly split into groups. Part of the link may run in the opposite direction: in a struggling family, or for a child with difficulties, the screen gets switched on more often.

The most alarming numbers come from Japan: one-year-olds who spent four or more hours a day in front of a screen were almost five times as likely to lag in communication by age two as those who watched less than an hour3. But the study did not distinguish screen content, and a screening — a short questionnaire check — is not a diagnosis. In a Canadian observational study with checks at two, three and five years, the link from early screen time to later development turned out to be small.

There is also a counterexample. When Sesame Street reached some areas of the US earlier than others, children who had access to it went on to do better in school, especially boys4. This is a natural experiment — a comparison set up by history itself. So what matters is content and opportunity, not the mere fact of a glowing screen.

Why? A child under two learns when three things coincide: their attention, a live person who responds, and a clear signal. Video gives the signal without the response. Until age six, children on average learn noticeably less from video than from a person, and the gap narrows with age. But a video call, where a person responds to the child, works: toddlers aged 24–30 months learned new verbs through one, but not from a recording (a small study). More on the windows of early development in chapter 05.

Teenagers and the feed: three different questions

The argument “social media caused a depression epidemic” mixes three questions. Can the feed harm a specific person? What is the average effect? What share of the overall rise in teen depression does it explain? A “yes” to the first does not answer the third.

An analysis of data on hundreds of thousands of teenagers found that all digital time combined explains no more than 0.4% of the variation in well-being5. That is not “0.4% of depression is caused by social media,” and it is not proof of harmlessness: an average figure does not rule out harm to vulnerable people. In a British cohort, 14-year-olds on social media five or more hours a day had depression symptom scores higher by about half among girls and by a third among boys than peers who spent one to three hours6. These are questionnaire scores, not the probability of a diagnosis; and the comparison is within each sex, so it does not prove that girls are by nature more vulnerable. Bullying, lack of sleep, body image and self-esteem show up alongside it in the data, but a snapshot study cannot show which way each arrow points.

Causal evidence exists, but it is narrow. In an experiment with families, recreational screen time was cut to three hours a week for two weeks — and parents reported fewer difficulties in their children (on average about nine years old)7. It was a short experiment, and the ratings came from the parents themselves, who knew which group they were in. No rule of “three hours for life” follows from it.

School phone bans show that what is easy to control is not necessarily what matters most. In England, schools with strict rules did see less phone use in class, but this changed neither overall phone time nor well-being8. The study is observational: it does not prove bans are useless either. Distraction, learning, bullying and mood need to be measured separately.

Science has not established a safe “age for a first smartphone.”

The idol: a familiar face as an argument

A face seen many times becomes familiar, and the familiar can come across as trustworthy. Then entertainment, “who I am” and persuasion fuse into one.

An experiment shows that the persuasion works: children aged 9–11 were randomly shown influencer posts with unhealthy snacks, healthy ones, or non-food products1. After the unhealthy ads, the children ate on average about 450 calories versus about 360 — a quarter more. Ads for healthy food produced no mirror effect: a cheerful “good-for-you” message does not necessarily work as an antidote. And the experiment measured one snack, not weight over a year.

A large review of nearly a hundred studies confirms this: food marketing increases immediate intake, choice of the advertised product, and food preferences9. There is much less data on long-term weight and teeth. And all of this concerns food: for toy unboxing videos, nagging parents and in-game purchases there are no comparable verified figures.

It is plausible, but not proven, that the influence travels through “friendship” with an influencer you have never met (psychologists call this a parasocial relationship): the experiment shows persuasion, not a mechanism that runs through “friendship.”

The algorithm: the road exists, but not everyone walks it

Recommendations adjust to what a person has already watched. In an audit, British researchers set up test accounts posing as teenage boys on TikTok: the share of misogynistic videos in their recommendations rose from 13% to 56% in five days10. This is about what accounts created by scientists receive, not about how many real boys changed their views.

On YouTube the picture is more complicated. One audit found pathways that can lead into radical content. Tracking of real viewing showed that radical content is watched by a small audience11. And a survey combined with behavioral data showed that prior views, subscriptions and outside links play a part. Three methods see three different things: a possible path, actual viewing, and who is watching. A universal conveyor belt of “ordinary user → extremist” has not been proven.

It is worth breaking the chain into links: see → believe → buy → agree → act. Each link is a separate step, and they need to be measured separately.

At home in the interface ≠ able to check

“Digital natives” are deft with apps, but being at home in an interface does not by itself teach you to spot an ad, check who the author is, or catch a fabricated source. This is a skill, and it can be taught: in a school experiment, teenagers were taught “lateral reading” — leave a suspicious site and see what independent sources say about it. The skill proved teachable, although the exact size of the effect is unknown.

“Inoculation” against manipulation — a short video about a technique such as a false dilemma, after which people recognize it in new examples — has been tested on adults; in the field, on YouTube, the recognition effect is small12. How long it lasts and whether it works the same way in children aged 7–14 is unknown.

What this means for the self

Between 7 and 14, a third source of material for the self joins the body and the family: other people’s faces on a screen. Models for the self no longer come only from the class and the family (on the group, see chapter 13): the feed widens the circle of people a child compares themselves with.

Given — a familiar face can seem trustworthy, and recommendations adjust to what you watch. Knowing this does not switch it off: experiments show that ads change what children eat, and they do not show that a lesson about advertising makes them immune.

Built — tastes, words, jokes, first views. Part of what a teenager will call “my opinion” first arrives as someone else’s words, repeated. There is nothing pathological in this: the self has always been assembled from models — once from the adults of the tribe, now from influencers as well. The difference is that the models are now partly chosen by a recommendation system.

The ego can cling to an idol or an idea because they offer a ready-made place: “I’m one of those who…” (this is a hypothesis based on the mechanics, not a measured fact). Then criticism of the idol is felt as an attack on yourself, and the mask for others is assembled from the same kit.

Freedom lies not in refusing to be influenced (knowledge gives no immunity) but in breaking the chain into links. Seeing does not mean believing. Believing does not mean buying or acting. Between the links there is room for a question, and that room can be trained: source checking can be taught, a manipulation technique can be recognized. Not forever, but as a skill.

There is a flip side, too: the same sensitivity to other people’s models does not only carry harm. Social influence is not destructive in itself — norms against bullying can travel through the same channels. The question is not “screen or no screen” but “what, with whom and instead of what.”

Myths

Myth: screens cause a special disorder — “virtual autism.”
In fact: studies record delays flagged by screening, not a distinct type of autism caused by screens; sufficient evidence for such a diagnosis has not been found3. Developmental difficulties are assessed on their own terms, not by viewing history.

Myth: the 0.4% proves social media is harmless.
In fact: it is the average share of variation in overall well-being; it does not rule out harm from specific content or to vulnerable people5.

Myth: banning phones at school cures depression.
In fact: a ban reduces phone use in class, but not overall time or well-being — at least in the English data8.

Myth: if a child understands that something is an ad, it does not affect them.
In fact: recognizing an ad and declining to buy are different outcomes; experiments show that ads change what children eat, and they do not show that a lesson about advertising makes them immune1, 9.

Who plays on this

Food and product makers — through influencers whom children trust as familiar faces. The sign: the product is built into an entertainment post; in the experiment, ads for unhealthy food worked, and ads for healthy food did not1.

Platforms — recommendations adjust to what you watch. The sign: within a few days the feed narrows to one topic and heats up (as it did for the test accounts)10.

Sellers of ideas. A path to radical content exists, but there is no measured effect on the beliefs of real teenagers11. How ideas spread from head to head: chapter 26. How the image of the “real man” and the “proper girl” is sold: chapter 20.

The state also shapes first ideas, through school; the mechanics are in chapter 12.

What to do about it

A short screen experiment. For two weeks, cut recreational screens (in the tested experiment, to three hours a week), and plan in advance what will replace them7. Before and after, write down sleep, arguments, how things are going, and what the child says. Count it as working if something besides the parent’s impression changes. For an adult, the same for yourself: log time in the feed, sleep, mood, spending, then change one thing at a time.

Screens and sleep. No screens in the evening (in the tested version, after 9 p.m. on school days, for two weeks; a study without a control group), phone out of reach of the bed. Night mode by itself did not improve sleep (a study in young adults). The sign it is working: falling asleep earlier, as recorded in a sleep diary. More on sleep in chapter 17.

A question for the idol. When watching an influencer together with a child, ask: “Who benefits if we buy this or believe this?” Before a decision in a group, ask yourself: “What would I choose if no one were watching?” These questions follow from the mechanics but have not been tested as methods in their own right.

Check together. When you come across a sensational news story, leave the page and look at what others write about it and about the author. Practice recognizing manipulation techniques on fresh examples — and on honest emotional messages too, so you don’t slide into suspecting everything12. This is training, not a lifelong inoculation.

For children under two, that time is better spent with live people (a video call counts); if there is a screen, watch together and talk about what is on it.

Questions for yourself

  • Which of my “own” opinions was first heard on a screen?
  • At which link do I usually stop: seeing, believing, buying, acting?
  • Whom am I defending when criticism of an idol stings — them or myself?
  • What disappeared from my day when the feed came into it?

Part IV. 14–21: Puberty, Desire and the Search for Self

15. The hormones of growing up: what testosterone and estrogen really “want”

Over puberty, boys’ testosterone production rises roughly 30-fold. A review of 27 studies of healthy adolescents could not confirm that this rise changes their mood1. A large shift in a hormone does not guarantee a large shift in the mind. Hormones don’t “want” anything: they change the conditions under which the brain responds to a situation, and the response itself is shaped by context, experience and the story you tell about yourself.

How it works

Two modes of action

Sex steroids act on the brain in two ways. The organizing mode is a lasting rewiring of circuits during a sensitive window (the first such window is in the womb, chapter 02). The activating mode is a reversible effect that lasts while the hormone is in the blood: the hormone goes, the effect goes.

In rodents, puberty is a second organizing window: sex hormones shape the circuits of adult social and sexual behavior. In humans, the lasting part is barely proven. A review found “little good evidence” of long-term behavioral traces of pubertal hormones — only hints for gender identity and for some mental disorders that differ by sex2. In mice, yes; in humans, an open question.

Testosterone: what shows and what doesn’t

Everyone has the hormone. Testosterone and DHEA are normal female hormones, not only male ones; what differs is the amount, not the set of hormones.

Across studies, the link between testosterone and aggression is close to zero3. In men it is slightly more visible; in women it is indistinguishable from zero. Most telling are the experiments in which people were given the hormone: aggression does not reliably rise. There is a correlation, but no “add more and you get angrier” lever.

A bargaining experiment breaks the “testosterone = toughness” pattern4. Women who received testosterone made fairer offers. Those who merely believed they had received the hormone behaved less fairly. The expectation about the hormone pulled the opposite way from the hormone itself. The data fit the hypothesis that “testosterone tunes you to seek status — whatever earns respect in this group” better than the idea of automatic aggression (the evidence is partial: one game, no proven mechanism).

In adolescents, testosterone’s role is clearer in the reward system. The higher it is, the more strongly the striatum — a deep brain hub that weighs gains — responds to monetary reward, in both sexes. In long-term follow-up, changes in this response tracked changes in testosterone, with reward sensitivity peaking around age 19. This is a route to novelty seeking, not a proven cause of real-world risk-taking (more in chapter 16).

The hormone also follows life. Among Filipino men followed for four and a half years, those who became partnered fathers lost noticeably more morning and evening testosterone than those who stayed single5. So the hormone doesn’t just push behavior: taking on a life role goes hand in hand with a change in the hormone.

Estrogen, the cycle and sensitivity

In girls, puberty opens a gap in depression: from then on, women have more of it. Which signal drives this is unresolved. In one American study, the rise was explained by testosterone and estradiol, not by pubertal stage; after three more waves of measurement, only early maturation and testosterone remained. In a British cohort, symptoms tracked the stage of breast development, not age at first period. Different samples, markers and decades give different answers.

An experiment in adults pointed to the mechanism6. Researchers used a drug to “switch off” the ovaries of women with and without premenstrual syndrome, then added back estradiol or progesterone. In women with the syndrome, symptoms disappeared when the ovaries were switched off and returned with the hormone. In the others, the same hormones left mood unchanged. The issue was not abnormal levels — what differed was the brain’s sensitivity to normal fluctuations (a small study, but randomized).

This brings us to PMDD, a severe premenstrual form in which mood and functioning crash. When it is diagnosed strictly, from daily records, it is found in about 3% of women; in the general population, less often7. Studies that rely on recall and provisional diagnoses get a figure more than twice as high. Data on adolescents are scarce: their guidelines are borrowed from adult ones.

What the cycle does not do is impair thinking. A synthesis of about a hundred studies found no consistent shifts across phases in objectively measured cognitive tasks8. That does not make individual symptoms imaginary. And menstrual pain is nearly the norm: 71% of young women report it, and one in five misses school because of it.

The hormone amplifies what was already there

Early periods did not so much create new behavior problems as amplify the ones a girl already had9. The hormone seems to work like an amplifier: it turns up the volume on what exists rather than writing a new program. Later reviews add selection and psychosocial pathways alongside biological ones, and maturation itself is measured in so many different ways that this alone produces contradictions (why girls mature earlier — chapter 11).

Body, skin, sleep

Androgens bring body odor, oily hair, acne and a growth spurt. Acne affects about one in ten 10–24-year-olds worldwide, slightly more than in 1990. Puberty shifts the sleep clock later — this happens in other mammals too, not just in humans with phones; an early school bell turns the shift into short, late sleep (chapter 17). The link between sleep and testosterone is mixed (data on men only): in one small experiment, a week of five-hour nights lowered the hormone in ten young men, while a synthesis of studies found no significant effect of short partial sleep loss; a full day without sleep does lower it.

Sex: similarity is the rule, difference goes trait by trait

A synthesis of more than a hundred meta-analyses showed that most psychological sex differences are small, with distributions overlapping by about 92%10 (more in chapter 02). There are large differences too: the gap between interest in “things” and interest in “people” is noticeably larger in a huge synthesis of questionnaires11. But even here the curves overlap by about 64%: many people don’t resemble their group’s average. These are interests measured by questionnaire, not abilities. Each trait is a separate question: “on average men and women are the same” is as wrong as “they are different species.”

What this means for the self

Given: the timing and size of the hormonal rise, the brain’s sensitivity to its fluctuations, the amplified reward response. You don’t choose these. One nervous system barely notices the cycle; in another, mood swings every month — at the same levels.

Built: what this amplifier gets pointed at. If testosterone really does tune you toward status, then what earns status — fighting, grades, jokes, honesty — is decided by the group and the culture. Puberty seems to make louder what was already assembled in childhood and among peers. That is why the same hormone produces different things in different people.

The story is the layer you can change. The experiment where the expectation about the hormone behaved differently from the hormone itself is a model for the whole chapter. A teenager gets ready-made scripts from the culture: “raging hormones,” “testosterone makes you a man,” “she’s not herself at that time of the month.” The ego grabs them with both hands. The first hand is the excuse: “it’s not me, it’s the hormones” removes responsibility for an outburst and protects self-esteem. The second is the yardstick: “my testosterone is low, so I’m not man enough,” “my cycle is my weakness.” Both mistakes turn an amplifier into a script and a group average into a verdict on a person.

Freedom lies in the gap between the signal and its interpretation. The body supplies the volume: irritation, craving, sleeplessness, an urge to win. The self decides what it means and where to direct it. Once you know the hormone tends to amplify, you stop asking “what am I like, hormonally” and start asking “what am I amplifying right now.” The mask of the “real man” or the “proper girl” rests on the belief that the body wrote the role in advance; the data don’t support that (how this mask is sold — chapter 20).

Myths

Myth: “raging hormones” make teenagers unbearable.
In fact: links between hormones and mood exist but depend on non-biological conditions; in boys, a link between testosterone and mood could not be confirmed. There is more “storm and stress” in youth, but not for everyone, and less in traditional cultures.

Myth: testosterone makes you aggressive.
In fact: the link is close to zero, and administering the hormone does not reliably increase aggression. The hormone is no excuse for violence.

Myth: you think less clearly during your period.
In fact: a synthesis of about a hundred studies found no consistent shift in thinking across cycle phases.

Myth: abstaining from ejaculation raises testosterone and energy.
In fact: trials have not shown this, and the famous paper on it was retracted12 (more in chapter 49).

Who plays on this

The market for “hormonal identity”: testosterone boosters, abstinence marathons, courses in “male power” and “feminine energy” (chapter 29). The mechanism: sell a hormone as the key to who you are, and shame as a diagnosis. Warning signs: one molecule explains your character, a retracted or lone study is cited, a promise to raise your “level” instead of checking a specific complaint. What is actually known about fats, zinc and boosters is in What Works in Nutrition.

What to do about it

  • Log as you go; don’t reconstruct from memory. If it seems your mood follows your cycle, note mood, physical symptoms and how much they interfere every day for at least two cycles, with dates relative to your period. This is a diagnostic method, not treatment. Look for a repeating link to timing, whether there are symptom-free stretches, and whether it disrupts your life; without a link to the cycle, don’t blame hard days on hormones.
  • On hormonal contraception, watch your mood. In a Danish registry, adolescents on combined pills had almost twice the risk of a first antidepressant prescription13 — this is an observation, not a proven cause; adult trials show no average worsening. Track your mood from the start; if it worsens, review the method with a doctor rather than abruptly stopping contraception.
  • Ask yourself what earns respect here. If you feel the pull toward status, the amplifier is already on; the choice is which behavior earns it in this group and whether that is the price you want to pay.
  • Run experiments on yourself honestly. If you want to change something “for the sake of your hormones,” write down how you feel at baseline and watch what changes. The result will be about you, not about a general theory.

Questions for yourself

  • What do I blame on hormones so I don’t have to own it?
  • What role do I believe is written into my body — and who told me so?
  • What exactly is my body amplifying right now: excitement, anxiety, shame?
  • What earns respect among my peers — and is that my choice?

When to see a specialist

Mood crashes that, across two cycles of records, fall on premenstrual days and wreck school, work or relationships; a noticeable drop in mood after starting contraception; menstrual pain that makes you miss school. For children — a pediatrician if breasts began developing before age 8 or the testicles began to enlarge before age 9. Constant fatigue is a reason for a medical workup, not for an abstinence marathon.

16. Dopamine and the teenage brain: why risk pulls

A teenager takes more risks than an adult — but in lab tasks, no more than a child. The difference shows up where a decision is “hot”: arousal, instant results, an audience. So the issue is not that teenagers can’t see the danger; it’s how much a win here and now weighs for them — and in whose eyes. In these years the brain recalculates the price of risk, and the self learns from that recalculation.

How it works

Dopamine: not “nice,” but “want,” “learn,” “worth the effort”

Dopamine is released by neurons in the midbrain whose fibers run to the striatum (a deep hub where actions are selected) and to the frontal cortex. It has four well-described jobs.

The first is prediction error: a brief burst when things turn out better than expected. This is how the brain learns what is worth repeating (reliably shown in animals; in humans, drugs that raise or block dopamine change this signal and the tendency to repeat a winning choice). The second is “wanting”: everything linked to a reward starts to pull at your attention and your hands. The third is effort: dopamine in the nucleus accumbens (part of the striatum) is tied to the willingness to spend energy, attention and time. The fourth is novelty: something new, by itself, revs up the midbrain.

Pleasure is a different system. In experiments mostly on rodents, “liking” is produced by small “hot spots” running on opioid and endocannabinoid signals, and dopamine is not required for them1, 2. In humans the picture partly matches (small studies): in healthy men, dopamine release from amphetamine tracked the desire for more, not mood; a dopamine blocker did not reliably dampen euphoria. Still, there is no clean split: drugs that raise and lower dopamine change the pleasure people get from music, and dopamine is released at the peak of a musical experience. The honest summary: dopamine is the engine of motivation, learning and the thrill of the chase; in pleasure itself, its role is indirect.

What happens when the system is pushed from outside can be seen in Parkinson’s disease. Drugs that directly stimulate dopamine receptors (agonists) bring new compulsive urges — to gamble, shop, have sex, eat — in 17% of patients who take them, versus 7% of those who don’t (tested on thousands of patients). In the first case reports, the gambling urge went away after the drug was stopped. This is not a model of a teenager, but it is a vivid example: an urge can be chemistry, not character.

Gas before brakes

The classic framework is the “dual systems” model. From roughly 10 to 20, the reward system is especially responsive, while the prefrontal cortex — the front part of the brain that holds back impulses and keeps the plan — gains strength slowly, well into the twenties. For several years the “gas” outruns the “brakes,” most of all around friends and when aroused.

The age pattern holds up. When the same people aged 8–27 were scanned twice, two years apart, the nucleus accumbens response to reward rose toward adolescence and fell afterward; risk-taking in a balloon-inflation game followed the same arc, and the rising response went along with rising testosterone (in both sexes; what the hormones themselves do is in chapter 15). In a survey of thousands of people aged 10–30 across 11 countries, sensation seeking rose to a peak at 19, while self-regulation grew to a plateau at 23–263.

Culture changes the shape of the curve. Self-regulation grew with age almost everywhere, but the hump in sensation seeking appeared in only 7 of the 11 countries: in Jordan it rose steadily, and in Sweden, Colombia and Cyprus it showed no link to age3. The shape of the curve depends on the environment.

Direct data on teenage dopamine are scarce. One small imaging study showed that D1 receptors (one type of dopamine receptor) decline from 10 to 30, fastest in the cortex during the teen years. Claims of a “dramatic remodeling of the dopamine system” rest mostly on animals.

The weak link is “because”

That teenagers take more risks than adults is a fact. That this is because reward matures earlier than control is plausible, but not proven. A synthesis of lab studies found that teenagers’ extra risk-taking shows up only in “hot” tasks with instant feedback, and where a safe option exists, they take even fewer risks than children4. Studies linking brain response to real-world risk have produced mixed results.

The same pattern can be read another way. Risk is not one thing: sensation seeking and impulsive acts peak in youth, while impulsive choice and betting at known odds decline from childhood. Part of “risk” is exploring the unknown, and it can be useful. Teenagers may learn from reward better than adults: their hippocampus, the memory hub, works more closely with the striatum.

Reasoning reaches adult levels around 16; restraint in the heat of excitement and strong emotion arrives after 18. In one study, control under unpleasant emotions was worse in 18- to 21-year-olds than in those over 21. A teenager is not dumber than an adult — on average, their brakes are weaker precisely in the heat of the moment.

An audience changes the price of a choice

In experiments where participants were randomly left alone or with peers, the presence of friends raised risk-taking in 13- to 16-year-olds more than in adults; on a driving simulator, friends watching amplified both risk and reward-area activity in teenagers5, 6. The audience’s approval seems to become part of the win. Reason does not switch off — the price changes.

The same channel carries protection. In dozens of schools, students who were influential in their networks promoted norms against conflict — and within a year, reports of conflict dropped by 30%7. What peers amplify is not harm but the local norm. On the price of belonging, see chapter 13.

An audience with special status has a stronger effect. In a field experiment, young men on skateboards took more risks and fell more often in front of an attractive female observer (one study, no large replications, testosterone’s role unproven).

Substances during construction

In these years the brain prunes excess synapses (contacts between neurons), wraps its pathways in myelin (insulation on the fibers) and remodels its dopamine and nicotine systems. A substance that hits these systems lands on a construction site (data mostly from animals).

Alcohol. Teenagers were scanned before their first drink and one to two years later: in those who started drinking heavily, frontal gray matter shrank faster; in moderate drinkers, somewhere in between (a good study, but family and unmeasured factors can’t be ruled out). An early first drink is linked to later addiction, but in twins this link was entirely explained by family factors — it is more a marker than a cause.

Nicotine. Teenagers who tried vaping have 3.6–4 times the odds of later taking up smoking; whether that is cause or a shared tendency, observational studies can’t settle.

Cannabis. The concentration of THC (the active compound) in samples seized in the US rose sharply over twenty years. Daily use of high-potency cannabis is linked to almost five times the odds of psychosis (losing touch with reality: delusions, hallucinations)8; starting by 15 combined with daily high-potency use is linked to an earlier first episode. Causation is not fully proven: a predisposition to schizophrenia itself raises the pull toward cannabis. Psychosis overall is rare — about 21 cases per 100,000 people a year. In Danish registries, the link between cannabis use disorder and schizophrenia is strongest in young men aged 16–20. The claim that IQ drops permanently is disputed: in twins, no decline was found compared with a sibling who didn’t use, and the deficit nearly disappears after three days without cannabis.

What this means for the self

Given — a window. From roughly 10 to 19, the pull toward the new and intense grows on average (in most countries studied), while restraint in the heat of excitement lags behind. This may not be a defect (the explanation is a hypothesis): a species that has to leave the nest, find a mate and a rank in the group benefits from years when the unknown attracts more than it frightens.

Built — what the window opens onto. The shape of the curve differs across countries, and peers can carry both risk and a norm against it. Sensation seeking is common raw material; whether it becomes skateboarding, science, racing, music or substances depends largely on access, the audience, and what the group rewards with approval.

Here the ego learns to show itself. Risk in front of an audience can be a way to prove who you are: brave, grown-up, one of us. Other people’s eyes enter the tally of the win (how the self is assembled from other people’s eyes — chapter 19). Hence a dangerous substitution: “I want” gets read as “this is me.” Dopamine says it’s worth reaching for, not that you like it or that it suits you. A strong pull toward the feed, a bet or a substance is a “want” signal — not the voice of character and not proof that you like it.

Rebuilt — more than people tend to think. Among serious young offenders (mostly young men), roughly two-thirds of the drop in crime from 15 to 25 was explained by life changes — work, a partner, different friends, psychological maturing — not by the brain’s clock alone9. The self grows out of risk through roles and ties, not just by waiting for the frontal lobes to mature. Hence freedom: what you steer is not the urge itself, but where it goes and who will be watching.

Myths

Myth: dopamine is the pleasure molecule. In fact: it is mostly “want,” learning and readiness for effort; other signals produce pleasure, though dopamine partly colors it too.

Myth: the brain matures by 25. In fact: there is no single date — different parts of the brain mature anywhere from 14 into the thirties10 (more in chapter 21).

Myth: teenagers take risks because they don’t understand the danger. In fact: by 16 they reason almost like adults; what lags is restraint in the heat of excitement and in front of an audience.

Myth: a “dopamine detox” resets the reward system. In fact: no one has measured any “reset,” and the method’s own author asked that the name not be taken literally11 (more in chapter 28).

Who plays on this

Products are built around “want.” Unpredictable rewards target prediction error directly: a “near miss” on a slot machine activates the same areas as a win and strengthens the urge to keep going. On social media, people adjust how often they post to the pace of likes, and in teenagers reward areas respond more strongly to photos with many likes12, 13. No one has measured a “dopamine hit” from a notification in humans — it is a metaphor for checking in hope of a reward.

Spending on loot boxes — chests of random loot in games — is linked to problem gambling (cross-sectional data). In long-term studies, alcohol advertising predicted that teenagers would start drinking earlier and drink more.

Signs: the reward arrives unpredictably; there are approval counters; “almost made it” nudges you toward one more try.

What to do about it

  • Change the environment instead of lecturing the frontal lobes. The hottest window is around 16–20 and around friends: it makes more sense to limit access (alcohol, vapes, the car) than to count on persuasion. Alcohol “at home, so they learn” doesn’t protect: teenagers who are given it drink full servings more often14. The sign it’s working is fewer situations, not more promises.
  • Limits in advance, before the audience. Where showing off can maim, the rule is set before the group gathers: how much, and who drives. In the heat of the moment, the one deciding is no longer the one who thought it through.
  • Break one cue-driven habit. One family of apps — no more than 10 minutes per platform a day for three weeks, or a one- to four-week break; notifications off, phone out of the bedroom (the last one hasn’t been tested directly). Measure minutes and mood before and after; this was tested on adults and students, so expect a small effect, not a rebirth.
  • If cannabis — later, less often, weaker. The riskiest combination: starting in early adolescence, daily use, high-potency strains, psychosis in the family. Text-message support programs modestly help young adults quit vaping: after seven months they abstain more often than without them (not tested under 18).

Questions for yourself

  • What do I take for my own desire, and what is just a learned pull toward a signal?
  • Who do I take risks in front of — and what am I trying to prove to them?
  • Which norm in my circle amplifies risk, and which one protects?
  • Where is someone selling my urge back to me — and by what signs can I see it?

When to see a specialist

If new compulsive urges — to gamble, shop, have sex, eat — appear in the first months after starting or raising the dose of dopamine agonist drugs, tell your doctor: this is a known side effect, and in the first case reports it went away after the drug was stopped.

17. Sleep: the brain’s night shift — and why a teenager can’t fall asleep at ten

A teenager who can’t fall asleep at ten and get up at seven isn’t lazy: in puberty the internal clock shifts later, not only in humans but, by all indications, in other mammals too1. Sleep is not the self switching off but a night shift: the brain sorts the day’s experience, recalibrates anxiety and supports immunity. How you feel and see yourself in the morning was partly decided by sleep.

How it works

Two forces: pressure and the clock

Two processes decide when and how long you sleep. The first is sleep pressure: it builds with every waking hour and is discharged during sleep. The leading candidate for its carrier is adenosine, the molecule caffeine blocks; sensitivity to caffeine depends on genes in this system. The second is the circadian clock: an internal rhythm that opens and closes the “window” for sleep regardless of tiredness. The two forces interact constantly; organs have their own clocks too, set by food and metabolism.

The human clock runs slightly longer than a day — on average about 24 hours 10 minutes. Light resets it every day. Light reaches special cells in the retina; the signal travels to the suprachiasmatic nuclei — the “master clock” in the hypothalamus — and suppresses melatonin production. Melatonin is a signal that says “it’s night now,” not a sleeping pill: it rises in the dark and peaks around 3 a.m. (shifted by chronotype). In healthy people its rise begins anywhere from 6 p.m. to past midnight.

Even ordinary room light in the evening delayed melatonin onset in almost all volunteers aged 18–30 and shortened the “melatonin night” by about an hour and a half2. A week in a tent under natural light shifted the clock earlier, most strongly in “night owls”; a weekend produced about two-thirds of that shift (small studies, young adults).

The night by stages

Sleep runs in cycles of about 90 minutes: light sleep, deep slow-wave sleep and REM sleep (rapid eye movement, when the most vivid dreams happen). Deep sleep dominates early in the night, REM toward morning. With age the shares of deep and REM sleep fall, falling asleep takes longer, and night awakenings increase. In three preindustrial societies where sleep was measured with instruments, people slept in one nighttime block, falling asleep several hours after sunset, as the temperature dropped.

What sleep does

Memory is the best-proven function. In slow-wave sleep, neurons in the hippocampus (the structure that holds fresh memories) replay the day’s activity patterns, in sync with slow waves and cortical “spindles,” and memory gradually moves into the cortex; REM sleep may consolidate it. The effect is real, but smaller than in popular books: losing sleep after learning hurts memory only slightly, losing it before learning hurts noticeably more, and many of the studies themselves are small and subject to publication bias3.

Emotions are more complicated than “overnight therapy.” The idea that REM sleep processes emotions overnight is a model, not a fact: a meta-analysis found no evidence that sleep consolidates emotional memories better than neutral ones4. What is better shown is what sleep loss does. After a sleepless night, the amygdala (the threat-detection center) reacted more strongly to negative images, while its connection to the prefrontal cortex — the front part of the brain that reins it in — weakened (one small study). A meta-analysis of 154 experiments in people aged 7–79 showed that any sleep loss — total, partial, fragmented — reduces positive emotions and raises anxiety; the bodily response to emotional stimuli tends to be blunted, and the picture for negative emotions is mixed5. The chain runs: body (weaker brake on the amygdala) → feeling (more anxious, less joy) → meaning (events read darker) → action (irritability that others return in kind). Even a small night-to-night reduction in sleep predicted next-day anxiety (an association, not a proven cause).

Attention and decisions. A single sleepless night greatly increases lapses in simple attention but barely touches reasoning accuracy. Chronic restriction (4–6 hours in bed for two weeks) builds a deficit in proportion to the sleep lost, while subjective sleepiness plateaus: you stop noticing how much worse you are performing.

Immunity. Sleep and the immune system affect each other: people who slept less than 6 hours got sick more often after exposure to a cold virus (an imprecise estimate), and short sleep around a vaccination was linked to a weaker antibody response (small samples).

“Flushing the brain” is an open question. In mice, the space between brain cells widened during sleep and beta-amyloid was cleared faster. But two other mouse studies did not confirm this, and one found the opposite: clearance dropped during sleep and under anesthesia6. In humans the data are only indirect: waves of cerebrospinal fluid during slow-wave sleep, poorer clearance of a tracer after one sleepless night (a small group). No human study has shown that deep sleep flushes out specific toxins.

Why a teenager can’t fall asleep at ten

In puberty, sleep pressure builds more slowly and the clock shifts later. Evening light, screens and socializing amplify the shift, and an early school bell cuts off the morning — the “perfect storm” model. How much of this is biology and how much environment is debated; but the fact that adolescents of other mammals show the same shift says it is not a generational whim.

Chronotype — the tendency to be a “lark” or an “owl” — is intermediate in most people. From childhood it drifts later, peaks in “owlishness” around 19–20, then drifts back; more than half of the lifetime range of chronotype falls between 15 and 25. Genetic studies have found hundreds of loci linked to being a “lark,” but even in people who carry most of these variants, sleep shifts by only minutes; modern evening light moves the clock too.

Moving school start times is the best-tested lever. A delay of 50–65 minutes in five schools produced about 40 minutes more sleep on school nights, measured by wristband, with no later bedtime7; part of the gain came out of weekend “catch-up” sleep. A start of 8:30–8:59 is better than 8:00–8:29. A Cochrane review rates the evidence as very weak and the effect on grades as mixed. In Russia, a 9:00 start instead of 8:00 was associated with longer sleep (cross-sectional); in China, half of teenagers sleep less than 8 hours on weekdays.
