Sleep loss hits a teenager’s mood directly: five nights of 6.5 hours in bed versus 10 made teens aged 14–17 more tense, angrier and more confused (a randomized experiment). Across studies, sleep problems more often precede adolescent depression than follow it. Short sleep is also linked to risk — alcohol, driving, fights, unprotected sex — but mostly in cross-sectional data; why teenagers are drawn to risk is in chapter 16.

How much you need

The norms are expert consensus, not doses from experiments: 8–10 hours for teenagers, 7–9 for adults, 7–8 for older adults. Mortality in large cohorts is lowest at around 7 hours and rises in both directions8; long sleep is partly a marker of illness, not its cause. Natural short sleepers exist — rare families with mutations, not the typical person on 5–6 hours. Instrument-measured adult sleep did not shrink from 1960 to 2013; children’s sleep did, unevenly across countries.

Regularity may matter as much as hours. In a large British sample, regularity of sleep timing predicted mortality more strongly than duration9. “Catching up on weekends” helps only partly: in the lab, catch-up sleep did not protect against overeating, clock shifts or falling insulin sensitivity; in observational data, among people under 65, short weekday sleep with long weekend sleep did not raise mortality.

Sex and age

Women report insomnia roughly 1.4–1.6 times more often than men; the gap grows with age and is smaller in East Asia, and in China no gap was found. Yet women’s sleep, when measured, is no worse: on average they have more deep slow-wave sleep, and with age sleep steadily worsened in men but not in women. A complaint is not the same as less sleep: sex hormones and different ways of talking about symptoms probably both contribute. After a child’s birth, mothers lost about an hour of sleep per night, fathers noticeably less; sleep had not fully recovered until the child was six (Germany).

Men more often have sleep apnea — pauses in breathing during sleep. Eight nights of 5 hours’ sleep lowered testosterone in a small group of young men, but a meta-analysis found no effect from partial sleep loss, only from total. Men’s chronotype is slightly later in youth, the distributions overlap almost completely, and with age the gap reverses. Almost all the data come from young Western people.

What this means for the self

Body: partly given. Chronotype, the length of your internal day and sensitivity to caffeine come from genes, but each gene moves sleep by minutes. The age-related shift “toward owl” at 15–25 happens to most people. You can’t choose it; you can stop mistaking it for character.

Ties: someone else’s schedule. The school sets the bell, which means the state does; work sets the wake-up time; the evening belongs to the group that is online until 1 a.m. A teenager with a shifted clock in a system with an early bell is chronically sleep-deprived and gets called “lazy” and “disorganized” for it. The ego picks up the label before it figures out the mechanism: “I’m an owl,” “I can’t get up,” “I have weak willpower.” Sleep loss lowers joy and raises anxiety, and the self readily blames this on its own inadequacy, though the cause may lie in the night.

Story: what you believe about your sleep. How you perceive your sleep itself steers the day. False feedback saying “you slept badly” worsened daytime well-being in people with insomnia10, while the message “you have more REM sleep than the average person” improved results on some tests. Anxious pursuit of perfect tracker numbers is described in clinical cases as a problem in its own right — orthosomnia. An ego that turns sleep into an exam risks failing it: sleep doesn’t yield to effort, and anxiety about it is one of the targets of insomnia therapy.

Where freedom lies. The clock obeys light and routine — this is the most malleable layer. A week of natural light in a tent shifted it earlier; morning light with a fixed wake-up time helped teenagers with a delayed phase. The story about yourself (“I’m a hopeless owl,” “I need exactly 8 hours”) gets rebuilt once you see the mechanism: a late teenage rhythm is a stage, not a diagnosis; the norm is a range, not a number. Freedom is not in defeating biology by willpower but in stopping the war with it and fitting your schedule into it where possible.

Myths

Myth: everyone needs exactly 8 hours. In fact: 8 is the middle of the adult 7–9-hour range, and in cohorts mortality is lowest at around 78. If you have insomnia and try to “top up” to eight by lying in bed longer, your sleep becomes more broken; treatment does the opposite — it restricts time in bed.

Myth: in deep sleep the brain is flushed of toxins — this is proven. In fact: in mice the data contradict each other, in humans they are only indirect6. Promises of a “brain detox” are selling a hypothesis.

Myth: a phone disrupts sleep mainly through blue light. In fact: the iPhone’s night mode changed nothing in young adults over a week, and the effect of blue-blocking glasses is not established. The link to poor sleep exists even in children and teenagers whose phone simply lies nearby: what probably matters more is that the screen eats up time and its content keeps you stimulated.

Myth: a nightcap helps you fall asleep. In fact: as little as about two drinks cuts and delays REM sleep, and falling asleep speeds up only at high doses; the second half of the night gets fragmented. Alcohol is a sedative, not a sleep aid.

Who plays on this

The melatonin market. The supplement is sold as a “natural sleeping pill,” but it is a weak clock-shifter: on average you fall asleep 7 minutes faster and sleep 8 minutes longer. The actual melatonin content of products differed sharply from their labels11. Pediatric poisonings in the US rose 530% over ten years, with hospitalizations and two deaths12. The sign: “natural means safe” and gummy bears for kids.

Sleep trackers. They promise to “improve your sleep”; no adequate study has shown this. They detect sleep well but wakefulness poorly, and estimate stages inconsistently. The sign: deep-sleep percentage as a grade for the night.

Energy drinks and caffeine. A notable share of children in various countries drank energy drinks over the past year, boys more often; the link to poor sleep is weak and correlational. The feed and games compete for the same evening (how that works is covered in chapters 14 and 28).

School and the state. Bell time is a political decision. The sign that your school is playing against sleep: a start before 8:30.

What to do about it

Light as the clock’s steering wheel. In the morning, daylight (outdoors is better); for the three hours before bed, dim light; in the bedroom, darkness. For a teenager with a delayed phase: a fixed wake-up time plus morning light; in a study of teenagers with a delayed phase, this combined with a few CBT sessions improved falling asleep and daytime sleepiness. The sign it’s working: in your diary, sleep onset and waking shift earlier.

Regularity along with duration. Sleep regularity may matter as much as hours, so the same wake-up time on weekdays and weekends is no worse than “topping up” hours. The check: a diary of wake-up and sleep-onset times.

Cutoffs. Last coffee at least 8–9 hours before bed if your sleep is fragile; screens — an evening “curfew” (in one study of schoolchildren, after 9 p.m.), the phone out of reach rather than in night mode (weak data). The check: a two-week diary of sleep onset and awakenings.

For chronic insomnia — CBT-I, not “hygiene.” Cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia is first-line in major guidelines: a diary, a fixed wake-up time, time in bed matched to actual sleep, bed only for sleep, work on anxiety about sleep. Falling asleep speeds up by about 19 minutes and nighttime wakefulness shrinks by 26, though total sleep grows little; in small comparisons the effect lasted longer than with pills, though it weakens by a year. Sleep hygiene rules alone are not enough for treatment. The sign: a growing share of time in bed that you actually sleep.

Questions for yourself

  • What do I consider my character (“owl,” “lazy,” “always irritable”) that may actually be sleep loss?
  • Whose schedule sets my sleep — mine or someone else’s — and where is the slack in it?
  • Do I judge my night by how I live my day, or by a number in an app?
  • What do I do in the last hour before bed — and who profits from it?

When to see a specialist

Insomnia lasting months and getting in the way of your day calls for CBT-I (in person or through a validated digital program); sleep restriction for people who drive for work or have epilepsy, bipolar disorder or suspected apnea — only under supervision. Suspected pauses in breathing during sleep — see a doctor. Melatonin for a child — only when prescribed and at the dose a doctor sets. If a teenager’s sleep collapses along with their mood, that is a reason to talk to a specialist (more in chapter 19).

18. First Attraction: Falling in Love, Orientation, Porn and Shame

No single gene decides whom a person will love, and no study has shown that upbringing has any effect on it. First attraction arrives on its own, before the self has a chance to approve it. So the main question of this age is not “whom will I fall for?” but what the self will do with what has already arrived: own it, hide it, or go to war with it.

How it works

Falling in love: a drive, not a disease

When people who are intensely in love are shown a photo of their beloved, the scan shows activity in the ventral tegmental area and the caudate nucleus — hubs of the reward system, which drives you toward a goal1. The study is small and does not measure dopamine directly: what it shows is activity in brain regions, not chemistry. In Beijing, the same pattern appeared in Chinese participants in love, and activity in some of these regions predicted how happy a person would be in the relationship a year and a half later. So the mechanism is not a Western quirk, though the samples are small.

This is where the popular idea that “love is an addiction” comes from. Shared brain regions do not make love a drug; scientists still argue whether to call ordinary strong craving an “addiction” or only the kind that wrecks a life. Another myth — “being in love is chemically similar to obsessive-compulsive disorder” — rests on one small study that measured the serotonin transporter in blood cells, not in the brain, in people recently in love and in patients. Intrusive thoughts about a person do happen, but no diagnosis follows from them.

What does the species gain? One hypothesis: romantic love is a mechanism that keeps a couple together long enough to raise a child who requires an enormous investment. This is a framework, not a proven fact. No expiration date for passion has been found either: in one study, spouses still intensely in love (a selected group) had been married for 21 years on average. The rule “passion fades after two to four years” is not supported — for some it fades, for others it doesn’t.

Attraction: the questionnaire versus the meeting

A survey across dozens of countries found that men and women differ in their stated preferences only on average — not a law for every individual2. A stated ideal is a judgment made in the abstract, away from any actual meeting; in a live meeting, reciprocated interest, conversation and how it feels to be next to the person come into play (more in chapter 22).

Similarity attracts, not opposites: on average, similarity is linked to liking, but the link varies by trait — almost none for sociability, a noticeable one for political views3. The famous claim that women’s preferences shift around ovulation did not hold up: in a large study with hormone measurements, preference for masculine faces did not track the cycle4. This does not mean nothing changes across the cycle, but the calendar does not predict who will like whom.

Attraction also changes behavior in the presence of the person who attracts. In a field experiment, young skateboarders took more risks and fell more often when an attractive female observer was watching (one study, too early to generalize). Puberty hormones supply the fuel — testosterone in boys rises roughly 30-fold — but a big shift in a hormone does not guarantee a big shift in mood or behavior; more in chapters 15 and 16.

Orientation: many small causes, no main one

Orientation is not one dimension but three: whom you are drawn to, whom you are intimate with, and what you call yourself. They don’t always match, and estimates of “how many people are like this” depend on what was measured. Genes play a part, but a diffuse one. In Swedish twins, the heritability of same-sex behavior is a little over a third in men and about a fifth in women (imprecise estimates); shared family environment contributed nothing in men. The largest genetic search, of nearly half a million people, found only a few DNA regions; all the common variants together explain 8 to 25% of the differences and cannot predict a specific person’s orientation5. There is no “gay gene”: the much-publicized 1993 finding on the X chromosome did not replicate.

The womb leaves a trace too. In men, each older biological brother raises the odds of homosexuality by about a third, while stepbrothers and adoptive brothers have no effect — which points to pregnancy, not upbringing6. Women with a congenital excess of androgens (male sex hormones) are more often bisexual or homosexual, though most of them are heterosexual8. The brother effect is small in absolute terms and disputed (more in chapter 02).

As for upbringing, no study has shown that parents, family structure or early experience create orientation. Being unlike a “typical boy” or “typical girl” in childhood is statistically linked to later orientation, but it is not a test: you cannot predict anything about a particular child. The data do not show that orientation is a choice or a result of upbringing.

Porn: four different questions under one word

In England, 70% of young people aged 16–21 had seen porn before 18, and more than half came across it by accident9 (recollections, not observation).

After that, four different things get lumped together. The first is unwanted exposure to content. The second is learning: repetition sets expectations and scripts, though a direct “saw it — did it” link has not been proven. The third is a conflict with values. The fourth is a real loss of control. They can overlap, but you cannot infer one from another.

The most popular scare story is “porn causes erectile dysfunction in young men.” A review found mostly cross-sectional studies and isolated cases and almost no evidence that porn causes it; the link with dissatisfaction is stronger10. Causation has not been proven — but neither has “never, for anyone.”

Shame: when an act becomes a verdict on the self

Sexual shame is not “I did something bad” but “something is wrong with me as a sexual being”: a sense of being defective that is triggered by thoughts, desires and acts11. The moral incongruence model explains why a person may feel “addicted” to porn: their behavior clashes with their beliefs, and that contradiction in itself produces intense distress12. The model has been tested in people of different sexes and religions in different countries. It does not mean every complaint is “just religious shame”: moral conflict and loss of control can coexist, and they need to be assessed separately.

What this means for the self

Here, for the first time in life, the body brings the self a desire it can neither summon nor cancel. This is a test of the whole assembly.

Given. The direction of attraction: many genes, each contributing a little; conditions during pregnancy; hormones. The force of first love, and the way the reward system makes a person temporarily obsessed with one face. Neither is chosen, so neither is anything to be proud or ashamed of.

Built. The label, the script and the judgment. What to call yourself, what to count as “normal” sex, what to think of yourself after the first desire — this is the layer of ties and story. It is written by family, the group, faith, country — and increasingly by a screen that got there before any real experience did. The porn script, the preferences from a questionnaire, the “rules” from the feed are other people’s texts that the ego easily mistakes for its own.

Where the ego is. The ego does three things with first attraction. It pins self-esteem on it: “I was chosen — I’m worth something,” “I was rejected — I’m nothing.” It defends against an unacceptable desire: denies it, mocks it in others, loudly proclaims the opposite. And it stitches together a mask, showing the group the orientation, experience and interests that are accepted there. Shame is the lever behind this mask: it makes you hide not an act but a part of yourself.

Freedom. The direction of attraction is not chosen, and no one has shown that it can be redirected. Everything else remains: which label to take and when (or none at all), which scripts to keep and which to throw out as someone else’s, and above all how to connect desire with values without a war. Shame strikes at the whole self, not the act; judging an act as just an act is inner work, and in this area there are no proven techniques for it. How this fits into your overall self-image, and what to do when it hurts badly, is covered in chapter 19.

Myths

Myth: There is a “gay gene,” or upbringing makes someone gay.
In fact: Many genes, each with a tiny contribution, plus individual environment, including the womb; no effect of upbringing has been shown.

Myth: The brain in love is the brain of an addict.
In fact: The reward regions are shared, but that does not make love a disease; signs of a problem are loss of control, harm, pressure, danger — not the strength of the feeling.

Myth: Passion inevitably fades after 2–4 years.
In fact: There is no universal deadline; some couples are intensely in love even after 20 years of marriage.

Myth: People who consider themselves addicted to porn are just ashamed and watch as much as everyone else.
In fact: Moral conflict can intensify the feeling of addiction, but it has not been proven that such people watch no more than others; control and shame are assessed separately.

Who plays on this

Platforms and the feed. For most people, their first porn arrives by accident, including through social media, sometimes as early as the first years of school9. The sign: content you didn’t ask for.

“Reboot” sellers. Dopamine detox, “semen retention,” mandatory periods of porn abstinence, “restoring masculinity” — the effect of these protocols has not been shown. The sign: exact deadlines and a promise to “bring back your power.”

Pickup coaches. They advise “playing hard to get,” provoking jealousy, creating fear and danger so that the other person “confuses” arousal with attraction. Uncertainty about reciprocation did raise interest in one questionnaire experiment, but there is no evidence it works in real relationships. The sign: a technique where the other person is an object, not a participant. And an average difference in a preferences survey is not a rule for everyone either. How the “real man” and the “proper girl” are sold — in chapter 20; the dating market — in chapter 22.

What to do about it

  1. Separate the four questions about porn. Separately: was there anything unwanted; what expectations came from the screen; does this clash with my values; can I stop when I’ve decided to. Write down a baseline — level of worry, unwanted episodes, how things are going — and compare later. A change is data about you, not proof of a general theory.

  2. Learn compatibility through live meetings. Neither hormones, nor the calendar, nor an “ideal partner” list predicts whom you’ll like. What works is respectful interaction with real people. The sign it’s working: you judge by how you feel with the person, not by how well they match the questionnaire.

  3. Build closeness through conversation, not tactics. About forty-five minutes of gradually deepening questions produced a sense of closeness in an experiment, but not love. New activities you both enjoy are worth a try; measure them by enjoyment and a sense of connection, not by adrenaline. Never create fear and danger to spark someone else’s attraction. More on emotions in a couple — in Feeling for Two — and More.

  4. Decide about intimacy freely. Base decisions about sex on consent, readiness and protection, not on a promised outcome: among students, casual hookups “under pressure” predicted worse well-being, while freely chosen ones did not. On the body and hormones of a couple — in Alchemy Without Magic.

Questions for yourself

  • Which of my “rules” about sex and love came from people, and which from a screen?
  • What am I hiding from the group — an act or a part of myself?
  • If I weren’t ashamed, what would I learn about myself?
  • Is my interest in this person about them, or about how I’m seen next to them?

When to see a specialist

For persistent erection problems, see a doctor — without blaming porn in advance. Infatuation or obsession with a person that badly disrupts sleep and daily life; loss of control, coercion or danger — all are reasons to seek help.

19. Who am I: identity, the body, and the mirror of other people’s eyes — and when the mind hurts for real

Almost half of all the mental disorders a person will ever have begin before 18, and new cases peak at 14–151. These are the same years when the self first assembles itself into a story and looks at itself through other people’s eyes. The mirror it looks into mostly reflects the self back. The story it writes is a draft. And pain that won’t go away and gets in the way of life is not a character trait but a treatable condition.

How it works

A mirror that shows yourself

Self-esteem is built in a loop: you get feedback → interpret what it says about your strength, place and worth → update your opinion of yourself → approach the situation or avoid it → get new feedback. The weak link is interpretation. Research separates three things: what others actually think, what you think they think, and what you think of yourself. The link between the last two partly runs in reverse: you project a ready-made opinion of yourself onto other people’s faces, and interpretation is not a transparent reading of their minds.

Hence the “spotlight effect”: people believe their looks and slip-ups were noticed more than they actually were (tested in lab situations). This doesn’t mean “nobody is looking,” but “everyone is looking at me” is a hypothesis, not a fact.

Self-esteem: what is known about the curve

The popular picture — “self-esteem collapses in puberty, especially in girls, then slowly recovers” — doesn’t hold up. A meta-analysis of studies that followed the same people from ages 4 to 94 shows an average rise from 4 to 11 and a different pattern after that, but no universal collapse; in adulthood self-esteem rises on average2. Many such “curves” come from comparing different people at different ages, which answers a different question from how one person changes over time.

Girls and women report lower average self-esteem in surveys. This is a shift between the averages of two heavily overlapping distributions, not two types of people and not a proven innate difference (overlap figures are not available).

What self-esteem actually gives you has long been contested. An early large review found that high self-esteem does not make a person successful at everything. Later long-term studies find a modest benefit: it predicts some good outcomes. The two findings are compatible: self-esteem may help a little, while “praise everyone” programs still don’t work, because skills and relationships, in turn, raise self-esteem.

What matters more than the level is what it rests on. If your sense of worth depends entirely on one domain (looks, grades, approval), success there protects you briefly, while failure hits the whole self. That is what theory predicts, not a fully proven fact. The problem is not caring about your looks or results, but making them the condition of your right to respect.

The story about yourself

By adolescence, the abilities needed to build a coherent life story have matured: linking events in time, explaining why one led to another, deriving “what I’m like” from them. This is how narrative identity appears — an internal, changing story that connects a reworked past with an imagined future. It is one layer of the self, not the whole self: the sense of your own body, of “mine,” of belonging to a group came earlier (more in chapter 07).

The story is written for an audience and edited by its reactions. It has two recognizable moves. Redemption: a bad event led to something good. Contamination: something good was spoiled by what came after. Redemption stories are associated with better well-being on average, but this association rests on coded narratives and self-reports. There is no evidence that making a suffering person tell a good story heals them.

The search for yourself is often described with four states, at the crossing of “did you explore options” and “did you commit to something.” Not exploring and not committed — diffusion. Committed to someone else’s choice without exploring — foreclosure. Exploring — moratorium. Explored and committed — achieved identity. This is a map, not a ladder of merit: there is no required order and no universal deadline.

Self-concept clarity is how clearly and consistently you know what you’re like. It is not the same as a flattering self-image. A coherent identity is not necessarily accurate or ethical, and flexibility is not necessarily confusion; the value placed on consistency also differs between cultures.

The body in the mirror of the feed

Appearance is the most visible entry point into the self in these years. The mechanism: idealized image → comparison → shifted standard → the gap between it and your own body becomes more noticeable → dissatisfaction, mirror checking. Experiments confirm the first step: after idealized photos, body dissatisfaction rises noticeably more than after neutral ones3. The long-term link between total time on social media and body image, meanwhile, is weak. There is no contradiction: an experiment measures the immediate hit of specific content, while “hours online” lump together the useful, the neutral and the harmful.

The same goes for mood: overall screen time explains no more than 0.4% of the differences in teenagers’ well-being4, but those on social media five or more hours a day have more depression symptoms, with bullying, sleep and body image as links along the way5 (more in chapter 14). Why teen depression has risen since the 2010s is still unknown (sleep — chapter 17).

Body pressure hits both sexes, by different routes. Eating disorders are more common in women, but not only in them: in a given year, about 2.2% of women and 0.7% of men. Men are noticeably more likely than women to have ever tried steroids (the samples vary widely; this is not a figure for an ordinary schoolkid). How the industry sells the ideal body — chapter 20.

Feel it and name it

The model of the self is also built from the inside: interoception — perceiving the internal state of the body — supplies the raw material, and, according to the disputed theory of constructed emotion, the brain picks a concept for it (“anxiety,” “hunger,” “shame”). Difficulty recognizing and describing your own feelings is called alexithymia. It is a scale, not a diagnosis: about one adult in ten scores above the accepted cutoff (the cutoff is arbitrary). Men score slightly higher on average, the distributions overlap almost completely, and the difference lies mainly in a habit of focusing on external things rather than feelings, and in describing feelings rather than recognizing them. Childhood abuse and neglect are linked to alexithymia in adulthood. It is a skill, not a sentence — more in the book Feeling for Two — and More.

When the mind hurts for real

Different disorders start at different ages: neurodevelopmental disorders — median around 12, anxiety disorders — 17, eating disorders — 18, psychoses and addictions — 25, mood disorders — 31. The figure “half by 14” comes from one American survey based on adults’ recollections; according to a large meta-analysis, about a third begin by 14 and almost half by 18. So early detection is needed up to the mid-twenties, not only at school.

The sex gap in depression appears with puberty: at 13–15, girls’ odds of a diagnosis are three times those of boys; in more gender-equal countries the gap in diagnoses is larger6. About one teenager in six self-harms at least once in their life, starting on average at 13, more often girls7. Early puberty is a small risk factor for both sexes, not destiny (more in chapter 11). Heavy cannabis users have roughly four times the odds of psychosis, and daily users of high-potency cannabis even higher; this is an association, and causation is not fully proven.

What this means for the self

Given — the body and its timing: when puberty comes, how strong the signals from inside are. Also here: the risk of a first episode of a disorder peaks exactly when the self is being built. This is a coincidence of timing, not a personal failing.

Built — the mirror. Self-esteem is not an imprint of other people’s opinion but your own brain’s judgment, projection included. The ego chooses what your worth rests on and defends that choice: if there is only one support — looks, grades, likes — every day becomes an exam. The mask for others is built by the same mirror: people show what they think will be valued.

Rebuilt — the story. The story about yourself is edited throughout life; you can hold unresolved, mixed chapters without a mandatory happy ending. You can widen what your worth rests on — skills, relationships, deeds, not a single storefront.

One more point: how pain is explained. In experiments, explaining depression as “broken brain chemistry” does not reduce self-blame, but it does increase pessimism about recovery and makes medication seem more reliable than therapy8 (analogue experiments). Depression has many causes, and they change; a diagnosis is a condition, not a new identity. Freedom here is not inventing yourself from scratch, but choosing what to rest on and how to interpret what you see in other people’s eyes.

Myths

Myth: in adolescence, everyone’s self-esteem collapses. In fact: long-term studies show a rise up to age 11 and then a different pattern, but no universal collapse; in adulthood it rises on average.

Myth: depression is a lack of serotonin. In fact: a large review found no consistent link between depression and serotonin9 (more in chapter 44).

Myth: antidepressants are barely better than placebo — or, the other way around, miracle pills. In fact: in adults the advantage is small to moderate and grows with the severity of depression. In adolescents only fluoxetine beat placebo, and only on low-quality evidence10.

Myth: phones and social media caused the teen mental health crisis. In fact: the rise is real, but the average link between screen time and well-being is tiny, and the cause of the rise has not been established (more in chapter 14).

Who plays on this

The self-esteem market. “Raise your self-esteem and your grades will go up” programs sell confidence instead of skills. The sign: a promise of results from affirmations without skills or support.

Drug advertising. Antidepressant ads were built on the phrase “increases serotonin in the brain,” which, according to critics’ analysis, has little scientific support. The sign: a simple chemical fairy tale about a complex condition.

Coaches and groups. The demand to “find the gift in your trauma” is passed off as a method, because redemption stories are linked to well-being. The sign: a mandatory happy ending and pressure if your story doesn’t deliver one. Next door are pseudotherapies: NLP lacks sufficient evidence, “family constellations” have weak data, and some studies have recorded side effects11.

The feed. The best-established effect is the immediate impact of idealized images. The sign: after scrolling you feel worse about your body, not just tired.

What to do about it

Check the mirror. When it seems that “everyone noticed” or “they think badly of me,” write it down as a prediction and check it against concrete feedback from a concrete person. It’s working if you can see the gap between the prediction and the real feedback.

Talk to yourself differently after a slip. Name the difficulty, don’t pass a verdict on your whole self, choose the next step. In experiments, this kind of self-compassion increased the desire to fix the mistake rather than making people slack off. The sign: after a slip you return to the task faster and avoid it less.

Clean the feed by content, not by hours. Remove what drives body comparison; keep an eye on sleep and bullying. The sign: less body dissatisfaction after screens, and sleep doesn’t suffer.

Name feelings with two words. When you notice a feeling, label it: “angry and ashamed,” “anxious and tired.” In experiments, naming reduced the response of the amygdala — the anxiety center — and fear on re-exposure to something frightening (in people afraid of spiders). The sign: your journal fills with a wider range of words for feelings.

Questions for yourself

  • What single thing does my right to respect myself rest on?
  • What do I “know” about other people’s opinion of me without ever having checked it?
  • Whose story about me is this — mine, or the one people expect to hear from me?
  • What do I call character that might be a condition that can be treated?

When to see a specialist

If low mood, emptiness or anxiety persists and gets in the way of studying, working or socializing; if there is self-harm, thoughts of death, voices or strange beliefs, or agitation without sleep. For moderate and severe adolescent depression, psychotherapy works, and fluoxetine works only in combination with it: in a large trial, 71% responded to the combination versus 35% on placebo12. At the start of medication, suicidal thoughts need to be monitored (the risk is small but real); stopping should be gradual and done with a doctor. How to choose a therapist and a method — chapter 46.

20. The youth industry: beauty, muscles, games — how the “real man” and the “proper girl” are sold

Sex in advertising doesn’t sell the brand: across some fifty experiments, explicit imagery added nothing to the brand and often hurt it1. What does sell is a fix for the gap between how you see yourself and how you are “supposed” to be. The youth industry doesn’t really trade in creams, protein and games; it trades in that fix, and, by one plausible model, it widens the gap itself. Its raw material is the teenage self, which is just now choosing the yardstick it will measure itself by.

How it works

The comparison chain

The mechanism is short. An ideal image invites you to compare yourself with it → the comparison shifts your internal bar for “normal” → the gap between your body and the bar becomes noticeable → dissatisfaction, mirror checking and avoidance grow. Then a product or procedure arrives as the solution.

The first step has been tested in experiments: after a set of idealized body photos, people on average rate their own bodies noticeably worse, and the effect holds even after the data are cleaned2. The other steps of the chain are a plausible model, not a pathway proven at every step.

An important detail: in long-term observational studies, the link “hours on social media → body dissatisfaction” is much weaker. This is not a contradiction: an experiment measures the effect of specific content, while total time lumps together the useful, the neutral and the harmful. What seems to matter is not how much you watch but what.

Two yardsticks, one mechanism

The market sells two different images: “thin and smooth” and “lean and big.” Underneath both there is probably one comparison chain, but the evidence for each differs in quality.

For thinness the data are plentiful: a review of nearly eighty studies shows that the thin ideal worsens body image on average — but almost all of it is about women. The muscular yardstick needs its own research; you can’t just swap the pronouns in the conclusions about thinness.

The most concrete trace of the muscular yardstick is anabolic steroids. In mixed samples, about 6% of men and about 2% of women have tried them at least once3. This is not the share among ordinary teenagers (the samples are too varied), and taking steroids is not the same as a pathological obsession with muscle. There is observational evidence that in some men the body’s own testosterone takes a long time to recover after stopping, and this keeps them on the drugs4. What hormones do on their own in these years is covered in chapter 15.

Eating disorders are not a “women’s topic” either. On average they are three times more common in women than in men — but that is not the share of men among all patients, and men may be underdiagnosed.

The scalpel as an answer to the gap

The procedures market is huge: by plastic surgeons’ estimates, tens of millions of surgical and nonsurgical procedures were performed worldwide in 2024. That is a count of procedures, not people, and it speaks to scale, not benefit.

The promise that “surgery will raise your self-esteem” was tested in South Korea. All the studies in the review are cross-sectional surveys, so they cannot show that surgery causes a lasting rise in self-esteem5. For China and Russia, no comparable verified data exist.

The argument “attractive people get paid more” sounds like hard arithmetic: economists do describe an earnings gap linked to appearance. But no reliable general “beauty premium,” let alone a payoff from surgery, has been established: occupation, selection, health and discrimination all contribute.

Seeing yourself from the outside

There is a hypothesis of self-objectification: a person internalizes an observer’s view of their own body and starts constantly monitoring how they look. Attention gets spent on policing appearance. The famous swimsuit experiment, in which this supposedly impaired problem-solving, has not yet been confirmed by large replications — it is too early to treat it as proven.

Why sex doesn’t sell

An explicit scene grabs attention, but the attention stays on the scene, not the product. Recognizing an ad, remembering the brand, liking it and buying it are four different outcomes. The review of experiments finds no overall gain for the brand, and the more explicit the image, the worse the result. And even “purchase intent” in an experiment is not the same as sales.

Children buy before they think

In children the chain runs like this: a trusted or fun presentation → the product becomes desirable → the child eats it, asks for it, buys it. Food is the best-studied case. Food advertising immediately increases how much is eaten, shifts choice toward the advertised product (the odds of choosing it are nearly twice as high) and changes preferences; this has been tested on thousands of children and adolescents6. There are far fewer data on long-term weight.

An influencer’s post featuring chips and sweets raises how much children eat at snack time by about a quarter7, and knowing that “this is an ad” is no protection on its own (more in chapter 14).

Games: three different questions

The debate about games covers three things that often get lumped together.

Addiction. According to a review spanning 17 countries, gaming disorder is found in about 3% of gamers, about 2% when only the strictest studies are counted, and the figure depends heavily on the questionnaire8. This is about loss of control and harm to your life, not about enthusiasm or hours played.

Money. Loot boxes — paid “chests” with a random reward — mechanically resemble gambling. Spending on them is linked to signs of problem gambling more strongly than spending on any other in-game purchase9. The survey was of adult volunteers, so the direction of cause is unknown: loot boxes stoke gambling, gamblers are drawn to loot boxes, or both.

Aggression. In long-term observational studies, the link between violent games and aggression is tiny, and in a preregistered study of 14- to 15-year-olds it is practically absent10, 11. This is not proof of zero, but it is no reason for panic either. These data say nothing about real-world violence.

The manosphere and its mirrors

The manosphere is a network of forums and channels about “real men”: pickup artists, the “men’s rights movement,” “men going their own way,” incels. It sells through a shared pattern — pain → “you’re one of us” → someone to blame → a status ladder → a paid path to the top (the pattern and how this network has changed are in chapter 29).

Selling the “proper girl” — “feminine strategy” dating guides, the “traditional wife” image — is a similar commercial construction. On neither side is there evidence that any of these programs produces lasting relationships. The same pattern, however, does not mean the same harm. How “male power” and “feminine energy” are sold to adults is in chapter 29.

What this means for the self

Body — given and changing on its own. In these years it is rebuilding itself without asking you (more in chapters 11 and 15), and a gap between the body and the self-image can arise without any advertising. Advertising, by the model above, finds it and stretches it.

Ties — a yardstick from outside. The “real man” and the “proper girl” are not descriptions of biology but group yardsticks packaged as products. They touch three ancient tasks at once: belonging, rank in the group, finding a mate. Body and ties converge on one question: “Am I good enough?” (the price of belonging is covered in chapter 13; other people’s eyes as a mirror, in chapter 19).

Ego — the weak spot and the point of entry. The ego defends the self-image. If self-esteem is tied to the body or to rank, any image in which you are “worse” can feel like a threat, and you want to remove it fast. And that is exactly the remedy the market sells: cream, course, chest, steroid, surgery. By the logic of the mechanism, the purchase removes the threat briefly, and in the meantime the bar has time to shift (this has not been measured as a cycle). That is how a mask becomes a subscription.

The second defense is retreat into a group: a ready-made story in which others are to blame and you belong. This is how the manosphere sells; the price is someone else’s rules about who you are and whom to despise.

Story — freedom. You can’t rebuild your bones. What can be rebuilt is which yardstick you agree to measure yourself by and who handed it to you. Knowledge is not armor here: understanding that a photo is retouched is not the same as not comparing yourself with it. Freedom lies not in immunity but in control over what enters your field of view, and in separating one question from another: what do I want from my body, from a game, from relationships — and what of this was sold to me.

Myths

Myth: sex sells. In fact: explicit imagery catches the eye but doesn’t help the brand, and the more explicit, the worse.

Myth: violent games make teenagers aggressive. In fact: only partly; in stricter studies the link is tiny or nearly zero. Look at sleep, school, money and control over the game, not the genre.

Myth: anyone who games a lot is addicted. In fact: gaming disorder affects a small minority, and time spent playing doesn’t define it.

Myth: surgery will fix self-esteem. In fact: the data on this are all cross-sectional surveys, which cannot show causation.

Who plays on this

Platforms and influencers. The feed selects content and places a commercial offer next to it; whether algorithms change views has not been proven. The sign: after watching, you feel worse about your body, and at the end there’s a link to a product.

Game companies. The sign of a gambling mechanic: a random reward for money.

Sellers of gender yardsticks. Signs shared by “male” and “female” gurus: your pain is recast as a membership card; the promised result can’t be measured; the price rises in steps; they demand dependence on the group and rejection of “outsiders.” Red flags: isolation, humiliation, the idea of a right to someone else’s body, pressure and coercion. Ordinary training, self-care and wanting to be attractive have nothing to do with this.

Children’s advertising. The sign: the seller is someone the child trusts — an influencer, a character, a game.

What to do about it

Clean up the content, not just the time. Remove from your feed the accounts that leave you feeling worse about your body. It’s working if you catch yourself comparing and mirror checking less often. The data show a weaker link with total screen time.

Break the hit down step by step. After an unpleasant scroll through the feed, name it: what you saw, what you felt in your body, how you interpreted it, what you did, what it led to. Before a decision made in a group, ask yourself: “What would I choose without an audience?” Before a purchase or a new “truth” from an influencer: “Who benefits from my buying this or believing it?” (These exercises follow from the mechanism but have not been tested as a method in their own right.)

Games: check four areas separately. Sleep (more in chapter 17), school or work, relationships, control over the game and over money. If all four are fine, hours on their own are no cause for alarm. Better not to pay for random rewards at all.

Tested programs instead of homemade ones. School courses on body image and media literacy for ages 10–15 improve body image and the ability to read media13. Programs based on “cognitive dissonance,” in which participants take the ideal apart piece by piece themselves, look promising. Use the original program: homemade “body positivity lessons” that repeatedly show ideal bodies or rank body shapes can backfire.

Questions for yourself

  • Whose yardstick am I using to measure my body right now — and where did I get it?
  • What am I trying to fix with a purchase: my appearance, or the feeling “I’m not good enough”?
  • Which group tells me whom to despise — and what do I get in return?
  • What would I do with my body if no one ever saw it?

When to see a specialist

If food, weight or muscle occupy your thoughts for most of the day, or there is restriction, binge eating, vomiting or drugs taken for your physique, you need an assessment by a doctor — and being male doesn’t exempt you. The same applies if gaming or betting is wrecking your sleep, school, relationships and control over money.

Part V. 21–28: Freedom and Choice

21. Adulthood That Never Quite Starts: The Still-Maturing Brain and the Fear of Choosing

No study has found an age at which the brain “finishes maturing.” The best-known number, 25, rests on no study at all: nobody has found such a line1. The brain changes before thirty and after it, and adulthood arrives not on a timer inside the skull but through roles, money and decisions. The self here does not wait to be ready. It is built from what it has chosen.

How it works

What the scanner sees from 18 to 30

MRI, an image of the brain made with a magnetic field, shows two opposing processes. Gray matter (neuron bodies and their nearby connections) shrinks in volume, while white matter (the long wires between regions, wrapped in an insulating sheath called myelin) grows and becomes denser. This is usually read as pruning unneeded connections and insulating useful ones, but MRI cannot see synapses themselves. That is an interpretation, not an image.

Each layer has its own timeline. According to charts built from more than a hundred thousand scans, total gray matter volume peaks around age 6, deep nuclei around 14, and white matter around 292. The long association pathways that link distant areas of the cortex mature last and were still changing after adolescence in people followed up to age 32. In the brain’s wiring diagram (how the network is organized, not how much tissue it has), turning points fall at roughly 9, 32, 66 and 833. These are snapshots of different people, and they say nothing about decisions.

A caveat about the charts themselves: the samples are mostly Western and well-off, and the authors acknowledge this.

Where “25” came from

The number seems to be a by-product of imaging studies that stopped recruiting participants in their early twenties: the last point on the graph became the “finish line.” A well-known review put it carefully: the brain “continues to mature into the 20s.” In the same breath it stressed that how these changes relate to real behavior has barely been studied4.

The deeper problem is that there is no agreed marker of a “mature brain”1. Volume, connectivity, signal speed, network function: each has its own curve. Asking “when is the brain mature” is like asking “when is a city mature.” Streets, sewers and subway lines get built in different years.

Abilities mature out of step

The cold mind grows up early. Processing speed, suppressing an unwanted movement, and working memory (holding in mind what you are working with right now) reach adult levels at roughly 15, 14 and 19. In an online sample of tens of thousands of people, some abilities peaked by the end of high school, others held through early adulthood and declined in the thirties, and still others grew until forty (a snapshot, not a follow-up).

The hot mind grows up later. Across eleven countries, intellectual abilities reached adult levels around 16, while psychosocial maturity, the ability to hold back under strong emotion and excitement, emerged after 185. In one lab study, 18- to 21-year-olds were worse at maintaining control under negative arousal than people over 21; their frontoparietal regions were less active and the lower (ventromedial) part of the prefrontal cortex more active6. The prefrontal cortex is the front part of the brain that puts the brakes on impulses and keeps a plan on track.

Sensation seeking and reward-center activity peak around 19, and self-regulation plateaus by 23–267. This held in most of the eleven countries (China among them), a rare test of this question beyond the West. More on dopamine and risk in chapter 16. Some researchers have even proposed defining adolescence as ages 10–24, which shows how blurry the boundary is.

Why people “grow out of” stupid stuff

Offending rises sharply in the teen years, nearly tenfold, and then falls. “Adolescence-limited” offenders outnumber the small group who keep it up for life; this pattern has survived a quarter century of testing (data mostly on men).

Why the decline? In a sample of more than a thousand serious young offenders (mostly male) followed for seven years, work, marriage, a change of peers, psychosocial maturity and a reassessment of costs and benefits together explained 69% of the decline between ages 15 and 258. Not all of it, but not brain maturation alone either: roles and surroundings entered the explanation on equal footing with psychosocial maturity.

Adulthood as a social build

Psychologists describe a stage called “emerging adulthood”: the period from the end of adolescence to the end of the twenties when people try out options. Its logic is social: money and education make trying possible; trying changes commitments and the story you tell about yourself; work, a partner and caring for others later narrow and lock in the choice. This is a developmental theory, not a hormonal stage with a start and end date, and not a yardstick valid for every country and class.

There are data from Russia: several thousand people aged 24–28, followed for a year. Parenthood was linked to a lower sense of being “still in between,” and income to a higher one. Living with parents played no significant role. The whole model explains only a small share of the differences between people, and this is an association, not proof that a child “makes you an adult.”

Autonomy is not an address

In self-determination theory, autonomy is the sense that you endorse your own actions, not having your own apartment. You can live on your own and still be on the leash of someone else’s money, threats or approval. You can live with family and decide for yourself. Researchers explicitly separate autonomy from independence; there is also the concept of an “autonomous-related self,” where closeness and your own decision do not contradict each other.

Choice: when more is too much

The idea “more options, less happiness” became popular after a few striking experiments. A synthesis of 63 such findings found no significant effect on average9. A later one showed when overload does happen: a complex choice set, a difficult task, fuzzy preferences, and a specific goal for the choice10.

The second trap is affective forecasting. People misjudge how strongly and how long an event will please or hurt them, especially when their attention is fixed on that one event (moderate evidence; not everyone errs, and not always in the same way).

Personality: stable and still changing

These are two different questions. Rank-order stability is whether you keep your place among your peers. Mean-level change is whether a trait shifts for everyone on average. A synthesis of large longitudinal samples found that rank order stabilizes through childhood and adolescence and plateaus in young adulthood, but a plateau does not mean set in stone11.

Mean-level shifts go one way: roughly between 20 and 40, emotional stability, conscientiousness and social dominance (confidence with others) increase12. Newer data show smaller shifts than earlier work but confirm the rise in stability11. The mechanism is social investment: a role sets expectations, habit locks in the behavior, others respond to the new image, and it sticks. This is an explanation, not proof that marriage or a job necessarily improves character. More on changes toward forty in chapter 33.

Sex: different timelines, a shared brain

In girls, brain volume peaks at about 10.5 years, in boys at about 14.5; gray matter also peaks 1–2 years earlier. White matter keeps growing longer in males, while in females it grows mostly in mid-adolescence. This partly reflects earlier puberty in girls (chapter 11). Male brains are on average about 11% larger, but once size is accounted for, sex explains about 1% of the differences in structure13, and the distributions overlap heavily. “She matured earlier” is about volume curves, not about the maturity of decisions.

Windows of vulnerability

This same stretch is when first episodes of serious disorders appear: psychoses and addictions at an average of about 25, mood disorders at about 31 (data not tied to a single source). These are average ages of onset, not a forecast for any individual.

What this means for the self

Body: still under construction, but not holding the wheel. The wires between distant regions keep getting insulated until thirty, and the network reorganizes around 32. But the cold mind is already adult by 16–19, while the hot mind, the ability not to lose it when excited or hurt, catches up in the twenties. So the weak spot of the young self is not the ability to think but the ability to think in the heat of the moment. This is knowledge about the situation, not a verdict on the person.

Ties: they build adulthood no less than the brain does. Two thirds of the decline in offending is explained by work, a partner, new peers, a reassessment of costs and benefits, and psychosocial maturity together8. According to one explanation, personality strengthens through roles that demand the same thing day after day. Adulthood does not arrive from inside on schedule. It is built by commitments and by people who start to expect certain things from you.

Story: the main field. “It’s too early for me” is a common formula of the ego. It is an interpretation, not a measurement. It is convenient: it protects self-esteem from failure (“this isn’t losing, the game hasn’t started yet”) and disguises the fear of choosing as a scientific fact (“the brain will mature by 25”). The made-up date cuts both ways: it is used to excuse your own inaction and someone else’s harm, and it is used to take away an adult’s right to decide. Both versions lift responsibility off the self.

The fear of choosing is often not about the number of options but about their vagueness: you don’t know what you want, and the goal of the choice is undefined10. An ego that waits for a final identity before taking the first step risks getting stuck. In this theory, identity works the other way around: trying changes commitments, and commitments change the story you tell about yourself (on how identity takes shape, see chapter 19).

Where the freedom is. Personality does not freeze by thirty11, autonomy is not measured by an address, and the brain does not issue a certificate. The freedom is that you can start adulthood before “readiness” arrives: take on a role that will demand the behavior you need, and give it time to take hold.

Myths

Myth: the brain matures by age 25. In fact: no study has found this line1. White matter peaks around 29, the network reorganizes around 32, and intellectual abilities reach adult levels by 162, 3, 5.

Myth: a teenager’s “frontal lobes don’t work.” In fact: intellectual abilities reach adult levels around 16; what lags is restraint under emotion and excitement5.

Myth: the more choice, the unhappier people are. In fact: on average there is no effect9; overload happens with a complex task, fuzzy wants and a specific goal for the choice10.

Myth: life runs in seven-year stages, and each is a biological reset. In fact: no such reset has been established; the seven-year stages in this book are a way of telling the story (more in chapter 00).

What to do about it

Don’t decide at the peak of an emotion. The ability to reason is already adult by 16–19, while restraint under strong emotion matures later5. For major decisions (a move, a loan, a breakup), let the emotion settle before you choose. This is an inference from lab data, not a tested technique. Sign: the decision doesn’t rest on a momentary feeling.

Test, don’t imagine. Forecasting how something will feel gets the strength and duration of feelings wrong. Replace it with small, reversible trials: an internship instead of a career change. Sign: the decision rests on lived experience, not on a picture in your head.

Simplify the hard choice, not every choice. Overload happens when the task is complex and you don’t know what you want; fewer options are not always better. Narrow the hard choice and first name what you want from it. Sign: you can say it in one sentence.

Agree on rules, not on an apartment. If an adult child lives with their parents, discuss money, responsibilities, personal space, who makes which decisions, and a date when you will revisit the agreement. This is a reasonable application of theory, not a tested method. Sign: everyone can name what they decide for themselves.

Questions for yourself

  • What decision am I putting off with the excuse “it’s too early for me,” and what would have to happen for it to be “time”?
  • Where do I live on my own but not decide for myself, or live with family but decide for myself?
  • What role could I take on right now that would demand of me the person I want to become?
  • What exactly do I want from this choice, and can I say it in one sentence?

22. The dating market: apps, algorithms, and money made on loneliness

In a little over twenty years, the share of heterosexual couples in the US who met online rose from 2% to 39%, while the share who met through friends fell from a third to a fifth1. The task of “finding a mate,” once handled by family, neighborhood, and friends, has moved onto a platform that earns money from access and visibility. The platform hands out a metric — likes, matches, replies — and the self tends to read it as a verdict on itself.

How it works

What the app sells

An app widens the pool of possible encounters, ranks who gets shown to whom, and gives constant feedback. Its product is access and visibility, not relationships. Match Group’s revenue for 2025 ran to several billion dollars. Paid visibility creates an incentive to sell access; by itself, that does not prove the claim that “apps deliberately keep people from getting together.” Revenue measures neither harm nor successful couples.

The platform’s goals and the user’s need not coincide: the platform earns from visibility, while the user is looking for a relationship. No one has independently tested which app features lead to lasting relationships and which only drive engagement: no audited experiments of this kind exist.

Unequal attention — without “80/20”

Attention in online dating is distributed unequally; the data show it. But the popular figure “80% of women chase 20% of men” appears in no representative study2. In messages on one American dating site, across four cities, both men and women wrote to people about a quarter more “desirable” than themselves — by a ranking the researchers built from who writes to whom. That is a pull toward “slightly above yourself” in both sexes, not a law about outcast men.

You can measure different things: views, likes, matches, replies, dates, couples a year later. Each metric has its own denominator, and a low match count says nothing about a person’s place in a “ranking of humanity.”

More choice is not always worse, but the feed changes the mode

On average, choice overload does not occur3; it appears when the task is hard and preferences are unclear (more on this in chapter 21). Choosing a partner looks like such a task (this has not been tested directly).

In three experiments with online profiles, willingness to accept the next profile fell by about 29% over the course of browsing4. This is a “rejection mind-set”: the further you scroll, the easier it is to say “no.” The studies measured decisions about profiles, not future relationships, but it looks as if the feed trains you to reject.

Profile and meeting are different things

A large survey across dozens of countries confirmed average differences in stated ideals: men, on average, value youth and looks more; women, an older partner and wealth. These are average shifts, not exclusive preferences, and the overlap is huge: across psychological traits overall, the distributions for men and women overlap by roughly nine-tenths (assuming normal distributions).

At a live meeting the picture is different. In speed dating among American students, physical attractiveness mattered to interest as much for women as for men (data from one study, young sample). Detailed questionnaires filled out before the meeting could not predict which particular pairs would like each other5; later work found that stated and actual preferences matched on only four traits out of nine, and not with every method. First impressions, though, do predict something: in a synthesis of three speed-dating studies, they were linked to later desire and dates.

Hence the app’s paradox. It builds choice around the profile and a wish list — the very things that poorly predict which particular pair will click. Live contact — mutual interest, conversation, first impression — begins only after you meet.

Cycle hormones are not a navigator

The appealing story that “on fertile days women are drawn to masculine men” did not hold up in a large study. An early synthesis found a shift only in ratings of short-term attractiveness, and it was immediately disputed. A long study with hormone measurements found no compelling link between preference for masculine faces and hormonal status6. This does not mean nothing changes over the cycle — but you cannot rely on a cycle calendar to predict attraction or fidelity. More on hormones and desire in chapter 15 and in the book Alchemy Without Magic.

The profile: presentation, not deception

A profile is a mask the ego assembles for the market. When Americans’ profiles were compared with actual measurements, discrepancies in age, height, and weight turned out to be common but mostly small. A discrepancy is not proof of intent; it is more about showing yourself in the best light than about lying.

Less sex and loneliness are not the same thing

American surveys from 2000–2018 showed rising sexual inactivity among men aged 18–34 and women aged 25–34; among men, the rise was mostly among the unmarried7. Men’s inactivity is linked to low income and lack of full-time work — a link, not a proven cause. The surveys do not say what people want or whether they are satisfied, so “porn / apps / feminism is to blame” cannot be drawn from them.

Loneliness works differently. A synthesis of studies covering hundreds of thousands of people of all ages found that, on average, men and women are almost equally lonely8. There are two different hungers: social loneliness — lacking a support network; emotional loneliness — lacking a close partner. You can be without sex and not lonely, in a couple and lonely, with friends and without wanting a partner. A single frequency figure does not tell you which of these cases is yours.

Country and era

The dating market is a recent invention. In China, according to national surveys, parent-arranged marriages fell from about 20% to 5% by the late 1970s and then nearly disappeared. In places where choice passed from the family to the individual, urban users of Chinese apps described them as “a portal to a world of possibilities” (interviews, qualitative data). For Russia and China there are no comparable figures on the effects of apps; American and Australian data do not speak for the whole world.

Sex differences in willingness to have sex without commitment exist in every country studied, but they shrink where there is more political and economic equality and grow under harsh conditions (mostly student samples). This is a cross-country link, not a proven cause, but even what counts as “nature” shifts with the environment.

What this means for the self

Given — an ancient task. Finding a mate and not being left out is one of the seven ancient tasks. Its signals — the anxiety of loneliness, the sting of rejection, the joy of a “yes” — are old; the platform has given them a new source.

Built — a counter. The platform has turned rare signals into a stream of numbers. An ego looking for something to anchor its self-esteem may grab whatever is easiest to measure. That is how one app’s metric becomes a verdict on yourself: “few matches means I’m worse.” The platform’s metric gives no grounds for that.

App users feel worse on average: in a synthesis of two dozen studies, they had poorer mental health and well-being9. In an Australian survey of adults, users had higher odds of stress and depression, but their self-esteem was not significantly lower. Both studies are cross-sectional: it is unknown whether apps worsen mood or whether people who already feel bad come to them. For the self, the takeaway is concrete: look not at the average but at what the app does to you.

The ego’s defenses on the market (description, not measurement). Each one protects against the same thing — rejection.

  • Devaluation: “they’re all shallow there” — spares you the risk, but also the meetings.
  • A theory of the market: “it’s all about the 20% / hypergamy / money” — turns personal pain into a universal law in which you play no part.
  • Endless choice: keep scrolling, never meet — as long as you haven’t chosen, no one can reject you.
  • Disappearing: vanishing without a word feels gentler than rejecting. But in a simulated scenario, a clear rejection gave people more certainty and less urge to check and write again than ambiguity did10. Disappearing does not reveal an attachment style: it is a behavior, not a diagnosis.

Fear of being alone is a poor adviser. In several studies, people more afraid of ending up alone held on longer to relationships they were unhappy with, ended them less often, and chose less selectively11. This is a predictive link, not a proven cause; a possible mechanism: when the ego clings to “I’m not alone,” the value of any partner goes up.

Where freedom lies. How a couple met does not decide its fate. In one large American survey, couples who met online were even slightly more satisfied; in an international study, they were less satisfied. Neither proves cause. And after adjusting for era, how people met did not predict breakup. Freedom lies in separating three things: access to people (the app gives you that), the quality of conversation (you give that), and the durability of a relationship (two people build it; more in chapter 30). And in separating your own worth from the metric of whoever profits from you. What to do with the craving for approval, and where healthy self-love ends, is the subject of chapter 23.

Myths

Myth: 80% of women on apps compete for 20% of men. In fact: there are no representative data for this figure; in the data studied, both men and women write to people about a quarter “above” themselves2.

Myth: women “by nature” always seek a higher-status partner. In fact: preferences depend on context, and “educational hypergamy” (marrying a more educated partner) declines as women gain more education.

Myth: more matches mean better chances of a relationship. In fact: match count has not been tested as a predictor of a good pairing, and a long feed lowers willingness to accept the next person.

Myth: persistence after a “no” shows feeling and eventually works. In fact: “no” is a boundary, not hidden interest.

Who plays on this

Platforms. They earn from visibility. Sign: paid “boosting” of your profile. Paid visibility creates an incentive to sell access; it has not been proven that apps deliberately keep people from getting together.

Pickup coaches. They sell techniques like the “neg” — a jab that supposedly lowers the other person’s self-esteem and raises your attractiveness. There is no test showing that it works; a survey showed only that people perceive it negatively12. More on selling “male power” and “feminine energy” in chapter 29.

Ideological communities. “Involuntary celibate” forums package rejection into a theory: “the market is closed to people like you.” Studies describe loneliness and mental health problems among their members, but these samples do not represent everyone without sex or a partner. How such ideas spread is in chapter 26.

What to do about it

  1. Count the right metric. Write down: how many respectful conversations you had, how many meetings, how much money and how many hours went into it, how you feel. Don’t count matches. It’s working if the number of conversations and meetings grows, not the time in the feed.

  2. Limit the feed. Set a cap on time and money — a sensible personal experiment, not a proven treatment. Sign it’s working: fewer automatic “no”s, more conversations.

  3. Get to the meeting faster. A profile poorly predicts attraction; live contact does better. Move the chat to a safe meeting when the interest is mutual. A first date is information, not a verdict. Check important facts through conversation and time, not suspicion.

  4. Write down your non-negotiables before you get attached. Before you fall in love, spell out a few things you won’t do without in a relationship (values, habits). Partners tend to be more alike than opposite — compare values and habits without demanding identical personalities. Sign it’s working: you can end a relationship that violates the list without waiting for fear of being alone to decide for you11.

Questions for yourself

  • What am I actually missing: sex, a partner, friends, or recognition?
  • Whose judgment am I counting when I look at my number of matches?
  • What rejection does my theory of the “market” protect me from?
  • What in my profile is me, and what is a shop window I’m afraid to show in person?

When to see a specialist

If an app consistently worsens your mood, and rejection and loneliness turn into persistent low mood, that is a reason to talk to a psychologist or a doctor. If contact after a rejection becomes threatening, seek protection.

23. Self-love, selfishness, and narcissism: where the line runs

Today the word “narcissist” gets slapped on a boss, an ex, a mother, a whole generation. Yet the best-tested finding on the subject is boring: narcissists are not a separate breed; there is a scale, and everyone stands somewhere on it1. The line runs not between the “healthy” and the “narcissists” but through a single question: “I am worth something” or “I am worth more than others.”

How it works

A scale, not a diagnosis

Researchers compared two models: narcissism as a distinct category of people, or as a trait everyone has in different amounts. Across clinical data and four further datasets, with a preregistered test, the scale won. The diagnostic threshold (“narcissistic personality disorder” — when the trait wrecks work, relationships, and life) does not vanish as a result: a clinician needs it to see the severe cases. But it is a line people have drawn on a continuous scale, not a seam in nature.

In a large American survey, about 6% of adults had met the criteria at some point in their lives (one method’s count, not valid worldwide). This is not the share of “toxic partners,” nor the share of people on social media.

The core and two faces

A working model describes the core as a sense of entitlement to special treatment — “I’m owed more.” The cycle works like this: a situation promises admiration or threatens status → the person reads what it says about their importance → either puts themselves forward or defends by attacking → others respond with attention, withdrawal, or conflict. This is a theoretical construct, not a proven chain in the brain: no “narcissism hormone” and no single brain defect has been found2.

The core has two faces. Grandiose — confidence, display, a hunger for leadership; it often comes with a high opinion of oneself. Vulnerable — anxiety, touchiness, shame, unstable self-evaluation. These are not two types of people but two dimensions: they overlap, fluctuate, and can occur in one person at the same time.

Admiration and rivalry

It helps to distinguish two strategies. Admiration — promoting yourself: charming, dazzling, taking the stage. Rivalry — protecting yourself at others’ expense: belittling, taking revenge, showing contempt. In seven studies of couples, admiration better explained why a person is liked at first, and rivalry why they are hard to be with over the long run3. The two are linked, so a dazzling self-presentation does not by itself guarantee harmlessness. The total questionnaire score hides this difference: two people with the same “narcissism” can carry very different risks for those close to them.

Researchers also describe communal narcissism — superiority in the language of kindness and morality (“I’m the most caring,” not “I’m the strongest”). You cannot infer it just because someone volunteers or talks about values (the data on it here are weak).

Self-esteem is something else

Self-esteem is the judgment “I have worth.” Narcissism is the comparison “I deserve more than you.” The second does not necessarily conceal the reverse of the first: the idea that “any confidence is a mask for secret self-hatred” has no support.

Self-esteem does not take in other people’s opinions directly. A person gets feedback → interprets what it says about their abilities and acceptance → updates the evaluation → approaches the situation or avoids it → gets new feedback. Your existing self-image colors the interpretation: what “everyone thinks of me” is partly a projection of what I think myself. What makes self-esteem fragile is not caring about looks or success, but a condition: “I’m acceptable only if I win.”

Where it comes from

Parental overvaluation (“you’re better than other children”) predicted a rise in a child’s narcissism, warmth predicted healthy self-esteem4 (more in chapter 09). Coldness is not dismissed either: it also matters, but it acts on a separate facet — willingness to exploit others — not on “narcissism in general.”

The most awkward evidence for the “parents are to blame” theory comes from twin families. In a 2026 German study, genes accounted for 39 to 63% of the differences, and the resemblance between parents and children in this model was explained by genes, with no noticeable contribution from direct upbringing5. This does not mean that upbringing and abuse change nothing. But a child resembling a parent does not, by itself, prove the child learned it. More on family and the narcissistic parent in chapter 09, on genes in chapter 04.

Age, sex, generations

A synthesis of 51 longitudinal studies following people aged 8 to 77: all three facets — assertive, hostile, and anxious — decline with age on average, the anxious one most of all6. But a person’s position relative to their peers stays largely the same. A young person can become more modest and still remain the most arrogant in their circle. Maturing on average does not guarantee that any particular person changes.

Men on average score slightly higher, above all on entitlement to special treatment and willingness to exploit others; on vulnerable narcissism there is almost no difference7. With a difference this size, the distributions overlap almost entirely (this is a calculation, not a direct observation). These data cannot separate biology from gendered upbringing, let alone let you guess anything about a person from their sex.

The “narcissism epidemic among young people”: comparisons of students across decades contradict each other, and no general rise has been shown8. As for social media causing the disorder, there is no tested causal estimate.

The opposite extreme: erasing yourself

The opposite of narcissism is not self-denial. Psychologists distinguish caring for others from unmitigated caring: constant accommodation and neglect of your own needs. The mechanics: a person notices a need → assesses whether voicing it threatens belonging → negotiates or swallows it. Swallowing it ends the conflict for now and stores up fatigue and resentment. The link between unmitigated caring and poor well-being shows up in observational studies; it has not been shown that kindness itself does harm.

The difference between shame and guilt belongs here too. A synthesis of 108 studies: proneness to shame is linked to depression noticeably more strongly than proneness to guilt9. But guilt that has become general and endless (“I’m responsible for everything”) is linked to depression almost as strongly. The working line is not “guilt good, shame bad” but “I did something specific, and it can be fixed” versus “I am bad through and through” or “I must prevent anyone else’s pain.”

Giving can feel good, but not always. In a replication series, actually spending money on another person lifted mood; recalling such spending had no effect in one large experiment and a small one in another10. This is short-term mood, not a recipe for happiness.

What this means for the self

Given. Heredity accounts for a substantial share of the differences between people: from a third to two-thirds (in one German study). The temperament you arrived with also shaped how you were treated: a child, too, elicits reactions from parents. You chose neither your starting place on the scale nor your first mirrors.
