Built. From the overvaluation and warmth received in childhood, the ego partly assembles a formula of worth: “I’m loved” or “I’m ranked above others” (a moderate, observational link). The first does not require beating anyone. The second requires constant confirmation — and then self-esteem trades like a stock price, and the defenses work to prop it up: deflect criticism, belittle whoever is nearby. At the grandiose pole, the mask for others is brilliance; at the vulnerable pole, touchiness. At the self-erasing pole, the mask is being no trouble: “I don’t need anything.”

Rebuilt. Average trait levels shift with age on their own, but your position relative to others stays largely the same, so “it’ll pass on its own” is not a plan. What changes is not the label but specific moves: do I take revenge, can I accept a refusal, do I fix what I did. That is where your freedom lies. You can try replacing the condition of worth “I’m better” with “I’m responsible for my actions” (there is no tested recipe).

The line between self-love and selfishness is not measured by any scale. A working test of four questions (reasoning, not a tested method): is the act voluntary? does it preserve my basic resources — sleep, money, health? does it respect the other person’s rights? can I accept a reasonable refusal? If all four are “yes,” it is more likely self-care. If it requires that the other person not dare to refuse, that is already entitlement to special treatment.

This answers the biggest fear: “What if I’m a narcissist?” The question is not whether you have the trait — everyone does. The question is how much of it there is, how long it lasts, and what it breaks.

Myths

Myth: love yourself first — otherwise you can’t love another. In fact: no threshold of “self-love” below which you cannot love another has been shown (not scientifically tested). Attachment research looks at responsiveness in specific relationships; the condition “first become perfect” may shame vulnerable people (harm not measured).

Myth: every narcissist secretly hates themselves. In fact: grandiose and vulnerable are different, if overlapping, dimensions; a universal hidden low self-esteem has not been shown.

Myth: praise a child and you’ll raise a narcissist. In fact: what is linked to narcissism is overvaluation — “you’re better than others” — not warmth (more in chapter 09).

Myth: repeat “I’m worthy of love” and you’ll believe it. In fact: in people with low self-esteem, such repetition made mood worse11 (more in chapter 46).

Who plays on this

The label market. Online tests like “narcissistic mother” and “spot a narcissist by the signs” have not been tested for whether they can tell the disorder apart from depression, borderline traits, or simply a controlling parent. No estimate of this market’s size or of the harm it does was found. The tell: a label for the whole person instead of a description of behavior, and the promise that naming the diagnosis will solve the problem. More on living next to such a person in chapter 24.

Sellers of self-confidence. Confidence is sold as a substitute for skills and resources — through affirmations, “love yourself and everything will come.” The tell: an inner state is declared the condition for everything else, and no way to test it through action is offered.

Sellers of sacrifice. The flip side: “give more and you’ll be happier,” “true love endures.” The spending experiments involved small voluntary sums and short-term mood; they did not test coercion or exploitative relationships. The tell: generosity demanded beyond your means and under pressure. For how high-control groups do this, see chapter 25.

What to do about it

Self-compassion instead of scolding. When you make a mistake, three steps: acknowledge that it hurts; don’t stick a general label on yourself (“I’m worthless”); name a specific step to fix it. In four experiments, self-compassion strengthened the desire to fix a weakness or a wrongdoing rather than making people slack off12. Self-compassion programs reduce depression, but against active comparisons the effect is small and uncertain; the short three-step version itself has not been tested. The sign it’s working: less self-flagellation, more fixes actually made — not constant warmth toward yourself.

A log of actions instead of labels. Write down: what you did, in what situation, what came of it, how you fixed it, whether it happened again. Questions for yourself: did I take revenge? did I accept the refusal? did I fix something specific? If you suffer from someone else’s behavior, track your freedom, fear, sleep, money, and safety separately — not the other person’s apologies. That way, fine words don’t stand in for results (this is a tracking tool, not treatment).

With children — warmth without a ranking. “You worked hard on this,” “I enjoy being with you” instead of “you’re the best.” This rests on a long-term observational study, but it has not been proven as prevention of narcissism (the phrasings are examples, not tested scripts).

Give — voluntarily and within your means. A small expense on someone else can lift your mood. Track mood and financial strain separately; if there’s pressure or you can’t afford it, stop.

Questions for yourself

  • Does my worth rest on being loved, or on being above others?
  • What do I do when I’m refused: accept it, take offense, or take revenge?
  • When did I last fix something I did with a concrete action, not words?
  • What does it cost me to be no trouble to anyone — and who pays for it?

When to see a specialist

If a trait has been wrecking work and relationships for years; if being around someone has brought fear, insomnia, or loss of money or freedom; if shame and guilt have turned into persistent low mood. Self-compassion is a skill, not a substitute for treatment.

24. Close to a narcissist: partner, boss, friend — how to recognize it and what to do

To close a door, you don’t need a diagnosis of whoever is behind it. The word “narcissist” explains someone else’s behavior but doesn’t protect you from it: attention to actions does. The science of narcissism says three things. The trait is better measured by degree than by label. Harm should be judged by deeds. Change should be tested by time.

How it works

Not a type of person, but a scale

Narcissism is better described as a matter of more or less than as a split into “narcissists” and “normal people”1. At its core is “I’m entitled to more,” and it has a grandiose face and a vulnerable one (more in chapter 23). Clinicians need a diagnostic threshold; in life you meet not a category but a degree and specific behavior.

Two moves: admiration and rivalry

For the people nearby, the most useful distinction is between admiration (promoting yourself) and rivalry (defending yourself by attacking; more in chapter 23). Seven studies using partner reports and observations of couples showed that admiration explains why such a person is likable at the start, while rivalry explains why they are hard to be with later2.

The mechanism looks like this. A confident presentation draws attention. The first disagreements test whether the person’s worth rests on being right and being on top. If it does, the defensive “I’m entitled” kicks in: reproaches, contempt, counterattack. The partner’s trust and satisfaction drop. This is a likely trajectory, not a program: some vulnerable narcissists aren’t charming at all, and abuse also happens with no narcissism whatsoever.

A blow to the image is the main trigger

This is where the ego shows most clearly. A large meta-analysis (a study that pools many studies) found that narcissism is linked to aggression and violence, and that the link is noticeably stronger when the person has been hurt — criticized, rejected, humiliated (0.29 versus 0.12)3. The link holds for men and women, across ages, and in both individualist and collectivist cultures. These are correlations, not the percentage of people who will turn violent.

Two conclusions follow. If your refusal triggered rage, that rage is not your responsibility. And never “test” anyone by provoking them: danger is assessed by real threats, intimidation and escalation, not by a test score.

Empathy: able, but unwilling

To respond to someone else’s pain, you have to notice it, consider it important, feel it and turn it into action. It can break down at any step. A person can understand intellectually and still turn away, because the other’s pain threatens their image. In grandiose narcissism, empathy is only slightly reduced, and more in feeling than in understanding4. “No empathy at all” is false.

In experiments, a direct request — “put yourself in the other person’s place” — immediately strengthened the response of people high in narcissistic traits5. So the ability is there; what’s missing is motivation. But a response in the lab is neither a lasting change nor protection from abuse.

Self-understanding works the same way. Some high scorers know they are seen as arrogant and still think well of themselves. Asked directly, “Are you a narcissist?”, some of them honestly answer “yes.” For some this is accurate self-knowledge, for others a neutral description, for still others a source of pride. Accepting the label is not the same as remorse.

Work: winning the selection, not the job

In an interview, confidence and visibility look like leadership. A meta-analysis found that narcissism helps people become leaders, but subordinates and colleagues don’t rate these leaders as more effective; only the leaders rate themselves highly6. Part of this “emergence” effect is explained simply by sociability. No recipe like “moderate narcissism makes the best leader” follows from these data.

A rude boss is a separate issue. Systematic hostility from above doesn’t necessarily grow out of narcissism, and a high score doesn’t prove bullying. Signs of a “self-absorbed” CEO in company reports are useful for generating hypotheses, not for diagnosing a specific person.

Popular words: what’s behind them

In research, gaslighting means systematically undermining someone’s trust in their own perception and words, and it rests on power and inequality7. It is not just any argument, memory lapse or lie. A scale for it was recently validated in two cultures, but it doesn’t prove a link to narcissism specifically.

“Trauma bonding.” The classic study followed 75 women who had recently left abusive relationships. After six months their attachment to the former partner had weakened by about a quarter, and features of the past relationship largely explained how much of it remained8. The study doesn’t prove that alternating affection and cruelty bonds more strongly than a steady relationship. Fear, money, housing and prior attachment explain at least as much. “The brain got hooked on dopamine from the abuser” goes beyond the data.

“Love bombing” has been studied in one small student paper. How typical it is, even of high-control groups, hasn’t been measured9. The warning sign is not intense courtship but pressure, crossed boundaries and punishment for slowing things down.

“Narcissistic abuse,” “flying monkeys,” “gray rock” are not scientific categories or tested methods. Psychological abuse and coercion are studied without diagnosing the abuser.

Abuse is a separate axis

Not all conflict is the same. A situational flare-up in an argument and coercive control — a system of domination through surveillance, isolation, threats and money — work differently. Programs for abusers have small effects on average, and “learning to manage your anger” hasn’t been proven to work against domination. Among 393 engaged couples with no physical fighting, psychological aggression predicted the first physical aggression over the next two and a half years — by men and by women10. This is a risk marker, not a verdict.

Surveillance and isolation may already be coercion rather than an “early sign” of future abuse; no list of signs can precisely predict a couple’s fate. Digital control — checking a phone, social media — is linked to jealousy, anxious attachment and “dark” traits in the person doing the monitoring. Jealousy doesn’t prove love.

Treatment: a chance, no guarantees

A person with narcissistic personality disorder can change. A 2025 review of interventions found very few studies, mostly case reports11. In a large trial of schema therapy (with a three-year follow-up), narcissistic personality disorder was just one of several disorders. The first trial specifically on narcissistic personality disorder was published in 2026: in 114 people, both therapies reduced severity; one had a stronger effect on grandiosity, the other on vulnerability. But it is one study, and the courses differed in length. There is no proven medication for the disorder itself. “Never treatable” and “responds well to treatment” are both exaggerations. More on therapy in chapter 46.

What this means for the self

Close to a narcissist, two selves are under fire at once: theirs and yours.

Their ego rests on comparison. Worth has to be constantly confirmed: by admiration from outside or by victory over someone. You become a mirror. While you reflect admiration, you’re needed. When you reflect disagreement, you’re a threat. Hence, in this model, the early charm and the rage at refusal are two moves in a single defense of the image.

Your self risks reshaping itself around someone else’s image. Gaslighting, by definition, hits not your opinion but your trust in your own perception and words. Isolation cuts you off from the people who could help you check reality.

Given and built. Childhood abuse is linked to being victimized by a partner more often as an adult (a link, not a verdict; more on growing up with a narcissistic parent in chapter 09). A separate issue is the habit of forgetting yourself while caring for others: this is not the same as caring, and it is linked to distress (the data are observational). What harms is not kindness but self-forgetting.

The label trap. “He’s a narcissist” or “she’s a narcissist” is a convenient ego defense. It explains the pain and takes the question “what’s wrong with me” off your plate. But it has a price. The label puts someone else’s mind at the center, not your safety. It sends you looking for “stages of the cycle,” though no single script for them has been proven. And it lets you overlook similar moves in yourself: every ego knows rivalry and resentment at refusal; the difference is degree (where healthy self-love ends is covered in chapter 23).

Where the freedom is. You don’t need to prove someone else’s disorder to decide that a specific act is unacceptable. An accurate description of harm can coexist with a wrong diagnosis. Freedom means moving from the question “who are they?” to the question “what’s happening, what is it doing to me, and what can I do myself?”

Myths

Myth: narcissists feel no empathy. In fact: empathy is only slightly reduced, more in the feeling part, and the response grows on direct request4, 5. Don’t turn the person into a monster — but still hold them accountable for their actions.

Myth: a narcissist first idealizes, then devalues and discards — in a predictable cycle. In fact: charm at the start and conflict later have been shown; a single cycle with timelines has not2. Not every kind word is a “stage.”

Myth: if he admitted he’s a narcissist, he’s become aware and is changing. In fact: some high scorers readily admit the trait. The measure is whether he names the specific harm, accepts a refusal, makes amends, and sustains the change.

Myth: if he has become softer and more sympathetic, the danger has passed. In fact: treatment for those who were harmed reduces depression a little on average, but it isn’t proven to reduce repeat abuse. A sympathetic conversation is not proof of safety.

Who plays on this

The label market. Videos and courses on “narcissistic abuse,” “narcissist checklists,” “healing trauma bonds.” The scale of this market hasn’t been measured, but the signs are visible: a diagnosis from a single question, a cycle with stages and timelines, “dopamine addiction” presented as fact, a promise of healing. A short narcissism test does not replace a clinical assessment.

The employer at hiring. Confidence sells better than results. The sign: decisions rest on how someone shines in the interview, not on an independent assessment of the work6.

High-control groups. A warm welcome that becomes conditional: you’re loved as long as you obey9 (how typical this is hasn’t been measured). More in chapter 25.

What to do about it

Describe actions, not the person. Write down: what happened, when, in front of whom, what it led to, whether there was repair, whether it happened again. In a separate note, track your freedom, fear, sleep, money and safety — apart from their apologies. It’s working if after a month you see a pattern, not a jumble of impressions. At work, the same: dates, precise requests, witnesses, written clarification of duties, independent channels for complaints — while weighing the risk of retaliation and loss of income. Telling a boss “you’re a narcissist” is pointless: there’s no evidence it helps. These are risk-management recommendations, not a tested protocol.

Practice refusing, but don’t confuse it with protection. The formula: fact — your request or boundary — your action, which depends only on you. “I won’t take this on today. I can do A on Friday, or pass B to someone else.” A boundary is what you will do: “If there’s shouting, I’ll end the conversation.” Practice out loud, with feedback, starting with easy situations12. The sign it’s working: you make requests more often and avoid less — look at the consequences, not at the other person’s agreement. The goal is not for the other person to agree. Where refusal may provoke retaliation, safety comes first, not a well-turned phrase.

Forgiveness is not a return of trust. You can forgive without canceling boundaries. In newlyweds, how forgiveness related to self-respect and to continued aggression depended on the partner’s behavior. Look at changed actions, not at remorse and promises.

Test change with time. If you yourself want to change, keep the same journal: “Was there retaliation? Was the refusal accepted? Was something specific repaired?” If the other person is changing, asking them to put themselves in your place is optional, not your duty. Your safety shouldn’t depend on whether their therapy works.

Questions for yourself

  • What exactly does this person do — and what remains if you remove the word “narcissist”?
  • Who did I last check my version of events with — and are there fewer such people now?
  • What do I do when I’m refused or disagreed with?
  • Did the apologies change the actions — or only my feelings?

When to see a specialist

Surveillance, threats, intimidation, restriction of money, movement or contact, escalating aggression are reasons to seek independent help and a safety plan right away, without waiting for violence or a diagnosis. Couples therapy where there is fear and control isn’t always appropriate: first, a separate one-on-one conversation with a specialist.

25. Cults and high-control groups: why smart people end up there

Of more than a thousand people who attended residential workshops run by one well-known new religious movement, about nine in ten did not join, and most of those who did join left on their own within two years (a single historical observation)1. This sits poorly with the idea of all-powerful recruitment and does not support the picture of the gullible newcomer. The data suggest that what leads people into a high-control group is not a flaw of intellect but circumstances and steps: an unmet need, an invitation, a series of small commitments. According to former members’ accounts, a group becomes dangerous when it starts supplying the whole self — friends, work, meaning and self-image — and leaving takes all of it away at once.

How it works

Entry: not “who” but “when”

The working model of recruitment goes like this: a lack of belonging or a state of uncertainty → an attractive invitation → repeated meetings and a new explanation of life from new people → escalating commitments → growing dependence on the group2. This is a model, not a proven law: it was assembled from former members’ recollections. Recollections show the circumstances in which people joined, but they do not prove which traits brought them there.

Uncertainty–identity theory explains why clear “us versus them” boundaries are attractive: when the world is unclear, a group with clear rules offers a clear sense of self. This is a theoretical explanation (not a validated test): uncertainty does not doom anyone to an extreme group.

Loneliness is often named as the main way in. Direct data are scarce: in one study of older urban residents in China, loneliness was linked to vulnerability to fraud, but vulnerability was measured by questionnaire rather than by actual losses, and each person was surveyed only once — so cause and effect cannot be separated. The claim that loneliness by itself leads people into high-control groups has even weaker support.

Steps: the small “yes” and the price of what’s already invested

Three long-studied mechanisms drive escalating commitment3, 4.

  • Foot in the door. A small request can change how you describe yourself (“I’m someone who helps these people”), and the next, bigger request leans on the wish to be consistent. A review of these experiments shows successes, null results and backfire effects alike: it is not a push-button technique.
  • Cognitive dissonance — the unpleasant tension when your actions diverge from your beliefs.
  • Escalation of commitment — the more you have invested, the more the question “is this worth it now?” gives way to “how do I justify what I’ve already put in?” This has been tested on business and everyday decisions; how much it contributes specifically to cult recruitment has not been measured4.

In a famous 1959 experiment, people who went through an awkward, embarrassing “test” before joining a group later rated the group more highly5. This is one small laboratory study about ratings, not proof that pain binds a group together; the available data include no modern large-scale replication.

A similar fate befell the famous story that belief only grows stronger after a prophecy fails. As early as 1962, a paper appeared with the blunt title “a failure to replicate”6. People readjust in different ways after being proven wrong; there is no law that “belief always grows stronger.”

“Love bombing” — a flood of warmth at first contact — has not really been measured as a recruitment technique (more in chapter 24); what distinguishes groups is not warmth but whether it becomes conditional, dependent on obedience.

Trainings and charisma

Large group awareness trainings promise transformation. One study compared participants in such a training with similar people who did not attend, testing them before, after and about a year and a half later: the main finding was a short-lived sense of control over one’s life, with no broad lasting changes7. This design cannot assess rare severe harms. The finding cannot be generalized to other programs, including post-Soviet ones: each needs its own testing.

Charisma can be taught, but a persuasive voice is a delivery skill, not knowledge8 (more in chapter 29).

The popular argument that “the least competent are the most confident” is also shaky: part of the famous graph may be a statistical artifact of grouping people by noisy scores. That graph does not attach the label “they’re just dumb and overconfident” to any particular guru or follower.

Control: what can be measured

The term “brainwashing” promises more than science knows. In 1987 the relevant board of the American Psychological Association declined to accept a report on this dispute for lack of rigor and impartiality, and stated that there was insufficient evidence to take a position9. This is neither confirmation of the theory nor proof that coercion never happens.

What can be measured is something concrete: psychological abuse within a group. A scale for this was validated on self-identified former members of dozens of different groups10. Most of them were women, but that speaks to who seeks help and answers surveys, not to who ends up in groups: sex, age, intelligence and culture cannot be inferred from the makeup of such a sample. Popular checklists of warning signs of control are useful for organizing observations, but they have no validated threshold beyond which a group can be diagnosed.

Exit: what gets removed is not a belief but the supports

If a group provides friends, work, identity and moral certainty, leaving takes away all of it at once. So recovery is not swapping one belief for another but rebuilding ordinary ties, everyday self-reliance and a coherent story about yourself (this is an interpretation of former members’ accounts, not a tested model).

A French study interviewed 31 former members in depth, including a psychiatric assessment2. Many in this sample had serious difficulties, but the participants were found through victim-support associations and interviewed retrospectively — so it is impossible to tell whether this came before the group, because of the group, or because of who signs up for such studies. Family and friends outside the group featured in the accounts of how people left.

Online movements and multilevel marketing can demand intense involvement, but there are no reliable data on entry, exit and recovery there. The comparison with a high-control group is justified when real dependence, threats, isolation and punishment are visible — not merely unpopular views.

What this means for the self

A high-control group is a machine for assembling the self, and it takes over all three layers at once.

Ties are the main door. The tasks of “belonging” and “rank in the group” (chapter 13) do not disappear in adulthood. A new group offers a ready-made “we,” and behind it a ready-made self: who you are, what you value, whom you oppose. The fewer other “we”s a person has at the moment of contact — a move, a breakup, a lost job, uncertainty — the more room, according to the recruitment model, a single group can take up. The data speak of circumstances, not of a defect of intelligence.

The story gets rewritten step by step. Each small “yes” adds a line to your self-description: “I’m the one who stayed for the weekend,” “I’m the one who invested.” After a series of steps, leaving the group means admitting that the story of recent years was a mistake. This is where the ego kicks in: the self-image of a reasonable person is defended not by leaving but by justifying what has been invested. Intelligence offers no protection here: by the logic of dissonance, it can work to defend the investment rather than to check it.

The body is the third lever. Demands to give up sleep, privacy, money or outside contacts remove the supports on which sound judgment rests (what sleep loss does to the brain — chapter 17). This does not prove “brainwashing,” but it is a reason to stop, not a sign that the change is “working.”

Mask and meaning. The group also delivers the eighth task — meaning — packaged in absolute certainty. The mask of a group member holds as long as it is accepted — which is why what happens to those who doubt matters so much. The difference between a healthy community and a trap is not whether it is religious, secular or commercial. The difference lies in choice, alternatives and consequences: can you say “no,” is there life outside the group, what happens if you leave.

Where the freedom is. The data do not show that these mechanisms switch off choice: most of those invited declined, and most who joined in that observation left on their own. “Lost free will” is not a measurable scientific concept. Freedom here means holding several independent supports: people, money, occupations, sources of meaning. When the self stands on several legs, no single group can become everything to it.

Myths

Myth: people who end up in cults are stupid, gullible or ill. In fact: observations of recruitment and samples of former members do not yield such a profile. Some former members do have difficulties, but whether these existed before joining is unknown.

Myth: a cult “brainwashes” people, and they lose free will. In fact: abuse and restrictions within groups are measurable; an irreversible “will-less” state is not. Many people turn down recruitment or leave on their own. None of this justifies either blaming the victim or “rescuing” them by force: the data provide no grounds for the latter.

Myth: the harsher the initiation, the stronger the devotion — so ordeals are useful. In fact: one old laboratory study showed increased liking after an awkward test. That cannot justify humiliation or pain as a way to bond a group.

Myth: a charismatic, confident teacher must be a knowledgeable one. In fact: charisma is a trainable delivery, not expertise (more in chapter 29).

Who plays on this

What matters is not the label — religious, training, commercial, online — but choice, alternatives and consequences. Signs that call for slowing down (these are precautions, not a validated test):

  • warmth depends on obedience and cools when you disagree;
  • commitments grow in steps, and each next one is presented as the logical continuation of the last;
  • you are asked to give up sleep, privacy, money or contact with outsiders;
  • total transformation is promised instead of a limited, testable goal;
  • the question “where’s the data?” is answered with success stories and a confident tone.

How success and “power” are sold on the same model — chapter 29; how ideas replicate themselves in people’s heads — chapter 26.

What to do about it

  • Three questions before investing. Is the person convincing? Is the claim true? Does the proposed method improve the promised outcome — for whom, compared with what, for how long, at what cost? For a serious purchase or a health decision, only the last two matter.
  • Slow down commitments and watch the reaction. Say “no” to one request, take a pause, talk it over with an outsider, decline to pay right now. How the group reacts to disagreement, outside contacts and refusal is the main sign of whether warmth or self-interest prevails there.
  • Keep several supports. Friends, money, work and meaning should not all converge in one group.
  • If someone close to you is in a group, don’t cut the tie. Family and friends outside the group feature in the accounts of those who left. Calm, nonjudgmental contact and concrete help — with housing, money, documents, company — as needed. This is a reasonable precaution, not a tested program; forcible “extraction” is not justified by the data.

If you want support from a book rather than a guru: for adults with depression, structured self-help books with exercises showed benefit in a review, at follow-ups of three months to three years, while studies in younger people did not11. This applies to tested programs, not to any bestseller; what else actually works — chapter 46.

Questions for yourself

  • How many of my friends, how much of my money and meaning rest on a single community?
  • What am I justifying by how much I’ve already invested?
  • What happens when I say “no” there?
  • Do I believe the claim — or the person making it?

When to see a specialist

“Former member” is not a diagnosis. If after leaving, persistent symptoms get in the way of living, working or relating to people, it is worth seeing a specialist about them.

26. Mind viruses: how ideas, lies, and anger multiply in our heads

On American Twitter in 2016, 0.1% of users accounted for nearly 80% of all shares from fake-news sources1. A lie does not spread through every head like an infection. A few people push it, and the rest pick it up when it serves the self: it entertains, it signals “I’m one of us,” it supplies an enemy. An idea lives not in the network but in how each person who retells it rebuilds it anew to suit themselves.

How it works

Not a copy but a rebuild

The word “meme” was coined as a cultural analog of the gene: a unit that copies itself. As a vivid metaphor it works; as a scientific theory it does not. A gene is passed on as an exact copy. An idea is not: a person hears a story, interprets it through what they already know, remembers it altered, and retells it in their own words. The next person rebuilds it from scratch again.

So the fact that two retellings look alike does not by itself mean anything was “copied.” Laughter, too, spreads through a room without any packet of information being passed. The field that studies this is not “memetics” but cultural evolution: how learning, selection, and transmission change practices and beliefs within a group.

The field distinguishes three filters, each tested separately:

  • frequency — people disproportionately adopt what the majority does;
  • prestige — people adopt from those who look successful and important;
  • content — what concerns threats, disgust, and other people sticks and is remembered better.

What survives retelling

In transmission-chain experiments, where a story is passed from person to person, information that was negative or threatening, relevant to survival, or about other people survived better.

But these are different advantages at different stages. Choosing a story, remembering it, and passing it on are three separate steps, and winning at one does not mean winning at all of them. Positive content spreads too. There is no law that “bad always wins.”

Why lies run faster

In archival Twitter data, false stories spread faster and farther than true ones2. The likely mechanism: the unexpected grabs attention; a share serves entertainment, belonging, or a signal of “I’m one of us”; and in that moment accuracy gets too little attention. This is a plausible account, not proof: observation cannot separate the contributions of novelty, emotion, the feed, and the audience. On other platforms and topics the ratio may differ.

Who shares is uneven too. On Facebook, people over 65 shared articles from fake-news sites almost seven times as often as the youngest users3. This compares behavior in one country; it does not measure some “age-related gullibility” of the brain. The figure does not carry over to other countries.

Repetition makes things familiar, and familiar feels true

What is familiar feels correct. A repeated statement is, on average, rated as more truthful — a review of 51 studies confirmed this. The effect appears even when people could have known the right answer4. Knowledge is not useless — it just doesn’t always kick in before the feeling of “I’ve heard this somewhere.”

Anger as a commodity

The learning mechanism is simple. A person expresses moral condemnation, gets likes — and next time condemns more readily. Along the way, they pick up what their circle treats as normal. A feed that ranks partly by engagement can push such posts higher.

Across half a million tweets on three political topics, each additional moral-emotional word was associated with, on average, 20% more retweets5. This is an observed association, not an experiment in which the word was inserted on purpose. Across millions of posts by American media outlets and members of Congress, mentioning “outsiders” strongly predicted engagement. A mention is not necessarily an attack, and a reaction is not approval.

Bubbles are leaky

Algorithms definitely change what people see. In a large experiment on Twitter across seven countries, the algorithmic feed amplified right-wing parties in six of the seven (the study examined lawmakers’ tweets).

But seeing is not the same as changing your mind. On Facebook, about half of a typical user’s political content came from like-minded sources. When that share was cut by a third, people engaged less — and their political views did not change on eight preregistered measures6. This was a short experiment; it does not rule out an effect that builds up over years. Even the most ideological users have opposing voices among the accounts they follow. But exposure and attention are different things: opposing content can arrive and go unnoticed.

Polarization has followed different paths in different countries. Among wealthy countries over four decades, it rose most in the United States; elsewhere it rose in some and fell in others. A single technology can hardly explain such divergence.

The rabbit hole and first beliefs

TikTok accounts posing as teenage boys received more and more misogynistic content over five days: from 13% to 56% of recommendations. This is an audit of what the feed serves to invented profiles, not a measurement of how many real teenagers changed their views.

On YouTube the picture is more complex. A path to radical channels exists in the recommendations, but only a small share of people watch them, and they get there through prior views, subscriptions, and outside links. The chain “saw → believed → bought → approved → acted” is five different steps, and a study of one does not prove the others. How the feed and idols enter childhood — chapter 14.

Why people believe in conspiracies

There are several routes into conspiracy belief. Uncertainty creates demand for an explanation; threat creates demand for control; intergroup conflict makes accusation useful to your own side. A conspiracy turns chaos into a coherent story with allies and enemies. This is a working model, not a diagnosis of any particular person.

A meta-analysis of 170 studies found links between conspiracy belief and threat, anxiety, intuitive thinking, and personality traits. The samples are mostly from the United States, Britain, and Poland, the data are self-reports, and they do not prove cause. The famous “losing control makes you see patterns” experiment has not replicated in every attempt. There is no universal “percentage of conspiracy believers”: the figure depends on how the question is worded.

A separate branch is collective narcissism: “my nation deserves special recognition, and it isn’t getting it.” Unlike plain love of one’s group, it is linked to hostility and revenge. The data are mostly correlational.

Propaganda: access, repetition, distraction

Influence is not just persuasion: you can control what people have access to, repeat an interpretation until it becomes familiar, and distract. The effect is measurable but not all-powerful, and it can backfire (more in chapter 37).

What this means for the self

Given — the filters. Attention to threat, to what many people do, and to what a high-status person says are tendencies that experiments test separately. They fire without our choosing to.

Built — beliefs tied to belonging. A share is not just an opinion but also a signal of “I’m one of us.” An angry post is a way to confirm your rank in the group: likes for condemnation teach condemnation. This is how a belief becomes woven into the self-image, and the ego starts defending it as part of the self. A rebuttal can then feel not like news about the world but like a blow to you and your people. Collective narcissism is the same mechanism at group scale: self-esteem is tied to recognition of the nation, and that is linked to hostility.

The mask for others. In the feed, a person presents themselves through what they share and what they condemn. The platform pays in attention for certain kinds of self-presentation, and the mask adjusts to what pays. Over time you may find that “my views” are a set of reactions that were rewarded best.

Freedom lies in the step between “felt” and “shared.” The choice of what to pass on stays with the person. One lever is attention to accuracy: the question “is this true?” has to be switched on deliberately. And the distinction that matters is not “believe/don’t believe” but five steps: see, believe, approve, share, act. You can stop at any of them.

Anger, lies, and fear often spread through the tasks of belonging and rank in the group. Who builds closed groups on this — chapter 25; how the feed holds attention — chapter 28.

Myths

Myth: memes are the genes of culture, copying themselves. In fact: an idea is rebuilt each time in the head of whoever retells it; “meme” is a metaphor, not a discovered scientific fact.

Myth: debunking only strengthens a lie. In fact: five experiments covering dozens of topics and more than 10,000 people found no “backfire effect”9. A correction may not erase a lie completely — the residual influence lasts longer if the correction came late, the lie was repeated, and the original source was authoritative. But not erasing completely is not the same as strengthening.

Myth: everyone lives in sealed information bubbles. In fact: one-sided exposure is common, but few people are fully sealed in; cutting like-minded content by a third did not shift views in the experiment.

Myth: people believe in conspiracies out of anxiety and helplessness. In fact: these are only some of the links, alongside thinking style, group identity, and personality traits; no main cause has been found10.

Who plays on this

  • Platforms. The feed ranks partly by engagement, and engagement is strongly predicted by condemnation and “outsiders.” Sign: your feed has many posts about “outsiders” and posts with judgment words.
  • A small core of spreaders. A handful of accounts produce the lion’s share of fakes. Sign: the same fake-news sources again and again.
  • States. They manage access, repetition, and distraction, and sometimes fabricate posts. Sign: the topic of the day shifts abruptly when inconvenient news appears; alternatives are hard to find.
  • Content creators. Repeated encounters make them familiar, the familiar feels reliable, and entertainment blends with persuasion and selling; in children, influencers’ effect on what they eat has been shown experimentally. Sign: you trust someone because you’ve “watched them for a long time,” not because you checked. How success and “strength” are sold — chapter 29.

What to do about it

  • An accuracy pause. Before sharing, ask yourself: is the main claim true? If you’re not sure, look at the source. In a series of 20 experiments with adults, this short question reduced willingness to share false content without noticeably reducing willingness to share true content11. It works if you’re telling them apart — not if you’ve simply stopped believing everything.
  • Inoculation against techniques. Learn the standard techniques: emotional pressure, false dilemmas. Then look for them in new examples. In experiments this improves recognition of manipulation, but in a normal feed the effect is small, and how long it lasts is unknown: this is training, not immunity12. Sign that it works: you notice the technique both in the other camp and in your own, and you don’t file an honest emotional message under manipulation.
  • Lateral reading. Don’t read the page more carefully — leave it. Find out who the source is and track down the original. Short tips of this kind improved discernment even three weeks later, though ratings of true news also dropped slightly. Test yourself on both truth and falsehood.
  • How to debunk. First the correct version, then briefly where the error is, then the missing “why” link, and the correct version again (the sequence as a whole hasn’t been tested, but its parts — the correction and the replacement explanation — work). No humiliation: an argument in which the other person loses face hits their self, not the error. Don’t repeat the lie more than you have to.

Questions for yourself

  • Which of my beliefs do I defend as if I myself were under attack?
  • What did I share in the past week — and how much of it did I check?
  • What does my feed pay me attention for — and have I been doing it more?
  • What do I believe simply because I’ve heard it for a long time and from everywhere?

Part VI. 28–35: Building — Work, Partner, Children

27. Work and status: what a career does to the self — and who profits from the fear of falling behind

You lose your job, then find a new one — yet on average, life satisfaction doesn’t fully come back. The job comes back; the self doesn’t — not entirely. Work holds up more than your wallet: it holds your routine, the people around you, your rank in the group, and a short answer to the question “who are you?” The more of this hangs on a single support, the more that support costs you — and the easier it is to pull on.

How it works

Five different things under one word, “career”

When people say “work makes you happy” or “money isn’t everything,” they blur five different mechanisms:

  • money — changes your options and how much stress lands on you;
  • rank — how much you have compared with the person next to you; the same income means different things in different surroundings;
  • materialism — when money and things become the main measure of your worth;
  • workload — hours, volume, flexibility, job security;
  • meaning and ties — the sense of “I’m good at this” and “I’m among my own.”

Each pulls in its own direction. That’s why the research looks contradictory until you take it apart.

Money: there’s no ceiling

For a long time one number made the rounds: above roughly $75,000 a year in the US, happiness stops rising. Later, the authors of the competing studies sat down together and reanalyzed the data. The result: there is no general ceiling. The curve flattens only for the unhappiest people — for them, money adds little past a certain level; for everyone else, happiness keeps rising with income1. These are observational data, not a salary experiment, and the figures are in US dollars. The old number can’t be carried over to Russia or China, even adjusted for inflation: family, free services, safety and income inequality change what each ruble means.

Materialism: it’s not about the amount

A separate line of research is about values. People for whom money, possessions and image are the main measure of themselves feel worse, on average; this has been tested across a large body of studies2. But this is about orientation, not income. Climbing out of poverty, earning more, enjoying a thing you bought — none of that is harmful. What’s harmful is when the amount becomes the answer to “what am I worth?”

This is where contingent self-worth comes in: if the value of the self rests on success, every success and every failure hits harder. Self-esteem starts swinging along with results (the data are stronger on vulnerability and fluctuation than on stress as a direct effect). Hence the paradox: you have more resources, yet you have to prove yourself again and again — and that is just as expensive.

Losing a job: a blow to every support at once

Unemployment strips away income, routine, people, status and the ready answer “I’m an engineer” all at once. A new job restores some of it, but not all. A German panel study that followed tens of thousands of people over many years found that, on average, life satisfaction after job loss doesn’t fully recover — even after reemployment3. This is an average, not a scar everyone carries: mental health often improves after returning to work4.

A large body of studies shows the scale: psychological problems in about 34% of the unemployed versus 16% of the employed4. That means broader complaints, not diagnosed depression. Job loss hits men harder on average4, though how much harder hasn’t been calculated.

Workload: the evidence on how work is organized beats the call to love what you do

In a randomized experiment in China, university-educated office employees worked from home on Wednesdays and Fridays for six months. Quit rates fell from 7.2% to 4.8%, satisfaction rose, and performance reviews and promotions didn’t suffer. What was tested is two days at home for office workers — not fully remote work, and not a cure for loneliness.

In another project, about three thousand employees from more than a hundred organizations in six countries worked fewer hours for the same pay for six months. Well-being, sleep and fatigue improved where hours were actually cut, not squeezed into fewer days (the data are weaker: the companies volunteered to take part).

The World Health Organization describes burnout as a phenomenon tied specifically to work, not as a general label for any suffering. If low mood, emptiness and loss of interest have long since spread beyond work, the word “burnout” may be hiding depression.

Calling: a resource and a lever at once

Work you love is a resource, but once it becomes a condition of your self-worth, it turns difficulty into personal failure and rest into betrayal (more in chapter 34). Harmonious passion, which you can step away from, is linked mostly to good outcomes; obsessive passion, without which the self cracks, is linked to both good and bad5. Another trap is believing interest has to be “found” rather than developed: then difficulty reads as “this isn’t for me.”

A steadier support is goals that fit your values. In three long-term studies, such goals predicted sustained effort, goal attainment and well-being; the path, the authors suggest, runs through autonomy, competence and connection with people6. Autonomy here means “I stand behind this,” not “I’m on my own.”

What this means for the self

Given — not much. The need for rank in the group is ancient: rank, being needed, “I’m good at this” are built into how a person evaluates themselves. In adult life, work is the densest arena for this task: comparison, evaluation and role every single day.

Built — almost everything. A profession fuses with identity: “I’m a doctor,” “I’m a designer,” “I’m a manager.” This is convenient: a short answer, a ready mask for others, a clear ladder for self-esteem. But then the ego starts defending the job title rather than you. Criticism of a project sounds like criticism of you. The promotion of the person next to you feels like a personal defeat, because rank is a comparison, and it’s always being tallied. Losing your position brings down several layers at once: ties (colleagues, routine) and story (“who am I now?”).

Calling — the most delicate assembly. The deeper work has grown into the self, the more it gives and the easier it is to pull on. When someone’s work is their meaning, the people around them are quicker to treat extra hours as normal. Love of the work becomes an argument against paying for it.

The cost of parenthood sits here too. In Denmark, after the first child is born, mothers end up with an earnings gap of about 20% compared with fathers — through employment, hours and wage rate7. These are Danish data over a long horizon, not “forever” and not a law of nature. But the decision about who “steps back” from a career reshapes the family’s money — more on parenthood in chapter 32.

Where the freedom is. There are two.

The first is to spread your supports. The self is more stable when it stands on several legs: work, close people, body, a pursuit outside of money, your own group. Then losing one is a blow, not a collapse. For meaning that doesn’t reduce to a job title, see chapter 34.

The second is to separate the value of the work from the terms of the deal. Work can matter to you and still be underpaid. One doesn’t cancel the other. An ego that clings to the image of being “devoted to the cause” will deny this: admitting the terms are bad means doubting your own choice. That’s a defense, not a fact.

Myths

Myth: above $75,000 a year, money stops making you happier.
In fact: there’s no general ceiling; flattening was found only among the unhappiest, and for everyone else happiness rises with income1. The US number doesn’t carry over to other countries.

Myth: if money matters to you, you’re a materialist.
In fact: income, fear of poverty and materialistic values are different things. What’s harmful on average is the belief “money and image are the measure of me,” not the wish to earn2.

Myth: a calling always makes you happier.
In fact: “always” is false, and the label “they’re passionate about their work” helps justify poor treatment8 (more in chapter 34).

Myth: if it’s hard, it’s not for you.
In fact: difficulty isn’t a test of your destiny (more in chapter 34).

Who plays on this

Employers — through calling. In experiments, people more readily see unpaid overtime and tasks outside someone’s role as legitimate if the worker was described as “passionate about their work”8. The logic: “they’d have agreed anyway,” “the work is its own reward.” It works in reverse too: observers more readily see someone who is treated badly as passionate. What this costs in real hours and wages hasn’t been measured; what has been measured is how easily people believe it.

The course and coaching market — through the fear of falling behind. There is an honest part: workplace coaching with a specific goal shows an average positive effect in randomized trials9, 10. But the same review shows signs that unsuccessful studies were published less often, and the effect was shown on work tasks — not on “life transformation” and not on guaranteed income. The dishonest part: in the US, the regulator challenged unsubstantiated earnings claims by one online business school; in 2024, buyers got back more than $2.4 million11. This is one example from the regulator’s practice, not the share of fraudsters in the market — there’s no reliable estimate of the scale for either the US or Russia. Warning signs: income is shown through success stories without the full distribution, and a result is promised without a measurable goal. How success itself gets sold is the subject of chapter 29.

Fear of AI. Surveys record anxiety, but there’s no verified evidence that AI causes it, and no verified estimate of how many people it will actually replace. Whoever sells you “act before it’s too late” with a precise number is selling certainty the researchers don’t have.

What to do about it

A check for exploitation of your calling. When you’re asked for “a little more, for the sake of the work,” ask yourself: is it voluntary? paid? within my role? safe? does it leave room for rest? If the answer to even one is “no,” love of the work doesn’t replace pay and consent. (The judgments themselves have been studied in experiments; the protective power of this list has not been tested.)

A check on a goal: “mine or should.” Write the goal down. Then ask: do I stand behind it because it matters to me — or out of guilt and someone else’s pressure? Find one doable step and see whether the effort holds. This is an application of research on concordant goals6, not a protocol with a set dose. Sign it’s working: the effort holds without constant self-whipping.

Before a course or a coach — a contract. Before paying, pin down: what exactly I’ll get, how to measure it, the full price, refund terms, experience with this specific task, when we stop. Track behavior or a result visible from the outside, not how much you enjoy the sessions. Income testimonials are advertising until someone shows what everyone earned, not just the top earners.

When changing or losing a job — keep your supports. Deliberately preserve income, a daily routine, contact with people and roles outside work. If you have money and unpleasant chores, try a small purchase of time: in surveys and a small field experiment, this added happiness12. This isn’t a rule that “time always beats things,” especially when money is tight. Sign it’s working: you have an answer to “who am I?” that doesn’t include a job title.

Questions for yourself

  • If my position were taken away tomorrow, what would be left of my answer to “who am I”?
  • Whose approval am I really trying to earn?
  • Is someone using my love of the work in place of pay?
  • Do I want more money — or do I want to get ahead of specific people?

When to see a specialist

If loss of interest has spread beyond work, the low mood doesn’t lift on weekends or vacation, or thoughts of death appear — this isn’t “just burnout.” You need an assessment by a doctor or psychotherapist.

28. Doomscrolling: why you can’t put the feed down — and how to get your attention back

According to one working hypothesis, the feed holds you not through pleasure but through incompleteness: the next post might calm you, or it might frighten you again, and either way it keeps you scrolling. People often scroll not because they feel good, but because anxiety is looking for a place to stop and can’t find one. This is not a separate diagnosis and not a character flaw. It is a “check for threat” system running in an environment where the checking never ends.

How it works

The anxiety loop

Alarming news raises uncertainty. Checking promises clarity. The next item brings a new threat, and the cycle continues. This is a working hypothesis: it captures well how the body (arousal), feeling, meaning and action are linked, but the chain as a whole is not proven. There is no separate diagnosis called “doomscrolling.”

Doomscrolling can be measured: there are questionnaires, and their scores are linked to lower life satisfaction and worse well-being. But these are cross-sectional studies — one sample at one point in time, mostly young people. What is cause and what is effect is unknown: maybe the feed makes people feel worse, or maybe people who feel worse scroll more.

After the Boston Marathon bombing, those who watched the most coverage had higher acute stress1. This gave rise to the loud claim that “watching it on TV is worse than being there.” The data don’t support it: viewers were not randomly assigned, and a person in a bad state seeks out more news on their own.

Why the feed is dark

Threatening and morally charged content survives retelling better and spreads more widely on social media2, although there is no law that “bad wins” (more in chapter 26). In experiments with randomly assigned headlines, negative wording gets clicked more often, and a feed that selects content by reactions plausibly exploits this bias (not measured directly).

Dopamine: wanting, not enjoying

“A dopamine hit from a like” is an inaccurate phrase: dopamine signals a prediction error and gives a cue its pull — “I want” — while pleasure itself is produced mostly by other systems3. The “want” lever is powerful on its own: drugs that stimulate dopamine receptors can push people toward compulsive behavior4 (more in chapters 16 and 44).

Social media behavior follows the rules of reward learning: people post more often when their likes grow. In an experiment with a simulated Instagram, teenagers were more willing to like photos that already had many likes, including risky ones, and their brains’ reward and social-evaluation areas responded differently5. But what an MRI measures (blood flow) is not a measure of dopamine. Whether dopamine is released in response to a notification has never been measured in humans. Variable reward — maybe there’ll be something interesting, maybe not — is the same principle as a slot machine; infinite scroll plausibly amplifies it, but the separate contribution of each interface feature has not been measured.

Attention: interruption is real, the “goldfish” is not

A notification costs attention even if you don’t respond to it: in an experiment, people made more errors just from receiving the signal6. It grabs your attention, leaves an intention hanging, and then forces you to find your way back to the task. In a lab experiment with interruptions, people worked faster but with more stress: speed and well-being don’t necessarily rise together. That experiment gives no universal figure for “minutes lost per interruption.”

The famous “brain drain” experiment, in which the mere sight of a phone on the desk lowered mental performance, shrank when all the replications were pooled: a small but consistent negative effect remained only for working memory — the ability to hold several things in mind at once7. For other abilities there is no effect, or it is unstable.

Popular conclusions about the “multitasking generation” replicated poorly. Doing two things at once is one thing; being a person who says they often do so is another, and the second gets tangled up with other traits.

Who pays for the minute

A service gathers attention, turns it into impressions and sells advertisers access or a predicted response. For scale: Meta’s 2022 revenue was about $117 billion. That is not the price of your minute: ad effectiveness is measured very imprecisely even in large experiments. One thing is clear: the platform has a direct incentive to hold attention, while the evidence that the feed rewrites beliefs for the long term is weaker than commonly thought (how ideas multiply in people’s heads — chapter 26).

What this means for the self

Given — the body. Attention to threat, a craving for clarity, sensitivity to others’ judgment — these are old mechanisms of the tasks “safety” and “rank in the group.” In teenagers, the response to peer approval shows up in the likes experiment (chapter 16).

Built — habit and environment. A sound, a badge, the unlock gesture become cues that trigger checking before you’ve decided anything. The loop apparently builds up through repetition and environmental cues, not character. Hence the main point: change the environment rather than demand heroic willpower.

The ego — in the feed. The feed is a mirror where the self learns every minute whether it is seen, whether it is approved of, whether it belongs. Self-esteem can start to depend on a counter run by someone else’s algorithm. Scrolling the news also serves the self-image: “I’m informed, I’m on guard, I won’t miss anything.” It looks like a defense (a hypothesis, not measured): it gives a sense of control when there is no real control over the event. The cost: attention goes where nothing can be done and is missing where something can.

Where personal responsibility ends. If a job requires you to be reachable at all times, that is not a personal weakness but a condition of the job; what helps there is an agreement about availability, not a focus routine (on work and the fear of falling behind — chapter 27).

Freedom — in choosing what to replace. The main question is not “how much time do I spend on my phone” but “what did this hour push out.” The idea borrows a well-known error from economics — failing to count what a choice costs you — but as a method against the feed it has not been tested on its own. A person is neither an invulnerable rational observer nor passive feed for an algorithm. You can test specific changes on yourself and see which ones work.

Sex differences have not been established here. That most respondents in a Turkish sample on doomscrolling were women says who answered the survey, not who is more vulnerable. Most of the strong data come from the US; for Russia, China and older people, the available data include no comparable studies.

Myths

Myth: human attention has shrunk to 8 seconds — less than a goldfish’s. In fact: the figure comes from a report that attributed the goldfish to a third-party website; there was no “human versus goldfish” experiment, and the different kinds of attention (sustained, selective, switching) aren’t measured that way. Drop the myth rather than replacing it with another number.

Myth: phones cause addiction like a drug. In fact: some people really do lose control, and their lives suffer. But the figure of roughly one in four young people “addicted” by questionnaire reflects a survey cutoff in mostly cross-sectional studies, not a diagnosis8. The phone may be merely a delivery device for various activities or a way of coping with another problem. The “addict” label is especially inappropriate for teenagers.

Myth: a dopamine detox resets the reward system. In fact: the author of “dopamine fasting” himself wrote that the name shouldn’t be taken literally — it is a stimulus-control technique from behavioral therapy9. No study has measured a dopamine “reset.”

Myth: screens made teenagers unhappy. In fact: the average link between screen time and well-being is tiny, but that doesn’t rule out harm to vulnerable people (more in chapter 14).

Who plays on this

Platforms. Retention is their revenue: variable reward, infinite scroll, approval counters, selection by reactions (the contribution of each feature has not been measured).

Media. A negative headline gets clicked more often, and that is a commercial incentive (though not an explanation of every piece of bad news). The sign: the same threat arrives for the tenth time with no new facts and nothing you can do about it.

Stores and subscriptions. A large-scale review of shopping websites found interface tricks in widespread use11: hidden charges, obstacles to canceling, trick questions. In experiments, mild tricks more than doubled sign-ups, and aggressive ones raised them even further. Signs: a hidden price, extra steps to cancel, a trick question.

“Detox” sellers. They sell a “dopamine reset” and total-isolation protocols — with no evidence. How success and “strength” are sold — chapter 29.

What to do about it

Batch your notifications. Non-urgent ones — three times a day on a schedule; keep urgent channels (family, people in your care, work in emergency mode). Two weeks of this setup in a randomized experiment improved well-being and attention; turning notifications off completely may increase anxiety and fear of missing out12. Hourly batches helped less. Signs it’s working: fewer interruptions per day, less stress, nothing important lost.

Cut one habit, not everything at once. Pick one app or one family of apps. Limit it (10 minutes a day per platform for three weeks — this reduced loneliness and depression in students) or pause it for one to four weeks. Deactivating Facebook for a month in a large experiment produced a small improvement in mood10; according to a review of studies, a detox slightly reduces depression but doesn’t change stress or life satisfaction. The effects are small, tested on adults and students, and well-designed studies are few. Decide in advance where you’ll stay connected with the people who matter. Measure before and after: mood, sleep, getting things done — not just screen time.

Remove cues from your environment. Charge your phone outside the bedroom; while working, put it in another room. This is consistent with the mechanics of cue-based learning, but as a standalone recipe it hasn’t been tested: a cheap experiment, not a treatment. A grayscale screen reduced screen time in students; whether it makes anyone better off is unknown.

News in doses, not as a stream. If repeated alarming footage worsens your symptoms, cut back on it while keeping what you need for safety. A check question before each visit: what new thing will I learn, and what can I do with it? Science doesn’t know the “right” dose of news, especially during a real disaster.

Questions for yourself

  • What am I looking for when I open the feed for the third time in an hour: information, reassurance or approval?
  • What did today’s hour on my phone push out — and was that a choice or a habit?
  • How much of my self-esteem this week depended on the numbers under posts?
  • Which notification could I get once a day — with no loss at all?

When to see a specialist

If, despite your attempts, you can’t change the behavior, and sleep, work, responsibilities or relationships consistently suffer, the problem isn’t the hours — it’s a disrupted life. Anyone starting or increasing the dose of drugs that stimulate dopamine receptors should tell their doctor about new urges to gamble, shop, have sex or overeat.

29. Info-Hustlers and Gurus: How Success, “Feminine Energy” and “Masculine Power” Are Sold

Charisma can be trained: observers rate the speaker higher. No one has shown that accuracy of judgment rises along with it1. A whole market rests on this gap: it sells not a skill but a new self — rich, “full,” “alpha.” You pay for it less with money than with a self-image, one that later hurts to admit was a mistake.

How it works

Pain, explanation, membership, invoice

The scheme is the same for a success course, multi-level marketing and a “masculine power” training. Take an ordinary pain — loneliness, insecurity, money, sex, dissatisfaction with yourself. Give it a total explanation: “you don’t have a money mindset,” “you’re in your masculine energy,” “you’re a beta.” Offer a group where that explanation is shared, and a paid path upward. This is an analytical model, not a proven account of every participant’s path: there are no representative data on who joins and why.

There is a theory of why it works: under uncertainty, people are drawn to a group with clear boundaries and a clear answer to “who are we?” (uncertainty-identity theory — an explanation, not a test; more in chapter 25). On the fear of falling behind your peers, see chapter 27.

The denominator decides the story

The main trick of money promises is to show the peak and hide the base. In 2024 the US regulator examined 70 public income disclosures from multi-level marketing companies: many participants earned nothing, and the overwhelming majority earned under $1,000 a year2. Some disclosures left out those who earned nothing or counted only “active” members; none accounted for all expenses. These figures are before costs, not profit.

An independent survey gave a similar picture: 47% of former and current participants said they lost money, about a quarter broke even, another quarter came out ahead3. These are recollections, not bookkeeping. The popular figure “99% lose” is not supported by these data — what is supported is “most lose or get almost nothing.” Almost half first joined between the ages of 18 and 25.

There is a cost to the self, too. The business was pitched to friends, so admitting it made no money means admitting a mistake in front of them. The financial distortion is visible in the documents; this psychological chain is plausible but not proven for everyone.

Regulators draw different lines. In China, licensed direct selling has been legally separated from banned pyramid schemes since 2005. In 2024 the Bank of Russia identified about 9,000 projects showing signs of illegal financial activity — but these are not 9,000 MLM companies or online schools. There is no reliable estimate of losses to the info-product business specifically, either in Russia or worldwide.

Courses and coaching: where the real benefit is

Coaching is not empty. A coach provides goals, feedback and accountability; at work this helps moderately on average. That is what pooled analyses of studies show — one of them limited to randomized trials — though with a risk of bias toward published successes4. In one review the effect was slightly larger in women, but how much larger has not been tested; you cannot conclude from this that “women need it more.”

The benefit depends on a bounded goal: a specific work skill, a measurable behavior. “Turn your life around,” “make your first million,” “become yourself” are not goals but promises, and there are no data on them. When an income promise is unsupported, it becomes a legal matter: in a case against an American online school that sold an “internet business” course, buyers got back more than $2.4 million. This is an example, not an estimate of how many courses deceive.

Manifestation: action works, not the Universe

A dream can set a direction. Then the road forks. One branch is mental contrasting: picture what you want, then name the main obstacle right now and write an “if X happens, I will do Y” plan. A meta-analysis (a summary of many studies) found a modest gain in goal attainment, with a caveat about selective publication5. The presumed mechanism is behavioral: the obstacle is linked to an action in advance.

Positive fantasy without an obstacle is a different intervention. In a school experiment, children were taught either to link an obstacle to an action or simply to think about good things, and grades, attendance and behavior were compared; the mechanism itself was not tested directly. In three studies, belief in “attraction” went along with risky investments and past bankruptcies6 — a self-reported association, not proof that belief ruins you. For vision boards and “money mindset” courses there are no effect estimates at all.

“Feminine energy” and “masculine power”

The gender guru works like the money guru but sells sex instead of money. An ordinary problem — loneliness, doubts about sex, dissatisfaction — is declared a lack of “femininity” or “masculinity,” and the product is the path to it.

“Feminine energy” has never been described as a physiological fact. When marketing tries to tie it to the body, it runs into the law: a well-known brand paid a $145,000 penalty for unsupported claims about jade and quartz eggs — claims about hormones, the cycle, the bladder and depression7. This is a ruling about advertising, not a study of harm.

“Masculine power” through abstaining from ejaculation follows the same pattern: there are no trials showing a sustained rise in testosterone and energy from abstinence, and the paper on a “testosterone peak on day seven” was retracted (more in chapter 49). And testosterone is no button for confidence, morality or success in the first place (for how hormones actually work, see chapter 15).

Neither “energy” nor abstinence guarantees how a partner will behave. The advice “put them down to attract them” has been studied only for how people perceive it — and they perceive it badly; no one has shown whether it works. The claim that “women by nature always seek higher status” has been refuted: preferences depend on context, and as women’s education rises, “marrying up” in education becomes rarer.

The “men’s rights” and “incel” sphere — a market and a network, not a diagnosis

An analysis of tens of millions of posts on forums and subreddits showed a shift: the older pickup communities and the men’s rights movement are being crowded out by newer and more toxic spaces of “incels” and “men going their own way”10. This is about content and account migration, not about how many boys watch it or what a single video does to a person. The main open question is what comes first: does a person arrive already resentful, harden through engagement, or both? Counting posts cannot tell these apart.

In one comparison, “incels” themselves showed higher depression but not higher anxiety or stress; which is cause and which is effect is unknown. The claim “they are lonely because women are too picky” is unproven and dangerous.

Mirror markets — women’s “dating strategies,” “traditional wife” content — also lack data showing any benefit for relationships. The same business structure does not mean the same effects.

Typologies: recognition is not prediction

A vague, flattering description (“sometimes you’re the life of the party, sometimes you need to be alone”) feels like an accurate portrait of you — this is the Barnum, or Forer, effect (more in chapter 48).

Then the label organizes the self and belonging: “I’m an INTJ,” “I’m a Four,” “I’m a Generator.” The sense that it helps is real; the accuracy is not. MBTI types have not been validated as a tool for hiring or choosing a partner11. A review of more than a hundred Enneagram samples found mixed reliability and usually fewer than the nine promised factors. A double-blind test of astrology did not confirm natal charts. Graphology on neutral texts predicts almost nothing. For Human Design and numerology, no sufficient independent tests were found. Measuring personality on scales (the Big Five, for example) is a different story, one with data behind it — but it is not destiny either.

Levers of persuasion

Scarcity, social proof and authority are levers of moderate effect, not buttons; authority lets the speaker’s status stand in for checking the claim (more in chapter 45). Knowing the techniques protects only partly — see chapter 47.

What this means for the self

Given: the need to belong, to have a rank in the group, to find and keep a mate, to understand what you live for. These ancient tasks cannot be switched off. Also given is a vulnerability to uncertainty: according to the theory, uncertainty pulls you toward a clear explanation, even an unverified one.

Built: the market sells not a method but an identity. With the purchase, a person gets a label (“I’m in my masculine energy,” “I’m an entrepreneur,” “I’m an Eight”) and a group that confirms it. The label is built into the self-image — and the ego starts defending it. Hence a likely trap (an inference, not a measurement): money spent on the self is harder to write off as lost than money spent on a thing. Admitting the course didn’t work means admitting that the person you take yourself to be was wrong. So failure is easier to rewrite as “I didn’t believe enough,” “I didn’t work through my blocks,” “I haven’t grown into the next level yet.” This defense is exactly what the market monetizes — with the next, more expensive level.

Charisma tightens the trap from the other side. Confident delivery, a vivid frame and the group’s shared language make the speaker persuasive, not accurate. The student’s ego readily confuses the two: if the teacher is sure, then I am safe.

Where the freedom is: pull apart three questions the market glues together. Is the person persuasive? Is the claim true? Does the method improve exactly the promised result? Only the last two justify a decision about money and health. Self-esteem then rests not on a label but on what you did and what changed in the world outside. This is not a rejection of growth: working on yourself is real when the goal is bounded and checkable (for what actually helps, see chapter 46). Leaning on a group is not the enemy either: the question is not whether it is religious, secular or commercial, but whether you chose it, whether there are alternatives, and what the consequences are.

Myths

Myth: A top distributor shows how much a newcomer will earn. In fact: disclosures drop the zero earners and stay silent about expenses; most get under $1,000 a year before costs2.

Myth: If you picture success vividly enough, it will be attracted to you. In fact: what helps is imagination plus an obstacle plus an “if–then” plan, and the effect is modest5; belief in attraction is linked to risky investments but not proven to cause them6.

Myth: NLP is an effective therapy. In fact: in a review of ten small studies, of the five randomized ones, four found no advantage and one did12.

Myth: A confident, charismatic teacher is an expert. In fact: training raises charisma ratings, but a rise in accuracy and honesty has not been shown1.

Who plays on this

Multi-level marketing — through stories of the “top earners” instead of the median of everyone who joined, and through pressure to recruit friends. Online schools and success coaches — through income screenshots, the promise of total transformation, a ladder of ever more expensive levels, and explaining failure by your “blocks.” Gender gurus on both sides — by turning an ordinary problem into a lack of “energy” or “power,” and through medical promises without medical data. “Male supremacy” networks — through the link “your pain = membership,” hierarchy inside the group, shame, entitlement to someone else’s body, and coercion. Typologies and esoterica — when the label starts being used for hiring, choosing a partner or “typing” a child. The common sign: they demand dependence but offer no exit terms and no measurable result.

What to do about it
