  1. Calculate the denominator before buying. Ask for the median across everyone who joined, including the zero earners, subtract expenses and divide by hours. If there are no such figures, the income promise is unsupported. Sign it’s working: you decide by a spreadsheet, not by someone else’s story.
  2. Buy a service with boundaries. A specific result, evidence, the full price, refund terms, qualifications for exactly this task, and a stopping rule (“if there’s no such change by such a date, I’m out”). It works if you track behavior or an external result, not your satisfaction with the guru.
  3. Dream by the scheme. Wish → best outcome → main obstacle right now → “if X, then Y.” Track the steps taken and the external results, not the strength of your belief. Failure is a signal to revise the plan, not your “purity.”
  4. Before a large expense, build in a pause and a second voice. Call back on a number you looked up yourself, compare with an alternative, ask someone outside the group, wait. Knowing the techniques protects only partly; a pause and a second voice are sensible insurance, though their effect hasn’t been measured (on how ideas and anger spread from person to person, see chapter 26).

Questions for yourself

  • What exactly am I buying: a skill or a new self-image?
  • How do I explain my past failure with a program like this — and who benefits from that explanation?
  • Does this label set me apart from most people — or does it just sound nice?
  • What should change outside, and by when, for me to admit it isn’t working?

When to see a specialist

If fatigue, erection problems or low mood persist, and instead of a medical workup you are offered abstinence, “energy” or a course — see a doctor. If a group cuts you off from loved ones, humiliates you, demands more and more money or justifies pressure on someone else’s body — this is no longer self-development; you need help from outside (on high-control groups — chapter 25).

30. The couple as a system: what a partner does to your hormones and your self

Prairie voles, the little animals that taught us the “chemistry of fidelity,” still form pairs and care for their young without the oxytocin receptor. There is no “love hormone”: the chemistry in a couple adjusts to life together rather than switching it on. Yet after a breakup, self-image loses clarity: part of the self had rested on the other person. That is what makes a couple both useful and dangerous.

How it works

The body adjusts to life together

Living together changes sleep, routine, how often you have sex, where stress comes from and how much you care for someone else. All of this can shift hormones. The feedback runs the other way too: hormonal makeup and personality influence who ends up in which relationships. Untangling these two directions is the central difficulty of the whole topic.

The clearest example comes from the Philippines: in the same men, testosterone dropped after they became fathers1. This is not a disease and not a loss of “masculinity”; more in chapter 32.

Partners’ bodily states can shift in sync — heart rate, tension, stress hormones. In newlywed couples, cortisol (the stress hormone) profiles converged over the first years of marriage2. This convergence proves neither mutual understanding, nor a healthy bond, nor an “exchange of energy”: often two people are simply living through the same events. And stress in one partner doesn’t always “infect” the other.

Even sleep changes: healthy young couples who shared a bed got roughly 10% more REM sleep (one study). Separate beds don’t harm the relationship, though: this is a sleep measure, not a condition for closeness.

Oxytocin: not the love hormone

Oxytocin is made by the hypothalamus, a deep brain region that governs hormone release. Two roles are proven: contractions in childbirth and milk release. Everything else is murkier. One hypothesis: oxytocin regulates how salient social signals are — faces, voices, touch — working in tandem with dopamine. Hence the key point: the effect depends on the person and the situation. The same shift can heighten both warmth toward your own people and wariness toward strangers (in experiments on men — defensive, not offensive, aggression toward an outgroup; one study).

The “trust hormone” story began with a 2005 experiment: students given intranasal oxytocin more often handed all their money to a “partner.” Later checks dismantled it. A replication with a preregistered plan and a large sample found no effect3; a 2026 paper concluded the effect is too small to matter. Most intranasal oxytocin experiments are too small, so most published findings are probably not real. Very little of a nasal dose appears to reach the cerebrospinal fluid.

Nor can “love” be measured with a lab test. At rest, oxytocin in the blood is barely related to oxytocin in the brain (a correlation of about 0.08, meaning almost none)4. Older assay kits also picked up unrelated molecules. A level in blood or saliva is not a bond meter.

Even in prairie voles — the classic animal that taught us the “chemistry of fidelity” — animals without the oxytocin receptor still formed pairs, gave birth and cared for their young5. Transferring the gene for the vasopressin receptor (a related hormone) into the brain of a promiscuous vole species strengthened partner preference — but these are rodents, not people. In humans, one twin sample linked a variant of this gene to pair-bonding traits in men; the finding has not been confirmed by replication.

Oxytocin rises with arousal and orgasm in both sexes, so “sex bonds women but not men” does not follow from the chemistry. Oxytocin and vasopressin changed the brain’s response during a social game differently in men and in women (one study). Love engages the reward system, but that doesn’t make it literally a drug addiction: the warning sign is loss of control and harm, not the strength of the feeling.

Touch works, but without magic numbers

Hugging a partner before a stressful task reduced cortisol release in women but not in men6. Warm touch before stress lowered cortisol and heart rate in women, while blood oxytocin didn’t change. A four-week warm-touch program for spouses lowered stress markers. In adults, frequent hugs accounted for about a third of the protective effect of social support against catching a cold (self-report, an association). A synthesis of touch experiments shows a noticeable benefit, but blinded trials are impossible here, and small studies inflate the effect. Nobody has tested “twenty seconds”: no study has ever varied hug duration.

Hormonal contraception: one person’s chemistry inside a couple

Combined pills suppress ovulation, flatten the monthly cycle of sex hormones, lower free testosterone and raise the protein that binds it. How this affects mood and desire is unknown.

Mood. In Danish registries (about a million women aged 15–34), those on combined pills had roughly a quarter higher risk of starting antidepressants, and adolescents aged 15–19 had 1.8 times the risk, peaking about six months after starting7. This is an association, not a proven cause. In the only placebo-controlled trial in healthy women, the pill slightly lowered general well-being — energy and self-control — over three months, without an increase in depressive symptoms. Bottom line: on average a small downward shift, concentrated in adolescents and the first months, with wide variation between people; for some, mood actually improves.

Desire. In a review of studies, 85% of combined-pill users reported the same or greater desire, the rest less. Loss of interest is more often linked to injections, the ring and the implant (compared with the copper IUD; an association, not a cause).

Partner choice. The hypothesis: the pill flattens cycle-related shifts in taste, and a partner chosen while on the pill seems less attractive after stopping it. Of four samples tested, three showed nothing8, and the largest long-term study found that taste in faces doesn’t track hormones at all. The mechanism is unproven. Brain scans show differences in some regions in pill users; what they mean is unknown.

The self expands through another

The self-expansion model: a partner opens access to new activities, views, people and resources — and they become part of “me.” In experiments, shared novel activities improved relationship quality in the short term; how much, and how often, is not established.

The “Michelangelo effect”: a partner who supports your own ideal self helps you move toward it. The key is whose ideal. Supporting a goal the person chose themselves and sculpting them to the partner’s taste are opposite things.

Partners tend to be similar rather than complementary: “opposites attract” doesn’t hold as a general rule. Similarity can exist before meeting, come from a shared environment or grow over time — and on its own doesn’t prove that a similar partner brings more happiness. A synthesis of 20 studies found no evidence that intercultural couples are on average less satisfied with their relationship than same-culture couples.

Influence runs both ways: among young Germans followed for four years, personality and relationships changed each other. This doesn’t mean a first partner “makes you an adult” or that being single stunts development. Relationship satisfaction on average declines after the first child in both partners — but it also declines in childless newlyweds married just as long9. Conflict in parents’ marriage is linked to more conflict in their adult children’s marriages (one long-term study; an association, not a proven cause) — perhaps this is one way a model of a couple gets passed on too.

After a breakup, the content of self-image changes and its clarity drops — the answer to “who am I” becomes blurry10. Grief for a person and a temporary loss of a coherent story about yourself are different processes. This doesn’t mean it happens to everyone, or forever.

Jealousy: a signal easily turned into a lever

The sequence: a perceived threat to the couple → bodily arousal → the meaning “I could be left” → action, from a conversation to surveillance. The partner’s response can calm things down, ignite a fight or push toward hiding. This is a map for understanding, not proof that jealousy by itself leads to any particular act.

The famous “women are hurt more by emotional infidelity, men by sexual” depends heavily on how you ask: a forced choice of “which is worse” and rating each infidelity on a scale give different results. In thousands of Swedish twin pairs, men reacted relatively more strongly to sexual infidelity; the heritability of jealousy is about a third, the same in both sexes11. In a representative Australian sample, the evolutionary explanation was partly supported in heterosexuals and not supported in everyone else. An attractive female rival stings women slightly more than an attractive male rival stings men; the classic “men are stung more by a dominant rival” did not replicate. Average differences don’t mean anyone is indifferent to the “other” kind of infidelity.

Jealousy about a partner’s social media predicts electronic surveillance, and surveillance predicts lower relationship satisfaction a year later12. These are not interchangeable forms of “caring”: affection and asking for reassurance are one thing; banning friends, demanding passwords and secret tracking are another. There is no evidence that surveillance strengthens relationships.

Fear of being alone predicts that a person stays longer in unsatisfying relationships, leaves on their own less often and is less selective about partners. This is a prediction, not proof that the fear itself creates a bad match.

What this means for the self

The body is given and constantly retuned. A partner becomes part of the environment the brain uses to calibrate sleep, stress and sex hormones. A testosterone shift after a child’s birth and converging cortisol are neither a verdict nor a failure, but traces of life together. Chemistry here is context, not a button: no hormone switches love on or excuses infidelity.

Ties are the second mirror after parents. The first “we” was with whoever raised you (chapter 06); a couple is the second major “we.” A partner can expand the self or shrink it. The ego easily builds the couple into the self-image and the mask for others: “I’m the one who was chosen,” “we’re a successful couple.” Self-esteem can come to rest on one person’s gaze. Perhaps this is where jealousy gets its force: it defends not only the bond but also the self’s place in its own story.

In a couple, the ego’s defenses show in actions. Jealousy is a feeling; control is an act. Surveillance, bans, punishment for refusing access are control, whatever feeling stands behind them; perhaps it’s an attempt to drown out the fear of abandonment at the other’s expense (a hypothesis). Fear of being alone works differently: a person settles for less just to keep the role of “someone in a couple.”

The story is where freedom lies. After a breakup the clarity of self-image drops — apparently part of the self rested on the role. So the self can be assembled deliberately: which roles and activities the partner added, which remain yours without them. The main lever is the question “whose ideal?”: relationships that support your own goal expand the self; relationships that sculpt you to someone else’s taste replace it.

Myths

Myth: oxytocin is the hormone of love and trust; more oxytocin means a stronger bond. In fact: preregistered replications found no effect on trust3, blood levels barely reflect the brain4, and voles without the oxytocin receptor still form pairs5.

Myth: hormones determine behavior in a couple — “testosterone makes people cheat.” In fact: hormones themselves change with life together, as in the Filipino fathers1. This argues against the rule “one hormone, one act,” and hormones excuse nothing.

Myth: the pill either harms no one or depresses everyone and makes you pick the wrong partner. In fact: on average a small mood shift, stronger in adolescents and in the first months7; the “wrong partner” hypothesis failed in three of four tests8.

Myth: a child will hold a cracking relationship together. In fact: satisfaction after a child’s birth on average declines in both partners9 — more in chapter 32.

Who plays on this

The “oxytocin” market. “Trust hormone” sprays lean on a story science has already taken apart. The tell: a promise to produce trust or attachment with chemistry.

Phone spyware. Commercial secret-tracking apps exist; there are no reliable data on how widespread they are. The tell: a product that works only as long as the other person doesn’t know about it.

The loneliness market. Fear of being alone lowers selectivity13; on money made from loneliness — chapter 22; on gurus of “feminine energy” and “masculine power” — chapter 29.

What to do about it

Standards before attachment. Define your minimum requirements for a partner (for example, respect for a “no”) before attachment grows strong. Don’t move the bar for a particular person.

A pause in a fight — by agreement. In the lab, even pauses of 5–15 seconds reduced aggression between partners in a competitive task. In an ordinary conflict, agree in advance: who calls a pause, how, and when you return to the conversation. Sign it’s working: fights escalate less often. Sign it isn’t: the pause has turned into punishing silence or disappearing.

Warmth before something hard. A hug or touch from someone you trust — before a stressful event, not after. Ask first: unwanted touch is itself a threat. Don’t count the seconds.

Mood when starting the pill — on paper. A short daily mood log for two cycles before starting and three months after is a sensible self-check (not tested as a method by anyone). If mood drops noticeably, talk to a doctor about switching methods rather than abruptly dropping pregnancy protection.

Structured couple work. For couples with recurring difficulties there is an eight-hour online program, tested in a trial on US couples (self-report); it helped most those who already had personal difficulties.

Questions for yourself

  • Whose ideal self does my partner support — mine or theirs?
  • What would remain of my self if the role of being “in a couple” were removed?
  • Is my jealousy a feeling, or has it already become a right to control something?
  • Am I holding on to the relationship out of fear of being alone rather than by choice?

When to see a specialist

Secret surveillance, threats, interference with work and friendships, punishment for refusing access — this is control; if retaliation is possible, start with a plan for physical and digital safety, not with exposure. If the weight of it keeps you from eating, sleeping, working or caring for loved ones, or if your mood dropped sharply after starting the pill — you need in-person help. A threat to harm yourself or others calls for urgent local help, without waiting.

31. Having children: the biological clocks of men and women — without panic and without fairy tales

The best-known fertility figure — “after 35, one woman in three won’t get pregnant within a year” — has been traced back to French parish records from the eighteenth century1. The clocks are real, though, and they run in both sexes; they just don’t work the way the horror stories claim. Eggs age through declining quality, sperm through copying errors, and the decision about children is made not in the body but where the body, the couple, money and your story about yourself meet. This is where the self easily hands the wheel to fear, to advertising or to someone else’s “that’s how it’s done.”

How it works

Wanting, planning, giving birth — three different things

The wish for a child becomes a birth through a chain of filters: whether there is a partner, health, contraception, income, housing, someone to provide care, and whether the wish itself has changed. So “I want children,” “I want them soon” and “children were eventually born” are different variables. Government money can shift the timing without changing how many children a person ultimately wants. A review by economists stresses childcare and how well work and family fit together, rather than any single cash lever.

The egg: a clock of quality

The supply of follicles (the small sacs in which eggs mature) is laid down before birth and dwindles throughout life. But for pregnancy, quality matters more than number: in an aging egg, chromosomes separate with errors during division. Three consequences follow: conception takes more cycles, more embryos fail to implant or are lost, and at some point no normal embryos remain. In embryo biopsies during IVF (in vitro fertilization), a cycle without a single chromosomally normal embryo occurred in a few percent of cases at ages 26–37, a third at 42 and half at 44 (clinic patients only)2. Chromosomal errors are irreversible: lifestyle can improve the environment in which eggs mature, but it cannot turn back the chromosomes’ age.

Under natural conditions the picture is gentler than on the posters. In a European cohort of couples who kept a diary of cycles and intercourse, complete infertility stayed at about one percent at any age. The share not conceiving within 12 cycles was 8% at 19–26 and 18% at 35–39; roughly half of these “infertile” couples conceived in the second year3. In a North American cohort of online volunteers, the chance per cycle fell by about two fifths by 37–39 and by more than half by 40–454. The curve declines gently until the mid-thirties and steeply from about 37; there is no cliff at 35.

Miscarriage is the same mechanism showing up later. Across all pregnancies in Norway over five years, the risk was lowest at 25–29 (about 10%), rose quickly after 30 and reached half at 45 and older; it is also higher after previous miscarriages. Registries miss the earliest losses, so the real shares are higher.

The final growth of a follicle before ovulation takes about three months. That is the window in which deficiencies can be corrected — but chromosomal age cannot be rewritten.

Where “one in three” comes from

Demographers in the 1980s used historical data precisely because modern data are distorted by contraception, and they saw only a moderate rise in infertility until the late thirties. The figure is often attributed to a 2004 model, but that is a calculation, not a measurement: in it, three quarters of those who start trying at 30 have a live birth within a year, two thirds at 35 and fewer than half at 40. “One in three” matches this model for live birth at 35, overstates conception failure among modern couples (18%) and sits in the middle for cohorts that don’t time intercourse. The honest wording is “from one in five to one in three, depending on what you count.”

Sperm: a clock of copying errors

Here the mechanism is different. The stem cells that produce sperm divide throughout life, and every copy of DNA is a chance for error. In Icelandic families, each year of the father’s age added about two new mutations to the child5. The mother adds some too, but noticeably fewer, and even when fathers are young, they contribute about three times as many new mutations. Rare diseases of “paternal age” (achondroplasia, for example) arise differently: mutant precursor cells in the aging testis gain a growth advantage and expand as a clone.

Sperm quality also declines with age: volume, motility, the share of normal forms, DNA integrity — but not concentration (a synthesis of dozens of studies). The effect on time to conception is disputed: in one cohort, the man’s age began to interfere from the late thirties; in another, after adjusting for the partner’s age, no effect was found (there were few men over 45). Bottom line: the decline is real, but much smaller than in women.

Risks to the child have to be read in absolute numbers. In a large cohort from five countries, fathers 50+ had a higher risk of autism in their children than fathers aged 20–29; in absolute terms, roughly 0.9% instead of 0.5%, so the overwhelming majority of these fathers’ children receive no diagnosis6. The curve is U-shaped: mothers under 20 and over 40 show a similar small increase. Part of the link with mental disorders, according to geneticists’ models, is explained not by new mutations but by a shared inherited predisposition that itself delays fatherhood. Paternal age on its own is not a reason for invasive prenatal testing.

The maturation cycle of a sperm cell is about 64 days. Lifestyle in the 2–3 months before conception can change sperm; in humans, marks on sperm DNA shift with weight, smoking and cannabis, but what this means for the child has so far been shown only in rats and mice.

The brain shuts down the factory: testosterone and steroids

The testes work on signals from the pituitary. The brain reads any external androgen as “there’s enough testosterone” and withdraws those signals, so sperm production stops. In studies of hormonal male contraception, sperm concentration returned to normal after a median of three and a half months, in nine out of ten within a year, and in all within two7. After long anabolic cycles it takes longer: in a small sample of men with past steroid use, half had no sperm in their ejaculate, and stopping often brought months of low testosterone, weak libido and depressive episodes.

Technology: the clocks don’t stop

IVF does not cancel age, because it works with the same eggs. In UK data, the live-birth rate after a first cycle is about one in three under 40, roughly one in eight at 40–42 and very low after 42; with donor eggs there is no age-related decline. By one model’s estimate, IVF recovers only half of the births lost by delaying from 30 to 35, and less than a third when delaying from 35 to 40.

Egg freezing is partial insurance. In a synthesis of ten studies, about one in nine women who froze their eggs returned to use them, and a little more than a quarter of those who returned had a child: more than half if they froze before 35, and one in five if they froze at 40 or later8. You need at least 8–10 mature eggs, ideally 15. In one clinic, among those who returned, fresh stimulation worked no worse than thawing. The UK regulator states outright that it cannot publish reliable success figures for frozen eggs.

AMH is a hormone made by small growing follicles. It shows how many are left and how many eggs stimulation will yield, but not their quality and not the chance of conceiving. Among women aged 30–44 without infertility, the chance of getting pregnant within six months with low AMH barely differed from the chance with normal AMH — about two thirds in both groups9. Age predicts better.

What this means for the self

Given. The age of your reproductive cells is neither a merit nor a fault. Chromosome-separation errors in eggs and copying errors in sperm accumulate regardless of character, achievements or self-belief. This is a layer of the body that doesn’t negotiate.

Built. Timing is a product of ties: the partner, work, housing, who will provide care, and what counts as “on time” in your family and country. The average age of fathers in the US rose by three and a half years from 1972 to 2015.

Story — where the ego works loudest. Fertility easily becomes a verdict on yourself: “am I a complete woman,” “am I a real man.” An AMH number or a semen analysis starts to sound like a sentence on the person, though it is a measure of an organ. Here the ego easily slips into three defenses (a logical classification, not measured proportions of people). Denial: “men have no clock,” “my AMH is good — I’ve got time.” The illusion of control: frozen eggs or future IVF as a mask of “I’ve got everything under control.” Catastrophe: “after 35 it’s too late” — a fear that pushes you to decide just to have decided.

Myths gather on both sides of the choice. In a representative survey in Michigan, people who were childfree by choice did not differ, after adjustment, from others in life satisfaction — the data show no inevitable unhappiness. In two Polish samples, about one parent in ten regretted parenthood; this is not evidence that they don’t love their child, and it is unclear what is cause and what is effect. The happiness gap between parents and non-parents depends on the country and on family-support policy. None of these figures predicts any one person’s fate.

Freedom. You don’t choose the clocks. You choose which questions you answer in your own words and which in someone else’s. The decision about children honestly breaks down into four parts: do I want this, what support will there be, how much uncertainty can I bear, and who will pay the irreversible price. Anything beyond that is someone else’s voice, and it helps to recognize it. What a child does to parents’ brains and lives is the subject of chapter 32; how a couple changes hormones and the self, of chapter 30; what happens after the reproductive years end, of chapter 38.

Myths

Myth: after 35 it’s a cliff. In fact: a gentle decline that gets steeper from about 37; about 18% of couples tracking their cycle fail to conceive within a year at 35–39, and about half of them conceive in the second year.

Myth: a good AMH means you can wait. In fact: AMH did not predict natural conception in women aged 30–44; miscarriages and chromosomal errors rise with age regardless of it.

Myth: egg freezing is an insurance policy. In fact: one woman in nine returns to use her eggs, a little more than a quarter of those who return have a child, and if the eggs were frozen at 40 or later, about one in five of those who return.

Myth: a man can become a father at any age at no cost. In fact: with age, sperm quality falls and the number of new mutations rises; the risks to the child are real but small in absolute numbers.

Who plays on this

Clinics and the egg-freezing market. In the US, the number of egg-freezing cycles grew more than tenfold from 2012 to 2022. The largest UK clinics present it as a “new technology,” a “solution for modern life” and “control over aging”; prices are unclear, information is one-sided, and none fully complied with advertising rules10. After two large tech companies began paying for employees’ egg freezing in 2014, the American press painted a “simplified and rosy” picture. In a sample of Instagram bloggers’ videos, most praised the procedure, and only a handful mentioned costs. The sign: they talk about control and freedom but give no figure for live births per thawed cycle by age.

Sellers of home tests. Three quarters of websites selling AMH tests directly to consumers (27 in the sample) promised it would show the chance of getting pregnant11. The sign: a measure of an organ is presented as the answer to “do I still have time.”

The state. By a model-based estimate, Russia’s maternity capital added only a small fraction of a child per woman in the long run (about a sixth)12 — a model calculation, not an experiment. Payments can shift timing without the same rise in the final number of children; this is an open question. The sign of a lever: the “when” decision is nudged with money toward the payment date, not toward your readiness.

The testosterone market and loud headlines. “Sperm counts have halved” comes from pooled analyses that are contested: in cohorts using a single measurement method, the decline is weaker or not visible. The danger is the reaction to the headline: testosterone for “male vigor” in someone who wants children shuts down sperm production for months. How “male vigor” and “feminine energy” are sold — in chapter 29.

What to do about it

When to get tested: by age, not by anxiety. No pregnancy after 12 months of regular intercourse under 35, or after 6 months from 35, means it’s time to see a doctor, and both partners should go together. The sign it’s working: the decision is made on facts, not on a news item in the feed.

Preparation over three months. This rests on the sperm data, since the maturation cycle is about two months: in the 2–3 months before trying, a normal weight, no smoking, no heavy drinking or cannabis, and no testosterone or anabolic steroids (what this changes in the child’s health is not proven). The sign: a semen analysis before and after three months by WHO standards (remembering that a single test fluctuates a lot), and time to conception.

If you’re thinking about freezing. Do it before 35–37. Ask the clinic for its own figures: mature eggs per cycle at your age, survival after thawing, live births per thawed cycle. The target is 8–15 mature eggs. And set a date right away for when you’ll start trying or go for fresh IVF. The sign it’s working: freezing didn’t push that date back.

The decision — in writing, in four columns. Do I want this; what support actually exists; how much uncertainty can I bear; who will pay the irreversible price. The sign: each column holds your own words, not a quote from your parents, advertising or the state.

Questions for yourself

  • Do I want a child — or do I want to stop hearing the question “when”?
  • Which number about my body has become a verdict on me as a person?
  • What am I putting off until “later” — and do I know what that “later” costs at my age?
  • If no one had ever asked, would my answer be the same?

When to see a specialist

No pregnancy after 12 months under 35 or after 6 months from 35; several miscarriages in a row; past courses of testosterone or anabolic steroids in someone planning a child — especially with low mood and lost libido after stopping.

32. Becoming a parent: how a child rebuilds the brain and life of mother and father

In a small study, brain scans alone were enough to tell which women had been pregnant: gray-matter changes after a first birth last at least two years. A father’s brain changes too, even though he has no pregnancy hormones, and his testosterone drops by a quarter to a third. A child rebuilds all three layers of the self in both parents at once: body, ties and the story you tell about yourself.

How it works

The mother’s brain: remodeling, not loss

According to the proposed mechanism, the hormonal surge of a first pregnancy reduces gray-matter volume (the bodies of nerve cells) in areas of social understanding — the ones that process other people’s intentions and thinking about yourself and others. After birth, these same areas respond to your own baby, and the size of the change predicts the mother’s attachment to the child1. This looks more like the remodeling the brain goes through in adolescence than like damage. The shift lasts at least two years, and in a small group rescanned six years later it largely persisted (data are weak). Right after birth, volume partly recovers; in a second pregnancy the shift is similar but smaller.

“Mommy brain” is a real complaint with a small and inconsistent objective effect. In a review of twenty studies, memory and executive functions (planning, holding a task in mind) were worse in the third trimester, but the spread was wide; in a careful study that followed one group of women, tests showed no difference either at the end of pregnancy or three months after birth — only self-rated memory and mood were worse2. Sleep loss and mood affect the results. It has not been shown that the gray-matter change impairs thinking. What purpose it ultimately serves is also unknown.

The father’s brain and hormones: remodeled by caregiving

A father has no pregnancy hormones, so the candidates are different: the experience of caregiving and a drop in testosterone. Over the first months, fathers gained gray matter in the hypothalamus (the hormone regulator), the amygdala (the center for fast appraisal of threat and significance) and the striatum (part of the reward system), and lost it in other areas. In expectant fathers, as in mothers, volume shrank in the network tied to thoughts about yourself and others. Each sample has fewer than twenty men, so this is still a sketch.

The strongest hormone data come from the Philippines: hundreds of young men were followed for four and a half years. Those with higher testosterone were more likely to become fathers, and once they did, they lost on average (median) about a quarter of their morning and a third of their evening testosterone — most of all those who spent three or more hours a day with the child3. The link runs both ways: the hormone affects who pairs up, and partnership and caregiving affect the hormone. A review of dozens of studies confirms it: fathers and partnered men have lower testosterone (this is an association, not a proven cause). In a small sample of couples, fathers whose testosterone dropped more even before birth invested more in the child afterward. The takeaway is not “the man gets weaker”; rather, it seems the body shifts part of its resources from finding a mate to caregiving. More on hormones in the couple in chapter 30.

Sleep: the main hit to the body

A newborn does not tell day from night: its circadian rhythm is weak and takes shape after two months, and light is one of the main cues. Night sleep consolidates mostly between the first and fourth months: by three months, half of babies sleep from midnight to 5 a.m., yet at one year, one in seven still can’t sleep through that stretch. Between ages one and two, waking up to two and a half times a night is within the normal range, not a malfunction.

Parents’ sleep dips most in the first three months. In a large German sample, mothers lost about an hour of sleep a night, fathers a little over ten minutes; breastfeeding adds a little more loss4. The sex difference here is large: a strong effect in mothers, a weak one in fathers (fathers’ distributions before and after nearly overlap). Sleep had not fully returned to its pre-pregnancy level even six years later (this is the parents’ own report, and further children were born in those years). Sleep loss worsens mood, bad mood worsens sleep, and the loop closes.

Mood: from tears to the emergency room

Tears and mood swings after birth (the “baby blues”) affect many mothers, about two in five, and are a separate condition. Postpartum depression is something else: it affects about 17% of mothers and 8–10% of fathers, and it clusters within couples (if one partner is depressed, the other is more often depressed too)5. Rates vary about threefold by region — from European studies to the Middle East. In mothers, the most severe cases — those requiring hospitalization — peak on days 10–19 after birth; in fathers, fatherhood did not raise the risk of hospitalization, and their depression is mostly treated on an outpatient basis.

Postpartum psychosis is rare (fractions of a case per thousand births) and a threat to the lives of mother and child. According to reviews, it is triggered by shifts in hormones, immunity and circadian rhythms in vulnerable women; in some, bipolar disorder develops later.

Frightening thoughts are the most hidden part. A new parent’s brain scans intensely for threats to a helpless infant and produces vivid unwanted images. Thoughts of accidental harm occur in nearly all mothers, thoughts of harming the baby on purpose in nearly half. In a large sample of mothers, aggression toward the child was equally rare (about 3%) among those who had such thoughts and those who did not6. A peak of anxious intrusive thoughts around birth occurs in both parents (there are no large studies in fathers). Such a thought turns into obsessive-compulsive disorder when it is taken as proof of danger and met with checking, avoidance or requests for reassurance.

The couple, money and country

Relationship satisfaction after a first child drops on average, slightly to moderately, in both partners7. But it also drops in childless newlyweds: part of the decline is simply time passing in a relationship, not the child. Having a child as a way to fix a relationship has not been tested.

The costs are split unequally. According to Danish registries, in the long run, after a child mothers’ earnings fall about 20% behind fathers’ — through employment, hours and hourly wage8. This measures money, not how mothers value motherhood, and does not necessarily apply to other countries. There is evidence that the more housework falls on a woman, the lower her desire for a male partner (surveys; causation not proven). In Russia, about 2% of fathers take parental leave. In Sweden, a reform that let fathers take leave days flexibly in the first weeks reduced postpartum complications in mothers.

Whether parents are unhappier than the childless depends on the country. In a comparison of rich countries, the gap is smaller where there is paid leave and help with childcare, and largest in the US9 (this is an association, not an experiment). Parental burnout is higher in individualistic countries (the cross-country comparison shows an association, not a cause). In two Polish samples, about one parent in ten regretted having children (this is not a global figure).

What this means for the self

Parenthood is a layer of the self built around another person’s body. Before a child, the model of “me” is responsible for one body; after, the brain retunes attention, sleep and anxiety to someone else’s. Part of the shift is given: the mother’s gray matter, the father’s testosterone, the infant’s night wakings. But even the “given” here depends on behavior: in fathers the hormone drops more with more caregiving, and in mothers sleep also depends on feeding. The body adjusts to what a person does with their hands.

Ties are rebuilt. The couple turns from two people into a care system, and the task of keeping a mate competes with the task of raising your own for the same hours of sleep. Who takes the nights, the leave and the income loss is often decided not in words but by default — and that default is linked to the mother’s income and (according to surveys) to desire.

The story changes last. A new role appears — “parent” — and the ego easily makes it the measure of self-esteem: am I a good parent or a bad one. Hence the defenses. Frightening thoughts are shameful to name because they strike at the image of the “loving parent” — and avoidance and checking are exactly what turn such a thought into a disorder. Exhaustion, a father’s depression, regret are hidden behind the mask of the happy parent. Meaning and burden live side by side: a child can add significance and take away the day’s joy at the same time, and one does not disprove the other.

Freedom here lies in two places. The first is separating yourself from the role: a thought is not an intention, regret is not lack of love, exhaustion is not failure. The second is seeing that much of what seems to be “just how it is” is in fact set by agreements, policy and money — which means it can be rebuilt. What the child gets from the parents is in chapters 08 and 09.

Myths

Myth: a child will bring the couple closer. In fact: satisfaction drops slightly on average in both partners; a child does not heal a relationship, it adds to its load.

Myth: pregnancy “shrinks” the brain and makes a mother dumber. In fact: gray-matter volume decreases and stays that way for years, but the objective decline in thinking is small and inconsistent, and the shift itself is linked to attachment to the child.

Myth: postpartum depression happens only to mothers. In fact: it affects 8–10% of fathers, more often when the mother is depressed.

Myth: a thought of harming your child means you are dangerous. In fact: such thoughts occur in nearly half of mothers and are not linked to aggression.

Who plays on this

A valued role is both a support and a lever: the image of the “good parent” can legitimize unpaid labor. The warning sign: someone appeals to your shame about the role rather than to an agreement about the work. The market sells “attachment hormones” — oxytocin sprays and “boosters” for bonding with your baby — with no evidence behind them. The advice “never sleep with your baby” without a plan B ends with an exhausted parent falling asleep with the infant on the couch — the most dangerous place of all.

What to do about it

Agree before the birth. Who takes which nights, who takes which hours, how leave, money and retirement savings are split, and when this gets revisited. Six-hour programs for couples expecting a first child noticeably helped some couples in a small study10; in a review of many trials of parenthood preparation the effect is small — not insurance, but not nothing. How to tell it’s working: the agreement is actually carried out, not just stated.

Protect sleep. The first three months are the hardest; the goal is one unbroken stretch of sleep per night for each parent. From six months, if the child’s sleep is a problem, behavioral methods help: gradually lengthening the pauses before brief check-ins, or moving bedtime later and step by step bringing it back earlier (gentler). In trials, no harm to the child’s attachment or mental health was found by age six11, and maternal depression at two years was less common (15% vs. 26%). Before 4–6 months such methods do not help, and a sick child should not be sleep-trained. How to tell it’s working: a sleep diary (time to fall asleep, number of wakings, longest stretch) and the parent’s mood.

Safe infant sleep. On the back, on a firm flat surface, in the parents’ room but in the baby’s own crib, without soft pillows and without overheating. Bed sharing raises the odds of sudden infant death roughly threefold, roughly sixfold if the mother smokes, with the highest risk before three months; without other risks the absolute risk is small but not zero12. If you might fall asleep with your baby, choose the safest place in advance: never a couch or armchair, no smoking, alcohol or sleeping pills.

Handle frightening thoughts. Name it: “this is an intrusive thought.” Tell your partner or a doctor. Keep caring for your child rather than avoiding them. Don’t check or ask for reassurance over and over. The measure: how much time a day goes to the thoughts and rituals, and which activities with your child you have started to avoid.

Questions for yourself

  • What in our split of caregiving did we agree on in words, and what settled by default?
  • What thought or feeling about my child do I not say out loud because it spoils my image of a good parent?
  • Where is my exhaustion a lack of sleep and help, and where is it a judgment of myself?
  • What do I expect from the child for myself: meaning, fixing the couple, my place in the family line?

When to see a specialist

Get help urgently in case of confusion, voices, delusional ideas, several days without sleep, suicidal thoughts, or if a thought of harming the child seems justified: this may be the onset of postpartum psychosis, most often in the first 2–3 weeks. If rituals and avoidance take over the day, see a therapist for cognitive behavioral therapy with exposure (gradually facing the thought without rituals); the data are modest, but it is used for postpartum OCD. Low mood in either parent, the father included, is a reason to get checked; snoring and pauses in breathing in an infant call for a doctor, not a sleep program.

Part VII. 35–42: Midway — Influence and Meaning

33. The Midlife Crisis: The Myth, the “U-Curve,” and What Actually Changes by Forty

The happiness curve that bottoms out in the late forties is one of the most cited graphs about age and one of the most misread. It is drawn from a crowd, not from a person. Turning forty doesn’t start a timer inside the self: workloads, roles and habits change, and character keeps changing after thirty too.

How it works

Character: two different questions

“Do people change with age?” is really two different questions.

Rank-order stability — does a person keep their place among peers: if you were the most anxious in the group, are you still? Mean-level change — does a trait shift for everyone on average. Both can happen at once: everyone becomes a bit more organized, while the ranking barely changes.

The largest synthesis of long-term studies covers hundreds of thousands of people1. Stability rises fastest in childhood and adolescence and plateaus in young adulthood. A plateau is not a freeze: adults keep changing.

What shifts on average: conscientiousness (being organized, reliable, able to finish things), emotional stability and social assertiveness all rise, most visibly between roughly 20 and 402. An updated synthesis puts the overall shift lower than earlier work did, but confirms the rise in emotional stability1. These are averages: different traits, ages and people follow different paths.

How a role rewrites character

The proposed mechanism is called the social investment principle. The chain runs like this: a role (a job, a partner, a child) creates expectations → you do what is expected, again and again → habits and self-image change → the people around you respond to the new behavior → their response locks it in.

Comparisons between countries where roles arrive at different ages test this logic, but do not prove cause. These data do not support the conclusion “get married or have a child and you’ll grow up.”

The U-curve: what it is made of

Well-being at any age is a sum of opposing forces. On the plus side: accumulated resources and experience in managing emotions. On the minus side: illness, pressure at work, losses, caring for children and aging parents at the same time. On top of that come generation effects (people born in different years lived in different conditions) and period effects (crises, wars, upheavals that hit everyone at once).

This is the main catch. When a statistician “controls for” income, unemployment or marital status, this can clarify the link — or it can cut out the very path through which age acts. The shape of the curve depends on that choice.

A review shows that the U appears or disappears depending on the country, the set of controls, who was sampled and how long they were followed; in some large pooled analyses, the U disappears once the calculation changes3. When the same people are followed for years, there is not one shape but several. One large American study found this pattern: almost no change from twenty to forty, a rise from forty to sixty, a decline by seventy. Russian data, depending on the analysis, yield a U, an S and other shapes.

The debate is not “big data versus denial.” It is an argument against a mandatory schedule — not against the fact that a particular person can have hard years in midlife.

“Crisis” — what was actually measured

There are two different questions: how common the crisis is, and what causes it. Interviews in which people recall whether they had a crisis measure the story a person tells about themselves4. “Age vs. satisfaction” graphs measure average ratings. Neither shows a biological crisis around forty.

Erikson’s frame of “generativity versus stagnation” (the wish to leave a mark, to nurture, to teach — or the sense that life is stuck) is useful for talking about midlife. But it is a way of describing, not a diagnosis and not a proven age-driven mechanism.

The mind doesn’t have a single peak either: different abilities rise and fall at different times, and there is no precise schedule of which one peaks in which year. Nor is there a reliable figure for infidelity by age: these data mix lifetime admissions, current behavior, length of marriage, generation and opportunity.

The couple at midlife

A synthesis on relationship satisfaction (many samples, hundreds of thousands of people) finds wide variation by age and other conditions5. A 16-year study of American couples identified different paths: “high and steady” and “medium-low or declining”6. There is no single curve for all couples.

After the first child, marital satisfaction drops on average for both partners, but part of the decline is simply time7; a child doesn’t heal a relationship, and brief preparation programs help some couples8 (more in chapter 32).

In Finnish registry data, divorce risk is low in the first months, then rises, peaks and gradually declines — and this shape holds after adjusting for couples’ characteristics. There is no “seven-year itch” as a set date in these data.

Passion doesn’t have to fade on a timer either. In a brain-imaging study of a selected group of spouses who were intensely in love, the marriages had lasted 21 years on average9. In a random survey in the U.S., about 40% of those married more than ten years called themselves “very intensely in love” (self-report, one country)10.

Thoughts of divorce are common and fluid. Among married Americans aged 25–50, a quarter had thought about divorce in the past six months; a year later, 31% of them had stopped thinking about it, and 36% of those who hadn’t been thinking about it had started11. More on the couple as a system in chapter 30, and on emotions and conflicts in Feeling for Two — and More.

What this means for the self

By forty, all three layers of the self carry more load than ever, but none of them switches by the calendar.

Body — given, and it accumulates. Health, sleep, the first chronic illnesses, your parents’ age — these are inputs to the curve, not age itself. For what really changes in the body later, see chapters 38 and 40.

Ties — roles that hold the shape. By midlife you hold more roles at once than at any other time: worker, partner, parent, child of aging parents. Each role is a set of other people’s expectations, and by the logic of social investment, expectations lock behavior in: the people around you keep responding to you the same way, and their response locks in your habitual behavior. A mask put on for a role can fuse with your self-image — and that is both a trap and a tool.

Story — where the ego works. The “midlife crisis” is a ready-made plot the culture hands out in advance. It can suit the ego: it offers an explanation (“it’s my age”), permission (“I’m entitled to this”) and protection from asking about the specific cause. A retrospective “I had a crisis” does not by itself prove that the crisis is a pattern.

The same plot works in reverse: “I’m forty, my character can’t change now.” The data don’t support this — adults keep changing, and age says nothing about your reliability or anyone else’s1. The mistake is the same on both sides: a crowd average is taken for personal fate.

For self-esteem, average differences between the sexes are reported, but their size is not established, and a difference in averages is not two different types of people. For deliberate personality change, no effect of sex has been shown (which is not the same as “there are no differences”).

Freedom — in breaking it down. If things are hard, the question isn’t “how old am I” but “what exactly is pressing on me”: an illness, a job loss, an adult child’s troubles, caring for a parent. These can be acted on; age can’t. Meaning is the subject of chapter 34.

Myths

Myth: Most people go through a crisis around forty.
In fact: The data don’t show this; a universal crisis is a generalization from disparate events and average curves3, 4. The harm of the myth is that a serious condition gets written off as “a phase.”

Myth: Character sets by thirty.
In fact: Refuted by data on hundreds of thousands of people: stability rises, but adults keep changing1.

Myth: Everyone’s happiness dips in the late forties and then comes back on its own.
In fact: The U shows up in some data and models, but it is not a law for the individual; among people followed for years, the shapes differ3.

Myth: Life runs in seven-year stages with a biological reset.
In fact: No such reset has been found1; this book’s seven-year stages are a way of telling the story (more in the prologue).

What to do about it

Break the load down by source. Instead of waiting for an age-based “rebound,” write down what specifically is pressing on you: health, work, loss, caregiving, relationships. The best-supported changes are in relationships, treatment and working conditions, not a single shift in attitude. The sign: every item has a concrete action, not “wait.”

Change behavior, not “the trait.” Pick one action tied to the trait and a situation for it: not “become more sociable” but “be the first to start a conversation at meetings.” Repeat it. Wanting alone isn’t enough, and how you see yourself can improve with no change in behavior. The sign: when you check again later, an outside observer names specific examples.

Look at your own couple, not at “the normal curve.” Track recurring patterns, not one bad month. Sort out what a thought of divorce actually is: a passing flicker, something that keeps coming back, a plan, or a sign of danger — decisions are made on patterns and actions, not on a single thought. Don’t wait for the kids to move out to fix a harmful relationship, and don’t have a child to fix it.

Use age as context, not as a verdict. Don’t conclude “won’t change anymore” or “that’s what’s expected at this age” — for yourself or anyone else — from the number in a passport.

Questions for yourself

  • What specifically is pressing on me right now — and how much of it am I writing off to age?
  • What role have I played so long that I can no longer tell it apart from myself?
  • What one behavior is worth changing — if age is no obstacle?
  • Whose story of a “crisis” am I trying on — and does it fit my actual events?

When to see a specialist

If a severe condition gets in the way of living and working, it is not “an expected phase,” and you don’t need to wait it out. If a relationship involves humiliation and intimidation (psychological aggression early in marriage predicts physical aggression12), or if thoughts of divorce are tied to danger, get help right away.

34. Purpose: find it or grow it — what is known about the meaning of life

People who say they have something to live for live longer, on average1. But no one has shown that meaning can be prescribed to yourself like a drug, and there is no evidence of a ready-made purpose lying somewhere, waiting to be found. Meaning is not buried treasure but work the self does: it is assembled from several sources and maintained, the way fitness is maintained. And it is one of the handiest handles others can use to take hold of the self.

How it works

Three different meanings under one word

The word “meaning” lumps together three different things2. Coherence: life makes sense, events add up to a story. Direction: there are goals, you know where you are going. Significance: life feels worth living. Any one can exist without the other two: you can understand your life clearly and not see where it is heading; you can have a goal and not feel that any of it is worth anything.

This is a frame for thinking, not three modules found in the brain. But it shows why “find your purpose” is a vague instruction: it rolls all three layers into one.

Having meaning and searching for meaning are measured separately2. Searching is in itself neither an illness nor a success: it can be exploration, it can be anxiety, it can be both at once. A person who is searching is not necessarily lost.

Meaning and the body: what has been measured

The strongest figure comes from a pooled analysis of observational studies in which more than 130,000 people were followed for years after rating whether their life had a purpose. Those with a stronger sense of purpose had roughly a 17% lower risk of dying during follow-up, and a similarly lower risk of cardiovascular events, after adjusting for other factors1. The association was similar across ages and countries (which does not prove the effect is the same everywhere).

The Japanese Ohsaki study tested this in a culture outside the West. Tens of thousands of people answered one question: do you have ikigai, something that makes life worth living? Over seven years, mortality among those who answered “no” was about one and a half times higher3.

These are observations, not experiments. The time order is clearer here than in a one-off survey: meaning first, death later. But illness could have eroded the sense of purpose before the first measurement, and money, friends and health can sustain both meaning and life at the same time. The honest conclusion: meaning goes along with a long life, but it has not been shown that “prescribing” yourself a goal will extend it.

Can meaning be grown

Thirty-three randomized trials, in which people were randomly split into those who went through a program and those who did not, showed that programs targeting meaning noticeably raise the sense of it compared with no intervention4. But the programs vary widely, and so do the results. Part of the effect may come from expectation and attention. So meaning apparently responds to work, but there is no single exercise with a proven dose.

There is also an unexpected source. In a diary study, adults logged their sex lives and well-being for 21 days: sex preceded a rise in mood and sense of meaning, and no reverse link was found5. The study is small, but it hints that significance is tied to the body too (more on the chemistry between two people in Alchemy Without Magic).

Find it or develop it: two theories of interest

There are two views of interest. The first: it is ready-made inside you, and you need to find it. The second: it grows from invested time and skill. In experiments, those who believe the first lose interest more easily when the work gets hard: difficulty reads as a sign of “not for me” (the data are experimental; how well this holds over a career and across cultures has not been tested).

The mechanism: if interest is supposed to come ready-made, difficulty looks like a refutation (“so it’s not for me”), and the work is dropped before skill has time to grow.

Your goal or someone else’s

In three longitudinal studies, goals that matched a person’s interests and values produced more sustained effort, more achievement and more well-being than goals pursued “out of duty” or under pressure6. The proposed chain runs through three basic needs: deciding for yourself, being capable, being connected to people. “Deciding for yourself” here means “endorsing from within,” not “being alone.”

But a goal that is your own is not automatically good for you. Its content, feasibility and resources matter too: a goal can be genuinely yours and still unreachable.

Passion that holds you up, and passion that holds you

Work you love gives attention, practice, mastery and belonging. But it can also become a condition of self-worth: then difficulty feels like personal failure, rest like betrayal, and “no” like a threat to the self.

Psychologists distinguish harmonious and obsessive passion. A review of 94 studies: harmonious passion, where the activity has a place in life but does not take it over, is linked mostly to good outcomes; obsessive passion, where the sense of self collapses without the activity, is linked to both good and bad7. The pattern was similar for men and women and across ages, after adjusting for culture and occupation. No one has shown that an exercise can turn one form into the other.

How meaning becomes a handle

In experiments, observers more readily consider unpaid overtime and tasks outside someone’s role acceptable if the worker was described as “passionate about their work”8. The logic: “they would have volunteered anyway,” “the work is its own reward for them.” And the reverse: if a person is treated badly, observers are more likely to decide that they are “passionate,” so poor treatment itself generates the explanation “but they love it.” These are judgments in an experiment, not a count of real exploitation at work; how often this happens in life is an open question.

This leads to the chapter’s least obvious point. A valued role is both a resource and a lever. A calling sustains effort and justifies unpaid labor. Family gives meaning and loads the cost of caregiving onto someone. Meaning can motivate and at the same time make refusal look like betraying yourself.

Meaning after adversity

A common idea: adversity helps only if you have “processed” it, not just “talked it through.” No such strict rule has been shown9. The review on this topic is integrative, not conclusive; searching for meaning after a blow and meaning already found are different states, and they are measured differently. Nor has it been shown that recovery requires drawing a “lesson” from trauma.

Forecasting happiness

People misjudge how strongly and for how long an event will please or hurt them, especially when all their attention is on that one event (the data are robust, but not everyone is wrong, and not always). “Once I find my calling, everything will fall into place” is exactly this kind of forecast: imagination inflates one change and erases everything else that will still be part of that life.

What this means for the self

Little is given. Nothing in these data points to a built-in mission you must guess. What is given are needs (deciding for yourself, being capable, being with people) and the brain’s ability to assemble events into a story. Meaning is the product of that assembly, not its raw material.

Much is built. A self-image can rest on one or two roles: profession, family, a cause. The ego ties self-esteem to them, and the role stops being an occupation and becomes a support for the self. While the role holds, it is a resource. When it cracks (being fired, the children leaving, illness), what sags may be not the work but the person. This has not been measured directly, but it follows from the logic of the data: the fewer the sources of significance, the more fragile the self.

The ego’s defenses work subtly here. Obsessive passion can serve as a defense: “I exist as long as I’m doing this.” So can belief in a purpose already found: within it, difficulty reads as a sign of error. And there is the mask of being “passionate about your work”: from the outside, it legitimizes overwork8.

Where freedom lies. The three layers of meaning can be grown separately. Coherence, through the story you tell yourself about what you have lived. Direction, through goals you endorse from within rather than drag along out of guilt6. Significance, through people, the body and work that feel worthwhile. The practical conclusion: build several sources of significance rather than search for one predetermined mission.

It helps to replace the question “what is my purpose” with another: what is my meaning already made of, and how many supports does it have (for what actually changes by forty, see chapter 33).

Myths

Myth: ikigai is an ancient Japanese recipe: find the overlap of what you love, what you are good at, what you can be paid for and what the world needs. In fact: the four circles are a modern pop career-advice diagram, not an ancient recipe. In Japanese research, ikigai is a single question, “do you have something that makes life worth living,” and the link to a long life was shown for that question, not for the diagram3.

Myth: if it got hard, it’s not for you. In fact: difficulty is not a test of purpose. In experiments, belief in “ready-made” interest can undermine engagement when things get hard (the data are experimental).

Myth: a calling always makes a person happier. In fact: “always” is false. Obsessive passion goes along with both good and bad, and the label “passionate about their work” makes it easier to treat a person badly7, 8.

Myth: people with a purpose live longer, so find a purpose and you will live longer. In fact: the link exists, but it has not been shown that an assigned purpose extends life1. The flip side of this myth is harmful: a sick person can be led to believe they “didn’t want to live badly enough.”

Who plays on this

Employers. “We’re a family,” “we’re not here for the money,” “but you love this work”: language that converts meaning into unpaid hours. The tell: you ask about pay, deadlines or time off and get an answer about values. Experiments show that, from the outside, such language really does make overwork seem “normal”8.

The purpose-finding market. The ikigai diagram, promises to “find your calling,” exercises with a “scientifically proven” effect. Signs: the promise of one mission instead of several supports; the promise of longevity or happiness from a correctly found goal; effectiveness figures for whole programs pinned to one particular worksheet. Meaning programs do have proven benefits, but those benefits do not carry over to just any worksheet4 (on how success is sold, see chapter 29).

What to do about it

Check whether a goal is your own. Write down the goal. Next to it, answer honestly: am I doing this because I endorse it, or out of guilt, fear, someone else’s expectations? Then the next feasible step. Later, see whether the effort holds up in practice, not just in your promises to yourself. This is an application of the research on self-concordant goals6, not a tested exercise with a dose.

Check a calling for exploitation. Five questions for work you love: is it voluntary? paid? within my role? safe? does it leave room for rest? If the answer to some of them is “no,” meaning is working as a lever, not a support. The questions themselves have not been tested for effectiveness, but the trap they guard against has been shown in experiments8.

Small, reversible trials instead of forecasts. Instead of guessing whether “this is for me,” try it small and reversibly: a project, a course, volunteering. Go by actual experience: what holds your attention after the novelty wears off and the difficulty begins. The sign it is working: the decision rests on what you have lived, not on what you have imagined.

Count your supports. Write down what your significance is assembled from right now: people, body, work. If everything rests on one item, grow a second and a third while the first is still standing.

Questions for yourself

  • If my job were taken from me tomorrow, what would be left of the self?
  • Which of my goals am I dragging along out of guilt while calling it a calling?
  • Where do I say “but I love this” when I should be discussing pay or rest?
  • Which pursuit did I drop at the first plateau, telling myself “it’s not for me”?

35. Power and Influence: What They Do to a Person — and How to Be Useful

The most famous study of how power corrupts, the Stanford Prison Experiment, does not prove what it is cited to prove: the guards were told outright how to behave1. Power does not turn a person into a different species. It changes what pays off for them, what they see and what they are told — and through that it rebuilds the self of whoever holds it and of those who depend on it.

How it works

Power is control over what others need

Power is not a character trait but a position: you have something others need (money, evaluation, access, a decision). A chain follows: others depend on you → your attention shifts toward rewards and your own goals → impulse control and attention to others may weaken → subordinates start filtering what they tell you. The last link is the most insidious: a person in power learns from distorted data. This is a working theory assembled from varied experiments, not a law that power always forges the same kind of personality.

In experiments where people were briefly made to feel powerful, they were less likely to take another person’s perspective spontaneously. But “less likely spontaneously” does not mean “unable”: the task, goals and accountability change the result, and a large replication has not confirmed this effect. The same goes for hypocrisy: in some experiments, the “powerful” judged themselves and others asymmetrically — a reason to ask about double standards, not a conclusion that “all bosses are hypocrites.”

The body: a status hormone, not a dominance button

The appealing story — “strike a power pose and your testosterone and confidence will rise” — did not survive a large replication. Feeling powerful is a self-report; testosterone is a blood test; success is a third outcome. None of them confirms the others.

Testosterone seems tied more to status than to aggression as such — and it depends on context. In a controlled experiment, women given testosterone made fairer offers in bargaining, while those who merely believed they had received it behaved less fairly2. The belief about the hormone pushed behavior in the opposite direction. The hypothesis that the link between testosterone and status depends on cortisol (a stress hormone) is being tested, but there is no reliable overall estimate. The conclusion is simple: hormones are not irrelevant, but “testosterone is the power button” is the wrong model. For the hormones of a couple, see Alchemy Without Magic.

Two paths to the top: prestige and dominance

There are two ways to gain standing in a group. Prestige: you are granted influence because you can do what others value. Dominance: influence is taken through fear and coercion. From outside, both look like “the one in charge,” but they rest on different things: the first on usefulness, the second on threat. Even dominance as people judge it in partners breaks down into three dimensions — social, financial and physical.

The stakes are ancient. In a large US survey, holding a managerial position was linked to the number of children for men but not for women — mainly because men without power more often stayed childless; for women, the link between income and number of children followed an arc3. This is a snapshot, not proof of cause, but it ties the task of rank in the group to the task of finding a mate.

The link between rank and health is also real: in long-term studies of civil servants, those lower in the hierarchy have worse health. But this is observational, and it cannot be reduced to “mindset,” posture or a hormone.

Those who rise and those who cope are different people

Confidence, visibility and drive impress during selection. Once a person is at the top, the job demands something else: taking in unpleasant information, coordinating people, tolerating correction. A meta-analysis shows that narcissism is linked to becoming a leader but not to how well they lead as rated by others; narcissists rate themselves higher4. Part of the effect comes down to plain sociability. There are signs the relationship follows an arc, but “hire moderate narcissists” does not follow — no one has found an optimal level.

A boss’s narcissism and the bullying of subordinates are different things: systematic hostility from supervisors is studied separately, and its cause is not necessarily narcissism. Indirect signs of CEO self-admiration (such as the size of their photo in the annual report) are useful for hypotheses about organizations, not for diagnosing an individual. More on the narcissistic boss in chapter 24.

Obedience: the situation presses, but does not decide for you

A person may follow the majority for three reasons: it seems to know better; disagreeing threatens exclusion; an authority calls obedience a useful duty. From outside the behavior looks the same, but the defenses differ: better data, answering in private, an ally, the right to ask questions.

A synthesis of studies from many countries using Asch’s famous line-judgment experiment found that conformity changed over time and across cultures, and was higher in samples from more collectivist countries5. The classic “one-third” is the share of responses in a specific setup, not “a third of people can’t think for themselves.” For how the group shapes a teenager, see chapter 13.

Milgram’s experiment is also no longer read as “ordinary people obey automatically.” A modern replication deliberately stopped at 150 volts, so it did not repeat the experiment in full. Another reading: people went further not mechanically but because they identified with the scientific mission. This is a hypothesis, but if it holds, obedience rests on “we are doing something important,” not on a button in the head.

Punishment and trust: who punishes whom

In economic games played by groups of university students on several continents, participants could fine one another6. Some fined not the greedy but those who had contributed as much or more — “antisocial punishment.” Where there was more of it, cooperation fared worse. In this study, Minsk and Samara are just groups of students, not a measure of all Belarusians or Russians.

Trust is not a virtue but a calculation of how a person will behave and what happens if they cheat. You can trust family and distrust strangers, believe in the courts and not believe a particular official. The idea that “the Soviet past makes people distrustful and helpless for generations” goes beyond the data. The verified studies measure specific things: East Germans’ preference for state care after the GDR, a tendency to cheat on one task, the transmission of reactions to repression across three generations in Crimean Tatar families7. There is no single “post-Soviet syndrome” in them. For how the state writes your biography, see chapter 37.

Influencing the masses: nudges and dark patterns

Soft influence works through friction: a default setting spares you effort, a reminder makes an option visible, simplification removes a barrier. The same mechanism helps you do what you intended — or pulls you where you would not go if the choice were clear.

The nudge is sold as an almost free lever. In 126 trials by government teams, the average gain was 1.4 percentage points versus 8.7 in academic publications8. Part of the gap is explained by selective publication and underpowered studies. After correcting for this, one reanalysis finds that the average effect almost disappears — although individual nudges can work9.

The flip side is stronger. In interface experiments, mild “dark patterns” (a hidden opt-out, confusing presentation) more than doubled sign-ups for a dubious service, and aggressive ones nearly quadrupled them, though with backlash; people with less education were more susceptible10. The best defense here is the choice environment, not willpower.

Class is not a moral diagnosis

A high-profile study claiming “upper class predicts unethical behavior” combined disparate measures: behavior on the road, self-report, cheating in a game. Two later field tests on real roads failed to replicate the “expensive car — doesn’t yield” link11. Resources can change the cost of mistakes and whose help you need — this affects individual acts, but does not determine moral character. The reverse stereotype (“the poor are more honest”) has no data behind it either.

Helping: benefit to the helper and benefit to the helped

Acts of kindness give the helper a small, short-lived boost in well-being. This comes from a review of experiments that relied mostly on self-reports from young Western participants. In a large synthesis, the link between prosociality and well-being is small, and stronger for some measures of meaning than for simple pleasure12. Experiments found no clear differences by sex or age (the subgroups are small).

But benefit to the helper is not proof of benefit to the recipient. Compassion (caring about another’s distress) and empathic distress (absorbing another’s pain as your own) are different processes. More help is not always better, and years of volunteering by people who may already be healthier and freer cannot be turned into the recipe “help others and you won’t get sick.”

What this means for the self

Power enters all three layers of the self, and each has its own trap.

The body is given, but it does not command. The link between hormones and status depends on context, a pose does not make you strong, and chemistry does not excuse an act. The testosterone experiment shows how easily a story about the body is mistaken for the body itself2.

Ties are where power really lives. It exists only in relationships: who depends on whom, who tells whom what. Hence the main risk for the one at the top: the mirror stops being honest. People bring what you want to hear, and the model of “me” can come to rest on flattery. The ego takes this for truth: “they listen to me, so I’m right.” For the person below, the risk is the reverse: to stay “one of us,” the ego may surrender its judgment and call it loyalty.

The story is where it’s decided who you have become. The ego’s most convenient defense in power is “the role made me do it.” The critique of the Stanford experiment strips it of its scientific support1: every role comes with instructions, incentives, oversight — and choice. The second defense is “I’m at the top because I’m better.” The leadership data suggest that the path to the top and the quality of the work measure different things4. If your self-esteem has fused with your position, losing the post can feel like losing the self; if it rests on usefulness, the post is just a tool.

This is also where freedom lies. Prestige and dominance are ways of holding a place, not types of people. Influence through usefulness demands the same as good leadership: taking in unpleasant information, tolerating correction, leaving others the right to say “no.” This is not morality but mechanics: by the logic of the mechanism, this path does not cut you off from the truth.

Myths

Myth: give an ordinary person power and they will become cruel.
In fact: the key experiment the myth rests on was staged: the guards were given instructions, and data were collected selectively1. This does not mean power never makes abuse easier, but “the role made me do it” has no scientific support.

Myth: a power pose raises testosterone and confidence.
In fact: the hormonal part was not confirmed in a larger sample. Feeling powerful is a separate outcome, and it does not prove a hormonal mechanism.
