Myth: the rich are less ethical.
In fact: the road data did not replicate11; results depend on which behavior is measured and how.

Myth: nudging is a powerful, almost free substitute for reform.
In fact: in real-world programs the effect is often small, and after correcting for selective publication the average almost disappears8, 9.

Who plays on this

Interfaces and subscriptions. The same techniques that help you do what you intended are used to sign you up for what you don’t need: a hidden opt-out, confusing presentation10. The sign: one click to get in, a maze to get out.

States and media. An independent TV channel in Russia influenced the 1999 vote; Chinese authorities publish fabricated posts on a massive scale to distract rather than argue; a school curriculum reform in China has been studied as a lever of ideology. Genuine support and limited information coexist: high approval (of social credit systems, for example) does not prove that people speak freely, without looking over their shoulder. Behavior can change without a change in personal beliefs. More in chapter 37.

Leader selection. Organizations that hire for charisma and confidence get people who are better at rising, not better at leading4. The sign: the decision is made after an interview, without independent data on past performance.

What to do about it

If you have power, check what reaches you. Ask yourself regularly: who can challenge my decision? When was I last told something unpleasant, and did I use it? Whose interests are not represented at the table? This is derived from the mechanism, not tested as a method. The sign it’s working: people start bringing you bad news before it becomes a disaster.

If you depend on someone else’s power, make behavior verifiable instead of diagnosing. Record dates, exact requests, witnesses and consequences; ask for your duties to be clarified in writing; know the independent complaint channels, and realistically assess the risk of retaliation and loss of income. Neither calling your boss a “narcissist” to their face nor the “gray rock” tactic has been tested in controlled studies. The sign: you have facts a third person can evaluate.

Audit your defaults. Subscriptions, auto-payments, notifications: which of these did you choose yourself? Count the unwanted sign-ups, the time it takes to cancel, the unexpected charges. This tracking itself has not been tested as a remedy, but the target is the right one — the environment, not willpower.

Help within a limit. Ask what kind of help is needed; name your limit (“I can give one hour”); check whether things actually got better for the person you helped. A limited promise is no less moral than a boundless one — and it can be kept. For how to be useful without becoming someone’s feed, see chapter 47.

Questions for yourself

  • Does my influence rest on what I can do, or on others’ fear of losing me?
  • When did someone who depends on me last push back?
  • In which role do I tell myself “I had no choice”?
  • If my position were taken away tomorrow, what would be left of the self?

36. Faith and Spiritual Seeking: Support, Practice or Trap

Women who attended religious services more than once a week died noticeably less often over the years of follow-up than those who never attended1. Meditation, usually pictured as pure calm, causes unpleasant effects in some practitioners and sometimes gets in the way of work and daily life2, 3. Both things are true, and there is no contradiction. Judging by the data, faith and spiritual practices act less through what you believe than through what they do to your body, your ties and the story you tell about yourself. That is why the same practice can be a support, a tool or a trap.

How it works

What a congregation actually does

The strongest data come from a large American cohort of nurses, women followed for years. Attending services more than once a week was linked to a risk of death roughly a third lower than in those who never attended; once a week, roughly a quarter lower; less often, roughly an eighth lower1. This is relative risk, not “a third less chance of dying” for any given person. And it is an observation, not an experiment: nobody was assigned to attend.

The hypothesized pathway does not run through belief directly. Regular gatherings → familiar people and a shared weekly rhythm → practical help, expectations and meaning → different behavior and a different response to trouble → health. Social support and not smoking each statistically account for roughly a fifth of the link1. The rest is unexplained — and statistics here are not proof.

The link with depression runs both ways. Women who attended services more often were later less likely to develop depression; but depression also later reduced their attendance4. Perhaps a depressed person stops going — and drops out of the network that was holding them up. This does not support the neat story “pray and you’ll feel better.”

An important limitation: all these numbers are about adult American women. The data do not allow extending them to men, children or other countries.

Why the effect depends on the country

“Believers are happier everywhere” is wrong. The psychological benefit of religion is tied to how much the people around you value it — that is, to the social value of faith in the country5. What seems to work is not dogma but your place in the group.

Faith also has a flip side — inner struggle: doubt, fear of punishment, conflict with the community. In people with chronic illnesses, this struggle and psychological distress feed each other in a loop (an observation; it does not prove cause)6. The same community that gives one person support gives another shame.

Even measuring faith is hard. Religious affiliation, switching from one religion to another, attending services and personal belief are different things. In China, the answer to “what religion do you belong to” depends especially heavily on the wording: formal affiliation and everyday practices diverge7.

Gods as the glue of large societies

One theory holds that belief in all-seeing, punishing gods, together with costly rituals, makes people’s promises more credible — and so helps strangers cooperate in large societies. This is an explanation, not proof that cooperation is impossible without religion.

A high-profile attempt to test it against history — a study of 414 societies over 10,000 years, published in one of the leading scientific journals — claimed that complex societies appeared before “moralizing gods.” In 2021 the paper was retracted. Its conclusion cannot be considered established — but the retraction does not prove the opposite either. Covering many societies does not remove the uncertainty in dating or the gaps in the data.

Synchrony is better studied. When people move in time together, their attention aligns, their sense of connection grows, and sometimes so does their willingness to help. This has been tested in dozens of experiments; the effect on helping is small and hard to replicate8. It does not prove lasting trust, kindness to strangers, or that the mechanism is specifically religious.

Faith is passed down in families: long-term studies across several generations show a link between parents’ and children’s religiosity9. This is a link, not a parenting recipe (how country and faith get into a child before language — in chapter 10).

Meditation: it works, but it’s not magic

Paying attention to sensations can change how a person notices discomfort and how they respond to it. But a meditation program is also an instructor, expectations, a group and time for yourself. So the honest question is not “is meditation better than nothing” but “is it better than another sensible activity.”

To the first question, the answer is yes. A synthesis of more than a hundred trials in adults outside clinical settings: mindfulness programs reduce anxiety, low mood and stress compared with no activity10. To the second, no: superiority over specific active alternatives has not been shown10. This does not mean it helps everyone; three quarters of trial participants are women.

In children the picture is different. In a British school trial, more than eight thousand students around age 12 received either mindfulness lessons or the usual curriculum. After a year there was no difference in depression risk, emotional state or well-being11. The adult data do not justify mandatory meditation in schools.

The cost of practice

Meditation is not harmless for everyone. In a synthesis of dozens of studies, adverse effects occur on average in about 8% of practitioners; in rigorous experiments, about 4%; in observational studies, about a third2. The spread is huge because researchers look and ask in different ways. A review of clinical practice gives an even wider range: anywhere from a quarter of practitioners to most of them report unpleasant effects, some to the point of being unable to function3. This does not mean every five-minute session is risky — but the risk isn’t zero either.

Who is more vulnerable? Childhood trauma and symptoms of post-traumatic stress, even below the threshold for a diagnosis, predict adverse effects and worse program outcomes12. Having no psychiatric history does not guarantee protection2.

Altered states: experience is not truth

Mystical experience can be induced. In a double-blind experiment, a high dose of psilocybin (the substance in mushrooms) in a calm, supportive setting produced experiences that people later called deeply and lastingly meaningful to them13. Circular breathwork can also lead to an altered state — partly through a drop in carbon dioxide in the blood14. Some people slip into such states more easily in the first place: the capacity for “absorption” — going completely into an image or sensation — is linked to a tendency to sense a presence, “messages” and the dissolving of the self15.

“Ego dissolution” — the feeling that the boundaries of the self have vanished — is measured with a questionnaire, and responses to it are internally consistent16. But the questionnaire records a reported experience, not whether it tells the truth about the cosmos.

The therapeutic effect is a separate question. In a trial of psilocybin against a standard antidepressant in adults with depression, both groups with psychological support, the primary outcome did not differ statistically after six weeks17. This is a small clinical trial, not a model for a retreat.

In a large survey of people who used ayahuasca (a hallucinogenic brew from the Amazon), almost 70% had acute physical reactions, 2% needed medical help, and most had difficult psychological experiences in the following weeks or months18. Many later interpreted the difficulties as beneficial; but the survey is self-selected, and risk rates cannot be derived from it.

What this means for the self

Spiritual seeking touches all three layers of the self at once — and mixes them up.

Body — the starting conditions. You don’t choose a tendency toward absorption or the imprint of childhood trauma. An altered state is the nervous system at work: carbon dioxide, a substance, a synchronized rhythm. It is a real experience, and it can stay meaningful for years. But the sense that “I dissolved” is a signal from the body, not a certificate about the universe.

Ties — the main thing a congregation does. The likely pathway of benefit runs through people, rhythm and help (only part of the link is explained) — that is, through the ancient task of belonging. Hence the paradox: the most effective part of faith may depend least on dogma. And it is fragile: anyone who drops out of the network — in depression, in doubt, in conflict — risks losing support when it is needed more.

Story — meaning, the eighth task. Faith offers a ready-made story about yourself: where I come from, what for, what happens after. The ego holds onto it as a frame: self-esteem through being “right,” defense through “we have the truth,” a mask through ritual and dress. The more the self has invested in this story, the more doubt hurts — which is one way to understand the link between inner struggle and distress6 (this is an interpretation, not a measurement). Leaving a faith can feel like losing yourself, even though what is lost is one version of the story.

The ego can use spirituality too: “I’m beyond ego” easily becomes a new status. Difficulties in practice get framed as “growth,” and getting worse as “purification”; defending the self-image of “I’m on the right path” drowns out the body’s signals.

Where the freedom is. The line between support and trap does not run along “religious — secular — commercial.” It runs along three questions: are you choosing freely, do you have alternatives and ties outside the group, and what are the consequences — are you living better. A limited, testable goal (“less anxiety by spring”) is a practice. A promise of total transformation and answers to everything is a signal that someone is pulling the lever of meaning (how cults and gurus work — in chapters 25 and 29; where meaning comes from in general — in chapter 34).

Myths

Myth: believers are happier everywhere. In fact: the benefit is tied to how much faith is valued in the country; inner struggle with faith goes along with distress5, 6.

Myth: meditation beats every other way of coping with stress and low mood. In fact: it beats nothing; beating specific sensible alternatives has not been shown10.

Myth: meditation is harmless, and any difficulty is a sign of progress. In fact: adverse effects happen and sometimes hit work and life; a positive interpretation does not make them safe2, 3.

Myth: a powerful experience of the self dissolving proves a higher reality. In fact: the questionnaire measures what was experienced, not whether its interpretation is true16. Prayer for the sick by others has not been supported by good trials either19.

Who plays on this

  • Retreats and “shamanic” tourism. By default, there is none of the clinical support the trials provide. Sign: no medical screening or monitoring, and difficulties declared “purification” in advance.
  • Schools. They apply results from adult volunteers to children, even though a large school trial found no benefit11. Sign: it’s mandatory and outcomes aren’t evaluated.
  • High-control communities. They hold people through belonging and shame, and make doubt and leaving hard. Sign: ties outside the group are devalued, questions are punished. Child abuse in religious organizations has been measured across an entire country (Australia) — these are not just isolated stories20.
  • Gurus of total promises. Open-ended practice instead of a testable goal, an answer to everything instead of limits. Sign: a promise that cannot be disproven.

What to do about it

  1. Separate the people from the dogma. If a community gives you warm ties and rhythm, that is valuable in itself. Check: you have more help and less shame, not the other way around. If doubt is pushing you out of the network, keep the people, even if you change your faith.
  2. Treat practice as an experiment. Choose a described, tested program with a live instructor who warns about side effects. Track not just minutes of practice but also sleep, mood and ability to work. If your life gets worse, don’t step it up.
  3. Altered states — with the cost in mind. What matters is the dose, the place, who is with you and what happens if it goes badly. Judge a powerful experience by what changed in your everyday life, not by the intensity of the moment. More on breathing techniques in Keys.
  4. The three-question test. Every so often: am I here by choice? Do I have a life outside this? Am I better off — by the facts, not by the group’s word?

Questions for yourself

  • What would I lose if I doubted: the faith — or the people?
  • Does my practice make me calmer — or make me “better than others”?
  • What result do I expect — and how will I know it isn’t there?
  • Who benefits from my difficulties being counted as “growth”?

When to see a specialist

If anxiety persists during or after practice, thoughts of suicide appear, or you can’t manage ordinary tasks — stop the practice and get an assessment from a doctor or psychotherapist. Especially if you have a history of serious trauma.

37. State and Country: How Power Writes Your Biography — and How to Live in Another Country

In 1999, in areas that could receive NTV — then still an independent channel — the ruling party’s vote share was 8.9 percentage points lower1. Yet in the same country, support for the leader, measured indirectly so that people did not have to reveal their own views, turned out high — close to what the direct poll showed. Both facts are true at once. The state does not install a ready-made self in anyone. It decides what is available to you, what you hear more often, what pays to say out loud and what gets punished — and the self is assembled within those conditions.

How it works

What gets into your head: access, repetition, distraction

Influence has several distinct paths. You can decide what is available at all. You can make everything else costly to find. You can repeat one interpretation until it feels familiar. You can show that “everyone thinks so.” You can draw attention away. None of these paths requires everyone to sincerely believe every message.

An archive of the Chinese state’s online posting showed that fabricated posts do not argue with critics; they distract2. The researchers estimated the scale at roughly half a billion fabricated posts a year (a historical estimate from a single archive, not an exact count). The takeaway: information control and coercion are different mechanisms that work together, and no overall ratio between them has been established. The claim that “power rests mainly on fear” is not supported by the data.

Repetition works even on people who could know the truth. The illusory truth effect — a false statement seems truer after being repeated — is confirmed by a review of 51 studies and by experiments in which people could know the relevant fact3, 4. The brain mistakes “familiar” for “true.” This is a shift in probability, not a verdict: knowledge helps, just not always.

Influence is not all-powerful and can backfire. Serbian radio that reached Croatian villages did not win them over to the Serbian side — there, people voted more often for Croatian nationalists, and anti-Serbian graffiti appeared more often5. What decides is not only the message but who is speaking and who you take yourself to be in relation to them.

School: what you know, what you say, what you believe

A curriculum chooses which events matter, who the hero is and who the threat; an exam rewards whoever repeats the expected explanation. How school does this to a child is covered in chapter 12; here what matters is the adult outcome.

China changed its textbooks in stages between 2004 and 2010 — almost an experiment. Students who learned from the new books trusted the government more, viewed democracy differently and were more critical of markets; the effects on behavior were mixed6. This was tested at a single elite university, not on the population at large. In Russia, a comparison of student cohorts showed answers close to the official line, but the authors themselves cannot tell sincere change from mere adaptation.

The right answer on an exam, an answer in a survey and personal conviction are three different things. In a year-long experiment in Rwanda, a radio soap opera about reconciliation changed people’s sense of what is “accepted” — and some of their actions — more noticeably than their personal views7. A story can change what a person considers the norm for others more than what they believe themselves.

Threat and the body: you can’t read ideology from a pulse

A popular idea: conservatives’ bodies react more strongly to threat. A preregistered direct replication of the famous experiment and two more studies in the US and the Netherlands (skin conductance, facial muscles in response to frightening images) found no such difference8. This does not mean threat never changes views. It means fear, talk about fear and support for harsh measures are separate measures, and you cannot read a person’s politics off their body.

Surveillance: silence is neither consent nor protest

Knowing you are watched enlarges the imagined audience for everything you do (that, according to the authors, is the mechanism). If being misunderstood is costly, a person avoids the topic or chooses their words carefully. After the Snowden revelations, views of “dangerous” Wikipedia articles fell — but this is aggregate traffic, and it does not reveal any individual reader’s motive.

In China, an online survey showed that the overwhelming majority of respondents approve of social credit systems. The sample does not represent the country (skewed young and male), but the approval may be genuine. Three things can coexist: sincere support, support shaped by restricted information and silence out of caution. From the outside they look the same.

A regime’s legacy: specific traces, not a “Soviet character”

Years after reunification, East Germans more strongly expect the state to take responsibility for a range of things. One study found that, under certain conditions, they cheated more in a game, with the effect linked to how long they had lived under socialism — but the result has not been independently replicated. Among Crimean Tatars surveyed in 2014–2015 across three generations, the more a family had suffered in the 1944 deportation, the stronger its members’ ethnic identity and the more sharply defined their political reactions — but there was no religious radicalization.

Each study captures a specific trace on a specific measure: expectations of the state, behavior in a game, identity. None shows a single “post-Soviet syndrome” or innate helplessness. A cultural map of world values describes population averages; it is not a diagnosis of any individual.

Trust is not simply a virtue but a judgment: how the other will behave and what happens if they cheat. You can trust family and distrust strangers, trust the courts and distrust officials. In economic games in cities around the world, some participants punished not only the stingy but also those who contributed as much as they did or more to the common pot — and where there was more of this “antisocial punishment,” cooperation was lower9. This is a comparison of student groups, not a measurement of nations; Minsk and Samara appear in it as experiment sites, not as a portrait of Belarusians and Russians.

Love of country: two different things

Blind patriotism is the stance “we are always right” plus intolerance of criticism; it is linked to nationalism, a sense of external threat and seeking out only agreeable information. Constructive patriotism is love that allows for criticism aimed at improvement; it is linked to interest in politics, knowledge and participation (student surveys, correlation, not cause). Collective narcissism is the conviction that my group is exceptional but underappreciated. With it, criticism reads as insult, ambiguity as hostility, and striking back seems fair; it is linked to prejudice, revenge and schadenfreude. This is not about love of country as such but about a demand for recognition.

Another country

Moving changes not “the culture in your head” but conditions: language, legal status, familiar feedback, your social circle, access to resources. These conditions can affect stress and how fully you take part in life, and through them, the sense of “I belong here” or “I’m an outsider.”

A large review found that people who hold on to both cultures are, on average, better adjusted (correlation, not cause). But a longitudinal review showed that the “integration” strategy itself explains little about how well a person settles in. Perhaps it runs the other way: those who have an easier time to begin with find it simpler to live in two worlds.

Among migrants in Europe and several other countries, psychoses are diagnosed about twice as often as among locals10. Adjusting for socioeconomic differences narrows the gap but does not close it. This is relative risk: most migrants do not become ill, and forced flight and voluntary relocation for work are different stories.

In two languages, a person may express their personality and describe themselves differently. This is one self in different contexts, not two personalities.

If you move for a partner, a residence permit tied to your spouse imposes specific legal and bargaining costs on the partner who moved, and moving for someone else’s career redistributes work and connections within the couple. What this does to a couple is covered in Feeling for Two — and More.

What this means for the self

Given — time and place. Your country of birth, its language, the school curriculum of your year, the TV channels of your childhood, your family’s history under that regime. You don’t choose them, just as you don’t choose your set of chromosomes. The early layer — what a country puts into the cradle — is covered in chapter 10.

Built — the habits of the ties layer. What is familiar, and therefore seems true. Whom you consider your own, and whom you expect to cheat you. What you expect from the state. What can be said out loud. None of this is “national character”; these are learned responses to recurring situations and stimuli. When the situation changes, the response can change, though not right away.

The ego takes the country into its self-image. “I’m Russian,” “I’m Chinese,” “I’m European” — a source of support, if the sense of belonging is settled. But if the self’s worth rests on the group’s exceptionalism, any criticism of the country lands as a personal blow, and the ego defends itself the way it does when self-esteem is under attack: with resentment and a counterstrike. The same mechanism on the scale of one person is covered in chapter 23.

A mask is a tool too. Where words are punished, a person operates in two registers: what they say and what they think. The data do not prove that this inevitably breaks or splits the self — behavior, personal beliefs, anxiety and identity are measured separately, and there are no reliable measurements of this effect on the self. Caution in a dangerous environment is adaptation, not self-betrayal; how long it keeps changing self-image is unknown.

Moving exposes the assembly. At home, the ties layer is invisible, like air. In another country, the familiar feedback the self used to check itself against is cut off, and you can see how much of the self was the place and how much was you. This can be painful, but it lets you see your story from the outside.

Freedom lies in sorting by layers. You cannot undo where you grew up. You can distinguish which of your beliefs is knowledge, which is a familiar textbook formula, which is a norm you attribute to “everyone,” and which is something you have tested yourself. And you can love your country while criticizing it: in these data, constructive love of country is linked to engagement, not alienation.

Myths

Myth: a Soviet upbringing made people helpless and dishonest. In fact: studies capture specific traces — expectations of the state, behavior in a game, family memory of repression; no single syndrome or innate dishonesty has been shown11, 12, 13. Judging a person by their country’s history is a harmful mistake.

Myth: if you know the facts, a repeated lie won’t fool you. In fact: repetition shifts judgments of truth even in those who could know the truth. Knowledge helps but does not make you immune.

Myth: under an authoritarian regime, all support is fake. In fact: in Russia, a measurement that let people avoid revealing their own views put support at about 80% — close to the direct poll (a historical measurement, not a current one)14. Sincere support and restricted information can coexist.

Myth: by paying for births, the state strongly raises the birth rate. In fact: a model-based estimate puts the effect of Russia’s maternity capital at about 0.15 children per woman in the long run15; births may simply come earlier without any increase in the number of children.

Who plays on this

The state. Levers: the curriculum, ownership of channels, flooding the feed with distraction, making it costly to search for the “wrong” things, surveillance, money for desired behavior (for example, maternity capital). Signs: the same formula comes from many places at once; an inconvenient topic is not refuted but drowned out with distractions; an alternative source can be found, but it takes time and carries risk.

The rhetoric of exceptionalism. Whoever uses it — the government, the opposition, a diaspora, a blogger — the signs are the same: “we are underappreciated,” criticism is equated with hostility, any ambiguity is read as an attack, revenge is presented as justice. This lever works on the tasks of belonging and rank in the group; how ideas and anger spread from head to head is covered in chapter 26, and how high-control groups work in chapter 25.

Our media and theirs. Foreign propaganda can backfire and strengthen the opposite side. Sign: the message targets your belonging, not your conclusions — you agree or argue before checking anything.

What to do about it

Ask what exactly the message changes. Knowledge, the sense that “everyone thinks so,” an action and a personal conviction are different things. Next time a phrase seems obvious, check: do I know this, or have I just heard it many times? Sign that it works: you start catching yourself thinking “familiar, so true” and asking how you actually know.

Compare sources and arguments, not opinions. Training in critical thinking — comparing claims, evidence, sources and counterarguments on real material — has a small effect on average, though programs vary widely. Sign that it works: you reason better about a new topic, rather than simply agreeing more often with whoever taught you.

Trust separately. Separate family, strangers, the courts, the government, the media: whom you trust and in what, based on what experience, and what you will do if they deceive you. It works if, instead of “I trust everyone” or “I trust no one,” you end up with a map of separate ratings.

Before moving, a plan for both of you; after, a tally of concrete things. Before leaving, write out for each partner: their own legal options, money, work, language, an exit plan. Afterward, track not the “integration” label but reliable people nearby, how much you take part in life in the new language, and access to doctors, banks and paperwork. You can keep ties in both countries, but not out of a sense of moral debt.

Questions for yourself

  • Which of my beliefs about my country and the world do I never check, because I’ve simply heard them many times?
  • When someone criticizes my country, do I feel hurt for it or for myself?
  • What do I say out loud while thinking otherwise — and am I already starting to think the way I speak?
  • Without language, status and familiar faces — what in me remains mine?

Part VIII. 42–56: Rebuilding

38. Menopause and “andropause”: what really changes in the body and the mind

One sex really does have a hormonal transition in midlife: the ovaries wind down over a few years, and it shows on the calendar. The other has almost nothing of the kind: testosterone falls by a percent or so a year, and a noticeable share of that decline comes from weight and illness, not age. Yet the market sells both the same story: “you’re running out, buy yourself back.” But the body is one thing; the story the ego mistakes for the body is another.

How it works

Menopause: the supply runs out, not femininity

When the follicles (the small sacs in which eggs mature and estradiol is made) run out, estradiol production first turns erratic — this is perimenopause — then falls, and periods stop. The process is divided into standard stages, recommended for use across all populations and body types.

Timing. In a large American cohort the median age at the final menstrual period was 52.5, with no difference between races after adjustment. Across 46 studies from 24 countries the average age is 48.8: earlier in Africa, Latin America and Asia, later in Europe and Australia; smoking moves menopause about a year earlier. Under British guidelines, a healthy woman aged 45 or older with typical symptoms is diagnosed without blood tests.

Hot flashes: years, not months

Hot flashes and night sweats affect roughly half to four-fifths of women, more often in low-income countries than in rich ones. They are most strongly linked to late perimenopause, and also to weight, smoking and anxiety. Frequent hot flashes last a median of 7.4 years, 4.5 of them after the final period; if they start before the transition or early in it, they last longer — 11.8 years; if only after menopause, about 3.41 (three thousand American women followed for 17 years).

Mood, memory, sleep

Mood. In perimenopause the odds of depressive symptoms are about 1.4 times higher; after menopause there is no significant rise2. The vulnerable window is the transition itself, not the years after it. In one small trial, transdermal estradiol with progesterone early in the transition nearly halved the share of women who developed clinically significant depressive symptoms (one study, one clinic).

Memory. In perimenopause learning weakens: on repeated testing, the usual gain from practice disappears. After menopause scores recover (4 years of follow-up); in cross-sectional studies, delayed verbal memory in postmenopausal women is still worse. Early surgical removal of the ovaries is a separate case: it is linked to a higher risk of cognitive impairment and dementia, and the younger the age at surgery, the higher the risk3. The ordinary transition, going by four years of follow-up, is a temporary dip; what comes later is unknown.

Sleep. Trouble falling asleep and night waking increase over the course of the transition, especially in women woken by hot flashes. Overall the shift is small and is not explained by age alone. Women in general complain of insomnia about one and a half times as often as men, yet their measured sleep is no worse: on average they even get more deep slow-wave sleep. Complaints and measured sleep are not the same thing (more on sleep in chapter 17).

Bone and sex

Bone is lost not at the start of the transition, as is often said, but at the end: fastest in late perimenopause and the first 1–2 years after the final period, about 10% over the transition4. The driver is falling estrogen, not progesterone. After that, loss slows but continues for years.

By late perimenopause desire drops and pain during sex increases; dryness of the mucous membranes is twice as common after menopause as before it. This is local tissue without estrogen, and it is treated locally — with vaginal estrogen, which, unlike the other forms, is not linked to a higher risk of breast cancer.

Hormone therapy: the fear and the ads were both wrong

In 2002 a large trial of combined therapy was stopped: there were more heart attacks, strokes, blood clots and breast cancers, and fewer hip fractures and colon cancers. But the participants averaged 63 years of age (pooled data) — older than most people who start treatment. After 18 years, all-cause mortality in the treated and untreated groups turned out to be the same5.

The risk depends on what, how, how much and when. Estrogen plus a progestogen raises breast cancer risk, and the longer it is taken, the more: five years of use starting at 50 means roughly one extra case per 50 women by age 69 (with a daily progestogen; if the link is causal); with estrogen alone, noticeably fewer6. The type of progestogen matters: with micronized progesterone, a French cohort found no rise in risk; with others, it did. Pills raise the risk of blood clots; a patch or gel through the skin does not7. Starting before 60 or within 10 years of menopause gives a favorable balance for symptoms and bone. Therapy is not prescribed to prevent heart disease or dementia: no protection has been shown.

Without hormones

The best tested: hypnosis (five sessions: 74% fewer hot flashes than with attention alone, no hypnosis; followed for 12 weeks)8, cognitive behavioral therapy, some antidepressants, and a new class — neurokinin-3 receptor blockers, which cut two or three more hot flashes a day than placebo. In 2024 one of them received a warning about rare severe liver injury. In women with excess weight, weight loss reduced bothersome hot flashes. No benefit has been shown for exercise as a treatment for hot flashes, for soy, or for supplements.

Culture changes not only the words but the symptoms

Japanese and Chinese women in the US report symptoms less often than their white neighbors. In surveys in China, complaints are more strongly tied to attitudes toward aging than to the stage of menopause itself. In Japan, feeling cold is listed among the main symptoms. And in an urban Chinese cohort followed for years, most women did have hot flashes; they just didn’t last as long — a median of 4.5 years. Among women in Hangzhou, muscle and joint pain come first, then sexual problems and fatigue — in about half. The differences, according to reviews, are real, but the methods are not comparable, and culture shapes what counts as a symptom and what gets said out loud.

“Andropause”: a slow descent instead of a cliff

The axis is simple: the hypothalamus (a part of the brain) signals the pituitary (the gland below it), the pituitary signals the testes, and testosterone in the blood feeds back to damp the signal. With age, the testes respond more weakly. But obesity and illness strike higher up — at the signal itself: at a body mass index of 30 or more, total testosterone is noticeably lower.

In men aged 40–70, total testosterone falls about 1% a year, free testosterone faster; apparently healthy men have higher levels than peers with illnesses. True age-related hypogonadism — at least three sexual symptoms (weak morning erections, low desire, erection problems) plus low testosterone on tests — was found in 2.1% of European men aged 40–799. A separate puzzle: American men’s testosterone is lower in each successive generation, and in one study this was not explained by age, health or lifestyle.

Erections age along with blood vessels: the yearly rate of new erectile dysfunction is almost four times higher at 60–69 than at 40–49, and higher still with diabetes, hypertension and heart disease. Erections depend on blood vessels, not just on hormones.

Testosterone therapy

In men 65 and older with symptoms and low levels, a year of gel improved sex life and, slightly, mood, but not vitality. In a large trial in men with low testosterone and cardiac risk, serious cardiac events did not increase, but atrial fibrillation, acute kidney injury, pulmonary embolism and fractures did10, 11. External testosterone suppresses sperm production. Weight loss on its own raises testosterone — most of all after bariatric surgery.

What this means for the self

Given. The supply of eggs is finite, the transition will come, its timing differs between populations, and smoking brings it forward. Men are given a slow descent of the axis and blood vessels that age. None of this is chosen.

Built. A noticeable part of what gets called “andropause” is tied to weight and illness. Weight is a lever, and it is in your hands. Part of the burden of menopause can be built too: hot flashes are linked to smoking, weight and anxiety, and among Chinese women complaints are more strongly tied to attitudes toward aging than to the stage of menopause.

The transition hits the self from two sides. From below — the body layer: a body that stayed in the background for decades starts sending signals — heat, insomnia, memory lapses, a different desire. The brain assembles self-image from the body’s signals; when they change, the self temporarily loses its familiar footing. Perhaps that is why mood is most vulnerable during the transition rather than in the years after it (a hypothesis, not measured). From above — the story layer: culture hands over a ready-made frame, “end of youth, end of sex, start of decline.” The ego grabs it, because any explanation beats not knowing.

The ego’s defenses here are predictable. Denial (“this won’t happen to me” — and years of untreated hot flashes and insomnia). Catastrophizing (“my brain is going” — though the memory dip is temporary). The mask: “andropause” sounds like a breakdown of manhood, and it is easier to buy testosterone than to lose weight or see a cardiologist. In all three cases the ego protects the image, and the body pays.

Freedom lies in separating the layers. Hot flashes are not a life sentence: they have a time limit and proven remedies. The memory dip, going by four years of follow-up, is temporary. Falling testosterone is more often about weight than about “the end of a man.” Once the body’s signal is separated from the story about it, what remains is a concrete problem with proven solutions, not an identity crisis. How the self changes in these years in general — chapter 33; what the body has in store at fifty — chapter 40.

Myths

Myth: hormone therapy is poison and causes cancer. In fact: the risk depends on type, route, duration and age at start; all-cause mortality over 18 years did not change, and fear costs years of untreated symptoms. The reverse myth — “hormones preserve youth and protect against dementia” — is also wrong.

Myth: men hit andropause at 50, just like menopause. In fact: there is no cliff; symptomatic hypogonadism was found in 2.1% of European men aged 40–79, in the rest, levels decline slowly, and a noticeable share of the decline is weight and illness.

Myth: soy, black cohosh and supplements treat hot flashes. In fact: there is no consistent benefit, and specialist guidelines advise against them. In men, soy does not “feminize” either: on average it does not affect testosterone or estradiol.

Myth: Asian women don’t get hot flashes. In fact: they report them less often in surveys, but in an urban Chinese cohort most women had hot flashes.

Who plays on this

Testosterone advertising. From 2000 to 2011, prescriptions grew in most countries, especially of skin-applied forms — a sign of older buyers; the authors link this to guidelines that endorsed treating “age-related deficiency.” In the US, each additional month of advertising in a region was associated with more testing and more new prescriptions12 (an association at the regional level). The tell: they sell “vitality” and “getting yourself back” — and vitality did not improve in the trial.

Anti-aging clinics. Growth hormone in healthy older people changed body composition but caused swelling and joint pain and has not been proven to rejuvenate. The tell: “natural,” “bioidentical,” “custom-compounded for you” — for pharmacy-compounded hormones, the reviewed evidence includes no reliable studies.

Both markets probably pull the same lever — the fear of losing your partner and your place in the group (a hypothesis, not directly measured). How “male power” and “feminine energy” are sold — chapter 29.

What to do about it

A symptom diary. Two weeks before any treatment and again after 12 weeks: the number and severity of hot flashes every day. A treatment works if the numbers change, not just how you feel.

Testosterone — only for symptoms. With sexual symptoms: a morning blood test, then a repeat. Deal with weight, sleep, alcohol and illnesses first. If you are planning children, external testosterone is not an option.

Decide on hormones by four questions: how much the symptoms get in the way, how many years have passed since menopause (under 10 and before 60 — a better balance), whether there is a risk of blood clots (then through the skin), which progestogen. Not for preventing heart or brain disease.

If hormones aren’t suitable — hypnosis or cognitive behavioral therapy as the base; for insomnia during the transition, one review proposes cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia as the first line (the data are weak).

Questions for yourself

  • What exactly bothers me — the symptom itself, or the story about what it means?
  • How much of my “aging” is actually weight, not age?
  • What do I fear in hormones — the data, or a 2002 headline?
  • Who is selling me “getting myself back” right now — and what has that seller measured about it?

When to see a specialist

Persistent low mood in perimenopause: the odds of depression are higher in this period. Early menopause or ovaries removed before natural menopause — a reason to discuss bone and brain. Worsening erections — a reason to check blood vessels, blood sugar and blood pressure, not just buy a pill.

39. Children Leave, Parents Age: Life Between Generations

In a large American study of families, a quarter of adult children stopped speaking to their father at least once, and most of those estrangements later ended1. Family in adult life is neither a solid given nor a life sentence. It is a tie that breaks and heals. By midlife the self sits between two generations at once: grown children change who you have been for the last twenty years, and aging parents change who you have always been.

How it works

Love and duty are not opposites

Researchers describe adult family ties as ambivalence: attachment and conflict, help and obligation live in the same relationship at the same time1. This is a frame for description, not a diagnosis. It does not tell you to put up with things, and it does not tell you to leave.

Two separate questions are easy to confuse here. First: how common is a given pattern of relationships? Second: does a person do better if the pattern is deliberately changed? Science answers the first fairly well and the second barely at all1.

Estrangement: not rare, and usually reversible

In American data (about eight thousand relationships with each parent), about 6% of adult children had a period of estrangement from their mother and 26% from their father1. The first estrangement began, on average, at about 26 with the mother and about 23 with the father. For more on prolonged emerging adulthood, see chapter 21.

Most estrangements are not final: about four in five with the mother and two-thirds with the father later end1. This is the picture in one country; data of this quality for Russia and China are not available here. And it is observation, not experiment: the numbers do not show whether cutting off or coming back helps. People leave relationships for very different reasons, and no one randomly assigned them to cut off or stay.

Children leaving: there is no “syndrome”

A single “empty nest syndrome” common to all parents has not been shown. Sadness after children move out and changes in a marriage are different things; they cannot be lumped into one outcome. How parents experience children leaving varies widely from family to family.

Relationship satisfaction changes with age and relationship length, but the curve “a dip while the kids are at home, then a rise” is no guarantee for any given couple2. Average curves mislead: unhappy couples divorce and drop out of the sample, generations differ, and short and long relationships are represented by different people. With the first child, satisfaction drops on average for both partners, but it also declines in childless couples who have been together just as long3. A child does not fix a relationship; preparation for parenthood helps some couples, but that is a different claim3, 4 (more in chapter 32).

Help flowing down: “only as happy as the least happy child”

Helping an adult child can express attachment and a sense of “I’m managing.” But anxiety, conflict, drained finances and lack of choice flip its meaning.

One study of parents aged 40–60 took into account the problems and successes of all their adult children, not just one5. The title of the paper is “only as happy as the least happy child.” It is an image, not a law (the exact strength of the link is not in the available data). But the structure matters: a parent’s well-being is not determined by whether “the kids have left,” and it does not come down to a single child.

Help flowing up: caring for a parent

Caring for an aging parent means a valued relationship plus tasks, supervision and often conflict with work. A pooled analysis compared caregivers with noncaregivers: caregivers had noticeably higher depression and stress and lower well-being, by about half a standard deviation6. The gap varies with who receives care, with gender and age, and with study quality.

This is a group comparison. It does not prove that caregiving alone is the cause, and it gives no figure for “daughters versus sons.” The popular “sandwich generation” is a prompt to ask about hours, sleep, money, tasks and backup, not a ready-made syndrome.

Researchers have also studied the feeling “I had no choice about becoming a caregiver”7. But that feeling is tangled up with income, available alternatives, family quarrels and the amount of care. This finding does not mean that “changing your attitude” is enough to make the burden disappear.

There are data from a randomized trial in several US cities: individually tailored multicomponent support for people caring for someone with dementia reduced clinical depression and improved some quality-of-life measures, with effects that differed across groups. The exact schedule of sessions could not be verified, so a single “family meeting” or advice to “think positive” is no equivalent substitute.

A parent’s death

Losing a parent is a separate event, not the same as caregiving. Long-term studies look at how age and gender affect recovery from it. These data give no exact “you’ll be done grieving in X” timeline and no universal difference between the sexes.

Culture: who owes whom

Culture sets the direction of duty. In China, in a national survey of people 45+, about 62.6% of men said their family paid a bride price to the bride’s family when they married, and 43.8% of women said their family provided a dowry8. Here marriage is bound up with exchange between generations. Pressure on sons to marry, according to Chinese men who live with men, shapes whether they disclose their orientation and how they decide about marriage9. For Russia, these data contain no comparable estimates for working caregivers.

What this means for the self

Midlife removes two supports the self has stood on for decades: the role of a parent who is needed every day, and the role of a child who still has elders.

Given — the tie. You don’t choose your parents or your children, and ambivalence toward them is normal, not a malfunction. You can love and be angry, help and resent it, at the same time. The ego wants a simple story: “we’re a warm family” or “they traumatized me.” Both stories simplify, and both protect — the first from guilt, the second from shame.

Built — the role. For twenty years, daily tasks confirmed “I’m a good parent.” When the children leave, self-esteem loses its source, and the ego may look for a new one: control over adult children, their successes as a showcase, their problems as a personal failure. The “only as happy as the least happy child” link shows how deeply a parent’s self is wired into other people’s lives5.

Built — the memory of childhood. By forty, everyone has a version of what their parents did to them. But an adult’s memory is not a direct observation of childhood: what people report about childhood maltreatment in hindsight and what was recorded at the time often do not match10. And even an accurate list of childhood adversity predicts risk well for a large group but poorly for an individual11. Your story about your childhood is a working model, not a transcript.

Mask — duty. Caring for a parent easily turns into an image: “the good son,” “the good daughter,” “I carry it all alone.” The mask of duty keeps up appearances for your relatives and for yourself, but it hides the real hours, the sleepless nights and the lost work. Calling forced caregiving a “free choice” does not lighten the load7.

Freedom — in the form of the tie, not the fact of it. You cannot change who your parents and children are. You can reshape the frequency, channel and content of contact, how care is divided, what you forgive and what you no longer trust. Cutting off and coming back are not the right and wrong answers here but two tools, neither of which science has recognized as a cure1.

Myths

Myth: when children move out, parents inevitably suffer — “empty nest syndrome.”
In fact: there is no universal syndrome; experiences vary widely, and sadness and changes in a marriage are different outcomes.

Myth: cutting off contact with a parent is the path to healing.
In fact: there is no evidence that estrangement on the whole improves adult children’s well-being; most estrangements later end1. The reverse is not proven either: reconciliation does not heal by itself.

Myth: a marriage sags while the kids are at home and then is sure to recover.
In fact: average curves are distorted by divorce and generational differences; nothing is guaranteed for any particular couple2, 3. Waiting for an empty nest to fix a harmful relationship is not a plan.

Myth: a child will hold a marriage together.
In fact: with the first child, satisfaction drops on average for both partners3 — more in chapter 32.

Who plays on this

The feed and online advice-givers. They sell a universal recipe: “block your toxic parents” or “family is sacred, endure it.” The sign: one answer for everyone, with no questions about safety, dependence or the person’s current life.

Family. Pressure to “forgive” can serve the person who did harm rather than the one who was harmed12. The sign: forgiveness is demanded as a condition for return, trust or inheritance.

A culture of duty. Where marriage and care are an exchange between generations, duty can weigh on decisions about marriage8, 9. The sign: “you must” is heard more often than “do you want to” and “can you.”

What to do about it

A boundary plan instead of “yes/no.” Name the specific behavior that doesn’t work for you, your boundary, your response if it is crossed, the practical dependencies (money, housing, care) and a date to review it. Options: less often, through a different channel, with certain topics off the table. This is a decision aid, not a tested treatment. It is working if there are fewer incidents and your life is better; when there is a threat, the lack of research is no reason to keep contact.

Separate love from resources. Before taking on care for a parent, count: hours per week, nighttime wake-ups, lost work, dangerous tasks, conflicts, who will step in for you. Look for real alternatives and respite, not a new name for duty. The sign it is working: your sleep and depression are under control, and someone can take over.

Forgiveness — separate from trust and contact. In a review of studies, structured forgiveness programs did increase forgiveness — compared both with no treatment and with other approaches12. Anxiety and low mood may also decrease (the exact effect is not established). Forgiving is voluntary; it does not oblige you to reconcile, trust or meet. Track your own distress and freedom of choice, not whether you have made up.

Look at all your children, not at “whether they’ve left.” If anxiety about one adult child colors everything, that is the link with parental well-being described above5. A useful question: what in that child’s life is actually in your power?

Questions for yourself

  • What story do I tell about my parents — and what in it protects me?
  • Who will I be when no one confirms me as a parent every day?
  • Is my caring for my parent a choice or a mask of choice? Who will step in if I go down?
  • What do I expect from forgiveness: relief for myself or the relationship back?

When to see a specialist

If caring for a loved one with dementia leaves you persistently low, your sleep is wrecked and there is no one to take over, you need structured caregiver support, not advice to “hang in there.” When there are threats, pressure and coercion in the family, base decisions about contact on safety, not duty.

40. The Body Sends the Bill: Stress, Sleep, Movement and Character by Fifty

Compare bodies of the same size, and a forty-five-year-old burns as much energy as a twenty-five-year-old: the “slowing metabolism” of midlife is a myth1. The weight and fatigue that arrive by fifty are mostly not some new mechanism breaking down but the sum of old ones: how a person slept for years, moved, responded to strain, drank, whom they lived with and what rank in the group they held. Almost every line of this bill is still open.

How it works

Stress: mobilization, not poison

The brain appraises a demand or a threat. The hypothalamus — a center at the base of the brain that controls hormones — signals the pituitary, the pituitary signals the adrenal glands, and cortisol enters the blood. In parallel, within seconds, the sympathetic nervous system — the “fight or flight” branch — switches on with adrenaline and noradrenaline. Cortisol releases energy, holds back inflammation, changes how memories are stored, and then shuts off its own circuit through feedback. It has a daily rhythm: a sharp rise after waking, a low point at night.

Cortisol rises most under two conditions: when the task is beyond your control and when others are judging you as you do it. When both coincide, the response is largest and slowest to fade (a synthesis of lab experiments). Many stressors don’t move cortisol at all.

The harm comes not from the hormone itself but from a disrupted pattern. When the system is called on too often, for too long, or isn’t allowed to switch off, wear accumulates — this is called allostatic load. In older people, a score built from a set of body measures predicted mortality and decline seven years out, but it is a research concept, not a clinical test: there is no single way to calculate it. Chronic stress has no single “signature” in the blood. Illness and death are linked to a flat daily profile, in which the morning peak and the nighttime dip are blunted; whether this is a cause or a trace of disease is unknown.

What doesn’t exist is “adrenal fatigue.” A systematic review found no support for it, and a review of burnout markers found no cortisol differences from healthy people. Chronic stress changes how the system is tuned; it doesn’t “burn out” the glands.

How much does work stress matter? High strain combined with low control raises the risk of heart attack by about a quarter but accounts for a small share of cases — far less than smoking does (nearly two hundred thousand workers, observational).

Rank in the group is written into the body

In a British study of civil servants, the lower the grade, the more heart disease, smoking and poor diet, and the less movement, control over work and support — and over twenty years this gradient did not narrow. In US tax data, the gap in life expectancy at forty between the richest and poorest percent is 14.6 years for men and 10.1 for women2. In men, low occupational status is linked to mortality much more weakly than current smoking is, but consistently (a synthesis of cohort studies). In primates, whether the dominant or the subordinate suffers more stress depends on how the group is organized: what matters is not rank as such but how much control and predictability it gives.

Ties work the same way. Social isolation raises the risk of death by about a third, the feeling of loneliness by about a seventh (the largest synthesis: more than two million people)3. The line “loneliness is as harmful as 15 cigarettes a day” is a conversion between different studies, not a measured dose; the link for smoking is much stronger. These links did not differ between men and women. All of this is observational: depression, illness and poverty can cause both isolation and death.

Sleep: the same clock, a different architecture

With age there is less deep slow-wave sleep and REM sleep, it takes longer to fall asleep, and you wake more often at night; after sixty, only the share of time in bed spent asleep keeps falling. Older people’s internal clock runs at the same pace as younger people’s, so waking early is not simply a “shortened” rhythm. Whether older people need less sleep or can’t produce the sleep they need is an open debate. For older adults, the benchmark is 7–8 hours. In Russia, difficulty falling asleep is roughly twice as common in older groups as in younger ones; in China, older samples have less insomnia — the age trend is not universal.

Short sleep in midlife is linked to dementia: six hours or less at 50 — risk about a fifth higher, at 60 — about a third higher4, 5. Genetic methods that cut through confounding barely confirm that sleep causes the disease, and in another cohort it was long sleep that predicted dementia — as an early sign of a process already underway. No trial has shown that lengthening sleep prevents dementia. Short sleep is a risk marker and a reason to look for insomnia and apnea (pauses in breathing during sleep), not a proven cause.

In people over sixty, sleeping pills add about 25 minutes of sleep but make memory and thinking lapses almost five times more frequent, and coordination problems and daytime grogginess several times more frequent6; international criteria for older adults advise avoiding them because of falls, fractures and confusion. Melatonin is a weak sleep aid (falling asleep minutes faster) and a useful clock-shifter for a late rhythm. A daytime nap of up to an hour boosts alertness; habitual daytime sleep longer than an hour is linked to cardiac risk, and napping and Alzheimer’s disease may feed each other. More on the mechanics of sleep in chapter 17.

Movement: what fades fastest

By age 75, strength is lost two to five times faster than muscle mass. That is why age-related muscle loss is now defined primarily by weakness, not by size. Strength training increases mass, strength and function — this is very well established, including after fifty; in cohort studies, the biggest drop in mortality comes with 30–60 minutes of strength work a week.

Average weight gain in midlife — about one and a half kilograms every four years — is the sum of food, sugary drinks, alcohol, screen hours and little movement. In menopause, fat is redistributed and lean mass falls, but weight doesn’t rise faster (more in chapter 38). Energy expenditure drops noticeably only after sixty, and partly because of muscle loss.

Steps. The number “10,000” is the name of a 1965 Japanese pedometer. The benefit rises smoothly and plateaus: around 6–8 thousand steps after sixty, 8–10 thousand when younger; each extra thousand from a low starting point is linked to about a sixth lower mortality. All of this is cohorts, not trials.

Mood. In depression, exercise reduces symptoms and in a few small trials is nearly on par with psychotherapy and antidepressants; in the highest-quality studies the effect is smaller, and blinded comparisons are almost nonexistent. The most noticeable effects come from walking or running, yoga and strength training (low certainty); the effect is larger at higher intensity. In one trial in people over fifty, four months of aerobic exercise produced a result comparable to an antidepressant (the drug worked faster), and ten months later those who exercised had fewer relapses.

Alcohol: heart protection was a counting error

Moderate drinkers looked healthier than nondrinkers because the “nondrinker” group included people who had quit due to illness. When this error is removed, small doses show no mortality benefit, and in women harm starts at lower amounts7. Genetic “natural experiments” — people with gene variants that make them drink less — show that stroke and blood pressure rise directly with alcohol, while heart protection is barely visible. The threshold above which mortality among drinkers rises is about 100 grams of pure alcohol a week; the level that minimizes harm to health across the population as a whole is zero (after forty the curve is J-shaped, with its minimum at small doses, while for young people the minimum is near zero). In Russia, the sharp swings in male mortality in 1984–1994 coincide with changes in alcohol consumption.

Character: a trait as an accumulator of habits

Conscientiousness — persistence, self-control, planning — is linked to a long life: in the least conscientious third of people, the risk of death is about a third higher, even after adjusting for education, marriage and habits8. The effect is comparable in size to that of social class and childhood intelligence. Part of the link runs through behavior (less tobacco, alcohol and risky driving, more movement), part through shared roots such as class and childhood intelligence. As a predictor of any one person’s fate, it is weak.

Neuroticism (a tendency toward anxiety and heavy emotions) is more complicated. Unadjusted, it slightly raises the risk of death, but adjusted for how a person rates their own health, it slightly lowers it, especially in those who consider themselves unhealthy. Maybe this is vigilance, maybe a statistical artifact. Optimism and a sense of purpose are linked to lower mortality, but they were measured once, and illness could itself have lowered both optimism and the chances of survival.

What this means for the self

Given. The cortisol rhythm, the age-related restructuring of sleep, the loss of strength over the years, the genes that decide how the body handles alcohol. These are not chosen. Part of what looks like choice is given too: class, place in the hierarchy and childhood intelligence pull both habits and traits along with them.

Built. Almost everything else is years of repetition. The bill at fifty is not a verdict on the body but a record of behavior — and often behavior imposed by circumstances: smoking and little movement are more common among low-grade employees, and that is an environment of little control and little support, not just weak will. That is why the body is a poor judge of morals. The bill shows what your living conditions were, not what kind of person you are.

The ego at work. The bill is unpleasant to read, and the ego puts up defenses. The first is a convenient explanation that lifts responsibility: “my metabolism slowed down,” “my adrenals are exhausted,” “five hours is enough for me” (the people for whom that is naturally enough are a handful, and they carry rare family gene variants). The label doesn’t just soothe; it hides the real cause: apnea, depression, muscle loss. The second defense is the reverse, self-flagellation: illness as proof that “I lived badly.” The data on purpose and optimism don’t support this: a link is not guilt, and a sick person doesn’t need to prove they “didn’t want to live enough.”

The third is the “I’m handling it” mask. The belief that stress is harmful predicted death only in combination with high stress (observational); you can reframe a racing heart before a hard task as mobilization, and this slightly improves performance on the task (not tested for health in adults). But a mindset doesn’t treat the environment: with strained work and no control, debt or abuse, it is the load itself that needs to change, and “the right view of stress” can become a reason to stay in it.

Where freedom lies. Character is not set in stone. Conscientiousness and emotional stability grow most between twenty and forty; deliberate work on a trait shifts it noticeably within a few months, and friends confirm the growth. Whether this extends life hasn’t been tested. Muscles respond to load after fifty too, insomnia yields to behavioral therapy without pills, and steps are counted from today’s level. Your self-image at fifty can be built not from a report on the past but from the fact that the body is still able to rebuild itself (what happens to the brain along the way is in chapter 41).

Myths

Myth: after thirty or forty, metabolism drops, and that’s where the weight comes from. In fact: energy expenditure adjusted for body size is stable from 20 to 60; weight is gained from food, alcohol, sitting and muscle loss.

Myth: chronic stress “burns out” the adrenal glands. In fact: no such condition has been found; the label gets in the way of finding the real cause of fatigue, and “adrenal” hormones taken without need can themselves suppress the glands for months9.

Myth: a glass of wine a day protects the heart. In fact: the effect was created by sick former drinkers in the comparison group; the best methods barely see heart protection, while the risk of stroke and high blood pressure rises with every dose.

Myth: health starts at 10,000 steps. In fact: that’s the name of a Japanese pedometer; the benefit rises smoothly and plateaus earlier, and any increase from a low level is linked to benefit10.

Who plays on this

The “stress” market sells not a change of conditions but a substance or a test. For saliva “adrenal” panels and “adrenal support” protocols there is no evidence of benefit. Ashwagandha is one of the best-selling herbs in the US, with revenue in the millions: in trials it lowers cortisol, but in one synthesis it did not lower perceived stress; liver injury with jaundice has been described, and in Denmark its sale is considered illegal. Melatonin is sold as harmless: the actual content in supplements differed from the label by −83% to +478%11, and calls to US poison control centers about children ingesting melatonin rose more than sixfold over nine years12. “Testosterone for every man over forty” — the benefit is moderate and only with symptoms and a low level; in a large trial, atrial fibrillation, kidney injury and blood clots in the lungs were more common.

Warning signs: a promise to fix “one hormone”; a test invented by the seller; treatment sold by the same person who makes the diagnosis. And one more: an employer or coach who suggests you “learn to love stress” instead of changing the overload.

What to do about it

  1. Strength work, 30–60 minutes a week. All major muscle groups, with the weight increasing gradually. Protein after 65 — at least 1.0–1.2 g per kilogram of body weight a day (with kidney disease — with a doctor; more on food in What Works in Nutrition). Sign it’s working: your working weights and grip strength go up, and you get up from a chair faster.
  2. Steps from your own level. For a week, carry your phone or a pedometer, take the average, add 1–2 thousand steps a day, and work toward 7 thousand (6–8 after sixty). Sign: your weekly average and walking speed go up. Steps don’t replace strength work.
  3. Insomnia — behavioral therapy, not a pill. This is the first line in all major guidelines; in people over sixty, two months of this therapy halved the risk of new or recurrent depression over the next three years (one trial). The core: a sleep diary for 1–2 weeks, a fixed wake-up time, time in bed roughly equal to how long you actually sleep, extended as sleep consolidates; bed only for sleep; if you lie awake for a long time, get up. In the first weeks you may sleep less and attention dips — for drivers and people with epilepsy, bipolar disorder or suspected apnea, only under a specialist’s supervision. Sign: the diary shows faster sleep onset and less wakefulness at night.
  4. Alcohol in grams. For a week, write down the grams of pure alcohol (a standard drink is 10 g). The goal is under 100 g a week plus alcohol-free days; across the population as a whole, harm is lowest at zero.

Questions for yourself

  • What explanation for my fatigue or weight is convenient for me, even though it’s untested?
  • Where in my life is there little control and a lot of judgment — and is it the load that needs to change, or my view of it?
  • Which of my habits are character traits, and which are the conditions I live in?
  • When did my muscles last get hard work that wasn’t out of necessity?

When to see a specialist

Persistent exhaustion — see a doctor to rule out adrenal insufficiency, depression and sleep apnea. Severe depression or suicidal thoughts — treatment is a must, with exercise only as an addition. Daily heavy drinking shouldn’t be stopped abruptly without medical help; with uncontrolled heart disease or joint problems, get a doctor’s clearance before taking on heavier loads.

Part IX. 56 and Beyond: Legacy

41. The aging brain: what fades, what grows, what depends on you

“The brain peaks at 25” is a conclusion drawn from a few speed tests. Vocabulary keeps growing until 50–65, and the ability to read other people’s emotions plateaus between 40 and 601. Brain aging is not a single descent but several curves with different peaks. How much of the loss you notice depends not on the age in your passport but on what has accumulated in your brain and what has been built in it.

How it works

Different abilities, different peaks

Abilities fall into two groups. “Fluid” ones: processing speed, working memory (how much you can hold in mind at once), reasoning. “Crystallized” ones: words, knowledge, experience.

Speed peaks in late adolescence and then declines almost linearly throughout adult life. Working memory holds until about 30. The decline in memory and reasoning speeds up later, while vocabulary grows into your 60s. Once you correct for people getting used to the tests, the first losses show up as early as 20–30.

In a British study of civil servants, reasoning declined over ten years even in people aged 45–49, and markedly more in those aged 65–70; vocabulary did not change2. Comparing different generations at a single point in time overstates the decline: older generations had less schooling, and this gap in schooling is larger for women. Men over 65 lost slightly more on average.

Reserve and maintenance

Why do some people think at 80 the way they did at 50, while others don’t? Two ideas explain the difference. Reserve is the efficiency and redundancy of the networks the brain has managed to build: they keep damage invisible from the outside for longer. Maintenance is how little damage has accumulated in the first place: amyloid and tau (proteins that clump into plaques and tangles in Alzheimer’s disease), vascular damage. The second is considered the main factor for memory in old age (a theory backed by brain imaging, but still a theory).

In this framework, dementia is the result of decades of accumulating damage set against the capacity to compensate for it. It is not a sudden disease of eighty-year-olds but a long process to which early life, midlife and old age each contribute differently.

Superagers are people over 80 with the memory of 50- to 60-year-olds. They have a thicker anterior cingulate cortex (a region linking attention, motivation and feelings), more of a particular type of large neuron, and are less likely to carry the risky APOE ε4 gene variant. Their cortex thins about half as fast as their peers’. The samples are dozens of people, and what makes them this way is unknown.

What grows

With age, attention and memory start to favor the good over the bad; a pooled analysis of a hundred studies confirms this3. The effect is stronger when your mind is not busy with a task. The explanation is socioemotional selectivity theory (more in chapter 43).

This is a shift in attention, not a guarantee of happiness. Illness, poverty, loneliness and loss remain separate blows, and the positivity filter does not protect against them. “Wisdom” has been measured narrowly, as the ability to reason about social conflicts; the data do not show that older people are wiser in everything.

What moves dementia risk

In 2024 the Lancet commission named 14 factors you can influence: little education, hearing loss, high “bad” cholesterol (LDL), depression, head injury, physical inactivity, diabetes, smoking, high blood pressure, obesity, heavy drinking, loneliness, polluted air, untreated vision loss. Removing them all could theoretically prevent up to 45% of cases4. The largest shares belong to hearing loss and cholesterol.

This is a ceiling, not a forecast. The calculation assumes all the factors are causes, although that is not proven for all of them; the shares overlap and do not add up. Individual trials deliver much less. The two-year FINGER program (diet, exercise, cognitive training, vascular risk control) slightly improved thinking; six years of vascular risk management in the Netherlands did not reduce dementia. Lowering blood pressure to a systolic (top) number below 120 reduced mild cognitive impairment (the stage between normal and dementia), but the result for dementia itself was not statistically significant.

An unexpected finding: in Wales, the shingles vaccine was offered by date of birth, which created a natural experiment. Over seven years, vaccinated people had 20% fewer new dementia diagnoses5 (one study, strong design).

Risk is already falling on its own: in Europe and the US, incidence drops with each decade, and in Framingham it fell by almost half over 30 years — but only among people with at least a high school education. The number of people with dementia will still grow, roughly tripling by 2050, because more people are living to old age.

Hearing: a channel of its own

Hearing loss is linked to dementia: every 10 decibels of loss adds roughly 16% to the risk (observational studies totaling one and a half million people)6. The mechanism is not proven.

The decisive trial gave a mixed result. Hearing aids did not slow cognitive decline over three years overall, but in people at high risk they did, by roughly half7. This is a subgroup, participants knew which group they were in, and some of the tests were given aloud. A hearing aid treats hearing and conversation; protecting the brain is a possible bonus for some people.

Body and brain

In people over 50, exercise improves thinking when sessions last 45–60 minutes at moderate intensity or higher. In one trial, aerobic training slightly enlarged the hippocampus (a memory structure) in older adults; pooled analyses point more to protection against shrinkage than to growth. Once dementia had begun, a four-month exercise program did not slow the decline. Movement and sleep are covered in detail in chapter 40.

There are observational data that sexual activity in later life is linked to better thinking, and that this link works differently in men and women. Whether it is cause or effect is unknown: healthy people are more likely to keep both. For more on a couple’s bodies in maturity, see Alchemy Without Magic.

Genes, sex and where they end

One copy of the APOE ε4 gene variant raises the odds of Alzheimer’s disease several times over, most strongly in people of East Asian descent. With two copies, almost everyone shows signs of the pathology by 65, and researchers call this a distinct genetic form of the disease. These are odds, not a sentence.

There are noticeably more women than men with dementia worldwide. The main reason is that women live longer: incidence between the sexes diverges only after 80–85. The dip in learning during perimenopause is temporary — more in chapter 38.

Lifespan overall is only weakly inherited: about a quarter according to twin studies, and less than 10% once you correct for spouses resembling each other in lifestyle8. Genes matter more only at the extreme: siblings of centenarians are several times more likely to reach a hundred.

What this means for the self
