Myth: the more you give, the happier you are, and the healthier your relationships. In fact: the benefit of helping is small and short-lived, and the spending experiments replicate only about half the time10, 11. Sacrifice doesn’t heal a relationship in which you’re being used.

Myth: pressure always breeds protest. In fact: pressure triggers anger and counterarguments, but a person can comply anyway6. And protest isn’t the same as the right decision.

Who plays on this

The market. Timers, “almost gone,” “thousands have already bought”: scarcity and the group. Sign: urgency you can’t verify.

Platforms. The commercial incentive that has been measured most is attention and engagement, not permanent capture of beliefs. Sign: notifications you didn’t ask for and a bottomless feed.

Scammers. A call or message you didn’t initiate, pressure, a request to pay now. Sign: you’re being rushed.

Groups and people who demand sacrifice. The levers of “belonging” and “being good” (high-control groups, chapter 25; living next to a narcissist, chapter 24). Sign: you’re asked to give more and more, and help without limits is presented as the norm.

What to do about it

1. Rules before pressure. Decide in advance: no payments during a call or message you weren’t expecting; call back only on a number you found yourself; a second opinion before any major decision; a pause before signing. If you’ve already invested, ask: “If I hadn’t paid before, would I choose this now?” It’s working if a rule has at least once stopped a decision that would later have turned out to be a mistake.

2. Learn to discern, not to suspect. Practice taking techniques apart on fresh examples, always mixed in with honest messages. Count not only the manipulations you caught but also the false alarms: if you’ve stopped believing the truth too, your protection has gone off course. Checking a source: leave the page, find out who’s writing it, trace it to the original source.

3. Change access instead of demanding heroism from yourself. Keep one channel for urgent things, batch all other notifications into three bundles a day, and test this for two weeks. If you’re cutting back on social media, cut one platform, for a set period, and decide in advance what will replace the useful contacts. Compare with a baseline week: screen time from your phone’s logs, sleep, stress, important things missed. It’s working if stress is lower and important things aren’t getting lost.

4. Help with a limit. Ask what kind of help is needed; name your limit (“an hour a week,” “this amount”); after a while, check whether the person is actually better off. For your own goals, separate “I want this” from “I have to, or I’ll be ashamed,” and find one doable step. Sign that it’s working: helping doesn’t build resentment, and your goals move without self-coercion. How to tell what works on yourself from what’s merely trendy: chapter 46.

Questions for yourself

  • What decision did I make in the past year under someone else’s urgency, and what rule would have stopped it?
  • Where does my “I’m just honest” actually mean “it’s more convenient for me”?
  • Whom do I help without asking whether it’s needed, and what am I buying for myself with it?
  • Which of my goals are held up by guilt rather than desire?

Part XI. Bonus

48. Horoscopes, fate and shifting responsibility: why the self is so eager to hand over the wheel

In a double-blind experiment (neither the astrologers nor the participants knew the answer), astrologers had to guess from a natal chart which of three psychological profiles belonged to its owner. They got it right 34% of the time against 33% by chance, though they themselves expected noticeably more1. Meanwhile, for decades roughly one in four Americans has believed in horoscopes, and the share barely moves2. It isn’t about the stars. The self needs someone to hold the wheel when its own hands aren’t enough — the sky, a personality type, fate, childhood, the crowd around it.

How it works

Loss of control looks for outside support

The chain goes like this: uncertainty or stress lowers the sense of control, the desire for a definite answer and structure grows, and the mind grabs onto a ready-made system. The craving for certainty is a separate, measurable trait: some people need an answer fast and then won’t let go of it; others tolerate “I don’t know” more calmly.

Each link has some evidence behind it. During the Gulf War, magical thinking was more common in areas under missile attack, especially among people who tolerate ambiguity poorly. In experiments that undermined people’s sense of control, belief that the future can be predicted rose, and that belief in turn restored their sense of control. Reduced control also strengthened belief in a God who runs the world and support for the state.

The central link is weaker than textbooks say. The headline finding “without control, people see patterns that aren’t there” failed to replicate in seven later experiments, and a review found signs that successes get published while failures don’t3. A link between loss of control and the search for structure probably exists, but its strength is unknown.

A horoscope is a ready answer to that demand: twelve labels, a forecast for every day, always at hand. The label becomes a piece of identity (“I’m a Scorpio”) and a currency in conversation (“so are you compatible?”).

Why someone else’s text feels accurate

In Forer’s famous experiment, each student was handed the same personality sketch, assembled from astrology books, presented as their personal test result. They rated its accuracy close to the maximum (one class, no control group). The mechanism: a phrase triggers a search in memory, memory finds fitting episodes, mismatches are forgotten; nobody compares the text with other people’s, and the feeling of recognition is taken as proof. Flattering words and an authoritative source work best.

This isn’t a failing of the gullible. Skeptics also rated a text higher if it was labeled astrological, and a flattering horoscope moved them toward belief4. Most psychology students judged a fake online test effective.

It also works in reverse: the label rewrites how people describe themselves. People described themselves with the traits of their sign only if they knew something about astrology. This is exactly what explains an old “positive” finding about signs: the effect shows up in those who had read the descriptions and disappears in those who hadn’t.

The same goes for typologies. The four letters of the MBTI assume that people split into two camps on each scale, but scores form a hump in the middle, not two piles; the scales themselves partly overlap with four of the “Big Five” traits. Studies of the Enneagram usually find fewer than nine factors, and the nine types have never once emerged from the data. For Human Design and numerology, no tests could be found. Measured traits are stable and predict a lot in life: people’s rank order on them grows steadily more stable from childhood to ages 50–70. But on any test, flattering feedback nudges you toward agreeing.

What was tested about the stars

The logic of the test is simple: if the sky at birth shapes character, people born minutes apart should be alike. In London, researchers compared more than two thousand people born in March 1958, on average less than five minutes apart, on dozens of traits at ages 11, 16 and 23 — from intelligence to occupation. The similarity was zero5. In two large samples, neither birth date nor sign was linked to personality or intelligence. In Swedish registries, couples “compatible” by sign did not marry more often or divorce less often. A review of more than forty controlled studies with hundreds of astrologers gives an effect of about zero, and agreement among astrologers themselves is weak5. Astrology’s defenders dispute Carlson’s design and point to small “emergent” effects, but no independent preregistered test exists.

Birth date does mean something — for reasons that have nothing to do with the stars. Children who end up the youngest in their class because of the school-entry cutoff lag noticeably behind in fourth grade. Winter births slightly raise the risk of schizophrenia (by a few percent, probably through conditions during pregnancy). And belief itself changes demographics: in the “year of the fire horse” (1966), Japanese couples avoided having children, and the birth rate dropped noticeably.

Locus of control: who I think is driving

The psychologist Rotter called the general belief “my actions lead to results” an “internal locus,” and “chance, powerful people or fate decide” an “external” one. The belief grows out of experience: an action worked — I believe action works — I try harder — it worked again.

In a British cohort, children with a more internal locus at age 10 were less likely at 30 to be obese and to have poor mental well-being6. Around the world, an external locus is moderately linked to depression and anxiety, more weakly in collectivist cultures. But the direction of cause is unclear. The sense of control predicted health only after age 65, not in middle age; poverty and illness themselves erode the sense of control. Where income and education really do limit choice, an external locus is partly just accurate. Both extremes are errors: the illusion of control — acting as if chance yields to skill — replicates reliably.

How the ego shifts the blame

Success is my doing; failure is down to circumstances. This bias has been found in almost every sample across hundreds of studies; it is strongest in children and older adults, weaker in Asians and in people with depression7. A threat to self-esteem strengthens it. The cost: you learn less from mistakes.

The excuse can even be set up in advance: skipping preparation, drinking, putting things off — so that failure “doesn’t count.” This self-handicapping is linked to worse academic performance and feeds a vicious circle.

In a group, responsibility gets divided by the number of people present. People put less effort into group tasks than into solo ones. But the legend of the indifferent crowd is exaggerated: in camera footage from three countries, at least one bystander intervened in almost every conflict, and the more people present, the likelier someone helped8.

Helplessness isn’t learned — it’s the default

The original theory held that uncontrollable pain teaches you that nothing depends on you. New animal data turn it upside down: passivity after prolonged uncontrollable stress is the default mode, and what is learned is control. The prefrontal cortex — the front part of the brain that holds plans — notices that action works and inhibits an ancient passivity center in the brainstem9. In humans, depression is linked to explaining failures as “it’s me, forever, in everything” (a link, not a cause). Children who respond to failure by looking for a cause (“I’m not capable”) give up; those who look for the next move often improve their results.

The belief “I can do it” is linked to success but largely reflects it: within one person, past results predict confidence more strongly than confidence predicts future results. When confidence was raised in experiments, behavior did change. So it is both a consequence and a lever.

The victim stance

Real harm is real: children who were bullied are more likely to have anxiety disorders as adults — even after adjusting for earlier problems. A hard childhood raises risks at the population level but predicts almost nothing about a specific person: the accuracy of the prediction is barely better than a coin toss. The past matters, but it doesn’t decide.

Researchers have described a distinct tendency to live in the victim stance: a need for recognition, a sense of moral rightness, weak empathy for the other side and rumination over grievances. It is linked to a sense of entitlement to revenge — the data so far come from one group, mostly from Israel, and the construct is disputed. The state can be induced in the lab: after recalling an injustice, people become more selfish.

Free will and fatalism

The headline experiment “those who don’t believe in free will cheat more” was not confirmed either in a preregistered multi-lab test or in a review of many experiments: reading about determinism changes what people say, not what they do10. “Neuroscience has proven that the decision is made before awareness” isn’t an established finding either: the readiness signal may simply be built up from the brain’s random noise.

Fatalism about health is linked to behavior: those who agree that almost nothing protects against cancer (roughly one in four Americans) exercise less and smoke more often. But often “what will be, will be” is a way of saying you lack money, time or access, not a firm conviction.

What this means for the self

The body sets the default: until control is proven, passivity (in animals). It seems the brain doesn’t start out believing in itself. The sense that “I’m driving” has to be earned through experiences in which action worked. This may also explain the urge to hand over the wheel under stress: not a character flaw but a fallback to the default mode.

Ties supply ready-made explanations. Culture hands out a vocabulary of fate — the sky, karma, “it was written,” “that’s just the system.” In Russia, belief in fate fell by more than half over twenty years, while 18–24-year-olds believe in horoscopes almost twice as often as older people. An external locus in different groups partly reflects real walls and partly a habitual way of talking.

The story is where the ego does its work. Every outside wheel solves a self-esteem problem. A sign or type is a ready-made self-image, and a flattering one. Circumstances are an alibi for failure. Fate removes guilt for inaction. Victim status brings recognition and the moral high ground. Each of these defenses relieves pain now and takes away some freedom later: if the sky is to blame, there’s nothing to change.

Freedom here lies in telling two questions apart: “Who is to blame for the past?” and “What do I do next?” Self-blame for a past action did not help survivors of violence; what helped was a sense of control over recovery11. Responsibility aimed forward, not at the past self, is the kind that works. Self-compassion after a slip led to more time spent preparing, not to laziness.

Sex differences here are averages, and the distributions overlap heavily: women on average believe in horoscopes more often (35% vs. 18% in the US)2 and, according to a review, more often give “external” answers on control scales; men cut their effort more in group tasks. The mechanism is the same for everyone.

Myths

Myth: only the gullible believe in horoscopes. In fact: skeptics also find a text more accurate when it’s labeled astrological, and a flattering forecast moves them toward belief4.

Myth: young people are flocking to astrology. In fact: in the US, belief barely changed over seven years; app sales are growing, but sales aren’t belief2.

Myth: take 100% responsibility for everything. In fact: self-blame for the past did not protect and came with more suffering; what helped was control over what to do next11.

Myth: a lucky charm or ritual improves performance. In fact: the “lucky golf ball” experiment was not confirmed in large replications, and headline papers on the benefits of rituals were retracted in 2024. What does work is a short routine of concrete actions before the task12.

Who plays on this

The prediction market. In 2019, revenue of the top ten US astrology apps grew by two-thirds, to $40 million. Cases of addiction to fortune-telling have been described; people in crisis are the most vulnerable. Sign: a forecast is sold as a decision about health, money or marriage.

Typology sellers. Letter or number types are used in hiring and matchmaking, though there is no evidence they work for this. Sign: you’re sorted into a category, and decisions are based on it rather than on what you actually do. For how “power” and “energy” are sold, see chapter 29.

Power and big systems. When people feel they control nothing, they more readily support a strong state and a God who runs things. For how this works in high-control groups, see chapter 25.

What to do about it

  1. A blind test of any reading. Take your horoscope, type or “chart” and two belonging to other people, remove the names and signs, shuffle them, and pick yours. This is the same design as the scientific tests in which people did no better than chance5. Measure: how many decisions you made in a month based on a chart or type. For a quick check, ask: “Would this sentence fit a random passerby?”
  2. After a failure — write about the causes you can change. Not “that’s how I am,” but strategy, preparation, effort, plus one next step. For some students who had failed an exam, this improved results, at the cost of feeling worse at first. It doesn’t apply if the failure really was beyond your control (illness, discrimination). Measure: the share of failures after which you changed your method, not just your mood.
  3. A routine instead of a lucky charm. Before an important action — 3–6 concrete steps (a breath, a cue word, an aiming point), the same each time. In sports this works even under pressure12; for exams and public speaking it hasn’t been tested.
  4. Self-compassion plus one move. Name what happened, tell yourself what you’d tell a friend, and choose one corrective action. Students who forgave themselves for procrastinating procrastinated less the next time. Without the corrective step, the exercise can turn into comfort without change.

Questions for yourself

  • What decision am I putting off right now because I’m waiting for a sign?
  • Which explanation of my failures is convenient for me — and what does it forbid me to change?
  • Where am I genuinely limited, and where have I just gotten used to saying so?
  • If my type or sign turned out to be different, what would I stop allowing myself?

When to see a specialist

If you put off treatment or spend money you can’t easily spare because of a forecast, if the pull toward fortune-telling keeps growing and the anxiety doesn’t go away — that’s a reason to see a psychotherapist for methods with proven benefit (more in chapter 46).

49. Masturbation: what almost everyone does and no one talks about — and what it says about the self

The most common sexual act humans perform is also the most hidden. In Britain, about three men in four and about four women in ten reported it in the past month, and after 70 it is more common than any sex with a partner. Yet public life shows almost no sign that anyone else does it. The topic shows well how the self lives in two versions: the one that acts and the one that talks about itself out loud.

How it works

How much, and who

The number depends on the question: “ever,” “in the past year” or “in the past month,” “touched yourself for pleasure” or “to orgasm,” face to face with an interviewer or in an anonymous questionnaire. The most reliable data come from national surveys. In Britain around the turn of the 2000s, about three men in four and more than a third of women aged 16–44 had done it in the past four weeks; eleven years later the shares had risen slightly for both sexes, and the gap between them had not narrowed1. In urban China in 2000, past-year figures were 35% of men and 13% of women, with people in their twenties closer to Western numbers2.

Age. It begins around 14 (based on data from German women and Korean military recruits): by 14, about two-thirds of boys and fewer than half of girls in the US had done it. Recent practice rises with age in boys but not in girls. Nor does it fade with age: at 60–75, four to six men in ten and about three women in ten in four European countries had done it in the past month. Among American women aged 40–65, it is less common after menopause, and orgasm occurs in about eight cases out of ten (across the whole sample).

Birth cohort matters more than calendar year. In Finland and Sweden, each successive generation did it more than the one before; in Estonia the same wave arrived about twenty years later, in St. Petersburg about thirty years later. The rise barely depended on whether people were in a relationship. No national data for Russia newer than 1996 could be found. Three forces drive it: early exposure to sexuality, access to partnered sex, and cultural permission.

Why surveys are off — in both directions

A sensitive question raises the cost of looking “abnormal.” Answers slide toward what a person considers acceptable for their group, more so in front of an interviewer and where the act is judged more harshly (for example, for women). In an experiment with a fake “lie detector” (people were told lies would be detected), sex differences in what people admitted nearly vanished; under anonymity they were moderate; when the experimenter could see the answers, they were largest. The effect was strongest precisely on questions about masturbation3.

Men on average report stronger sex drive (these are averages; the distributions overlap heavily), but after correcting for biased responding the difference shrinks by about a fifth4. Part of the gap between the sexes is real, part is how people answer. There is also a bias in the other direction: volunteers are less inhibited than people who decline, so voluntary online surveys inflate the numbers. Read any figure as a range.

The body: what happens and what doesn’t

After orgasm — in men and women, alone or with a partner — prolactin, a hormone linked to satiety, stays elevated for more than an hour. Arousal without orgasm does not do this. This may be a satiety signal that briefly dampens desire (small lab experiments). After sex with a partner, the prolactin rise is several times larger than after masturbation.

In one small experiment, testosterone did not change with orgasm; in another pilot, orgasm only smoothed its daily decline. Three weeks of abstinence raised the baseline level, but the response to orgasm stayed the same5. The famous “testosterone peak on day seven of abstinence” comes from a small study that was retracted in 2021; no replication has been found. Sperm is produced continuously — tens to hundreds of millions of cells a day; there is no basis for “saving” it for the sake of health.

Documented physical harm is limited. First, an “atypical” style: strong pressure or stimulation unlike partnered sex. Men with this style had erectile dysfunction more often (one cross-sectional study in China; cause and effect not separated). Second, the rare post-orgasmic illness syndrome: a flu-like state for 2–7 days after any ejaculation, probably immune in origin. The link to the prostate is weak: men who ejaculate often, by any means, have slightly less prostate cancer, but no link has been shown for masturbation as such. The data are observational.

Sleep and mood: many people are sure orgasm helps them fall asleep. In a diary study, this held only for sex with a partner. In a small experiment with sleep recordings, both gave a small benefit.

How a prohibition became “medicine”

In the 18th century, a moral prohibition was turned into a medical danger. The fear was given a bodily story — the loss of “vital fluid.” People took the prohibition on as their own. Medicine later abandoned the causal claim, but the fear stayed in the culture and keeps returning in new forms.

Tissot’s treatise (1760) was the first strictly medical text on the “harm.” Its power lay in coherent argument: readers experienced the prohibition as their own conclusion6. The roots of the doctrine of “conserving semen” go back to Ayurveda, around 600 BCE. In the 19th century, psychiatrists seriously described “masturbatory insanity,” and charlatans sold the idea to the public. In Russia, Anton Chekhov’s 1883 medical report shows “sexual neurasthenia from onanism” as a routine concept in the medicine of the day.

The fear of “losing semen” is alive today. In general clinics in Lahore, 30% of men complained of it, and the complaint went hand in hand with depression and fatigue7. In South Asia this is called dhat syndrome, but cases were described earlier in Britain, the US and Australia: this anxiety is not tied to one culture. According to one paper (a letter to a journal), a similar picture appears in Western “semen retention” and NoFap groups.

Where guilt comes from

Guilt seems to follow upbringing more than the act itself. It is higher with fundamentalism and deep religiosity, varies across cultures, and is lower among people who grew up in families with a relaxed attitude toward sex. There, “problematic” masturbation (more than one would like, with distress) is also less common. In a small survey, American students picked up stigma more often from media and peers than from parents and teachers. Guilt also occurs without religion: it was reported by most of the married Chinese men at an outpatient clinic who masturbated.

The key mechanism is moral incongruence. Distress is better explained by the gap between values and behavior than by the behavior itself8, 9 (the studies are mainly about porn, not masturbation). In a US national sample, about one man in nine and one woman in thirty said “I am addicted to porn.” This was predicted not only by frequency but also by religiosity, youth and that same incongruence. Suppressing thoughts makes them rebound, as a meta-analysis shows. The data are cross-sectional: it cannot be ruled out that for some people guilt comes from a real loss of control.

Childhood

Touching oneself is body exploration and self-soothing; it appears before any sexual meaning. Infants can have “gratification episodes”: the child stiffens, grunts, sweats. On average they begin around 10–11 months, and two-thirds of these children were referred to a neurologist on suspicion of epilepsy. In all children followed up (there were few), the episodes resolved by ages 1–310, 11. Pediatricians hold that most children show various sexual behaviors at some point. The warning sign is not the touching itself but compulsiveness, harm, behavior out of keeping with age, and what is going on in the family. Meaning comes later, with puberty and with how adults react. First desire, porn and teenage shame are covered in chapter 18.

In a couple

Three models: masturbation replaces missing sex, complements it, or lives separately. On average, replacement is more common in men and complementing in women. This is the prevailing pattern, not a law, and it depends on whether the person is satisfied. In Norway, both sexes had a group with “a lot of masturbation and high sexual satisfaction.” In Britain, when partnered sex is not going well, both sexes tend toward replacement. The behavior alone says little about dissatisfaction: it is more useful to ask whether the partners’ desire matches.

The best-tested practical result in this area is in women. “Directed masturbation” is a step-by-step program: learn your body, find touch that feels good, reach orgasm alone, then carry it over into sex with a partner. For those who have never had an orgasm, it is the most consistently supported psychological treatment12. In a controlled study, orgasm became attainable for 90% of participants versus 53% with standard treatment (small samples, old studies). Carrying it over to the couple takes separate practice: the more alike the techniques used alone and with a partner, the better orgasm goes with a partner (an observational link).

What this means for the self

The body — given. A possible satiety signal, continuous sperm production, touching oneself from infancy — all of this exists before words and before morality. The body does not know this is “shameful”: no physiological link between the act and guilt has been found.

Ties — the meaning came from outside. A parent’s reaction to a child touching themselves; peers’ jokes; 18th-century ideas returning on forums; a religious norm. Each layer delivers a verdict before the person has a chance to ask the question. Differences between countries seem largely to reflect permission, not desire: the Estonian wave followed the Finnish one by twenty years, and biology did not change in that time.

Story — what the self tells about itself. The same act can be filed as “weakness,” “dirt,” “relapse,” “addiction,” “self-care” or nothing at all. The word you choose becomes part of your self-image. If the prohibition sits in your values and your behavior doesn’t match, a crack appears: “I am not who I think I am.” The ego tries to close it in familiar ways — shame, strict control, the label “addiction.” The label sounds medical, but people call themselves “addicted” because of a mismatch with their own rules, not only because of frequency.

The mask — and two versions of the self. By its nature, masturbation lives backstage: the image for others is built without it. This can produce a system error (pluralistic ignorance): everyone sees the others’ silence, decides they themselves are the exception, and stays silent too — feeding everyone else the same false signal. For this topic, the error has not been measured directly, but the setup favors it. According to research on secrets in general (not this topic), the cost of a secret is not only the effort of hiding it but also the thoughts that keep returning to it: it was the frequency of those thoughts that predicted worse well-being13.

Self-knowledge. Knowing your own arousal pays off: treatment for women’s orgasm problems is built on it. That shame worsens self-knowledge is a reasonable hypothesis, not directly tested. How a partner changes body chemistry and the self is covered in chapter 30; sex and a couple’s hormones, in Alchemy Without Magic.

Where freedom lies. Guilt seems largely learned — which means it can be revisited (the data are correlational; programs to reduce guilt have not been tested). Freedom lies neither in “allow yourself everything” nor in “forbid it.” It lies in separating three questions — am I losing control, does this contradict my values, does this concern my partner — and answering each one on its own. Handing this choice to a doctrine of “vital energy” is the same move as handing the wheel to a horoscope (chapter 48).

Myths

Myth: It harms body and mind: weakness, acne, blindness, insanity.
In fact: No controlled study has shown harm from ordinary masturbation; these ideas are a legacy of 18th–19th-century degeneration theory. The harm comes from the belief itself: shame, anxiety, punishing children, paid abstinence courses.

Myth: Semen retention stores up energy and raises testosterone.
In fact: The “day-seven peak” study was retracted. A month of abstinence in “No Nut November” did not change a single measure of sexual well-being, and sex shortly before exercise did not change athletic performance (mostly in men).

Myth: If someone in a relationship does it, they aren’t getting enough sex.
In fact: Most people in relationships do it too. The link to dissatisfaction is weak and differs between the sexes; some people have plenty of both.

Myth: “I’m addicted” means it’s about quantity.
In fact: A high risk of compulsive, poorly controlled sexual behavior affects about one person in twenty, and that count included all kinds of sex, not just this one. The International Classification of Diseases explicitly states that distress arising solely from moral judgment is not a disorder.

Who plays on this

Money is made both from shame and from removing it. Shame sells “retention” and “reboot” courses: from a few dollars to about a hundred and fifty a week. None of the instructors studied had medical or psychological training; most cited only their own experience14. In one online survey, motivation for abstinence was predicted by belief in “harmfulness,” conservatism and low trust in science, not by problems with the behavior. An analysis of forum accounts suggests that the “relapse–recovery” frame itself keeps the distress going. Warning signs: promises of energy, confidence, a cure for erections or depression; “semen” as a resource; payment by the week.

Removing shame sells toys, “self-care” and apps. It is a market on the order of tens of billions of dollars a year, but the figures come from the sellers themselves, and they vary twofold. The “sexual wellness” boom does not prove health benefits. According to the available data, what protects is not purchases but accurate information and the absence of moral conflict.

What to do about it

Break it into three questions. Separately: control (I want less and can’t manage it; sleep, work, relationships suffer), values (it contradicts my beliefs), partner (does it concern my partner). Track frequency, how hard it feels afterward, and what is happening in your life (a personal experiment, not a tested method). Sign it’s working: it becomes clear which of the three questions is yours. The answer is about you, not proof of a general theory.

Don’t fight the thought. Suppression produces a rebound; this has been shown separately in religious teenagers. There are no tested ways to “do it less” (there is one small pilot of treatment for compulsive behavior without a control group). Sign it’s getting better: fewer intrusive returns of the thoughts, not just longer stretches without a “relapse.”

Tell a safe person. People who have confided real secrets have better well-being — through a sense of “I can cope” and less frequently returning thoughts (observations about secrets in general; not tested for this topic). The measure is how often those thoughts come. This doesn’t apply if the listener is hostile or if the behavior is punished where you live.

When a young child touches themselves. Stay calm, no shaming words. Explain that this is done in private. Pay attention not to the touching itself but to the frequency, the setting, trauma and sudden changes. If you can’t tell what an infant’s “episodes” are, film them for the doctor.

Questions for yourself

  • Whose voice does my guilt speak in — mine, my parents’, or a forum’s?
  • What do I consider normal for other people — and how do I know, if no one talks about it?
  • Is it hard for me because of what I do, or because it conflicts with my own rules?
  • Do I know my body well enough to tell a partner what works for me?

When to see a specialist

Loss of control with real damage to your life, pain or injury. Fatigue together with fear of “losing semen” — a reason to check for depression and anxiety. A flu-like state after every ejaculation. In a child — compulsive, harmful or age-inappropriate sexual behavior, especially against a background of abuse or sudden changes in the family.
